AUTHOR'S NOTE 

All characters, situations, names and incidents in this book are entirely imaginary and no reference therefore is either made or intended to any person living or dead, or to any one or anything which has actual existence. None of the scenes are taken from life, and if I have seemed to stress certain things, please remember that this is fiction. For all the organizations mentioned as well as the cities, countries, etc., I have the highest respect, and while in any place or time there may be occasionally something which one does not approve, it should be clearly understood that such things ought to be regarded as exceptions and not as the general run. The author alone takes the responsibility for whatever he says or writes herein—not a very heavy responsibility, it is true, since this is but a novel, yet one I hope you will enjoy.

As Constable Thomas Ingram Shields cast a glance over the rooftops visible from the window of the detectives' room of the Vancouver Criminal Investigation Branch of the Royal Canadian Mounted Police Force, the view which met his gaze made him pause a minute to reflect admiringly on its evidence of Canadian vitality and industry. It was a bright July afternoon of 1932 and Vancouver looked its best. Where but fifty years before there had been a single logging camp there now towered a great thriving seaport of 320,000 souls-Canada's third largest citynestled below a range of magnificent forested mountains that were typical of all British Columbia. Truly, the industrious beaver which graced the crest of the Canadian Pacific Railway stood as a symbol for Canada and Canadians in general, he reflected with pride. 

He quickly turned his attention once more to the task before him—that of making a tactful declaration that an Italian-born gentleman named Celona was unfit to become a Canadian citizen by naturalization, despite the fact that a well-known lawyer and a merchant of good repute had signed the routine slip as his sponsors. It was a ticklish proposition since Celona was one of the greatest haunt of vice operators on the whole Pacific Coast.

Footsteps sounded out in the corridor and presently in stalked Shields' immediate superior in the office, Corporal Bennett. "Uncoil your lanky six feet, Shields," Bennett advised him officiously. "Inspector Tweed wishes to see you. 

It's important." "Okay."

Tom rose to his full height and stretched, while Bennett regarded him with a mixture of envy and admiration.

What he could do with Shields' lithe, volatile strength and brains! Heredity had certainly given him a break in looks, too. Funny how lavish Nature can be on persons who don't know how to capitalize on their abilities, Bennett reflected, as Tom closed the door of the room and his footsteps trailed off down the corridor to Inspector 

Tweed's office. "Good morning, sir," Shields greeted his superior. "Good morning, Shields," Tweed responded brusquely.

There was a lack of congeniality in his tone and the set of his long aquiline features, however. "You did very well in your recruit's training when you joined the Force last year, Shields," Tweed reminded him. "You headed the class, in fact. You're a good athlete; university educated, very smart in appearance, I would say; but-"

Here Tweed paused and regarded Tom intently while Tom had the uneasy feeling of being the witness to a snake coiling its body prior to striking at him. "But a good policeman needs a supply of plain common sense ahead of all such things." "Are you suggesting I lack common sense, sir?" Tom asked frankly. Tweed evaded an answer. "I have your letter of the sixteenth here, Shields, addressed to me, regarding the Keufel and Hore-Sparling liquor interests.' "Yes, sir." 99 "You say here, Shields, that the Keufels have a fleet of fourteen fast gas boats, which leave Coal Harbour on a regular schedule with liquor, bound for the coast line of 

Washington, Oregon and California. Let's see: equipped with twin Packard motors, radio and Skoda machine guns, et cetera. How on earth could you possibly obtain all this information, Shields?" "I've been down to their Coal Harbour wharf several times under the name of John Davidson, trying to get a job as a wireless operator on one of their boats." "Well, did you see all this stuff with your own eyes, or are you just going by hearsay?" "I saw it all myself." "That's funny. I've had men go down there several

times straight from this office when different busybodies have come to us with this sort of report and not one of them has ever seen a thing to substantiate them." "Perhaps if they didn't drive down there in red serge tunics and boots and spurs-" Tom began, but Tweed reddened visibly and glowered at him. "Are you being impudent enough to criticize the Force, Shields?" "No, sir." "Well, you better not either." "Yes, sir.' 

"Here's something I absolutely don't understand, Shields: how in God's name did you manage to get all this information about the ownership of this alleged smuggling concern? You say the business is owned and conducted by the Keufels and the Hore-Sparling interests; two of the most influential and politically powerful families in western Canada. How could a fellow in your position possibly get to know that?" "The name of the outfit, sir, which runs the boats is the Pacific Coast Transit Company. I went down to the courthouse and paid the half-dollar scrutiny fee to look over their books. About eighty-five per cent of the stock in the concern is owned by the Keufels; the other fifteen per cent by the Hore-Sparlings." 

Tweed glowered straight ahead of him, opening and closing one hand in contemplative fashion. "The business of the enterprise is described in the courthouse records, sir, as shipping various goods, including liquor, down to Mexico in bond." "Well, and what the hell's that got to do with you or me, Shields? It's quite legal for anyone to do that, isn't it ?" "Yes, sir, but I've learned beyond any reasonable doubt from the crews of the boats that the stuff is unloaded along the United States coastline." "In that case I still say it's none of our damned business, Shields. I hope you're not suggesting that we do the police work for a lot of damned Yankees. They've

got that coastguard outfit they brag so much about. Why can't it look after such shipments?" "Because they're cleared from here with the legal stamp of approval of our government." "What's that got to do with it?" "I believe a whole lot, sir. If I'm not mistaken, the Canadian Government has undertaken to stop such shipments of liquor being smuggled into the United States. An international agreement was reached along those lines as a result of that rum-runner, the I'm Alone, being sunk." "Yes, Shields, but this stuff is apparently in legal bond and bound for Mexico.” 66 'Apparently is right, sir. Actually it is all bound for the United States. The Keufels ship enough stuff out of Coal Harbour in a month to add up to all the liquor Mexico imports from this country in a whole fiscal year." "How do you know that?" "I simply looked it up." "The Keufels must ship an awful lot of liquor then." "They do. Last night I sat up and figured it must all run to between thirty-five and forty million dollars since around 1926 and 1927.” "You're quite a clever young man to figure all those things out, Shields," Tweed said, suddenly changing his tone and swinging back reflectively in his swivel chair. "Ever hear tell of Shakespeare's 'Hamlet'?" "Yes, sir." "There was a young fellow who figured things out, too; wanted to dig down to the bottom of everything; even believed what ghosts told him. What happened to him? 

Well, he went bugs-clean bugs-first; then ended up on a slab like one of those stiffs you've seen in the morgue. 

It didn't get him any place in the long run. See? Shakespeare was a mighty wise old bird and understood life pretty well. Digging down too deep for the truth won't ever get you anywhere, Shields. It's a mighty rotten old world and you've got to make the best of it. Canada's no exception. "You know, it sometimes doesn't pay to scratch the surface of things too hard, because what you'll find under-

neath is liable to be mighty unpleasant. You may find that some of the big shots who run the community aren't so hot and that our system and politics are pretty rotten. 

Then what? One man like you or me can't clean things up. Then where are you? Not even back where you started, because things that looked and smelled so rosy before are liable to smell mighty strong of fertilizer once you get to know them. "Then, too, you can't go throwing your weight around at people like the Hore-Sparlings or Keufels without getting yourself in trouble. Take it from me, I don't care what they do. Old man Adolph Keufel is one of the biggest subscribers to campaign funds in both Dominion and 

Provincial politics. Don't you even know that? Why, the old boy was hard-boiled enough a few years ago to openly admit he subscribed to both the Liberal and Conservative campaign funds at the same election, just so's he'd be sure of having something on the winning horse." "I may be very dumb and unsophisticated, sir, but I don't see why that should alter the course of justice—just because people have money, I mean. 

"I'll say you're both dumb and unsophisticated, Shields. 'Just because people have money,' indeed! Why, that's the sort of a way a damned 'red' talks." "I beg your pardon, sir, but I'm no communist-" "I don't give a damn what you are, Shields. Just listen to me. When I was placed in this C.I.B. three years ago 

I was pretty nearly ruined by the Keufel crowd for trying to do just what you've got in mind. I only escaped losing my commission and being discharged from the Force, after eighteen years' service in it, by the skin of my teeth as a result. "I found out just what you've recently discovered two years ago, through a 'stool' named Harrison. I arranged to follow one of those Pacific Coast Transit boats down the coast and nab it as it turned into American coastal waters. Accordingly I had Harrison keep a lookout and chartered the fastest boat I could get to do the job in. I followed the Keufel boat down the coast just below Seattle and drew up alongside as they turned in. When we

boarded her we found old man Keufel and one of his sons on board with a party of friends. There wasn't a drop of liquor aboard, excepting what they were using among their guests. Someone-quite likely our own 'stool' Harrison-had tipped them off. The Keufels raised hell with the Government at Ottawa as a result and pretty nearly got me fired. They certainly hog-tied us over that and we got a very straight warning from Ottawa not to let that sort of thing happen again. "So, you see, it don't pay to play Hamlet, Shieldsnope, not on your tintype. In this old world virtue is lucky if it gets an even break with evil any day. Yes, sir, take it from me, I was lucky-very lucky. "They had a big government enquiry in 1926 into the Keufel liquor ring and it was spread around that they were going to put old man Keufel and his sons away for sure. Same as the United States Government went after 

Al Capone. They managed to get a former associate of 

Adolph Keufel, Jimmy Small, to testify against the big ring, but the Keufels were able to bring up enough other witnesses-money will do most anything—to refute his evidence. Consequence was, as soon as the enquiry was over, the Keufels got busy with crooked private detectives and framed Small on one of those things and put him away in a lunatic asylum-partly out of revenge; partly to discredit his evidence with the Government Commission. "The Keufels, Shields, will stop at nothing. They're the most dangerous and one of the most powerful families in Canada. If you try to check them you're just sticking your neck out. They're Polacks and plenty tough. Well educated, too." There was mingled fear and respect in 

Tweed's voice. "I understand, sir, that fifty thousand Canadian soldiers died in the Great War-supposedly for a better way of life." "So what, Shields?" "Just this, sir: do you think it's right to shrink from a job like nabbing a Polish racketeer and his gang who boss and corrupt the politics of this Canadian province, in view

of what our Canadian troops did? They were supposed to have fought that Germans would and could not do exactly what is happening anyway. Personally, I see no difference between being dictated to by a German or a Pole."

Tweed reddened and clenched and unclenched his fist. "I don't like that style of talk, Shields-I just don't like it. The Great War is a thing of the past-better forgotten completely." "Including the things that our troops are supposed to have fought for?" "See here, Shields, it isn't in the public interest to convict prominent persons like the Keufels or Hore-Sparlings of criminal offenses. When persons of that calibre are exposed as crooks—just supposing it were possible—public confidence in the status quo is weakened. Common, ordinary people, when they see a prominent man and church supporter, as some of these sort of people are, exposed as a law-breaker, get the idea that you've got to be crooked to get ahead in this world. See what I mean? 

It's sometimes best to let sleeping dogs lie. "That's what's the matter with the United States.

Everyone down there is out to debunk the big guys and clean society up. It can't be done. All they do is end up by finding out that some of their big shots once robbed a bank or went to reform school. Then where are they?

Nowhere. They've simply given their own confidence in human nature and their system of government a shakedown." "I still think they're right in that attitude, sir." "Why don't you become an American if you think that way, Shields? I'm too busy to discuss the matter further now.' 

When Tom returned to the C.I.B. offices late that afternoon, after calling on the manager of a branch bank to collect a counterfeit bill, Corporal Bennett welcomed him with a furtive smile that contained a queer mixture of condescension, sympathy and censure about it. "Shields," he dripped off his lips officiously, "you're not to bother coming down here tomorrow morning.

You're to report to the Sergeant-Major out at the barracks instead. You're going back on straight duty." "Who said I was? Why-?" "I don't know. Better ask Inspector Tweed." "I understand." "Too bad, Shields. I admire courage and brains and industry and all that sort of thing-but you know what police forces are. There're two cockney English inspectors on this force who couldn't write their names when they first enlisted. They've never been on an important case in their lives either. But they're good, strong imperialists-brass hats. So they just sat around on their rumps and waited for promotions to come along." "And looked the other way when big lawbreakers and corrupt politicians meant them to," Tom put in. "I know."

Corporal Bennett colored uneasily.

II 

STRAIGHT DUTY 

It is equivalent to a penal sentence, minus the social stigma, as Tom well knew, for a member of the Mounted Police, engaged on regular police duties, to be sent back to straight duty. The term is the official designation for an eternal round of stable fatigues, drills, riots, strikes and minor guard duties. It is given as a hard training to weed out and toughen up recruits; otherwise, as a hidden penalty to members on regular duty who make a faux pas.

Tom would not permit himself to believe that Inspector Tweed had had him removed from the C.I.B. on account of his investigation of the Keufel's activities-it shook his faith in the Force and even in Canada too muchpreferring, instead, to credit himself with lacking the requisite Tweed had mentioned, common sense. Then, one day, he was forced to make a sharp revision of this attitude. "Someone on the phone for yer, Shields," Corporal 

Symes, a cockney Englishman informed him. "E's bin winging for yer all morning. Come on, 'op on it, ye've bin on stwaight dooty a week now." "Is that Constable Tom Shields?" a slow, deliberate voice questioned Tom from the other end of the line. "Good. This is Hermann Keufel speaking. I'm the eldest son of Mister Adolph Keufel. My father and I are both very proud of our splendid Royal Canadian Mounted

Police and are very much interested in its keeping up its good name. We are especially interested in the large percentage of university men, like yourself, the Force is at present taking on and in its work of suppressing communism and other forms of radicalism that are alien to our British form of government and ideals." Tom de- 15

tected a guttural quality in his voice that was distinctly foreign, underlying his Canadian accent. "How about our Mounted Police Force catching a few of our big racketeers instead of indulging in red baiting?”"

Tom questioned unwisely. "I don't get you," Hermann Keufel retorted sharply. "Perhaps you're not interested in stringing along with the right people?" "No, I'm neither a fascist nor the owner of an itching palm." 

"I see." Hermann hung up abruptly.

When Tom returned to his barrack room he found Corporal Symes engaged in a heated discussion with two new recruits from Calgary, Paul Brooker and Bill Johnson. Symes' small frame and sandy hair was bristling with anger as he dogmatically stated: "You damned

Canoidians wants to get wise as to 'oo owns Canada. Us 

Bwitishers does an' just because we don't call yer a Cwown 

Colony it don't mean nufin'." "I resent that statement," Brooker told Symes forcefully. "I resent your attitude towards Canadians generally, too. You talk as if we were transient residents of a British penal colony." "Or Pitcairn Island," put in Johnson. "That's the way a couple of communistic young bostards would natuwally talk," Symes stormed at them. "Fwom now on, yer'll do nuffin' but scwub floors an' gwoom 'orses while I'm awound. I'll make yer wealize what wegimental woutine is weally like. I suppose yer'd both like to see the bwloody Stars an' Stwipes 'angin' over yer 'eads." "If you mean flying over this country-yes," Brooker answered, radiating a twinkle of amused contempt from his pair of hard, efficient blue eyes. He was a six-footer and built like a Greek god, excepting for his legs, which were slightly bowed from considerable riding. "Yes, sir," he added, winking at Johnson, "I had an uncle killed in the Great War, thanks largely to the cussedness of an Englishman like you," he told Symes, eyeing him keenly. "He was badly wounded in the knee at Vimy Ridge and

STRAIGHT DUTY 

17 was rushed back to a hospital behind the lines, where the doctors decided they'd have to amputate his leg. Owing to the shape he was in they didn't break the news to him and got his signature consenting to the operation by a trick. The morning the operation was to take place he lay in a daze, wondering why the attendants didn't bring him any breakfast as they were doing with the other hospital cases around him. On asking a cockney attendant, like Corporal Symes here, why this was so, he got the surly reply, 'Who d'yer think yer are, yer blinking Canoidian? They're taking orff yer leg at ten o'clock and we don't feed 'em wots going under the knife.' That was enough. Uncle Harry never came out of the anesthetic. 

We learned about it from one of the men who was lying in the same ward over there at the time." "And they expect us to go over there and fight for them again," said Johnson. "The hell we will." 

Symes' eyes glowered fiercely. "I'm goin' to weport you Bwooker and you Johnson and Shields to the Sergeant-Major for this," he spluttered angrily, looking more at Tom than the other two; whereupon he stomped off, banging his heels on the barrack floors, their noise trailing off loudly down the corridors. "What did he want to pick on me for?" Tom asked in amazement. "I didn't say a word.".

Maloney, an ex-professional British soldier-he was old for a constable in the R.C.M.P., being in his fortieslay on his barrack cot smoking a pipe. "Come 'ere Shields and you two, I'd 'ave a word wid you," he said. "Look 'ere, Shields, don't pay no attention to Symes. 'E's been out in India, like meself. Quite a few of us Englishmen who've been out there gits a wee bit queer at times. It's th' climate out there. I gits a little bit under 'er meself at times, but I knows it and when I feels 'er a coming on I just ups and goes out and gits good and plastered. You boys wants to watch your steps around 'im and old Sergeant Smokey 'Arris. There's a mean old cuss-been out in India, through the South African War, the last one, and just about the whole works.

You know what he's like, Shields. When that glary look comes into 'is or Symes' eyes, I figure it's 'igh time to steer clear of either of 'em. If either of 'em gits a grudge agin' a man it's just too bad for 'im. I've known of men to go clean out of their 'eads and commit suicide out in India, just through always being picked on by some officer or N.C.O. who didn't like 'em. It's the way the 'igher-ups 'as of taking it out on someone lower down in that part of the world and gettin' even with the country and climate for taking it out on them." "I must say he's a swell guy to promote to corporal, after less than six months' service with the Force," opined 

Brooker. "Too bad I wasn't born in England instead of

Canada-I'd be an inspector by now. There's something fishy about that beggar being promoted." "I'd suggest as you don't git Symes all 'ot and bothered about which flag flies over this 'ere country agin' any'ow," 

Maloney cautioned Brooker philosophically. "Why, 'ullo there, you old stool pigeon, Felix. 'Ow's the attic, cold?"

Maloney addressed the latter remark to a thick-set, pudgy man in civilian clothes who had sauntered up to them while he was speaking. "No, my room upstairs it is warm," the man responded. "Constable Shields, might I have a word with you alone?" "Me?" Tom hesitated. He knew the man only by sight and by reputation from the files at the C.I.B. office and was apprehensive that he wanted a loan. He was part 

German and part Polish and went by the name of Felix Fonck. He was employed as an undercover agent by the 

C.I.B. to spy upon radical groups, chiefly on the strength of his ability to speak three languages-German, Russian and French-fluently, and his record as a war-time spy in carrying out espionage against his native land, Germany, for the Allies. "What is it you want to see me about, Fonck?" "It is very urgent that I see you alone upstairs in my room," the man insisted.

There was something in his tone which impelled Tom to grant the interview as requested.

STRAIGHT DUTY 

19 "All right, but I'll have to be back in time to get on parade.' 99 Tom followed him up a narrow flight of winding stairs to the attic of the building and found himself in a small room containing a single cot, table and chair and some of the less agreeable body odors. On the table were spread some papers and odds and ends. Fonck sprawled on the cot and motioned to Tom to be seated in the chair. "Sit down, will you not, Shields," he invited Tom very politely. "Have a cigarette? Nein? You know, Shields,

I speak excellent French, Rusky, know a little Spanish, and German, of course, is my native tongue. My English is what you call-" "Not bad," said Tom. "Yes, not bad, perhaps but then, also not so good. Nein?" "Is that what you wanted to talk to me about?" "That and more important things, Shields. You see, I like you and feel I can trust you. You are a university man, n'est ce pas? Ja, I thought so. It is like this: I have many important reports to write out and would like some help with the English. In two, say three, years I write as good English as you. That is a faculty we Germans have of doing things just a little better than the other man, once we learn how. Ja? Nein? I don't mean the Saxons or Bavarians, they are only human." Fonck gave a halfcontemptuous shrug. "But the Prussians! I am a Prussian! Ja!" "And yet you-er-spied against your country in the 

Great War-" Tom let out with surprise. 

The German gave an embarrassed shrug. "That is a part of my life I discuss with no one, Shields," Fonck explained. "Never. It is what you Englanders and Canadians sometimes call a closed chapter, n'est ce pas? I will pay you well for helping me, if you so wish. The Force pays me well for my work." "Forget about the pay," Tom said a trifle gruffly. "I don't mind helping you, if it doesn't take too much of my time. What is the nature of these reports you want me to look over?"

"The activities of the Communist Party of Canada, the 

Canadian Labor Defense League and the Workers' Unity 

League." "Red outfits, eh?" "Ja." Fonck leaned forward on his cot and his voice fell to a whisper. "They are receiving money and instructions direct from Moscow and plan to start a revolution here in Vancouver. Soon, too. They have much support, even from the returned soldiers." "Sounds funny to me," Tom pondered. "I know some of the local reds well; in fact, I'm no tory myself; and most of them haven't enough guts to come in out of the rain unless the Government ran a shelter up over their heads. As for the returned soldiers, my dad happens to be one, although I'll admit he's an ex-officer and a diehard tory, and I can't see any organizations turning down Communism quicker than the Canadian and British legions. What makes you think that returned men are in on that stuff?" "I have proof, Shields." "It's got to be pretty good proof to convince me." "I have been seeing things happen with my own eyes,

Shields things I never would have thought would happen in a country like Canada. They might happen in 

Germany and in Russia-ja, always-but I never would have thought so in this country. Remember, it is now many years since the war and returned soldiers have stomachs to feed, just like any other men, and their wives and children have stomachs to get hungry with, just like any other women and children. If you were an old soldier, would you sit down and whistle 'God Save der King' while your wife and kids were sick and starving? Hunger and want will break down any loyalties. Ja. Some of your war veterans are plotting against the Government more than anyone else these days and they know how to shoot and face tear gas bombs and even death. Read this."

Tom took a manuscript held out to him by Fonck and began reading it slowly. It ran as follows:

STRAIGHT DUTY 

21 "The Officer Commanding, Vancouver, B. C. 1932 "R. C. M. Police, "'E' Division, Vancouver, B. C. "Sir: 

"Re: Canadian Labor Defense League, Work ers' Unity League and the Canadian Communist Party. "I have the honor to report that, acting on instructions received from you, I, Special Constable Felix Fonck, have been covering the activities of the above-named organizations from the evening of the 16th, ultimo on... "Why this is perfect English, Fonck," Tom protested with surprise. "I don't write any better myself. Why ask me to make corrections in a report like this?" "Yes, I know, Shields, but I copied all that first wording from a report just like mine that they showed me in the orderly room, which another man had turned in. I found out what ultimo meant from the sergeant."

He made the last statement in a wistful tone which made Tom almost like him. Tom continued reading: ". . . At the last meeting of the Canadian Labor Defense League I was in attendance at two days ago, these peoples decided to support a strike of mill workers out at New Westminstur (Westminster). They spent much time also discussing the exercution of two mens. 

Sacko and Vanseti (Sacco and Vanzetti), in the United States some years ago. They said many bad things about the Capitelist system and called the Mounted Police bad names. One of them got up and called the Prime Ministur of Canada an old crook. Another expressed the opinion that King George should abdikate and leave the British Isles free to become a Soviet republic. He spoke of the King as old mister George Winser. Most of those present seemed to be of the hobo class and were poorley dressed with, pirhaps, one or two of the real strikers amongst them. The speakers names, as nearly as I could make out, were Levinsky, Jorgensen and Smith. Levin-

sky spoke for a time in low Russian, which I could only understood in some places; otherwise he spoke broken 

English. Jorgensen spoke mostly in English, good and clearly, with a little Scandanavian in places. Smith spoke always in English. I counted the attendanse at around 

80 or 90. "After some difficulty, I managed to get into a meeting of the Canadian Communist Party, in the Royale Theatre, last night. It was a most very important meeting it turned out. There was nearly one thousand peoples present and they sang the Red Flag at the start, with much loudness and enthusiasm. Then one speaker, Cambell, got up like a preacher and told them that the day of wreckoning was near at hand when they would not be bothered with any more government or taxes or policemens and could go about their business in peace under the red flag like peoples do in Soviet Russia. He said that Vancouver was the best place on the whole North American continint to start a revolusion and that as soon as Canada became a soviet republic the United States could not help but follow the good example. He spoke at length on how well the little childrens were looked after in Soviet Russia by the state and what fine citisens they would make when they grew up. Someone asked him how long he had been in the Soviet Unions and he answered that he had not been there yet, but hoped to go there just as soon as he could pay his way. He had most of the womens in the audience crying as he told them about a lullaby the Russian peasant womens sanged their babies to sleep with. When asked by one of the audience if it wasn't true that babies were taken away from their mothers and cared for by the state over there, he broke down and began to cry himself and was carried off the stage in some kind of a fit, I believe. "The next speaker, called Huston or Muston, waved his arms in the air and rolled his eyes and repeated what the first speaker had said about a day of wreckoning having come. He seems a dangerous man to lead a crowd. He said that certain supplys (firearms?) that would help free the worker and unemploied worker from human bondage were on their way up to Vancouver from California and

STRAIGHT DUTY 

23 would be in town any day. He said they had two thousand returned soldiers, who knew what it meant to face hell and shell fire for the lousy capitelists and armament mungers, now ready to fight for the real cause of freeing the down-trodden working man from his oppressor, the slave-owning capitelist, who lived at Shawnesy heights and the West end of town and drove about in a big limousine, raping the daughters of working mens. He asked any returned soldiers in the audience to stand up and fifty or sixty mens stood up. When he asked them if they would back him up, they said 'yes' in a loud choirus. He finished up by saying that it wouldn't be long now before all the fine houses at Shawnesy heights and the West end belonged to them, the people who deserved them, instead of the lousy capitelists. 

"The next, and last, speaker was called Branca or

Blanca, or something. The cheerman, who introduced him, only mumbled the name.

This man made numerous references to Carl Marx and Rousseau. He called Marx's 

Das Capital (which I have read) a far more important book than the Bible and one that every true communist and socialist should know off by heart. He said that

Moses had no sence of humor when he took down the Ten Commandments and got the signels 'balled up' coming over the air. He seemed a learned sort of man. He ended up his speech by running down the present system and government. He afterwards got up again and suggested that the meeting adopt as a slogan, 'A firing squad for the Governor General of Canada and the gelatine for R. B. Bennet.' I think the resolution was passed, since there were a few 'eyes' and no 'neighs.' There was a collection taken which someone said amounted to $40.68 from the platform. Some copies of the Unemployed 

Worker were sold. "I have the honour to be, Sir, "Your obedient servant, 

"S/C FELIX FONCK." 

"You mean guillotine and not gelatine, I think," Tom remarked. "It's spelt G-U-I-L-L-O-T-I-N-E, if I'm not

mistaken. There are a bunch of other mistakes in spelling and grammar-chiefly spelling-as well. On the whole, though, it isn't bad, but I'll have to revise my statement that it's better than I could do-at least a bit, anyway. It's certainly pretty hot stuff-especially that about bringing the arms and supplies up here from California. There may be some truth in it, too." "And you will fix it up for me, Shields?" "Why sure, it won't take me more than five minutes." "Thank you, Tom. Maybe you will be doing something very important. Maybe the O.C., he will be glad to read it tomorrow morning. Nein? Ya? I think so."

III MAN'S STUPIDITY TOWARDS MAN 

A big riot is a colorful spectacle, if viewed from the safe precincts of an office window eight or nine stories above the level of hostilities. From a closer range, however, the pleasure derived from the spectacle is apt to follow the law of diminishing returns.

A well-trained, mounted riot squad, such as those turned out by the Mounted Police, will charge a mob in V-formation, with the point of the V, an expert horseman, penetrating the crowd first. There is a full troop of thirtytwo mounted men in the V and it hits the foremost members of the mob at a light canter or a brisk trot, when properly executed. Of those individuals not immediately affected by the charge, but mixed up, farther back, with the crowd, some are able to find their way into a welcoming doorway. Others are not so fortunate. In a crowd of eight thousand people packed into the confines of a rectangle, the width of a city street and the length of about two blocks the average individual has not a great degree of freedom. If he is lucky, he may be near a door leading onto the street; but, even so, the odds are still against him, for cautious merchants have a way of closing shop and locking such doors in the event of a riot brewing. Be he agitator or curious bystander in the crowd, once the trouble starts, he is apt to find himself swept up in a common maelstrom of primitive flight. In such cases, locked doors are smashed in by brute force by hysterical members of the crowd seeking shelter and plate glass windows are crushed in by the high compressive forces set up in a mass of elastic, resilient humanity pressing on the outside of the glass.

The batons in the hands of the mounted policemen work with the speed and fury of violin bows in a symphony 25

orchestra playing Liszt's Hungarian Rhapsody Number 

Two, while a few poorly-aimed missles hurtle lazily through the air. Soon scores are lying on the pavements and street car tracks, unconscious and bleeding. 

In the case of one of the unemployed riots which took place in Vancouver not long after Tom was relieved of his position at the C.I.B., the charge led down Hastings 

Street from in front of the Cenotaph erected in honor of those men killed in the Great War. Many of their living comrades were among the crowd, but that made little difference; the charge must go on. It costs little to honor the dead with monuments and beautiful dedicatory epistles; it costs a lot to sustain idle, hungry bodies with food and clothe them, however. On this subtle premise much of the sentimental superstructure of British idealism hangs. Such acts as erecting a large monument to Lord 

Nelson and then allowing the woman who had been his mistress and inspiration to die in poverty in an old attic are characteristic of this psychology, which has been handed on down to the present generation. 

The crowd retreats before the charge of the red coats to a tall building which houses the general offices and draughting rooms of the British Columbia Electric Railway Company upstairs and a station for its interurban trains on the street level. At the station, part of the crowd wisely disperses down an alley and a side street, but at least a half, panic-stricken and following the example of a group of its members, swarms into the station. At a command from an inspector the mounties swing off Hastings Street into the depot in full formation.

One mountie, the cockney Symes who, at the start, has lagged nervously behind his comrades, is right up near the front now that the crowd is obviously on the run, brandishing his long baton romantically. He has become jubiliantly, hysterically enthusiastic over the proceedings and is thwacking any and all pedestrians with a furious, sadistic abandon. He has just clubbed a hatless, fat woman, with dishevelled hair and a torn dress, into unconsciousness by dealing her several blows over the head with his steel-cored, fibre baton and is about to deal in a similar

MAN'S STUPIDITY TOWARDS MAN 27 

manner with her ten-year-old son who is clutching at his prostrate mother's twitching fingers, too terrified to utter a sound or duck. He is only prevented from accomplishing this end by the strong fingers and flexible wrist of Brooker's right hand grasping hold of his arm. "Go easy, Symes, this isn't India," Paul shouts in his ear.

The crowd is packed on the concrete platforms of the station like the proverbial sardines in a can without possessing the advantages of having a lubricant like olive oil between them. The impact of the charge is so great that many of those at the far end of the building are thrust out at the other end of it like a mass of figures in one of Walter Disney's Silly Symphonies, and are scattered all over the railway tracks of the interurban railway yards at the rear. In less than two minutes three or four thousand people have been pushed or catapulted through the station in a wild scene possessing all the elements of a big clown act-to the neutral spectator viewing it from an upstairs office window. Apparently that is how some of the news hawks have obtained their impression of the Hastings Street fracas, for the newspapers refer to it humorously as the Battle of Hastings. Some of the more serious aspects of the affair are overlooked. Scores have been injured, some of whom are nursing serious injuries secretly at home; scores of others are under arrest in the city gaol. 

The second Battle of Hastings, while not as historically important as the original staged in 1066 A.D., has broken the back of what there was of an open Communist movement in western Canada, for even men willing to die for a cause will balk at being pushed through a plate glass window for it. From now on, good radicals have to humiliate themselves by going under the label of socialists, united fronters, social creditors or co-operative commonwealthers to attract an audience. This means reading at least two or three books and perusing a lot of pendantically-worded literature in pamphlet form; for whereas 

Communism is a simple, childish creed, on a par with believing in Santa Claus-the younger one's mental age,

the nobler and more solid the appeal-such popular fallacies as social credit are apt to be sugar-coated and concealed in so many high-sounding, verbose phrases by their authors that they often make difficult reading for even the educated.

One section of radical sentiment is not cowed by the result of the Hastings Street riot. This is the radical wing of the Great War veterans. These men who have faced 

German shell-fire and poison gas for three or four years overseas are not to be frightened by a policeman mounted on a horse. Shortly after the riot a large group of them hold a meeting and decide to stage a parade to Shaughnessy Military Hospital to protest a scaling down of veterans' wound pensions by the Government. 

One of them, a man who has sustained serious head injuries fighting for his country, becomes emotionally carried away by it all and suggests burning down the stables at the R.C.M.P. barracks as a reprisal for the cuts and bruises received by war veterans in the Hastings Street fracas. Bitter memories of the mounted charge bring forth loud applause from the audience at this suggestion. 

When the Mounted Police stool-pigeons at the meeting report this incident to the officer commanding "E" Division, ten men, with loaded revolvers, are placed on night guard at Fairmont barracks every evening instead of the usual two. 

The morning following the Hastings Street fracas, Sergeant Smokey Harris delivered a lecture to the straight duty class on how to conduct themselves under similar circumstances in the future. "Your conduct was splendid," he began, "despite the fact that many of you are green at the job. You gave those reds hell." "Isn't it possible some of them were innocent bystanders, sergeant?" Paul Brooker questioned him. "I'm ashamed at a member of the Force asking such a question," Harris responded severely. "When I joined the Force in 1898 as a trumpeter-a mere boy in my 'teens -we accepted all our duties without question. When the

MAN'S STUPIDITY TOWARDS MAN 29 

Boer War broke out most of my set went over to South Africa and helped beat hell out of the Boers. We didn't stop to ask if any of those square heads were innocent bystanders or not; we just gave 'em what was coming to them. Automobiles, movies and radios have softened you younger generation up. You've become soft and Americanized going to sob-stuff movies and listening to Yankee radio programs. If I had my way, it would be illegal for a loyal Britisher to go to an American movie or tune in one of their radio programs. To a straight-thinking policeman a man only falls into two classes: he's either on the side of the law or against it. All Communists and other reds are criminals under section ninety-eight of our 

Criminal Code." 

"What about freedom of speech?" Constable Johnson asked. "In theory it's fine, Johnson, but I'm a practical man myself. Thank God, with the Conservative Party in power we have a practical government, too. Now to get down to business: it seems that the war veterans are letting themselves be led along by a bunch of dirty, yellow reds. Nothing to smile at in that. Most reds are both dirty and yellow. I feel deeply mortified over it all, as 

I'm a returned man myself." "The old hypocrite forgets to mention that he never saw any fighting overseas," Brooker whispered in Tom's ear. "He was a military policeman-one of the hated non-combatant M.P.'s." "They say that the Union Jack is to head the procession," Sergeant Harris continued, "when the veterans parade to the Shaughnessy Military Hospital in an attempt to blackmail the Government into raising their already fat pensions." "Their pensions are so fat," Paul whispered, "that some of them get less than ten dollars a month for war disabilities that prevent them doing another day's work." "But the Union Jack doesn't prevent the parade from being a Communist gathering in disguise," Harris decided dogmatically. "I hear the vets are going to wear their war medals, which means that a lot of my old friends

will have to visit the hock shops downtown. Their piece de resistance" (Smokey smacked his lips as he let go the phrase defiantly at the class, many of whom he suspected regarded him as an unlettered, bullying old drunkard, who had twice been demoted for his love of the bottle) "is a man who was awarded the V. C. during the Great War. 

He's a low-down, good-for-nothing fellow, who whines all the time about what the war did to him and others just like him and sponges off all and sundry who will give him a drink. He's a sort of leader and if there's any monkey business starts, I want you to lay into him hard.” "Let me take care of the wotten boundar, Sergeant,"

Corporal Symes volunteered gallantly. 

Smokey threw Symes an approving glance. "Don't go too hard on the poor old blighters" he cautioned none too severely. "Those batons of yours have a steel core and some of the vets have silver plates in their numb skulls, as a result of war wounds. Steel is harder than silver, I understand. So take it easy unless they forget themselves." "Very well, sergeant," Corporal Symes acquiesced respect fully. "That yes-man, Symes, gives me a pain," Brooker whispered to Tom. "Men like that get promoted in this force because it's run by officious Englishmen who can only get along with their own type. Any old tramp from the slums of London or Liverpool gets promoted ahead of a Canadian with a university degree in this outfit." 

Paul's whisper had risen in a crescendo as he spoke, until it came close to being of the footlight variety, and Sergeant 

Harris suddenly stopped lecturing to stand looking at him sternly with his hands on his hips. "Another stage whisper out of you, Brooker, and you'd have landed yourself in the guard room," Smokey informed him, glaring at him with a pair of dilated eyes which held a queer mixture of authority and alcoholic dissipation.

After the lecture, Paul took Tom to one side. "This red-baiting stuff nauseates me, Tom," he admitted frankly, "there's something phoney about the whole business. 

Communists are about as plentiful in Canada as cigar store

MAN'S STUPIDITY TOWARDS MAN 31 

Indians. It's as if some of the more ruthless big shots are building up a bogey man, for a witch hunt, to side-track public attention from more vital matters and, perhaps, even put over some form of Fascism on the Canadian people. "I don't know, Paul, but I've often had some such thought in the back of my mind. I never felt vital and real somehow when we were beating up those mobs downtown. I felt as if I were committing the crime of assault and battery, rather than serving society. I felt guilty and brutal in my subconscious mind." "Me too. Has it ever occurred to you, Tom, that those stool-pigeons who cover alleged radical meetings are merely unemployed spies whose salaries and expense accounts cease the moment they fail to report anything sensational?" The usual twinkle in Brooker's blue eyes had given way to a serious steadfastness. "That of itself, is a hidden bribe to them to tell lies and exaggerate and to act as agents provocateur for inciting riots. Take Felix 

Fonck, for instance. There you have a typical lower type foreigner-he's half German, half Polish-who could, perhaps, make a bare living at manual labor. Do you have any idea what he gets paid for playing the role of Judas Iscariot?" "I saw one of his monthly expense accounts down at the C.I.B. once," Tom admitted uneasily. "It ran to about three hundred dollars. But Fonck seems sincere-" "Three hundred dollars! Why some business executives don't get that much, Tom. As for the alleged danger attached to such espionage: there just isn't any, because the stool pigeons are on the right side of the law. Added to that, the people who attend those alleged radical meetings are often respectable and go there out of disgust at governmental graft and brazen favoritism in the Civil Service. Therefore, the absurdly high outlay for such work is simply a veiled bribe by the party in power to scare the public by a straw bogey man, the red menace, and keep their thoughts off more pressing domestic problems. Our Canadian Government has gone into the business of sorting out packages containing thirty pieces of

silver for a gang of hired Judas Iscariots. The red menace, indeed! Are you game to snoop on Fonck in our spare time, Tom-follow him to one of those meetings he covers and see for ourselves what really happens there?" "Yes and I've got a better idea than shadowing him like a couple of vaudeville detectives. I correct the spelling and grammar of some of his secret reports that go into the O. C. I've got to know him quite well on that account and can probably find out what meeting he's covering tonight. I believe it's an important one, too. We can go there independently of him then and won't run the risk of arousing his suspicions.' 

"We don't want him to recognize us at the meeting, Tom." "No. I'll borrow a couple of disguises from one of the detectives around supper time."

IV THE SHADOW OF THE KREMLIN 

That evening Tom borrowed two sets of disguises from one of the regular plain-clothes members of the force who lived in barracks. They had been packed away in moth balls in the bottom of a trunk for some months and smelled accordingly. When he and Paul had put them on and dressed in two shabby, misfit suits of clothes, they were open to the criticism that they looked too conspicuously "down-at-heel" not to attract some attention even at an unemployed gathering.

They were conscious of this as soon as they arrived at the meeting and were met by stares of speculation. 

Their whiskers, especially, seemed to attract attention and cause puzzled looks to appear on the faces of some of the gathering, as if wondering if they were brethren imported direct from Moscow, or representatives of the House of David. 

One old chap, whose breath smelt strongly of garlic and decaying molars as he spoke, came over to where they were seated and commenced speaking to Tom in a foreign dialect. Tom nodded at everything he said until the nape of his neck began to ache. He had been taught French in high school and at university and could recognize the cadences of Italian, German, Spanish and Chinook when he heard them spoken. He concluded, therefore, by a process of elimination, that the man might be speaking 

Russian. The old fellow finally stopped speaking and waited for Tom to say something.

There was a large, cheap print of Lenin on one of the walls of the hall, probably cut from a poster of some sort. 

Tom suddenly went down on his knees with a look of reverence on his face and stared up at the image of the man who had helped make history, completely ignoring 33

the mere mortal sitting beside him with the decayed molars. Someone close at hand whispered loudly: "Gee that guy sure knows his communism!" and Tom arose greatly relieved to find that the faulty set of teeth had moved away from him, three or four seats, with a puzzled expression on his face.

Paul suddenly nudged Tom's elbow as the chairman of the meeting arose from his seat to call the meeting to order. He was a young Scotchman of medium height, with brushed back hair and the air of a preacher of the gospel about him. He rolled his Rs very noticeably. "Brethren," he began benignly, "our subject for this evening, after being chosen with our usual forethought, has been divided into two parts. Brother Smith, who is well known to you all, will analyse, or attempt to analyse in the brief time at his disposal, Karl Marx's Labor Theory of Value. Later in the evening, we will be privileged to hear an address from Brother Jameson, who, while he is a newcomer to our midst, is an old-timer as far as Vancouver and the West Coast is concerned. Brother 

Jameson will discuss as his topic the inherent weaknesses of the system under which we are at present living, or rather existing. After each address, questions will be in order for five or ten minutes and the speakers will attempt to answer them to the best of their ability. By the by, I see we have two new faces in our midst, brethren. We're always glad to welcome strangers from afar into the fold. 

Will the two bearded gentlemen over there please stand up? Come, boys, don't be bashful; you're out of the icy blasts of the cruel Capitalists' World while you're in this hall. You're among friends. Come on brothers and sisters, let's give them a big hand."

Tom and Paul stood up rather sheepishly and received an amazingly hearty and stupid burst of applause. They caught a glimpse of Felix Fonck as they did so. He was sitting near the front of the hall, his chin resting on one of his hands, and eyeing them intently. He didn't seem to recognize either of them, however. 

Paul garbled something about a day of reckoning being nigh and struck a Rasputin-like pose amid a fresh burst

THE SHADOW OF THE KREMLIN 

35 of applause. In the midst of the applause he turned to 

Tom and whispered in his ear: "This bloody set of horse's tail feathers smells too much of moth balls." As they sat down, several of the audience seemed to be speculating on the important, whispered secret which had passed between the two wonderfully disreputable-looking brothers. Were they cogitating on a red-blooded, Canadian "taking of the Bastille"?

Smith, the first speaker, spoke in a scholarly and reserved manner on one phase of Karl Marx's writings, to wit, his Labor Theory of Value. He denied that Marx was out of date and made the assertion that most people who wrote about the old maestro deliberately catered to a capitalist public by magnifying his weak points, while they touched all too lightly on his strong arguments. The net result was a subtle attack on the man they were ostensibly eulogizing.

After Smith had answered a few colorless, nervous questions posed by members of the audience, the chairman called on the second speaker of the evening, Jameson. 

This man criticised the party system in Canadian politics and the way in which he alleged the Canadian people were being plundered by a group of financial racketeers. He finished with an assault on the Capitalist system in general, which was somewhat emotional and vague in its scope. One thing he said, however, struck Tom as being rather good: "The ideal of any system of society should be to make men and women and not to make money!" Jameson thundered. "Since the War, our whole system has degenerated into a scramble for the accursed dollar. We need to destroy this present set-up, or it will eventually destroy us; we need to isolate it, like leprosy, to the most backward countries of Asia and Europe only. The most valuable personal qualities under the Capitalist system are greed and the ruthless determination to trample the man who stands in your way under foot. We must eventually get down to a system of cooperation. That system is socialism and it is the only system that will get

the world, the worker of the world and the unemployed worker of the world out of this accursed muddle." 

When he had finished, several questions were asked and answered on the topic of the address. Suddenly an individual sprang to his feet and strode on to the platform with raised arms. "Listen, friends and fellow socialists, if I may call you such," he said in a slightly German accent, with the gesticulations of a born mobster, "you have just heard brother

Jameson say that our only way out of this terrible muddle is through socialism. I agree with him that our only possible salvation is through socialism; but, dear friends,

I ask you how can the socialist state be brought about in 

Canada? Not by talking about it. No. Nor by praying for it. No. What would Lenin's answer have been to wasting time on idle prayers? Possibly by leaving it to the other fellow then? Oh yeah? How then, I ask you? 

By action and action alone, dear friends, that is my answer. "We must get out on the streets and fight for our rights; go out and meet the blows of the yellow-legged 

Mounted Police with our more earnest and numerous blows; meet their gun-fire which killed those three unhappy miners at Estevan, also with gun-fire. That is the only way to get anywhere in this world, my friends-by using force and violence, even as our Capitalist overlords use it through the agencies of the corrupt police and courts which they own; by blasting our way, even as 

Mister R. B. Bennett and others believe in blasting theirs. 

We must push the accursed Capitalist, with his lousy coupon-clipping sessions, off the face of this earth, before the words liberty, equality and fraternity mean anything but just a line each in the dictionary. I have spoken." 

He struck a Lenin-like pose and assumed almost as good a poker face as that on the features of the large, coarse print on the wall, before sitting down amid feeble applause. "Felix Fonck!" Tom breathed with amazement in 

Paul's ear.

Paul seemed no less surprised than he, however, and leaned forward in his seat, staring silently at what was still

THE SHADOW OF THE KREMLIN 

37 visible of the thick-set Teuton's figure after he had sat down. "Our friend, brother Klutz," the chairman said, rising and smiling uneasily, "is what I'd style a direct actionist. 

I admire his enthusiasm much more than the means he advocates for bringing about a social change. I, personally, advocate and believe in, adhering to Constitutional means, rather than violent ones, in obtaining concessions for the masses living under the democratic form of government. Bloodshed and strife....

Tom and Paul slipped quietly out of the meeting, along with a few dozen others, as some girls with red ribbons in their hair began to rattle tin boxes containing coppers and a few nickels and dimes. The metallic rattling of the boxes was like a bugle sounding retreat to the ears of the less pecunious "brethren" it seemed. They walked along in silence for a time in the cool night air; then Paul broke the silence with: "Judas was a piker compared to that fellow. Judas only chiseled thirty pieces of silver for doing a really big job, while this Fonck really goes to town with his three hundred a month expense account for doing what he does-framing a bunch of half-witted reds. What do you think of it all, Tom?" "I think it would be a good thing if our Canadian politicians and press, instead of blabbing about an imaginary red menace, spent a little time fishing for the truth. They'd probably find that all the strikes in Canada are not the work of red agitators; but a few, at least, can be laid at the front door of greedy employers who pay married men, with a family, twenty cents an hour for hard, manual labor." "They're not interested in the truth when it points that way, Tom."

After they got back to barracks and checked in at the guard room, Tom went upstairs to Fonck's quarters. He found the German just getting into bed. He seemed surprised to see him, probably because it was well after lights out, but welcomed him with usual affability. Tom noted that he suddenly assumed an air of gravity which

was directly opposite to a relaxed, complacent one about him when he had first entered the room. "You look quite serious, Felix. Run into a tough meeting tonight?" "Did I, Tom? Why you should have just seen it. I have never been to a meeting like it before in my life; even in Russia or Poland. No, I swear I haven't. The speakers they get up and they shout: 'Down with the

Government,' 'To hell with the Government,' 'We want old Bennett's head put on the Cenotaph in Victory Square,' 'Send the Governor General's head back to old King

George in a box with ribbons on it, on his next birthday. 

Why, Tom, they were the most terrible peoples I have ever seen. But I have not told all yet. "There were two tall, evil-looking mens there with long, dark beards. I know them both. They are cousins and close friends of Maxim Litvinoff. I have met them before -once in Moscow during the second Bolshevik terror and again in Harbin, China, when the Soviet Third Internationale was attempting to turn Northern China communist. Who they are, no one knows, but wherever they go, there is troubles-revolution. They were known by 

Americans and English in the Far East as the Red Dust twins, because they spread the red doctrines, like a pepper shaker, wherever they went." "Full of pep, eh?" Tom yawned. "It is no joking matter, Tom." "No, I agree that it is no joking matter, Felix. What then?" "Well after the two bearded mens had been introduced to the meeting—" "That's the two pepper shaking-I mean gold dust, or rather red dust, twins, you mean. 99 "Yes, I mean the pepper—at least the Red Dust twins. 

What th' hell, Tom, you are getting me all balled up.

Anyway, after they had been introduced, one of them said that a day of reckoning was near at hand, in a way that made everyone in the hall tremble-even myself." "Your spoken English is improving every day, Felixquite marvellous."

THE SHADOW OF THE KREMLIN 

39 "Thank you, Tom. Anyway, the two of them then whispered some secrets and I learned that it was about the date that the big revolution is to start in Vancouver. 

The man who told me was near to them at the time. He has good hearing." "Yes, he must have." "I'm going to make out the report on it now, Tom. 

Will you give me a hand?" "No, not tonight, Josephine," Tom yawned, looking away from the German to conceal his contempt. "I'm too tired. Perhaps tomorrow morning, if there's time."

In the morning he and Paul went to see the Divisional 

Sergeant-Major and asked him to parade them before the Commanding Officer. The top N.C.O. made a jotting in a note book and told them to be ready at ten o'clock sharp, dressed in riding breeches and red serge tunics. "Red Dust twins, indeed!" Paul let out. "We ought to put those whiskers back on when we appear for orderly room, just to see the expression on Fonck's face." 

A short time afterwards, R.C.M.P. General Orders read, in part, as follows: 

Changes in personnel to date:

Dismissed: S/Const. Fonck, Felix.

THE RIDDLE OF INSPECTOR TWEED 

The morning after the dismissal of special operative Fonck, Inspector Tweed came up to Fairmont barracks to deliver a lecture on the Opium and Narcotics' Drug Act. He delivered it in an interesting and high-class manner that Tom had to admit, despite his prejudices, was even up to university standards. Tweed wore his force officer's dress uniform for the occasion which, strangely enough, is a navy blue, not scarlet, and closely resembles that of a naval officer and was a striking, though somewhat cadaverous, figure in it. He concluded the lecture with a romantic and rather adolescent eulogy on the force and how it almost "always got its man' that bore a striking contrast to the cynical and compromising attitude he had adopted at the C.I.B. office that morning when he had interviewed Tom. Tom couldn't help scanning the 

Inspector's face curiously and was soon aware of the fact that Tweed deliberately avoided his gaze. 99 After the lecture was over and the class had returned to their barrack rooms, prior to going to stable parade, Corporal Symes entered the room where Tom had his cot and barked his name out in a rasping, excited tenor. "What is it?" Tom asked. "Inspector Tweed wants to see yer downstairs," Symes informed him. "C'm on, 'op on it."

Tweed met him in the hall downstairs and led him into an office, where they were alone. "Apparently, Shields, my talks to you downtown fell on deaf ears," Tweed began with an obvious restraint, his gaunt, six-foot frame quivering slightly. "I don't quite understand you," Tom stated frankly, taken aback. 40

THE RIDDLE OF INSPECTOR TWEED 41 

"Do you remember what I told you about not trying too hard to pry the lid off everything?" "Why yes, sir." "Then why haven't you heeded that advice? Isn't it enough to be back acting as nursemaid to horses, rather than holding down a responsible and interesting position at our C.I.B. office?" 

Tom could say nothing. "When you discovered Fonck wasn't on the level, couldn't you just as easily have come to me first, rather than going over my head to the O.C.?" "Like I did with the Keufel liquor ring, I suppose," 

Tom said bitterly. 

Tweed reddened visibly. "None of your damned sarcasm, Shields. You knew I had engaged Fonck and was handling his expense account, didn't you?" "Why no, sir, I did not." "Well look out from now on, anyway, Shields. All 

I've got to say is, you've very definitely gotten off on the wrong foot around here as far as I'm concerned and 

I'm through with you.' 99 

As Shields left the office, Sergeant Harris came straddling down the hall on his horse-bandied legs, looking sterner than usual and knocked on the same door. As 

Tom went upstairs, he vanished inside the room. 

At afternoon stables, Tom had an uneasy feeling that Sergeant Harris was, every so often, eyeing him distastefully. On several occasions he stood right over him for a whole minute on end (as he might have done with the most inefficient of the new recruits) and swished his riding crop restively. When Smokey swished his whip in that manner, Tom had learned that his temper was up over something and that trouble was usually brewing for somebody. It was as sure a sign as the coiling of a snake prior to striking. Tom had so far been lucky enough to avoid being the object of one of Smokey's irrational fits of temper, but there was no telling when the winning streak might end.

Near the end of the stable period, he began to lead a restive stallion, graphically named Dynamite, to a water

trough. The animal became frightened at the swish of 

Harris' riding crop and began to lash out with its hind legs. With one furious kick it knocked two planks out of a stable partition and, before Tom had time to get matters under control again, Harris rushed up to him, his cheeks flushed, eyes glaring crazily, and shouted at him to quiet the horse down. 

The shouting further frightened Dynamite; and Tom, sensing that Harris was deliberately making it difficult for him to control the beast, let go of the lead rope attached to its halter. The animal bolted down the stable aisle, knocking over Corporal Symes and causing general pandemonium amongst the other horses. He was finally caught and placed back in his stall by Johnson. Harris then turned on Tom, clenching his riding crop in his hands, until the backs of them were a livid white. "You think you're smart, don't you, Shields?" he ground out through clenched teeth. "Well, you're not so smart. I happen to know all about you. You joined the force for a meal ticket and you were kicked out of the C.I.B. because you meddled in things down there above your head. Now I don't want any back chat or lip. I just won't stand for it. I know you've been to one of our damned Canadian universities and all that crap, but that don't mean a thing to me. All they do is turn out a bunch of Yankee-loving, God damned communists and bolsheviks, they do. I prefer a fellow with just good, plain common sense and no education at all to a car load like you. Now don't let me see you do anything like that again; and I don't want to hear no back chat from you neither."

Snickers kept ringing in Tom's ears for the remainder of the stable period, reminding him that it is such small, ugly incidents which make up the spice and gossip of barrack life and sometimes make or break a man in a military organization like the R.C.M.P.

That evening, he and Paul Brooker were detailed for mail-guard duty. The detail consisted of guarding the 

Royal Mail at the C.P.R. Station, Vancouver, in the evening. It was divided into two parts: the first, seeing that

THE RIDDLE OF INSPECTOR TWEED 43 

the incoming mail on a train, known as the Kettle Valley Limited, was safely unloaded and guarded until it reached the Post Office, early in the evening; the second, escorting the outgoing registered mail to the C. P. R. trans-continental train, later in the evening and remaining in the train's baggage car with it until the train pulled out at 

10:00 P.M. P.S.T. 

On arrival at the Station that evening in a khaki R.C.

M.P. truck, they were joined by a blue-coated C.P.R. policeman, who stood talking with them for about fifteen minutes before the Kettle Valley Limited pulled into the yards. Suddenly the blue-coat began to shout and gesticulate with his arms in the direction of the locomotive tender. The object of his excitement seemed to be a youth with a prospector's packsack slung on his back, who was hanging to the back of the tender.

The youth attempted to jump off onto a side track as the train slowed down, but his packsack got caught at the edge of the baggage coach behind the tender and he fell between it and the tender squarely over a rail. There was a horrible cry above the hiss of the locomotive, which came to a ghastly, mechanical halt, hissing steam, with the guilty sullenness of a big dog which has killed a chicken.

Tom followed Paul at a run to the scene of the accident, white-faced and curious, and arrived there just as the conductor of the train lurched out of the nearest coach. 

The youth was dead: horribly mangled and dismembered.

Paul went away and phoned for a conveyance to remove the remains in and it arrived a few minutes later. The pieces were placed in a long basket, where an M.D. nounced them dead. pro- While the doctor was thus engaged, Tom checked over the contents of the youth's packsack and found they included a small bundle of gold dust and nuggets in a cloth bag and a simple prospector's outfit. The boy was one of the small army of impoverished individualists who had taken to the hills, in scorn of going on relief. Society had rewarded him in its own peculiar way. 

After they had escorted the mail to the Post Office, they went to a restaurant to kill time before the outgoing

batch of mail was ready. Paul devoured a plate of bacon and eggs with his usual healthy gusto, while Tom found he could only handle a cup of coffee. "It's a crazy old world alright," Paul said, seeming to divine Tom's train of thought, as he guzzled his meal. "If there's a grain of justice in it, why should that young fellow have been mashed to a bloody pulp just now, while big crooks and lawbreakers go free, live the life of Riley and get their ugly, dominating features plastered on the society pages of newspapers. Strikes me there's something screwey in the whole set-up."

As they talked in this fashion, a newsboy entered the cafe. He was hugging a bundle of colored late editions under his arm and, as he came opposite them, stopped to ask them to buy a paper. "Late edition, sir. Park." 

All about the suicide in Stanley

Tom bought one to kill time and keep his eyes off 

Paul's bacon and eggs. "How did the Yanks make out today?" Paul questioned him. "Lost to Detroit," Tom said, scanning the baseball results. "Why, Holy smoke!" "What's the matter, Tom?" "What's the matter! . . . Why this suicide case is a man I know about. Inspector Tweed told me something about him. He was a witness against the big Keufel crowd in a government liquor enquiry about five years back. It mentions it here. Says he was found this afternoon in Stanley Park, lying under a park bench with a coat thrown over his head and a cup containing traces of potassium cyanide lying nearby. Apparently it was suicide-well naturally. It says he was a leading witness against big Britsh Columbia liquor interests a few years ago, in an enquiry which failed to establish any illegality of the concerns involved. It also says he was well-known locally and had been in poor health of recent months. The usual journalistic stuff. . . . Hmm, funny." "What's funny about it, Tom?" "Nothing that I can put my finger on. Still-"

THE RIDDLE OF INSPECTOR TWEED 45 

He experienced an urge to tell Paul about his interview with Inspector Tweed at the C.I.B., which had preceded his transfer back to barracks, but checked it. In the first place, Paul had the direct, quick-firing type of mind which jumps readily to conclusions-too readily, sometimes. He might jump too rapidly at the conclusion that Tweed was working hand-in-glove with the Keufels and was himself a crook; something which Tom could by no means feel absolutely certain of himself. He realized the danger to himself and Paul of such a snap decision if it proved erroneous. In the second place, he could not be sure that 

Paul wouldn't leap just as quickly at the opposite view: that he was prejudiced against Inspector Tweed for having had him transferred from the C.I.B. back to straight duty and was retaliating by inventing gossip behind the man's back. After all, he'd only known Paul a few months. 

When they returned to barracks, Tom still had the crumpled newspaper in his hands and commenced to scan the news item which was uppermost in his thoughts again, as he and Paul checked in at the guard room. 

He was aware of the door of the room suddenly swinging open and Paul nudging him and, turning around, found 

Inspector Tweed standing directly behind him, glancing over his shoulder. "Orderly officer: room 'shun!" Tom ordered, as it behooved the senior man under such circumstances. "Carry on, men," Tweed said, returning Tom's salute listlessly and removing the newspaper from his hands. 

His eyes became glued on the news item of the suicide and apparently oblivious to all else around him. "Any absentees or late passes, night guard?" he finally snapped. "No, sir." "How about the stables and garage?" "All in order, sir." "Very well then. Shields, I want just a word with you." "Yes, sir."

Tweed led him down a long passage until they were opposite the door of the Officers' Mess, which lay in a small alcove.

"I want to warn you, Shields, to forget that I ever mentioned Small's name to you," he said in an icily peremptory tone. "Do you understand? He's a thing of the past now. 

I'm telling you this for your own good. Good-night."

He disappeared into the Officers' Mess before Tom had time to answer him. He and Paul turned in quietly in the dark for it was after lights out. "What did Tweed want to see you about?" Paul yawned. "Going to take you back into the C.I.B.?" "No," Tom answered shortly. He lay awake for some time, thinking. In the first place he couldn't decide if 

Tweed was a crook or a hard-boiled realist who thought he saw the futility of fighting an enemy too powerful for him. Was he, in fact, in with the big liquor smuggling and vice interests and deliberately sabotaging the efforts of his own C.I.B. to cope with them; or was he sincere in calling a truce with a section of the underworld that was unconquerable, even to the R.C.M.P.? Secondly, why had he shown such a strong resentment over Fonck's dismissal? Perhaps Fonck had been a main cog in a redbaiting machine that was serving the purpose of diverting public attention away from the Keufels and enabling Tweed and others to apparently justify their existence by putting down an imaginary red menace. That line of reasoning would explain the Keufels' ardent support of the 

Citizens' League and the red-baiting activities of the

R.C.M.P. Such tactics are not uncommon where graft and corruption are rampant. When Al Capone ruled Chicago's underworld, Chicago leaders kept public attention diverted by miraculously discovering that certain citizens were "stool pigeons of King George of England."

Tom knew his Canadian history well enough to know that Canadian politicians, even including the great Sir 

John A. MacDonald, had perhaps evaded answering charges of graft and corruption on some occasions by using jingoistic diversions. In MacDonald's day it had been the "menace" of annexation by the United States.

Many Canadians have since wished the menace would turn out to be a reality. How the Canadian West has been exploited by the Dominion's absurd high tariffs, made pos-

THE RIDDLE OF INSPECTOR TWEED 47 

sible by incessant jingoism directed against the "Good Neighbor" to the South! More recently Uncle Sam has been displaced by Stalin as the outside "heavy" in the 

Canadian drama. 

Why had Tweed cautioned him to "forget" about James

Small? If Small's death had not been caused by suicide, as reported in the press, did Tweed harbor knowledge or suspicions of the truth? Had the Keufels had any part in a murder, they might have made themselves vulnerable to prosecution at long last. He decided the best place for him to uncover a lead would be at the wharves of the Pacific Coast Transit Company. Some of the Keufel rumrunners were fairly free talkers, since they regarded Adolph Keufel, with his immense wealth and political power, immune from the reach of the law and themselves along with him. Strangely enough, the wharves were located less than a half mile from Stanley Park, where Small's body had been found. Before dozing off, Tom decided to pay the wharves a visit the following night.

VI 

Next morning Paul Brooker and Tom Shields were out with the other members of the regular mounted troop taking their mounts over the jumps. They started with the bars at two feet and cleared this height in sections of fours. Soon they were clearing three, then three and a half feet. At the four-foot mark they lined up in troop formation and each man took his horse over individually when his turn came, under the critical eye of Smokey Harris.

To break out of the troop formation when it was not a man's turn to jump was, in the judgment of Smokey, as bad an offense as appearing on parade with dirty buttons and revolver combined; while a horse as little as a head out of alignment was grounds for immediate censure of its rider in Harris' own direct manner. 

Paul was riding Dynamite and, in attempting the fourfoot jump, gave the animal its head too soon and crashed headlong into three heavy cross-bars. The animal fell over in an almost complete somersault, apparently coming down solidly on top of Paul. The horse staggered to its feet, but Paul lay on the ground squirming. Tom immediately put spurs to his horse and arrived over the prostrate form of his chum. "Are you all right, Paul?" he asked as he dismounted in a jump. "Do I look as if I'm enjoying myself?" Paul smiled wanly. "I think I'm okay excepting for my shoulder. My neck feels a little funny, too.' "What in hell do you mean by breaking the ranks!"

Harris suddenly thundered in Tom's ear as he arrived at the jump. "Get back to where you belong or I'll put you under arrest. If you must do something, catch that bloody horse!" 48

Tom looked up in Harris' face to find it flushed a deep, alcoholic crimson, while his eyes had their occasionally dilated, furious set. "I thought he might be badly hurt, sir." "You thought, hell. You're not paid to think in the 

Mounted Police. Now get the hell after that horse." "Yes, sergeant," Tom choked, keeping his temper with extreme difficulty. "Don't 'yes sergeant' me in that tone of voice either, but get going." 

Tom threw himself angrily up into his saddle, eyeing his superior with a glance of mixed anger and contempt. 

Dynamite was far away in one corner of the R.C.M.P. grounds by this time and was demonstrating defiantly by his antics that he had no intention of being caught easily. 

Observing this, Harris ordered the rest of the troop to go after the horse and joined in the chase himself.

It was an absurd gesture, a whole troop of mounted men setting out to catch a single horse. Furthermore, Dynamite, ordinarily a hard animal to deal with, became amazingly elusive and contrary on this occasion. He completely and joyously out-guessed and out-ran everybody who was after him for several minutes.

Meanwhile a group of civilians, who were watching these proceedings from behind a wire fence, began to cheer and whistle every time that Dynamite evaded capture and to greet members of the troop with jeers and cat-calls. 

Like all predominantly physical types, Harris was given to strong, unreasoning loyalties; the foremost of which was to the Force. He could not stand any criticism or ridicule directed at it. He turned a deeper crimson than usual and ground his teeth audibly, as the jeers and catcalls reached his ears. 

Johnson, untidy from sweat and dust, finally cornered and caught the tiring Dynamite and brought him over to 

Harris. The sergeant, with crimson cheeks and purple, protruding blood vessels in his forehead, leaned over a leg the horse was limping on slightly and examined it until it seemed that the blood rushing to his head must surely
