drive him mad. He finally pronounced the limb sound. It was only then that he turned to ascertain how Brooker had fared. 

Paul's collar bone was broken and Harris sent him back to barracks with instructions to report to the Sergeant- Major, who would get in touch with the police surgeon by telephone. He then lined up the troop afresh. "For this most miserable demonstration you've just given," he said hoarsely, with tears of rage struggling for supremacy in his eyes and looking at the troop in general, but at Tom in particular, "I'm going to give you a taste of the sort of drill we used to have at Regina in the old days, when the Force wasn't filled with nincompoops and college graduates. Cross your stir'rups in front of your saddles !"

The troop did so brusquely. "Troop, form sections from the right! Hey, Warnock, damn you, stop acting like an old woman with the bellyache and keep that horse's head in line." 

For over three-quarters of an hour Harris made the troop ride, saddles without stirrups; setting a pace which varied continually from a brisk jog trot to a canter, the most punishing pace possible under such circumstances. 

He led the troop over jump after jump and the roughest terrain in the large R.C.M.P. domains, shouting his commands and blaspheming with a furious abandon. To increase the ordeal, he forbade any attempts to post in the saddle, and almost went into a tantrum when he caught two men, Phil Beardmore and Ted Burns, doing this in sheer self-defense, with agonized expressions on their faces. He apparently forgot that Beardmore's uncle was a provincial cabinet minister and traced his ancestry directly back to a female dog. Such forgetfulness was the one thing a few of the recruits liked about Smokey. "I've ridden horses bare back since I was eight years old," Johnson grunted uncomfortably to Tom, "and I can take it, but this is plain torture. It's worse than being caned in school." "For the last time, Johnson and Shields, stop talking

in the ranks or you'll find yourselves both in the guard room for a week!" "Yes, Sergeant."

Finally, exhausted and probably pursuing the line of least resistance, Beardmore and Burns fell off their mounts almost simultaneously. Phil got up wearily, only nursing a few bruises, but Burns sat up with a deep, crimson gash on his forehead, eyeing the world about him reproachfully. He had been unfortunate enough to land head first on top of a big stone. "Troop-lower your stirrups," stirrups," Smokey bellowed hoarsely, regarding the two casualties soberly. "File back to stables from the right!"

When the troop had unsaddled their mounts, given them a straw rub and thrown a horse blanket over them in their stalls—the blanket being turned inside out so as not to become soaked with sweat-Harris motioned Tom aside. "Look here, Shields," he stormed, "another break from you like the one you made on the ride this morning and

I'll see to it that you're thrown out of the Force with a disgraceful discharge. You may have passed your exams at the top of your class because you came fresh from the university, but I've heard all about your interfering in the affairs of prominent persons out of your class. Your conduct this morning was disgraceful.' "Was it disgraceful because I don't like to see our country run by a group of German and snooty British racketeers ?"

The question found Harris off guard. "Don't be impertinent!" He reddened, then turned white with anger and clutched his riding crop as if he were about to strike Tom. 

But he changed his mind and became suddenly calm. "Tact isn't one of your virtues, Shields," he said quietly. "If I were you I wouldn't try to be a hero; you're not suited to the part. People can't stand a stupid hero until after he's dead in this country. We hanged Louis Riel as a damned traitor, you know." "Thanks for the compliment, sergeant." 

Harris ignored the remark and turned on his heel and walked off. Outside the stable door he met Corporal

Symes and Tom watched the two walk over to the barracks in a deep confab. He smiled at the attentive, sycophantic manner in which Symes seemed to hang on Harris' every word.

As Tom was ready to leave barracks in the evening, dressed in his civilian clothes, Corporal Symes swaggered up to him in the barrack room aisle. He was like a malicious, sandy-haired fighting cock bent on trouble. Actually, appearances were deceptive in his case, in that Symes was never to be found in a fight. That made him none the less dangerous as an antagonist, however, for Symes was a born intriguer and could usually start the ball rolling against anyone he disliked and then step aside to witness the fun. Besides which, the two stripes on his sleeve, even though they hung there precariously, meant a lot in the R.C.M.P. "Do you wealize, Shields," he began pedantically, "what your conduct today would 'ave meant in a good h'Impewial wegiment ?" "No, I don't," Tom admitted, avoiding any note of sarcasm with an effort. "Well, it would 'ave just meant," Symes said very seriously, "that you'd 'ave been tied to a gun cawwige and flogged; then thrown in a 'orse twough by your comwades." "Thank goodness this isn't a good English regiment then," Tom replied good-naturedly, avoiding the temptation to imitate Symes' cockney accent. "The R.C.M.P. is bad enough as it is.' 99 Paul Brooker and Johnson, who were listening, laughed at this and their laughter made Symes' little fiery eyes flash with anger. He turned and appealed to Burns and 

Beardmore, who had been thrown off during the ride. The stripes on his arm had some effect on them. "You know, Shields," Burns told Tom, attempting to humour Symes by assuming the rôle of inquisitor, “I've got absolutely nothing against you over the ride this morning, but I don't like the way you took top marks in all the written exams around here last winter. After all, you had a big edge over us fellows, coming as you did

fresh from university and you didn't need to rub it in." "I had no such thought in mind," Tom stated. "I only tried to make good marks like the rest of you." "Don't it mean a thing to you, Tom," Phil Beardmore groaned tragically from his cot, "that my arm is sprained and my rear is as raw as a T-bone steak from that nightmare this morning?” "I'm sorry," Tom said, "but what can I do about it now?" "Nothing, I guess," Phil groaned. "You look h'as if yer were in a 'uwwy, Shields," Symes announced, standing in Tom's way. "That blue suit wouldn't look so nice h'if it were soaked in a 'orse twough, along with you. 'Ow would yer skirt like yer then?" "My what?" "Yer skirt. Yer know-chippy."

Much to everyone's consternation, Tom gripped the corporal by the collar of his tunic, as his temper got the better of him. "Look here, Symes, I may have caused some of the men trouble on the ride this morning by getting in wrong with Harris, but that doesn't mean you can refer to any women friends of mine by that vulgar London term, chippy. I don't know whether those two stripes would permit you to talk that way towards subordinates over in England, but they certainly won't permit you to get away with it here. This is Canada and we're reasonably democratic, like the United States. You've been out here long enough now to know it's no longer overrun with redskins, half-breeds and buffalo." "That's telling him off," Johnson laughed gleefully. "Boy, I wish all us Canadians had nerve enough to speak out to lime juicers that way."

Symes reddened up to his ears and gurgled something about the younger generation of Canadians being pro- American. Tom's grip on his collar tightened. "That doesn't let you out of what happened this morning, Shields," Beardmore put in. "No, how about it?" Burns grumbled.

"I think you chaps are all wet, picking on Shields in this way," opined a dark, slim youngster with a cultured English accent from the background. The reticent young man was one Ronald Ames, heir to a big estate and title in England. He was a vestige of the days gone by when the Force had been swamped with his kind—young blacksheep of good English and Scotch families who had done. everything from embezzling charitable funds to making love to an attractive mother-in-law in their own land. "Dash it all, fellows, the whole thing strikes me as being a bally lot of balderdash and tomfoolery. All Shields did was to ride up to see if Brooker was hurt and to help him and, if the rest of us had any guts, we'd have done the same instead of staying in line like a bunch of mummies. 

Anyone can see that Smokey is a bit nerts at times and has it in for Shields." "Listen to the bally h'old Earl's son," Symes scoffed at his fellow countryman, wishing that Shields would let go of him. "You don't want it to get around to Smokey, Ames, that you said he's nerts," Beardmore threatened. "Ames is right, nevertheless," growled one, Michael

O'Reilly Callahan, who had hitherto kept in the background as an interested spectator. He had drunk himself out of a fairly good legal partnership over in his native Dublin, Ireland, before coming to Canada and joining the Mounted Police and now joined in the argument on Shields' side more out of a natural dislike for all Englishmen of Symes' type than any interest in the argument itself. He eyed Symes with a hardness that made the 

Cockney quail. "Let's dwop the 'ole blarsted h'argument," Symes suddenly suggested as Tom released the grip on his collar. 

As Tom left the barrack room, Paul followed him outside. "I was going to raise plenty of trouble in spite of my arm," he told Tom, "if Symes had gotten his way with. that crowd. It was good of Mike to save me the trouble. 

I've just learned something this afternoon, working on that soft job they've put me on on account of this arm. 

There's a draft of men leaving here for regular police

duty in Alberta this very week and our names are on the list. Boy, won't I be glad to get away from here and old 

Harris and Symes and the general stupidity of barrack life. . . ."

Tom listened to Paul continuing to run down barrack life, riot squad duty and various other aspects of life in the Royal Mounted in silence. Actually, his mind was occupied wondering whether or not Tweed had had a hand in his coming transfer out of British Columbia to Alberta. Perhaps he considered firing him from the C.I.B. not sufficient.

VII COAL HARBOUR 

Tom went over to the West End of the city by street car to reach Coal Harbour. At the last minute he decided to pay his father a visit before venturing down there. This was only a casual matter, as his father lived on Haro Street in the old residential part of town, less than a mile from the wharves of the Pacific Coast Transit Company. 

This did not mean that the neighborhood was a disreputable one as, on the contrary, it had once been the best residential part of town; but in Vancouver, like most fastgrowing cities, the lines of demarcation between good and bad parts of the city are somewhat flexible and not too sharply defined.

Tom decided that someone else should be informed of what he knew about the big British Columbia liquor ring and what he also suspected about it and Inspector Tweed, before he went further into the matter alone-just in case something happened to him. He would have liked to confide the matter to Paul Brooker, but hadn't known him long enough to be quite sure of him. Nor was he completely at ease about confiding it to his father; he was simply the obvious party to see.

Major Shields was a rare specimen of the one in about a hundred Englishmen who take up a permanent residence in Canada or the United States without ever, even remotely, catching or discerning the robust, realistic spirit of the country. He had come out to Canada a few years after the turn of the century and was as English by 1932 as the day when he first set foot on Canadian soil. In the 

Great War, despite his Canadian domicile, he had preferred to serve in His Majesty's Imperial forces, rather than with the Canadians, where pay and opportunities for promotion were better. He had served with distinction, 56

COAL HARBOUR 

57 too, but had then returned to British Columbia into a civilian life, where the men he had daily contact with had war memories of one sort, he another. He was a strong tory and imperialist and had no conception of the substrata of corruption in Canadian politics; or, if he had, kept it a close secret from everyone else. He naively assumed, in conversation, that all corruption on the North American Continent was confined to the United States and was concentrated in Chicago and New York-never 

Montreal, Toronto or Vancouver.

It was these considerations which placed a perplexed frown on Tom's aquiline features as he considered broaching the subject to his father. Could he make him understand the situation in all its complicated ramifications, or would he be as if talking to him in a foreign dialect? 

His father's greeting to him was cordial as usual. He viewed Tom from under a set of bushy eyebrows that crowned a pair of kindly, but too small, eyes. The smallness of Major Shields' eyes gave him a look of habitual semi-bewilderment and clearly suggested a lack of keen insight and discernment. Possibly this quality had helped him sustain a certain strange pride and vanity, which would have departed from any ordinary man who had encountered the reverses and lack of real success he had achieved on the Canadian scene. He thought well of the 

Royal Mounted and their red tunics and was proud of the fact that he had been instrumental in helping Tom get on the force. "My dear boy, what you say is preposterous," he told

Tom emphatically. "If such people as the Keufels even existed, they'd have been in jail long ago. Why our good

British Canadian Mounted Police are far too fine a body of men to put up with any such nonsense as that. Why you talk as if the Royal Canadian Mounted Police were an ordinary police force! No, old boy, I'm afraid I can't swallow that sort of talk. You must be thoroughly mistaken about the whole thing. You shouldn't talk about your superior officers the way you just have about Inspector Tweed. It just isn't done. 

If Inspector Tweed talked to you in that sort of way,

you either misunderstood him, or he has no business being on the Force."

So that was that. Typically English, his father talking about what should or should not be and then completely refusing to look unpleasant realities squarely in the eye. 

Tom changed the subject and chatted with him some time about tennis and the weather; then took his leave of him. 

He realized, as he paced the streets in the cooling evening air, that he had to make a momentous decision: he must either abandon the desire, which had become almost an obsession with him, to bring the big British Columbia liquor smuggling ring to justice; else face the fact that he would have to work alone on his own initiative in the matter, possibly risking his whole future and even his life and receiving little or no thanks or credit-his superiors would probably take care that they got it—if he succeeded in the long run.

The logical decision to make was clear and obvious to him that summer's evening, but it was not the decision he liked. Tom, at heart, was a born democrat and liberal, despite his tory stock, and the inequalities and injustices of life enraged and irked him to a marked degree. It had become quickly apparent to him on becoming a policeman that the justice meted out to the rich and poor on the Canadian scene are two entirely different matters, differing from one another as much as night from day. This perception had aroused a deep desire in him to bring any of the big lawbreakers to justice, if he ever got the chance. 

Now that the chance confronted him, even though it was dim, he hated to let it slip. Besides, he had inherited a bull-dog tenacity from his father.

At the Coal Harbour wharves Tom met Gottfried Von 

Altenburg, skipper of one of the Keufel boats, the Puritan, just back from a trip "somewhere along the California coast." "Alty" was the son of a German baron and had been captain of a U-boat in the World War. 

He was a man of conflicting personality, having a zest for action, governed by an intelligence of a reflective and higher order. This showed in his present occupation as a rum-runner, in which he excelled, but held in con-

COAL HARBOUR 

59 tempt, along with the racketeers, crooked politicians and others connected with it. He played their game but despised it. His outspokeness and ridicule of all pretense and hypocrisy connected with the huge smuggling clique he worked for had frequently been a source of embarrassment to his employers, but they had kept him on the job because he was the best skipper in their fleet and because, if they let him go, there was nothing to prevent his talking even more freely. "Hello there, Jack," he greeted Shields pleasantly, "when are we going to get in some more tennis out in the Park?" (He called Tom Jack because he knew him as 

John Davidson and referred to the public tennis courts in Stanley Park). "Soon, I hope," Tom replied. "Do you need that wireless operator yet?" "Yes, I do, Davidson, providing you can get up to around fifteen words a minute. You know, it's a crime that a decent young fellow, like you, should have to join this racket-especially working for a bunch of criminal, third-rate fellows like the Keufels. Back home in Germany, I wouldn't bloody well have them clean my shoes, but here in Canada-oh well, what can one expect in a democracy!"

Tom smiled tolerantly, despite the fact that the German's reference to the democratic form of government did not please him. "The more I see of democracy," Altenburg continued, "the more I think it a crime against humanity that we Germans lost the war." (Here he was obviously indulging in a bit of psychological compensation). "I'll tell you right now, Jack, before we go any further into this thing, that you're hitching yourself up to a crowd that won't stop at anything-including murder and corrupting the government. Why, they've just had to bump off a poor devil, who knew too much, these last couple of days. Of course a coroner's jury found it was suicide, or death by misadventure. If the Keufels ever had their private detective gangsters riddle a man so full of lead he weighed a ton, a jury would be found to decide that death was

caused by lead poisoning. I play around with them because there's money in it-that's all." "The man you were talking about just now, Gottfried -what was his name?" 

Altenburg looked at Tom sharply. "One never asks too many questions around here, Davidson," he answered crisply "it just isn't healthy. If you land a job around here, that's the first thing you've got to learn."

At that instant, a car swung onto the wharf and came thumping slowly over its planks with its headlights turned full on the two of them. A man jumped out of the back seat and hurried over to them. "What's this man doing here, Alty?" he demanded. "What's his name?" 

Altenburg looked surprised and glanced at Tom. "My name's John Rolfe Davidson," Tom volunteered blandly.

The man started and seemed satisfied with the information. "Ain't you the same guy what was once christened 

Thomas, something-th'-other, Shields?" he demanded, looking at Tom narrowly. Tom remained silent. "Well, anyway, just step right inside this car a minute, will you, Mr. Shields? I'd like to have a woid with you. This wharf is private property and the owners don't like strangers hanging around it. There's a few questions I'd like to ask you. I'm a private detective." He flashed a silverplated badge on his waistcoat. "Oh, all right."

Tom followed the man to the car. He noticed there was a chauffeur in the front seat and that the back of the car was very dark. He acquiesced to the man's invitation to step in at the back first and almost instantly felt a hand clamped over his mouth, while two others gripped his wrists. His struggles to free himself or to get rid of the hand over his mouth proved futile. He got a last glimpse of Altenburg scratching his head and watching the car with a puzzled expression on his face as it backed up off the dock.

The chauffeur drove down Georgia street, paralleling the harbour front, and into Stanley Park without receiv-

COAL HARBOUR 

61 ing any instructions from the occupants of the back seat of the car. When they emerged on a quiet, secluded section of the Park's twisting driveways, the hand was removed from his mouth and he was able to look about him to either side. In the initial dazzling glare from the car's headlights on the dock, he had formed only a vague impression of the man who had spoken to him. Now, gradually accustomed to the darkness in the back seat of the car, he got a better one. He was a man of medium height and heavy build with a flat, irregular nose that appeared to have been broken at one time by a blow from a sharp-edged tool. The man on the other side of him was a tall, heavy-set individual with long, sombre features. "Well, how'd you like to be put in a barrel of cement, Constable Shields?" the former asked him with a grim chuckle. "I don't know what you mean," Tom said curtly. "How do you know I'm a Constable ?" "A little birdie told me." "The little birdie goes tweed . . . tweed . . . tweed," the tall, inscrutible man mocked. "Shut up," his companion ordered. "You know, you've been meddlin' where you shouldn't of, Shields. One or two other men who have made the same mistake have ended up at the bottom o' Howe Sound or Burrard Inlet, encased in a barrel o' cement. One of these days, if they ever invent diving suits to go deep enough, some diver is goin' to find a few queer relics of our old cement agehuman skulls stickin' out o' barrels of cement down there. 

Ha, ha, ha. Dead men might tell tales, sometimes, but never with a quarter mile of deep, green water over their heads!"

While he laughed, Tom gave a sudden wrench and got one of his hands clear. With it, he managed to locate the handle of the door by the other man and threw it open, plunging at it as it swung loose. He couldn't have broken both men's grips at once in a trial of strength but, threatened with being dragged out of the moving automobile, the man nearest the door loosened his grip on Tom as a natural reflex and Tom found himself crashing and skid-

ding along a stretch of asphalt highway on knees, elbows and shoulders.

He was slightly stunned by the jolt given his entire system by crashing to the asphalt, although his head was uninjured. He realized dimly that his clothing was torn and that the flesh on one of his knees was badly lacerated from the skid he had taken. He staggered to his feet, his fingers clutching his injured knee and moist with blood. 

As he did so, the car pulled to a stop with a screech and a pistol shot flashed and cracked from its side window.

He crashed into some underbrush and trees at the side of the road for cover, while his knee stung painfully. He had gone some twenty paces, when he experienced a sensation like a terrific blow on the head and fell forward, the dim world about him completely blackening out.

VIII RECOMPENSE 

Tom was aroused from unconsciousness and then semiconsciousness by the cool night air stinging the cuts on his head and knee in light, uncomfortable wisps. His head ached intolerably and he felt sick and weak. His hair was matted and saturated with blood and he slowly realized that he had sustained a glancing bullet wound in the scalp, which had gone right across the top of his head in a diagonal direction. He sensed where the wound was without touching it, as it was too sensitive to touch directly. He could feel the stickiness of blood all over his face and shoulders and then guessed his captors had left him for dead. Possibly another car had shown up in the vicinity and had startled them into leaving before delivering the coup de grace. At any rate, he was still alive. 

He raised himself with an effort and discovered that his legs had no desire to behave at first, but finally mastered them sufficiently to walk out of the underbrush onto the road. He had gone about a hundred yards, when he heard a car coming along the road from behind him and slipped back into the woods and underbrush. The car stopped near the point he had just emerged from and he recognized the voices of the two men who had but recently been his captors.

He discovered a bridle path nearby and followed it cautiously and quietly for some distance, until it led him to a small bridge. He knew the bridge well. It cut over a narrow neck of water joining a shallow lagoon, known as Lost Lagoon, with the waters of English bay. He had been in swimming in English bay nearly every summer for about fifteen years. As he walked across the bridge and skirted the public tennis courts where he'd played considerable tennis, including some singles with 63

Von Altenburg, he was impressed with the quietness of everything around him. Coming to a lighted street-he guessed it must be Beverly-he glanced at his watch and was startled to find it was nearly half-past twelve. That accounted for the quietness. It also meant that he must have been lying unconscious and semi-conscious out in the Park a considerable time.

He had to walk nearly a mile to the nearest hospital and, when he got there, slumped down heavily in the waiting room. After some questioning by a nurse, which he responded to wearily and automatically, he was led down a passage to an infirmary, where a chatty, flaxenhaired interne of about his own age took charge of him. "My, my that's a terrible knee you've got," he muttutted. "How on earth did you do that?" "Fell out of a car," Tom told him. "How about that scalp wound?" “A bullet made it.”

A look of horrific surprise came over the interne's chubby countenance. "I'll have to get all the details of this er-incident, Mr. Shields. The police demand a report from us on all such cases." "Yes, I know. You'll be killing two birds with one stone in my case. I happen to be a policeman. Just have the switchboard notify the R.C.M.P. barracks I'm here." 

Tom was relieved to find that neither of his wounds was serious and the young interne was able to repair both of them himself, although the scalp wound required seventeen stitches and the temporary loss of most of his hair. 

After it was all over he found Sergeant Greenleaf waiting for him out in the waiting room. Greenleaf was the orderly sergeant for that week and was a good sort. "I'm sorry to hear about this, Shields," he told him. "If you feel well enough to return to barracks tonight,

I have a car waiting outside to take you back in.” "Yes, sergeant, I'm all right."

Greenleaf drove him back to Fairmont barracks in silence. When they arrived at the guard room, they were met by Inspector Tweed.

RECOMPENSE 

65 "Have you placed Constable Shields under arrest yet, sergeant?" Tweed questioned Greenleaf, with a contemptuous smirk on his features, glancing away from both of them. "Why er-no, sir. I didn't realize-" "Well do so now then." "On what charge, sir?" "Disorderly conduct while out from barracks on pass; and reporting late from a one-thirty pass.' 

"But he was detained in hospital, sir-" "Did you hear what I said, sergeant?" "Yes, sir."

After spending the balance of the night on a cot of steel ribs in the R.C.M.P. guard-room "cage," Tom was awakened by the guard at reveille. He experienced a penetrating headache, which reminded him of something the interne had told him the night before about the bullet only missing his skull by the thickness of a sheet of paper. "How about letting me sleep?" Tom requested. "I have nothing ahead of me until I go on the carpet before the old man at around ten o'clock."

The night guard happened to be Burns, however, who had become thoroughly inoculated with correctitude since being in the Force. "Orders are orders," Burns reminded him, "which means you'll have to get up and roll up your blanket." 

Tom obeyed and remained sitting, instead of lying, on his steel berth in the cage until after breakfast, when he was allowed to shave and don his red serge tunic and dress slacks (his injured knee would not permit of breeches) to appear before the O.C.

In any military organization, like the R.C.M.P., a man charged with an offense is at a tremendous disadvantage. 

He must stand stiffly at attention while his case is on, both while he is giving evidence pleading his case and listening to that of others. The very position of attention, when prolonged, tends to exhaust his mental and nervous energy, making an adequate defense difficult. Added to this, he must constantly be on guard to observe the military etiquette, remembering to call his superior officers "sir"

and referring, in the third person, to men by their exact rank, such as acting sergeant so-and-so, in charge of suchand-such. Moreover, if there happens to be a senior officer with a grudge against him, who can gain easy access to the Officer Commanding's ear before the case comes up, he is at a still further disadvantage. Tom had a vague feeling that Inspector Tweed had played such a role in his case the instant he entered the orderly room. "Constable Shields," the O.C. read from a paper in front of him, "it is charged against you that at 'E' Division, Vancouver, on yesterday's and today's dates, you did, while out on pass for the evening, first, indulge in disorderly conduct unbecoming a member of this Force, receiving, as a result, injuries which will keep you off full duty for a time; secondly, you did absent yourself without leave from barracks, by failing to report back here within the specified limit of your late pass. How do you plead to these charges?" "Not guilty, sir," Tom stated, his head aching and swimming and aware of the fact he belonged in a hospital, rather than facing the two charges in the orderly room. "Sergeant Greenleaf." "Yes, sir?" "What were the circumstances of this case?" "I am the orderly sergeant for this week, sir. Last night the night guard, Constable Burns, rang me up on the telephone and informed me that he had just been notified that Shields was in hospital with a bad scalp wound-made by a bullet—and other injuries." "What time was this?" "One-fifteen A.M., sir." "That definitely lets him off the-let's see-the second charge, then. He had a one-thirty pass, had he not?" "Yes, sir, it's filed with the charge sheet." "What then, sergeant?" "I proceeded down to the hospital in the Buick sedan and had to wait a considerable time when his injuries. were being attended to there. I then drove him back here and placed him under arrest at the orders of Inspector 

Tweed, the orderly officer of the week."

RECOMPENSE 

67 "Inspector Tweed, why was Constable Shields placed under arrest?" "His general appearance and a slight odour of alcohol on his breath, along with the irregularity of his late arrival back in barracks, induced me to take what appeared to be the proper line of action, sir." "Do you mean he was drunk, Inspector?" "No, sir, I wouldn't like to quite say that," Tweed conceded benevolently. "He probably wasn't drunk, sir.” 

The O.C. turned towards Sergeant Greenleaf. "Did you notice anything peculiar about Constable Shields, which might have indicated he was drunk, Greenleaf?" "Nothing that I can remember, sir." "Did you smell his breath, sergeant?" "Er-no, sir." "Why not?" "There didn't seem to be any call for my doing so, sir." "You mean, he appeared sober to you?" "Yes, sir." "Hmm... funny." funny." The O. C. looked at Tom with an honest, puzzled pair of eyes. He was obviously in a dilemma. Correct procedure demanded that he take

Tweed's (an officer's) word against Greenleaf's (an 

N.C.O.'s) but his judgment seemed to sway him towards a compromise. "Shields," he finally said, "I'm disappointed in a man like you getting into a mess of this sort. What have you got to say for yourself?"

Tom was feeling nauseated by the strain of standing at attention in a hot room in his tight red serge tunic in his injured condition. He pulled himself together with an effort, however. "I wasn't drunk, sir," he insisted, as if his tongue were made of lead. "I didn't even have a single drink, sir. 

I'm not a drinking man." "Then what, in Heaven's name, Shields, was this all about?"

Had Tom been feeling well and alert, the question would have served as his cue to go about castigating and denouncing Tweed; as it was, he merely explained, falter-

ingly: "I-I was investigating some smuggling activities, sir... 99 "Investigating 'smuggling activities'? What were these smuggling activities you were investigating while on a late pass which excused you from duty?" "The activities of liquor smugglers at Coal Harbour, sir." "Liquor smugglers at Coal Harbour? Why I was under the impression we had that situation under control. 

Do you know of any liquor smuggling going on at Coal 

Harbour these days, Inspector Tweed?" "None, sir." "Well you're the man who should know, if anybody does." "There are a lot of false rumours floating around on the subject, sir, which have no basis in fact," Tweed explained. "If Constable Shields had only come to me first,

I would have put his mind at rest on the matter." "But I did. . . . 

"That's enough, Constable Shields," the O.C. interrupted him. "I was your age once myself. I used to have the occasional glass of beer and it didn't do me any harm. But I always kept away from water fronts at night alone, because some of the worst characters in the world congregate there and a man's life isn't safe among them.

Let this be a lesson to you. As you're shortly to be transferred to Lethbridge, Alberta, I am going to be lenient and dismiss this charge against you with a warning to avoid such company in the future. Is that quite clear, Shields?" "Yes, sir." matter. "However, what has been done to you is a very serious It might even be classed as attempted murder. 

Do you recollect what these people you had trouble with looked like?" "Very clearly, sir." "Good. I want you, then, to write out a description of each of them. You might even accompany the descriptions with a sketch each. Inspector Tweed-" "Yes, sir ?"

RECOMPENSE 

69 "I'll leave the matter in your hands and hold you responsible for attempting to locate these persons.' 99 "Very good, sir-if it's possible to locate them I'll do it." He made the statement with just the right note of sarcasm, so that his real inference, 'Very good, sir, but I don't believe such persons exist' was apparent, without being too obvious. "You will, of course, be expected to meet your own medical expenses arising out of this case, Shields," Tom heard the O.C. saying, as if through an atmosphere of steam. He was wondering if he could avoid fainting from pain and exhaustion at the time. "Very good, sir," he responded thickly. "Case dismissed. Next case, please, Sergeant-Major!" "Yes, sir. Detail: right turn, quick march."

Inspector Tweed had won out again.

IX  

The City of Lethbridge has enlarged considerably in a few decades, along with the balance of Western Canadian towns and cities; but its growth compared to that of Vancouver, Winnipeg or Calgary has been slow and hesitant. In a few decades, it has straggled out of the confines of a village, then a town, into a small city of about sixteen thousand population, combining the features of a coal mining town (on a diminishing scale) with those of an urban centre for the agricultural life of the Alberta Foothills. It is built over ground catacombed with the tunnels of worked-out coal mines, some of which operated prior to the coming of the C.P.R. 

Lethbridge still holds unproudly to vestiges of its "cattle town" days, when cowboys rode into town in whooping bands, on pay night, down its main thoroughfare to the nearest saloon or brothel, to the accompaniment of shouting and rowdyism. It still has the brothels; liquor is still consumed in good volume in certain sections of the community, when pay cheques forthcome; small, adolescent brawls still have a way of not infrequently occurring at week-end, public dances.

The R.C.M.P. "Square" is the same, good-sized plot of ground that has been used by the Force as the site for one of its main posts in Western Canada, since buffalo roamed the prairies in tens of thousands and Indian chief Poundmaker was making full-column news in the leaflets of low-grade paper, which passed as newspapers in Eastern Canada in days gone by.

The city has branched out from its original location towards this plot of ground and surrounded it with numerous bungalow residences, a church and big, red-brick Y.M.C.A. The Square contains the old, unattractive 70

buildings of the R.C.M.P. barracks, orderly room, guardroom and quarters for the married personnel; together with a large stable, built of rough-hewn timbers and logs, which has stood since the days of the aforementioned buffalo and Chief Poundmaker. Beneath the floor planks of this stable and two or three feet of soil are reputed to lie the corpses of hanged men, in a remarkable state of preservation due to a daily lubrication from the juices of stable droppings from above.

In place of Smokey Harris, the men of the Vancouver draft quickly discovered a new pet hate in the person of 

Sergeant John Schlitz of Lethbridge. Schlitz was a large, red-faced Englishman with a bristling moustache and pair of big, fierce eyes. Although English, his ancestors had come to England from Germany and Schlitz was a typical Prussian in many respects. His Prussian efficiency, however, had been perverted by an army life into a mania for correctitude in small details and adherence to the letter of the law to the nth degree.

Paul Brooker had been unanimously voted the punster of the month soon after his arrival in "K" Division when, on being asked what he thought of Sergeant Schlitz, he had replied: "Well, personally, I think he's the Schlitz."

A day or two after the arrival of the Vancouver draft, Schlitz took Tom to one side, eyeing him quizzically. “Well, what was your trouble, Shields?" he questioned. "I'm afraid I don't quite get what you mean, sergeant." "Why did they transfer you here from Vancouver, Shields? That's what I mean. You look intelligent. 

You're smart at drill and riding and they tell me you've been to university. You know, that's a pretty good combination for this Force and this isn't as good a division as Vancouver is. I can understand their sending Brooker or Johnson or Callahan down here. They're good men, too, but they've all had good big doses of C.B. against their names and, besides, Johnson and Brooker are a trifle 'red', from what I hear. Ames: he needs toughening up. 

Warnock..." Schlitz dismissed him with a shrug. "But you-what's the answer, Shields? Was it some sort of scandal that was hushed up for the good of the Force?"

Tom remained silent. "A woman, Shields? That's excusable enough." "No, sir." "A little bit of drinking or gambling on the side?" "No, sir." "Well what in hell, Shields, did you do to be sent down here?" 

Tom remained silent. "You know, Shields, this is an interesting town. It's got the biggest red light district of any city for its size in Western Canada. The main part is protected from raids, because it has old man Keufel back of it. He lives in Vancouver, where you come from. Ever hear tell of him?"

Tom started sharply and glanced at his superior with surprise. A glint of satisfaction came into Schlitz's eyes. "Yes, I've heard of him," Tom admitted.

Schlitz then dismissed him and Tom went away puzzled as to what had prompted Schlitz to mention the name of Keufel in connection with the Lethbridge vice district. 

Had it been entirely accidental and merely in connection with a veiled and insulting warning to him to keep away from the vice district? Men like Schlitz can be amazingly coarse and inconsiderate of another person's feelings at times. Had Schlitz, on the other hand, heard some vague rumours about his entanglement with the Keufels in Vancouver and was he ascertaining if Tom was talkative on the subject, either for the information of Tweed, or to satisfy his own curiosity? 

Tom didn't like Lethbridge-quite apart from Sergeant

Schlitz. It was barely a city and he had the average city youth's yearning for things to happen daily as a matter of course and for places to go to spend one's hours of leisure. 

Notwithstanding the synthetic glamour cast around it, a policeman's life of contacts with suicides, armed robberies, auto accidents and the various other incidents which go make up the life, is a sordid and depressing one. Until the skating and hockey season opened, around Christmas time, his main amusement consisted of going to movies. to

with Paul Brooker at the city's only operating movie house (the other two having closed with the depression). 

With the opening of the skating arena came hockey practices and scheduled league games, with their thrills and spills on a smooth sheet of ice in quest of the elusive rubber puck. This almost made up for the loss of the better show and dance places of Vancouver-especially when factors like Tweed, Smokey Harris and Symes were taken into account. Tom, Paul Brooker and Johnson played for the same team at centre, left and right wing respectively.

Inlying picket is a term denoting a detail of one or two men who have to remain in barracks of an evening, or over a holiday, in case an emergency arises. One evening, when

Tom was performing this arduous duty by lying on his cot reading the movie reviews in a magazine, Gorman walked up to the cot and dealt its metal foot a lusty kick with his high boot. "Snap out of it, laddie," he said jocosely. "Say, I suppose it would be asking something to suggest that you go the rounds of the red light district with me-sort of beneath the dignity of a college graduate and all that. We've just got a tip that one of them's handing out cocaine to its customers."

Tom's ears reddened. There was no necessity for 

Gorman to adopt that mock condescension towards him since, as inlying picket, it was his duty to accompany the man on a case, whether he liked it or not. He had formed a dislike for Gorman already. The man was a big, lazy brute, fifty pounds heavier than the Official maximum weight of a hundred and seventy-five pounds for the R.C.M.P., who had got on the Force through a recommendation from some city squad. He was rated as a good, practical policeman by his superiors; enjoyed making sarcastic references to the idealism, slim waist lines and clean-cut appearance of some of the younger, and newer, members of the Force. Rumor had it that he was used, among other duties, to watch the off-duty conduct of the other members of the Sub-Division for the Officer Commanding especially in regard to liquor and women.

"What do you think college graduates are, a bunch of pansies?" Tom retorted, reaching for his tunic. 

Lethbridge is probably the most prostituted town or city of any considerable size in Western Canada. The profession is semi-legalized within its precincts and medical examination of the women involved is compulsory at set intervals in the "favored" district. Tom learned this for the first time that evening as he and Gorman went through house after house of an exclusively red light district that rivalled the largest single one of its kind in Vancouver, a city twenty times the size of Lethbridge, and met a galaxy of girls with worried eyes, haggard, over-painted faces and lips, with a typically mocking smudge of a smile, smeared thick with rouge. 'Down the line' is not merely a meaningless saying in the coal city of the Alberta foothills, for the district is sharply defined, and confined, by intersecting streets.

In one of these places the proprietress asked Gorman pointedly if he was "out trying to catch some of the boys again" and Tom was conscious of a nervous, spluttering effort Gorman made to change the subject and of an affability he adopted towards him immediately afterwards. 

He decided that the boys Gorman was out trying to catch were not criminals, but mounted policemen out on a tear and the rumours he had heard about Gorman in barracks were not without foundation. "I see where I'll have to keep a secret if I'm going to save you from a ducking in a horse trough filled with ice cold water, in below-zero weather, with a horse blanket thrown over your head," Tom reflected aloud, with sugarcoated malice, as they passed on to the next maison de joie. "That would be just too duckey for words, wouldn't it ?"

Gorman remained silent. This time it was his turn to redden to the ears. Finally they arrived at a house brightly lighted and freshly painted a sickly, canary yellow, which stood out biliously from its darkened, sinister surroundings. "This is the last one we'll go into, Shields," he said with more respect in his voice than hitherto.

Tom sighed with relief. He knew that Paul frequented such places occasionally, when the spirit moved him, and was afraid they might run across him during one of their "visits". They had failed to detect any signs of the elusive cocaine or its application to warrant making a search of any of the places they had entered and this further confirmed Tom's suspicions that Gorman wasn't really looking for the drug.

In the canary yellow house Tom met with that which he had consciously feared, but sub-consciously yearned for, on setting out that evening. One of the girls in this mansion of vice was surprisingly beautiful. She was a striking brunette, with a soft, languishing loveliness becoming the conventioned conception of a princess. She looked startlingly out of place sitting beside three lined, over-rouged professional sisters-suggesting weird, ghoulish maids-in-waiting to her.

Her eyes met his and for many seconds he felt awkward and dumbfounded. He sensed that his face was a near crimson and that it remained so even after he and Gorman had left the house and were out in the cold, night air, crunching along beside one another on a snow covered, wooden sidewalk. He felt conscious of her gaze following him.

He resolved then to keep away from the canary yellow house. Why had he made such a resolve? There had been no conscious notion on his part to have any relations with the beautiful prostitute. He usually didn't make a resolution without some basis. Strange, how complex a human mind is. After all, he was entitled to keep the company of what women he chose. Hadn't life jilted him? What use was virtue in a world that was run, not by the virtuous, but by the Adolph Keufels? No, that sort of attitude was maudlin, self-pitying-dangerous, too.

During the next two weeks he played some hockey, attended four movies and brooded over the beautiful brunette. Why wouldn't she stay out of his mind? Perhaps he didn't want her to. Anyway, brooding over her was

making him absent-minded. He might as well satisfy a physical craving as torture his mind. 

One evening he set out from barracks alone. He had a feeling of guilt about Paul. They had become the closest of friends and Paul had trusted him with many deep, personal secrets. Then, too, there was hockey. Paul and 

Johnson both depended on him as pivotal man in their three-man combination attack. However, a man must live his own life and not let others live it for him. 

He walked boldly up to the yellow house and entered it, without looking to either side of him. As though having read his thoughts, as well as seen him coming, the beautiful one darted out of the "drawing room" where the girls waited, slipped up to him and placed her arm through his. She acted like a normal, affectionate young woman welcoming her husband home. He had always imagined prostitutes hard, emotionless women, devoid of feelings or vitality-cynical, disillusioned females who had conceded defeat in life's battle and bore the scars of defeat in heavy rings under their eyes and unhealthy skins camouflaged under a mask of cosmetics. "I knew you would come," she told him affectionately.

He sensed that she was French by her accent. "Why were you so sure of that?" he asked, a trifle gruffly. The gruffness was due, in part, to nervousness; in part, to resentment at any inference that he was easily vulnerable to attractions of the opposite sex. "I dun' know," she answered simply, "I just guess, that is all. Do you wish to see me, mon cheri? Non?" "Mais oui. You see, I know two words of your language. You are French, aren't you?" "Oui." "You will come again, mon cheri?"

The girl said it pleadingly and there was a catch in her voice. Tom looked into her eyes and caught a pathetic, puzzled expression there which touched him. It spoke more eloquently than words, which were these, providing his interpretation was correct: "It is the woman in me that has triumphed over the professional, in your case,

tonight, but what is the use of telling you about it? You'd only think that it is something I know off by heart and tell to every man who buys his way into my life." "Maybe I will come again. What is your name?" "Lucienne Comeaux." "How long have you been doing this, Lucienne?" "About two, say t'ree, month. What eez your first name? I do not ask for ze last one. They nevaire like to tell it." "Thomas is my name.' "Tomez." 

"Yes, but they call me Tom for short. How did you ever come to get into this in the first place, Lucienne?" "Oh, I dun' know." "Oh yes you do now. 

Be frank about it. I'll bet some man let you down-seduced you-promised to marry you -now didn't he?" "Mais oui, mon cheri, that eez how most us girls become, what you call heem-prostituée. Voila!" "Voila, my eye, Lucienne. You interest me, you know. 

Why don't you tell me your story. Every woman has a story, the same as every man, and I cross my heart I'll keep it a secret. I know a certain amount of French and, if you're stuck for English, you can parlez Français à moi." "Eh bien. Leo, il était mon homme. He big and strong, lak' you and dress, ah si chic!..."

Tom listened while the poor girl recited in none too good English, interspersed with French, how a good-looking, mysterious youth, Leo by name, had come to her native village in Southern France, like a Prince Charming in the

Cinderella story, and had seduced her by promise of marriage.

This Leo, compared to the yokels of her village she was used to associating with was a young god-clever, well-mannered, good at tennis and dancing. He was the very impersonation of a simple girl's vision of a knight on a white charger come to rescue her from a humdrum existence. He claimed to be the representative of an im-

portant industrial concern, making a secret examination of the locality for a factory site. 

She, the daughter of a well-to-do merchant of her village, had run away from home and travelled up to Paris with the young fellow. They were to have had a big, colorful, church wedding when they got there; but 

Leo suddenly discovered to his apparent dismay that his "aristocratic parents" wouldn't give their consent to the marriage. Lucienne asked him to take her to them so that she might plead their cause with them in person, but Leo refused the request on the grounds that they were too set in their ideas and that opposition merely caused them to retire into a comfortable and impenetrable shell. 

He finally prevailed upon her to live as his mistress until such time as the erring elders changed their minds, or until he obtained another position. It seemed an irate father had used influence to get him dismissed from the position he held when he met her.

They had lived together under circumstances of unwedded bliss for a few weeks, the bliss obeying the law of diminishing returns as they moved into steadily poorer quarters. Leo grumbled about lack of work and funds and Lucienne delivered all her small collection of jewelry and valuables over to him to sell and pawn to support them. "One of ze tings was a lil' brooch ma mére she give to me before elle mort," Lucienne said plaintively. 

One day, in apparently the deepest grief and mortification, Leo had asked her to walk the streets and "pick up" one or two gentlemen, as a temporary measure to keep them from starving. Lucienne had been horrified at the suggestion and had refused outright, saying she would prefer starvation. Days passed and one cold evening found Leo waving a dried crust in one hand and begging her on bended knee to reconsider her decision for his sake, as he could not bear hunger and the thought of starvation any longer.

With chattering teeth and trembling for lack of proper sustenance Lucienne had ventured out in the cold night air of the streets and had timidly accosted a well-dressed man whom Leo pointed out to her. That had been the

start. Others a sailor, an American, a student from the Sorbonne, a soldier, more soldiers and sailors-followed. Leo became his charming, healthy self once more and it did Lucienne's heart good to see him thus. The more pick-ups she made, the happier he seemed to be. 

As long as the money she got and immediately turned over to him made her man happy, she could put up with the humiliation and degradation. That is woman's nature. 

Weeks passed. They moved into much better quarters. 

Then one day Leo came to her with a very crestfallen look and informed her he had "made a big deal" with another man and that she would be "working for this other fellow for a time." Astounded at the news, she had nevertheless kept her composure and asked him how long the arrangement would be in effect. "Indefinitely," he had replied. She was broken hearted (Mais oui). She had fallen on her knees and implored her lover not to leave her; promised him that she would do anything rather than that; but to no avail. 

What could she do? She had no home to go to any more; to her family and friends she was, in effect, dead. 

What was left to her? The River Seine or the "other man." "I tink of zee Seine first," she said wistfully, "but den life eez so sweet and pretty wiz all zee birds and trees and all zat. N'est ce pas?" She gave an expressive, fatalistic little shrug and Tom saw her point all right. 

The "other man" turned out to be one of the big shot white slave dealers of the Parisian mart, or Inner Circle, the council-of-state of seventy percent of the world's traffic in women and girls. He had sent her to Canadato Lethbridge at a profit and here she was a regular performer in the ranks of the world's most ancient profession. Lethbridge had been her home for only two weeks. 

She had learned that her young Prince Charming was one of a group of good-looking youths engaged in the livelihood of traveling about France seducing pretty girls and young attractive women for delivery to the white slave dealers of the Parisian Inner Circle. For each woman, delivered in good health and properly "broken in" they

got anywhere from 2000 to 30,000 francs, depending upon her beauty, health, degree of culture and deportment. 

Some of them averaged about half a dozen girls a year. "Oh how she loathed and hated that reptile, Leo, now."

Tom listened to her sordid narrative, which would have impressed him as horrible even in the printed pages of a book, with a strangely tense disgust. "Et moi-" said Lucienne with a little shrug of pride, "I bring twentee thousan' franc." "Good God!" Tom exclaimed, "that's only twelve hundred dollars. I would never have believed there were men living who would do a thing like that for such a paltry 

A million, perhaps, but twelve hundred-" "Tink I maybe wort' more t'an twentee thousan' franc, Tom?" sum. "I'll say you are; any woman is. You didn't quite get my meaning, Lucienne. You poor, poor kid!"

They were suddenly disturbed by a gentle knocking on the door. "C'est Madame. You mus' go now Tom, mon cheri."

Tom felt a sudden surge of disgust rise within him, over and above that which he already felt, and his revulsion of feeling manifested itself outwardly. The girl detected the change in him and, with an amazing volatility, threw herself on him and kissed him passionately. "Tom-Tom, Tom, mon cheri," she wailed, "promise me you will come again. Oh, promise, Tom-I lak' you so, so much." "Okay," he yielded, strangely perplexed and overcome by her sudden show of passion and warm kisses. "You'll see me again soon. But tell me something, Lucienne: who is the party who brought you out to Canada? I mean, who is the man who bought you from those men over in 

France? Do you know his name?" "Non, I do not know 'is name, mon cheri. I'ear 'e eez veree beeg and rich man an' lives in a citee more farther west than even Lethbridge." "Is it Vancouver, by any chance?" "Ah oui, Vancouveer-Voila! How you guess heem?"

"Vancouver is a mighty big and bad city, Lucienne.

There are lots of powerful bad men there, the same as 

Paris or Chicago. One man in particular!" "Ah oui."

As Tom left the canary yellow house and crunched over the snow-covered sidewalk that led to the city's main thoroughfare, his head was slightly bent forward in deep meditation. Several ideas fought for supremacy in his thoughts. Holding the advantage for the moment, was the new conviction that Adolph Keufel was the powerful figure behind the large, protected vice district of Lethbridge. It checked with what both Sergeant Schlitz and 

Lucienne had told him. Next, came a deep gnawing pity for all the unfortunate girls he had seen living miserably in the district that night, mingled with a condemnation of society for creating such problems. He felt a tinge of shame, in fact, for letting the sleuth in him push the thought of Keufel ahead of these other considerations.

X 

People judge others by themselves. An insensitive man can enjoy an evening spent in the company of a woman, particularly a prostitute, in the same impersonal way that he might enjoy a beef steak or glass of beer. He might pass her on the street a year later and not recognize her —that is, actually not recognize her. The woman may have forgotten him, too, though the odds are considerably against it, for women remember such things. This is because insensitive persons and men, in particular, are at bottom (however much they might pretend otherwise), also undemocratic people. 

On the other hand, a sensitive man, at heart a true democrat-other things being equal-will often find it difficult to dismiss the same sort of thing as easily, though he might wish he could do so. For one thing, there are the woman's feelings to consider. Society may stamp her as lower than the dust; but she may, actually, be worth considerably more than this appraisal. A sensitive man will recognize the error and accept her, at least secretly, at, or near her true value. In doing this he is defying society and placing himself at a disadvantage, if he is not careful; for there is an enormous, unwieldy, theologicallyminded literature and tradition which frowns on the socalled "fallen woman" with an unreasoning and, therefore, unrelenting hate, which is always on the side of the ignorant and bigoted.

Tom belonged more in the sensitive category and discovered he could not treat Lucienne as a common little plaything, because he realized she was not that. He had found her, instead, a human and emotional creaturerather more simple, defenceless and beautiful by nature 82

than the average woman. She had merely been launched on a world which is dominated by cruelty and hatred and betrayed by it. The wonder to him was that the ordeal had left her with so rich and sparkling a personality.

Surely this was a test that few women would stand up under. What had happened to those other girls he had seen that night in all those houses? They bore the scars of defeat in every line of their make-up and their hard, sophisticated eyes and mouths. The same conditions that had left Lucienne standing had crushed and broken them beyond repair. Was the transition merely one of time? 

He shuddered at the thought.

The canary yellow house seemed to symbolize Lucienne's misfortunes to him. It was her gilded cage-perhaps not so gilded at that-while she was the unfortunate, trapped canary being exploited for mortal pleasure. Tom wondered about the dividing line between love and lust, which poets and members of the literati had a way of segregating into two separate and air tight compartments: the one fumigated and holy; the other reeking with the fumes of alcohol and air heated by the flames of passion. Did these literary craftsmen really know anything about life, or were they a group of inexperienced, romantic mountebanks and charlatans whom an ignorant world had been taking at their face value for years, decades, even centuries? Was there, in reality, any sharp line of demarcation, or did the thing follow a course laid down by all Nature in its pattern of the universe-the sensuous blending gradually with the spiritual, like the colors of a rainbow passing unnoticed, the one into the other, without any sharp line of division between them?

A thought that seemed inescapable gradually dominated Tom's mind: Could Lucienne be rescued from her present life? He sensed the thought had been lying half formed, in a sort of embryo state, in his mind for some time. The ultimate inference as he pursued it was closer to home and more alarming: Could he, himself, rescue her?

No, that was definitely out. He would pay the place another visit, however, for the purpose of meeting its

Madame and sounding her out on whether or not Adolph Keufel was backing her establishment. He was already sure in his own mind that he was, but wanted something more definite to go on than the hearsay gossip of Sergeant Schlitz, or the pathetic vagueness of little Lucienne. 

Strange, he reflected, how one strong man, like Adolph

Keufel, can reach out over half a continent and gain such vast power illegally. What a benefit it would be to mankind if only such energy and organizing ability were always diverted to solving ways out of the tangled economic prison that Man builds around himself!

Tom timed his next visit to the canary yellow house so that it fell two days before a pay day in some Lethbridge businesses and coal mines. This meant that the establishment's customers would be short of ready cash and the place liable to have an off night. Such, indeed, turned out to be the case and Tom was able to engage the Madame in a fairly amiable conversation. 

The Madame invited him and Lucienne to come into her special drawing-room, near the back of the house, which she reserved for herself and her most respectable customers only to enjoy a drink with her in front of an open fire. Tom's natural impulse was to refuse the invitation on the spot, but gladly accepted it on this occasion. "No you don't Tom, this party is on me," the Madame told him. "Just because people say I'm a mercenary old hag doesn't mean I'm too stingy to treat my guests to a drink when I invite them into my sanctuary.' 22 "Oh, all right, if you feel that way about it," Tom conceded. 

Madame's "sanctuary" was a brightly colored, gaudy room, furnished in not bad taste, considering a marked preference for vivid coloring. The dominant hues were a peacock blue, orange yellow and blood red, which had the effect of making Tom wince in startled amazement until he became accustomed to them. He decided they would form a less striking combination by daylight. "Believe it or not, Tom," their hostess ventured, as if trying to gauge his thoughts, while she poured him out a

drink, "I was a good girl once-appearances notwithstanding. It was quite a while before your time, though, I admit. I had to go to work as a waitress a good many years ago to support my two kid brothers when our father died. Our mother had passed on when the youngest of them was born. I was a good girl at that time, though getting a trifle too fresh and hard-boiled for my age, I guess. "Conditions for a working girl were a whole lot worse in those days than they are now, although they're plenty bad in some lines today, too, from what I hear. In my time there was considerably less social legislation than there is now concerning working girls and a hard-boiled boss was in a position to do anything he liked with one excepting, perhaps, make her love the child. "I finally got tired of being regularly seduced weekends by greasy, plump headwaiters and bull-headed, arrogant young souses of nephews and friends of my bosses and decided I might as well sail under my true colors, if the world wasn't going to give me a square deal and allow me to go straight and help my kid brothers legitimately. I've had the satisfaction of seeing both of them come out on top, though, and that's meant a lot to me. 

One's a lawyer with a good business in a fair-sized city of the Middle Western States. He's got a fine family and is damned well liked and respected in his community. 

Other one's an automobile dealer around the same locality and is doing quite well, too, from reports I get. I hear about them indirectly, because they both think I'm dead.”

She sighed and stopped abruptly, looking silently into the fire for several seconds, whose crimson flames licking sullenly upwards seemed to detect lines of suffering and turmoil in her unlovely but almost kindly face. "You know," she continued, getting up and sitting down beside Lucienne and placing a motherly arm about the girl's waist, "these damned politicians and moralists, who yip about women like me and the girls we assist and what terrible people we are give me a pain in the neck.

Why, some of the greatest women the world has ever

known did just what we are doing. This old earth wasn't created by, or for, a bunch of hypocritical, Bible-thumping nincompoops. The truth of the matter is, that a goodly percentage of the pioneering mothers of the West were just what we are before they got hitched up to some farmer or mining man. They were gold-diggers and so-called 'dance hall artists and entertainers'-which amounted to nearly the same thing. "Some of those damned medical pamphlets about sex the boys have shown me are a bloody crime. There wasn't such a word used as 'sex' in its present-day sense in my time. It wasn't until after the War, when swarms of them came back from France diseased that the thing developed into such a holy terror. Of course, there was always a certain amount of disease going around, but only a fraction of what there is today. A timid man reading some of those pamphlets they turn out nowadays could easily be scared into laying off the girlies for keeps.” "I doubt it," Tom opined. "All right, 'doubting Thomas,' but if what we do is so terrible, why don't some of your moralists and politicians try to improve conditions in this world, so that women don't have to walk the streets for a living and young, single men can afford a wife and home of their own. I've been in this racket for a long, long time now-I've grown old in it—and I've yet to meet a girl with, perhaps, the odd exception here and there, who was really bad and in it because she was absolutely good for nothing else. What is more, the people who run us down and get after the police to crack down on us all the time are the sort who have never known what it means to be hungry and not know where your next meal's coming from. They come from the upper ranks of society-exploiters of the masses -every time." "Talking of exploiters of the masses," Tom put in casually, "have you ever heard of one named Adolph Keufel?"

The woman's expression changed abruptly. The relaxed creases of her face became hard and stiff and she

eyed him sharply. "Yes, son," she said queerly, "but if 

I were you I'd keep that name off the tip of your tongue.

It's an open sesame to trouble in your profession. We just call him Number One, and if you want to rise in the world to be a police inspector or superintendent, you'd better not even call him that. Forget that there is such a person."

XI 

One cold morning Sergeant Schlitz entered the barrack hut to the accompaniment of his gruff voice and large, overshoed feet thumping off snow out in the hall. He was like a big, old grizzly bear as he entered the sleeping quarters on one side of the hall—with his bright red and purple nose and ears and his beaver cap and heavy buffalo skin overcoat. He eyed the faces about him quizically, and his gaze finally rested on Ronald Ames and Tom Shields. "Ames and Shields," he said, fingering his walrus moustache benevolently, "I've got a little job for you two.

There's a bohunk widow and her brats badgering the government for relief over at the east side of town. I want you to go down there and look her case over. Don't recommend any relief unless they absolutely need it, though. It's not as if they were good Scotch or English people. Here, Shields, take this card."

He handed Tom a card which read: Mrs. Grabowskiwidow, unemployed. Children: 2—1 girl (9) ; 1 boy (7).

Tom's knock on the Grabowski cottage door was answered by a pretty, brown-eyed girl of eight or nine, who looked a trifle pale and downcast. "Where is Mrs. Grabowski?" Tom asked her. "Mummy's sick in bed," the child answered. "She won't send for a doctor, cos we haven't any money to pay him if he comes." "And your brother; where is he?" "My kid brother, Nikki, 'sat school," she said in a tone that indicated she considered herself a relatively mature person. "I send him away every morning so's he'll get something to eat. Johnny Haines and the O'Shea girl share their lunches with him and that's even worth going to school for." 88

"I see," Tom smiled. "In my school days we had no inducement like that to go. By the way, what's your name?" "Nana." "That's a pretty name. Is your mother too sick to see us, Nana?" "Oh, no—at least I don't think so," she said thoughtfully. "Just a minute and I'll see." "That little one is not going to go wanting a beau when she grows up," Tom laughed as the child disappeared into a back room of the house. "Brr—this place is as cold as an ice box," Ames complained.

The child returned promptly with the news that her mother would like to see them and led them into a living room that was spotlessly clean and tidy. It, too, was cold and draughty. Tom made a rapid appraisal of its furnishings and decided that there was nothing of any appreciable value, excepting to the present occupants. Hard times had glutted the market with second-hand household effects.

The same was true of the kitchen, in which little Nana had the one source of warmth for the whole cottage burning sparingly in the stove. It-the kitchen range-was the one article of any appreciable value in that part of the house.

They passed into a bedroom and found Mrs. Grabowski lying in bed. She was a young woman, of certainly less than thirty, but sick and haggard looking in her present condition to the point where she looked five or ten years older than this at a first glance. There was a quilt and a single blanket covering her, from beneath which a soiled and tattered mattress peered out, ugly and defiled, as if symbolizing the inward poverty of the household. Their little hostess, all alert, quickly adjusted the quilt so that it covered the mattress and then sat up on the bed beside her mother and stroked her forehead.

Mrs. Grabowski informed them that her husband had been killed in one of the Lethbridge coal mines in the summer of 1931 and that she had kept house for herself and her young family since then on his life insurance money.

"Now the money, it is all used up," she explained in a broken voice. "I like to get work as scrubwoman, but no can get work. I am willing to work my hands to the bone for my lil' chil'ren, but what is the use? There is no work, no work."

Tom and Ames arranged to have Mrs. Grabowski removed to the hospital for treatment as an emergent case. 

So impressed were they with her case, that they were not satisfied with merely recommending that she receive the ordinary scale of relief, but also told the boys in barracks about her and realized quite a substantial sum of money on her behalf by "passing 'round the hat on pay day." 

While she was in the hospital they busied themselves in their free evenings, stocking her larder and fixing up the cottage pending her return. They were considerably assisted in this by Paul Brooker, Bill Johnson and Mike 

Callahan. Sergeant Schlitz regarded these activities with anything but favor, however. "Huh, Shields," he snorted through his bristling moustache, "s'a fine thing when members of this force stoop to doing the chores for a damned bohunk. As an Anglo- 

Saxon, you should know enough to keep above that sort of thing. 99 "It's that attitude, sergeant, that's going to cause this country to sever the British tie some day and join forces with our neighbor to the South." "As a loyal Englishman, Shields, I don't like that sort of talk. It isn't true, either. Decent Americans are just as contemptuous of their lower classes as we are, despite all their grand blather about democracy and the 'Land of Liberty. Those foreigners have no business drawing relief. They're bloody parasites. They should be allowed to starve, the same as they are in Europe, if they can't support themselves." "There are lots of people with good Anglo-Saxon names getting relief," Tom reminded him. "It so happens that this Grabowski woman has been longer in this country than you have, sergeant."

Schlitz' large, red face became even redder and he looked as if he'd like to throw Tom in the guardroom. "Every

bohunk and dago in this country is a potential red," he retorted angrily. "We never get any of the good, smart 

Fascist Italians, but always the damned red element that Mussolini has thrown out of his country. Why encourage them to stay here by letting them have relief? Did you look in her mattress?" "Certainly not, sergeant. Why should I?" "Sometimes these red foreigners hide their money there. 

They can't keep it in the bank and get relief at the same time, because of our check-up with the banks. So they draw it all out to fool us and their favorite hiding place is in a mattress, so they'll sleep on top of it at night. I've heard that one of them had eleven hundred dollars in an old mattress that nearly found its way to the city incinerator." 

Tom and Ames took poor Mrs. Grabowski's mattress completely apart, only to discover inside it some very badly compressed and soiled stuffing. They had to buy her a new one out of the funds subscribed for her at the barracks. Truly, the foreign and leftist elements in Canada are as much libelled and long suffering as the early Christians in Rome. If a Canadian Nero burned down Toronto or Winnipeg, it would invariably be blamed on the "red element."

About this time, a story appeared in a leading American magazine, with a large Canadian circulation, about the Mounted Police. The yarn was one of those in which the names of obscure lakes, rivers and towns are thrown about with a familiar abandon, just as if the author had actually been there. This is to give the tale “atmosphere,” on the assumption and hope that the reader has never been there, himself. In some of these cases the author has actually set foot in Canada-either of a week-end to sample Montreal beer, or to pay a visit to the "quints" at Callendar, Ont. In either case, he feels fully entitled to write reams of superficial composition about the superiority of law enforcement and public righteousness in Canada over that in the United States. This sometimes gets him a free pass to travel anywhere in Canada on that great Imperialistic ribbon of steel, the Canadian Pacific Railway. One such

writer has even gone so far as to express a preference for the Canadian capital, Ottawa, over Washington, D. C.sheer nonsense to anyone who has been in both places and noted the mud hole of a Rideau Canal and unsightly railway tracks cutting through the very heart of the former. 

In the story in question, a cross, marking the spot where the red-coated hero was laid at rest, bore the inscription: 

Here lies Constable John Sterling, R.N.W.M.P., died

December 1st, 1901, despite the fact that there had been no R.N.W.M.P. in 1901, the name of the force then being the N.W.M.P. only. The narrative also accused the mounties of chasing their man in a long, scarlet canoe with R.C.M.P. lettered a foot high on its side, with little or no regard for the feelings or optics of those who had to view such a monstrosity. In reality, members of the force ply the waters of lakes and rivers in black canoes, with a yellow band, about an inch-and-a-half wide, painted a little below the gunwale, with R.C.M.P. stenciled on this band in such small letters as not to be visible at any distance. The artist who had undertaken to illustrate the yarn, moreover, had also performed the feat of somewhat improving upon the force uniform.

Curly Warnock, who was something of a hack writer, having been a newspaperman before he joined the force, penned the following criticism of the story in question and mailed it to the editor of the magazine: 

Dear Mr. Editor: 

R.C.M. Police, Lethbridge, Alta. 1933

After reading your magazine for so many years and marveling at the authentic atmosphere surrounding tales like Blood Hounds, about the French Foreign Legion, Mad

Dogs, about the Russian Revolution and Crazy Cats, about the U. S. Marines, it sadly jolted me to read Snarling 

Wolves, about the Mounted Police.

First off, there was no R.N.W.M.P. in 1901, the late 

King Edward having conferred that title on the old N.W.M.P. in 1904. The author was doubtless looking

ahead, possibly to the destination of the good and gallant policeman's soul, when he inscribed his historic cross: "Constable John Sterling, R.N.W.M.P., died December 

1, 1901."

Being a member of the R.C.M.P., the present name of the same force assumed in 1920, I was both amazed and flattered at the magnificent creature the artist created for a red coat. Truly, he possessed all that is best in the Boy 

Scouts, Girl Guides, Blood Hounds, Mad Dogs and Crazy 

Cats in the way of dress. It caused me to blink at my empty beer glass in wonderment, then up at the ceiling, half anticipating a parade of pink elephants there rhumbing to sweet strains of the melodies from Rose Marie. Yours,

Constable W. J. WARNOCK 

The editor of the magazine who must have had, as one of his possessions a sense of humor, published this rather bitingly critical letter. It came out in print about a month later and Warnock began receiving letters from all parts of the United States and Canada about it, at the rate of half-a-dozen or so a day. Paul Brooker called it Curly's "fan mail" and would make some joke about it whenever the mail orderly handed the blushing Warnock his mail. 

Some of the letters Curly received from citizens of Uncle Sam were really startling in their combined naiveté and lack of knowledge concerning Canada, which a good many referred to variously as the Northland, North Country or, still more vaguely, the wide-open spaces. One of these letters was so unusual that he decided it would be mean of him not to show it to his comrades. Accordingly, as a sound preliminary step, he showed it to Shields and Brooker, whose comments and snickers quickly drew the remainder of the barrack room around them. Paul read the letter aloud to those present: 

Dear Constable Warnock: 

Street, Boston, Mass. Feb.

I have just been reading your article in 1933 Magazine and glimpsing at your picture.

My but you're

handsome.
