It gives me a real thrill, since I've always wanted to meet one of you hairy-chested he-men that always gets his man (and woman, too?) face to face- "You handsome, hairy-chested, old brute," let out Paul with a whoop of delight, grabbing Warnock around his almost hairless torso and stroking his bald pate affectionately. "Ouch, quit your horse play, you big gorilla, Brooker,"

Curly complained. "Some people are ticklish and human you know. Oww... "Go on, read some more, Paul," came from a chorus of voices. "Okay, young Lochinvar. Here goes. Let's see, I was at 'face to face . . . I wonder if you would answer a few questions for a young woman who plans taking a trip through what is known as the Canadian Northwest this summer. Don't please think I'm mad when I tell you that

I plan making the trip alone, without a male escort, and with only a light .32 automatic to protect my life, property, and virtue.' "Notice how she drops virtue back to third position, almost out of the money," Paul observed, shaking his head. "Terrible, terrible, these modern women. Just think of a woman like that leading our Curly astray. Tut, tut, tut." "Quit stalling, Paul, and read some more." "All right, if you can stand it, Hewston, I can. 'You see, my women friends are now all married, or have ties that keep them from accompanying me. They prefer the security of the fireside to the uncertain adventures of the wide open spaces and I really don't blame them either. 

In our American schools we are taught little or nothing about Canada and, when I reached college, I dropped geography from my curriculum. So, although a college graduate, my knowledge of your vast, undeveloped country is quite limited and current issues of our leading periodicals carry little to enlighten one on the subject. "I don't believe most of the stories I read about your country and would like some down-to-the-earth facts. For instance, what kind of roads are there? Are they good enough for driving an automobile on, outside of your

larger cities? If so, are there any stopping off places between cities like Toronto and Winnipeg? I don't mean towns, such as we have down here, but any kind of places, such as trading posts or small hotels, where a girl with a fair amount of good looks could obtain food and shelter for the night without being in danger of, what our fiction writers call, outraged?' “Gee, fellows, is my face getting red," Paul admitted. "Say, Tom ought to read this-he's a college grad.” "You're doing fine, Paul," Tom encouraged diplomatically. "Let's have the rest." "Another thing: are those mad trappers that we hear so much about in the newspapers really numerous? If not, I don't see many reasons why a young woman of twentyfour, with good looks and the average amount of common sense should not travel alone in Canada. I am American by birth, but a traveler by choice and realize full well that I have a lot to learn yet about your North country. I have spent most of my life in the big cities of the East, like New 

York and Boston.' 999 "Needless to jolly well say," opined Ames. "Just listen to this last paragraph," Paul urged: "In conclusion, Constable Warnock, do you think that it would be possible for a hot-house, fair weather flower like myself to survive in the harsh winds, blizzards and primitive passions of your North country?' The signature looks like 

Beatrice Gladstone, or Gallstone or something. "Oh!" groaned Callahan, writhing in apparent agony on his cot and burying his face in his pillow. "That's even too much for an Irishman. Curly, from now on, your name's mud around here." "I've got an idea for a reply to that letter, Curly," Paul said. "With your scribbling ability and my idea we ought to figure out a pretty hot line."

That evening Paul and Curly sat down at a table together, alongside Ames, who was writing a letter home to his mother in England. After many subdued chuckles and snickers they concocted the following missive, which they showed to 'Ronnie' only to receive the criticism that it was too silly and far-fetched:

Dear Miss Gladstone: 

R.C.M. Police, Lethbridge, Alta. -, 1933

In reply to your very interesting and enlightening letter, dated Feb., I might say that we do have automobile roads between cities, jumping off places and what nots in Canada; or, at least, flatter ourselves they are such. Having driven over a few thousand miles of them in a car myself, however, I admit the point is a debatable one in some instances. You see, we are in a state of semi-barbarism up here, as it were, as regards the delectable automobile, for, whereas you have one automobile to every five persons in the United States, we have only one to every 5.8 persons; a shocking and startling revelation of our Medievalism. What is more, at least 0.018765 of the 5.8 falls into the Eskimo and mad trapper class. 

As regards geography, we do have several cities that come outside the hamlet, or even the omelette, class. First, there is the delectable Montreal, a big cosmopolitan dive, with a population of over a million mad trappers, mad French-Canadians and still madder English-speaking Canadians. It is about the size of your cultured Boston and that cultural center of our other joint seashore, San 

Francisco, combined. You could also subtract your home town, Boston, from our Toronto, a hamlet with a horribly religious atmosphere about it, and still have a good-sized omelette left; or, vice-versa, you could subtract Toronto from Boston and get a minus quantity, X, which would represent all the nitwits who attempt to write about the "North Country" if lined up, shoulder to shoulder, in front of a firing squad, as they orter be (pardon my college spelling-I was only a little shaver with a degree from barber college before I joined the Mounties). 

As regards being, what our fiction writers call, “outraged" I think you'll be reasonably safe in paying us a visit. There are lots of nice hotels and places in the smaller towns between our cities, where Americans like you are decidedly welcome guests. I think I would leave your .32 automatic at home, however, as mad trappers

are out of season during the summer months and your person, property and virtue will be reasonably safe without it-excepting, perhaps, the latter, if you come to 

Lethbridge. For you see, I am around thirty, single, a mounted policeman who is lonely in these dreary wastes; 

I also know something about geography, especially contours, and have a weakness for college girls and ex-college girls who write to lonely mounties. Hoping to see you this summer, if all goes well, I well, I am, Yours for a college lass of virtue,

BILL WARNOCK. 

Curly received a rather aloof reply from "Beatrice" about three weeks later stating that, on account of certain artistic leanings in her nature, she had decided to spend her summer vacation on a tourist trip to Eurpoe, instead of coming North. "We probably lost a friendly tourist for Canada and hurt the girl's feelings by that goofy letter of ours, Paul," 

Curly opined uneasily. "What of it?" Paul growled. "A great Don Juan, like you, whose picture appears in magazines with a circulation all over the United States and Canada, shouldn't have taken the advice of a mere buck mountie like me. 

What do we want with a bunch of gaping tourists up in 

Canada, anyway? Let 'em go to Europe and go googlyeyed over a bunch of artistic hooey. When they come up here they usually bring only one shirt and a pair of pants, or one skirt, a blouse and a brassiere, and a ten spot and don't change either, anyway.' 

"Paul, you're positively primitive," said Ames reprovingly.

XII 

ANOTHER TRANSFER 

Tom was sitting on the edge of his cot, shining the buttons on his red serge tunic for a parade, one noon hour, when he was aware of Paul Brooker standing over him, eyeing him intently. Without any conscious direction, his thoughts had been mulling over the existence of the Keufel interests and speculating as to whether they really controlled a large part of the Lethbridge vice district, as Sergeant Schlitz had said they did. If it were so and they also controlled vice districts in other Western 

Canadian cities, in addition to their huge liquor smuggling activities, then they must constitute one of the largest and most powerful crime organizations in existence, he reflected. Trouble was, as Tweed had admitted, they made large contributions to political campaign funds. That made the law work with them, rather than against them. 

They were like a caterpillar that has broken through the firm, outside crust of an apple and, from then on, finds the going easy. International treaties, such as that between 

Canada and the United States over liquor smuggling, were being broken by them daily, at the risk of straining the relations between the two most friendly neighboring nations in the world. Girls, like the beautiful Lucienne, were their victims. . . .

This train of thought was suddenly broken by Sergeant Schlitz striding into the barrack room in his usual brusque, officious manner. His large, excited eyes seemed about evenly focused between Tom and Paul Brooker. "Brooker and Shields," he said, "you are both excused from parade. The O.C. has just notified me you are to be transferred to Calgary for riot squad duty tonight." "Very good, sergeant." They looked at one another uneasily. To Tom it meant another break in a possible 98

ANOTHER TRANSFER 

99 thread connecting the wealthy, semi-respectable Keufels with their underworld activities-this time with the Inner 

Circle of Parisian white slave traffickers. To Paul it meant the end of hockey for that year and becoming a unit of the one phase of the R.C.M.P. he despised: the riot squad detail.

As they stood on the railway platform that evening, prior to boarding a train for Calgary, Brooker orated quietly on the red-baiting activities of the Force to Tom and the other members of "K" Division who had come to see them off. "Because a bunch of spoiled brats of the upper classes get their big shot pappies and uncles panicked by reading

Karl Marx and talking about a trip to the Soviet Union to see the stuffed cadaver of Lenin, it's our job to splash the sidewalks with human blood. I wish they had to do their own dirty work. . . Paul stopped short as Sergeant

Schlitz strode up to them from the railway station. 

"Shields," Schlitz announced, "I have a special errand for you. They're sending a female lunatic down here and are holding up this train until she arrives. She's a nice person about forty-five and a real battleaxe. Seems she fell for a German farmer about ten years her junior.

She tried attracting his attention by catching his prize chickens, chopping off their heads, and sending their headless bodies staggering back into his lot. She attracted his attention all right. Funny courtship. Ha, ha, ha," 

Schlitz burst into hearty, raucous laughter at what he considered his own killing sense of humour. "I'm sending a police matron along, so's she won't run off with our own handsome Shields. Ha, ha, ha..." 

The group caught Schlitz's rough, but contagious, laugh and laughed with him. "Your sense of humour kills me," Tom announced reluctantly. "You'll take her all the way up to Panoka, Shields," 

Schlitz announced. "That's about a hundred and fifty miles North of Calgary. Then you're to return to Calgary and report for riot squad duty-the same as Brooker." "All right, sergeant." 

---

After he and Paul had installed the female lunatic, strapped to a stretcher, in a baggage car of the train, they lurched out onto the platform to bid a final round of farewells. Johnson took Tom to one side, as Schlitz wound into the preliminary stages of one of his long, rambling jokes. These were in the nature of a command performance, for although generally stale, Schlitz had a way of domineering his subordinates into listening to him. "Tom," Johnson said, "there's somebody I'd like you to meet up home at Calgary, besides dropping in on my folks a girl friend of mine. She's a school teachergood at games and a musician. I think you'd get along well with her." "What's her name?" Tom asked, as a bell began clanging the news that it was less than a minute to train time. 

Johnson made one or two false starts on the name in opposition to the bell and finally took an empty envelope from his pocket and wrote a name and address on the back of it. "Mary Andreachuk," Tom read without enthusiasm.

He didn't like the name, but put the piece of paper in his pocket out of consideration for Johnson's feelings. "Thanks a lot, Eddie, I'll look Mary up when I get there,' he promised. 66 

The last sound reaching his ears as the train commenced to move was Schlitz's prodigious voice announcing: 

And the poor man had turned so much in his grave, because of her unfaithfulness, that Saint Peter said: 'Madam, you must be looking for our figure skating champion, revolving Jones.' 999 

On Tom's return to Calgary the following evening, after delivering his charge into the hands of the mental hospital authorities at Panoka, he found himself installed in the finest R.C.M.P. quarters in the whole Dominion.

They took up the entire eighth floor of the new and grandiose Calgary Post Office Building and, in conveniences and accommodation, bore a marked contrast to most 

Mounted Police quarters.

Paul had selected a bed for him and unpacked most of

ANOTHER TRANSFER 

101 his equipment and placed it on the different pegs and shelves assigned for the purpose. "Guess who's been sent out from Vancouver to take charge of the riot squad here, Tom," Paul said, as Tom thanked him. "Who, Smokey Harris?" Tom asked uneasily. "No." "Must be Sergeant Greenleaf then," Tom let out with relief. "It's neither-it's Inspector Tweed." "Why that's impossible! Tweed's far too brainy a man to be put in charge of a riot squad," Tom declared, despite his prejudices. "That's his trouble, Tom, he's a little bit too brainy at times. I hear that's why they've given him the air in 

Vancouver. You know, I had a quarter-hour interview with him this morning and he asked me who the other man was they'd sent up from Lethbridge with me. When I told him it was you, you should have seen the look that came over those long, aristocratic features of his. Old 

Frankenstein himself couldn't have looked any worse and

Tweed's a good looking guy. It wasn't an ordinary look, like a fellow gets when he's sore, but a sort of cold, mean one. What have you ever done to the man to get him that way, Tom?" "I'll try to explain it to you one of these days, Paul." "Tweed's a bad guy to pick as an enemy, Tom. He's got plenty of drag in the government and will probably squirm out of the present jam he's in like old Houdini. 

He's worth a load of dough, too. Do you know, when I was having that interview with him, the phone rang and he talked over long distance with a broker in New York about buying a bunch of stocks while the market's still low. 

Seemed to figure Roosevelt was going to do something about the price of gold and conditions in general. Guess he isn't the smartest man in the world, though, or he wouldn't have been bounced down to this riot squad stuff from Vancouver." "How about his transfer from Vancouver, Paul?”

"Well, I heard one or two rumours amongst the boys down at the offices today. You know how the old grapevine works in this Force. You hear the darndest rumours and then they have a way of coming true with a big rumpus, as if no one knew anything about them, a couple of weeks later. Remember how I told you we were going to be transferred to Lethbridge?" "Yes, but what about Tweed?" "Well, the boys have it figured out that the U. S. Federal men have found an awful lot of narcotics getting into New York recently and they think quite a bit of it's been getting in on the silk trains that take the silk bales off the liners in from the Orient at Vancouver and then speed East with them. The smugglers must hide the dope inside the silk bales at Vancouver, since they are searched before and after being put aboard the ships. That means the R.C.M.P. and Tweed, in particular, have been falling down on the job. They figure there's a clique of wealthy 

Vancouver business men in on it somewhere getting a rake off, as the job couldn't be handled by small fry.' "Who are the boys who've been talking about it?" "Patenaude, a Montreal Frenchman, who's been working on the files at this office, seems to have most of the dope. He figures that Vancouver big shot-what the hell's his name Adolph Keufel, is in on it." 

Tom looked at Paul sharply. "How could he possibly know a thing like that, Paul?” "Don't ask me, Tom." "Where is Patenaude now?" "He was transferred up to Provincial Headquarters at Edmonton tonight. He was eating with us in the mess just before he left. Seems Tweed had something to do with it."

As Tom bathed and tidied up and changed into his civilian clothes, he wondered why Tweed had been transferred from Vancouver. The most significant thing that 

Paul had told him, from a purely personal angle, had been Tweed's reaction to the information that he, too, was in Calgary. For that reason, it overshadowed even Paul's mention of Adolph Keufel in Tom's thoughts. It sug-

ANOTHER TRANSFER 

103 gested that Tweed's transfer had been due, in part at least, to the exposure of the agent provocateur, Felix Fonck, or to some item concerning the Keufel liquor ring; rather than to the rumoured activities Paul had spoken of. Else why should Tweed adopt a vindictive attitude towards him -since he had been more sinned against, than sinning, where Tweed was concerned?

Paul had been assigned to night-guard duty that evening and Tom, feeling restless, decided to go out alone, rather than get in a bridge game with some of the new faces he had met around the quarters. He was about to flip a coin to determine whether it would be a movie or the wrestling match, when he remembered the girl's name 

Johnson had given him on leaving Lethbridge. He hadn't liked her name and, after delving into the pocket of his tunic and bringing forth the creased envelope with the name, Mary Andreachuk, on it, still didn't fancy it. He decided, however, that he owed his comrade, Johnson, the courtesy of phoning her up.

Mary, herself, answered his telephone call and Tom decided that her voice, at any rate, was all right-very pleasing, in fact. He became curious to know more about her and asked if he might call on her that evening. Her answer was in the affirmative; so he paraded before the orderly officer and obtained his signature to a late pass from barracks.

XIII 

A WOMAN AND AN OPEN FIRE 

The Andreachuks lived in a substantial and cosy house with sloping roofs in a good part of Calgary. It looked very inviting at night with its dark, sloping outline contrasted against the warm, orange glow of light issuing from the windows. Tom felt more at ease as he viewed the house and discerned its number. 

The name, Andreachuk, had suggested the familiar term "bohunk" to him, which, in turn, had aroused the conception of garlic, squalor and communistic literature. This prejudiced view is nothing uncommon among Anglo- Saxon North Americans of his class, even despite a good education. Canadian historians and colleges are usually too intent on glorifying the Britsh connection and in covering up the graft and subjugation inherent in Imperialism to spend much time reconciling the conflicting views and backgrounds of Canadians themselves. 

A tall, fair girl with blue eyes, high cheek bones and fine, sensitive features answered his knock at the door and greeted him with a friendly, but rather shy, “Oh, hello.' He was not quite sure whether or not it was the same voice he had heard over the telephone. He guessed her age to be somewhere between twenty and twenty-three. 

She was not beautiful, barely pretty; but there was a spiritual quality in the pattern of her face which attracted him and made him look twice. Her blondness puzzled him. Any Slavs he had known before were dark. Yet it seemed unquestionably natural with her. Could she be someone else? A married friend of the family or a sisterin-law? Usually such women were snapped up for wives young; though the depression had modified this practise, in all probability. 104

A WOMAN AND AN OPEN FIRE 105 

"Are you Miss Mary Andreachuk?" "Yes," she smiled. "Come on in, Constable Shieldsat least, I presume that's who it is. Don't let me down by starting to explain life insurance or vacuum cleaners to me now." "No," Tom promised. "I'm the one who phoned you up all right. I hope I don't look too much like a policeman, though. I can forgive you for mistaking me for a possible insurance or vacuum cleaner salesman.'

Mary laughed and her laughter was musical and pleasant, he admitted. 99 "Don't be sarcastic around this house," she warned him. "We don't allow any punsters or gag men." 

She led him through a hall into a drawing room, in which a fire of logs and box wood burned with a cheerful crackle in a large, open fireplace. The flames of the fire drove the darkness almost back to the walls of the room, flickering on the glass facings of book cases and the mahogany form of a grand piano and revealing the aquiline features of a young, dark, moustached man of about thirty, slumped down in an arm chair reading a book. He looked up as they entered the room. "My father and mother are out for the evening," Mary explained to Tom, "but this is my brother, Doctor Nicholas Andreachuk, who is better known around here as Nick, or Nicki. He's the brains of the family. Been back from post-graduate studies in Vienna about a year now." "Glad to know you, Doctor Andreachuk," Tom said. "Are you practising general surgery, or specializing in some particular branch, or is that an unethical question to ask a doctor?" "Quite ethical as far as I'm concerned," the young man admitted pleasantly, rising and shaking Tom's hand. "I'm specializing in nerve surgery, if that means anything to you. By the way, I didn't quite catch your name." "I forgot to tell you, Nick," said his sister, "this is 

Constable Shields of the Royal Canadian Mounted Police. 

Eddie Johnson, down at Lethbridge, gave him an introduction and asked him to look us up.'

"Glad to know you, Constable Shields. I know Eddie well. He's a great boy and quite a friend of Mary's too. 

How does he like the Mounties?" "He likes the Force fine and it likes him. He's one fellow that seems to be cut out for anything he wants to try." "I'm glad to hear that. Sorry I've got to be rushing off right away and must apologize. I've got to give a talk to a class of nurses this evening and you know what women are: awful hagglers after punctuality and never on time themselves. I'm supposed to be at the hospital in about ten or fifteen minutes. I hope we'll be seeing more of you. Drop around any time you have an evening to spare and don't wait on formalities like an engraved invitation card from this informal household." "Thanks," Tom said. He sat down on the piano stool and waited while Miss Andreachuk-Mary-saw her brother to the front door. "This is a nice grand piano," he opined, fingering the keys of the instrument, as she returned. "Steinway, eh? 

I've been told you're quite a violinist, Mary. Do you feel in the mood to play me something?" "Needless to say, Eddie Johnson told you that about me. I'm afraid he was probably exaggerating things a little. Can you play violin accompaniments, Constable 

Shields ?" "After a fashion, that is, yes and no, Mary. Let's take a crack at it together anyway. My first name's Thomas, or just plain Tom, you know, any time you feel like dispensing with formalities." "Oh all right, Tom. That music lamp turns on down at the wall. Just a minute and I'll get my fiddle out. 

Give me F will you.' 99 They played many old favorites together, including 

Rubenstein's Melody in F, Barcarolle, from "Tales of 

Hoffman," and one or two Strauss waltzes; interspersed with several newer and lighter pieces from the current talking films, Rio Rita, Whoopee and Forty-second Street.

Tom was amazed at the expression and depth of tone in her playing. He had never heard a woman player who

A WOMAN AND AN OPEN FIRE 107 

equalled her before. Kreisler and Heifetz, whom he had heard play in Vancouver, decidedly surpassed her, but they were men. His accompanying was no great shakes either. He wondered if her playing would sound as good in a large concert hall or auditorium as in the averagesized drawing room they were playing in. "You know, Mary," he told her, mopping his brow with a handkerchief after they had completed a selection, "if your playing sounds half as good on the concert stage as it does here you're committing a crime by not making music your career. Haven't your friends and relatives ever told you that? Why, you'd be a sensation!" "My mother and father have always been opposed to my becoming a professional musician, Tom. You see, music is pretty well knocked on the head these days, anyway, for average musicians like myself. There's the radio and canned music. You have to be an outstanding genius, like Kreisler, to make a go of it these days. I forget how many thousand professional musicians are out of work in New York alone today, but they are numerous, anyway. I was reading about it only a week or so ago. 

Most of them are probably a darn sight better than I am, too. So probably mother and dad are right in steering me away from a musical career and keeping my nose to the grindstone of high school teaching. Of course, I could easily tell them to mind their own business, if I wanted to, since I'm independent and a little past twenty-one," she added, with a toss of the head. "Does that mean you're twenty-three or four?" Tom questioned provocatively. "Neither. Don't be absurd, Tom. I was twenty-two only a couple of months ago. See, smarty." "That's all I want to know, Mary. You were born in this country, were you not?" "Yes, Nicki and I both. My father came out to Manitoba from the Old Country in 1893. He was a good doctor, but had quite a time of it improving his English at first. Most of his work was among the poorer section of the immigrant population, who were a whole lot faster at getting sick and having kids than paying their bills.

However, he did a lot of good among them and that is what a doctor is for. He met my mother in 1897 and they were married about two years later in Winnipeg. 

She's a Scotswoman and that's why I'm so fair; I take after her. The joke of the matter is, I'm even fairer than she is. However, my features and temperament are my dad's. Nick was born in 1903 and they moved out here to Alberta early in 1905, when he was only eighteen months old. I'm a native of Calgary." "So you were born in nineteen eleven, Mary." "Yes, eleven from thirty-three still makes twenty-two,

Tom. You can't catch a school teacher lying about her age as easily as all that." "So you're a member of the great, stupid, smug Canadian middle-class, the same as I, apart from racial differences, Mary." "I don't think the middle-class is stupid and smug, Tom; to me, it always seems to be the one class that escapes the degrading influences of poverty, along with the flabbiness and self-complacency of the wealthy and aristocratic classes.' "Come now, you're not lecturing before a class, Mary; but to a full-fledged adult, with a semi-adult mind. I wouldn't mind being just brim full of flabbiness and selfcomplacency, if I were wealthy. In fact, I rate flabbiness at the top of all virtues—providing it is accompanied by at least ten thousand a year—with self-complacency second by a nose. As for our stiff, middle-class morality, I think it's a lot of bunk and needs blasting out with a few sticks of dynamite, like an ice jam." "Well let's skip the middle-class topic then, Tom. You mentioned something about racial differences between us. 

I suppose if I tell you I'm a Canadian at heart, as well as in theory, you'll say something about the term Canadian covering a multitude of sins and bad blood." "Nothing of the sort, Mary. I think you're swell. I just like a good argument, that's all. By the way, why doesn't your family change its name to something like 

Andrews, instead of Andreachuk? You and Nick could pass as perfectly good Anglo-Saxons any day."

A WOMAN AND AN OPEN FIRE 109 

"That's a rotten idea, Tom. I'm sure Nick would be as much against it as I am. Why should we do such a thing? Our family name has a good and honorable record behind it, both here and over in Europe and as long as we're loyal to Canada and to British ideals, why should it trouble us? I'm part Anglo-Saxon myself, but see nothing to be ashamed of in my name." "I never quite looked at it that way before, Mary." "Well you know now, Tom. It's funny," she added, laughing, "we meeting one another like this for the first time and discussing such intimate subjects and quarreling over them like a couple of old cronies; isn't it, though?" "Yes, I suppose. Let's sit on the sofa, in front of the fire, even if we have to touch on taboo subjects and get one another's nanny." "Oh all right."

They sat on the sofa and Tom placed his arm about her waist, which was slender and sensitive; more so than he had anticipated. Their heads touched one another and they lolled back comfortably. "Ever have a feeling, Tom, about someone you'd just met that you'd know them for ages and ages?" "Yes, Mary." "You know, Tom, I like your brown eyes. They dance a little and they're rather tempting. You're so tall and handsome and, perhaps, a trifle clever, too. I imagine you're like every mountie, though, and have a sweetheart in every town and city you're stationed at. You're certainly a bunch of devils with the ladies, if half of what I've heard about you is true." "I think you're getting us confused with sailors, Mary." "Ugh, ugh. Eddie Johnson is nothing like you—I mean the conventional, lady-killer mountie. I'd trust him anywhere, but, then, Eddie and I are just like brother and sister." "Would you like me to be that way, Mary-I mean, like a brother." "Oh you couldn't be. Not with those interesting brown eyes and shaggy eyebrows: they're too ominous and wicked."

"I guess women can't be sisterly to ominous and wicked men," Tom said, squeezing Mary's waist tighter and stealing a kiss from her, which she resisted, just a little, "they've got to love 'em and let it go at that." "You can't be accused of a lack of self-confidence, Tom." "You know, Mary, a mountie isn't allowed to get married until he has put in six years service on the Force and I haven't a full year to my credit yet. Would you er -I mean, could a man make love to you, if he were not married to you? You know, the whole shooting match, everything.

He put the question in a bumbling, blustering manner that seemed to presage defeat and a face slapping at every word. Instead, Mary regarded him calmly and thoughtfully for a moment and then answered him a trifle coldly. "Not unless I were very, very much in love with him, Tom. There's something about true love which is bigger than the barriers of conventions, perhaps, but love like that is not something just contracted on the spur of the moment."

There was a chastity and firmness about her eyes as she spoke that made him glance away from her into the dying embers of the fire. "I'm sorry for speaking that way, Mary," he confessed quickly. "I must be becoming a crude sort of person. You know, a policeman's life isn't exactly an uplifting one, despite propaganda to the contrary. You run up against the lowest types of people. Then, too, the R.C.M.P. has degenerated under this Bennett Conservative government into a sort of Fascist organization. The Conservatives are always panicked by what they term the red menace.

The menace is mostly in their own imagination, but that hasn't stopped them turning the R.C.M.P. into a Europeantype of police organization for suppressing radicalism. 

The result is that the Force is filled with the lower types of strong-arm men and bullies, like the Fascist organizations over in Europe. In that sort of atmosphere, a man is bound to slip a notch or two morally. Meeting you is like a tonic. It's like coming out of a stuffy barrack

A WOMAN AND AN OPEN FIRE 111 

room, filled with odours and profanity, into clean, fresh air once again." "Why don't you leave the Force and go back to engineering, Tom?" "I'd like to, but I'm signed on for five years, worst luck. Besides, if I were to get out, I'd have to find a job and that's impossible in this country without pull. You should know that. Unless a man crawls about on his belly and licks the boots of everyone of any importance in this country, he doesn't stand a chance. Being kow-towed to and fawned upon is the only type of sociability our 

Canadian upper classes can appreciate. Unfortunately, belly crawling and boot licking are not among my accomplishments.' 

"What are among your accomplishments, Tom?" "Being a fairly good student; possessing a moderately clear liberal outlook; good powers of analysis; pretty fair athletic ability. The last thing is the only one that isn't a positive handicap on the Canadian scene. When can I see you again, Mary?" "I have a few essays to correct for my class tomorrow evening, but I should be through by eight o'clock. How about coming around then?" "Fine. Thanks."

When Tom had returned to barracks and barely climbed into bed, the night-guard stomped up to his cot and started shaking him. "I'm awake," he protested. "All right, well get into your breeches and boots and brown serge right away and strap on your side-arms,' the night-guard told him. "They're calling for you in a patrol car in five minutes. There's been a murder out in the country. Here, Brooker, wake up. You'll have to go along, too." "Our first murder case," Tom yawned to Paul, as he scrambled out of bed and reached for his breeches and .455 

Colt revolver. "What a life!" "Better than riot squad duty, anyway," Paul mumbled sleepily.

XIV HOMICIDE WITH MALICE AFORETHOUGHT 

"A farmer, called George Parsons, has been found dead on his farm, about twenty miles out of the city-murdered," the chauffeur informed them, as they settled beside him in the front seat of the car. "The call came through at one-forty. That's all I know, along with the locality." 

In about twenty minutes the car had taken them to the gate of a farm on a main country highway, around which a group of people were clustered and talking freely. Several flashlights were turned in their direction. “I have an idea this is the place," the police chauffeur told them. "I'll ask one of these people." He addressed one of a group loitering at a fence: "Is this the Parsons farm?" "It was George Parsons' farm, officer," an individual of about sixty drawled. "T'aint no more though-George's dead; murdered by some one." He spat a chew of tobacco juice. "I've knowed George for nigh on fifteen years come this-'

They drove up a driveway to the front of a farm house and the driver stopped the car abruptly, as its headlights revealed an object lying on the ground under a blanket. 

Paul jumped out of the car ahead of Tom and rolled the blanket half away, exposing the blood-soaked body of a man.

There are two commonplace reactions on viewing a corpse: the first, one of horror, mingled with thoughts of a hereafter, dictated by the instinct of self-preservation, which says briefly, "My God, that thing there might have been me"; the other, one of reluctant mirth, aroused by the contorted position of the object and the glassy helplessness about its eyes. Tom seemed to react in the former manner; Paul, however, couldn't quite repress a trace of a smile. 112

HOMICIDE WITH MALICE 

113 Two men were standing nearby and they suddenly moved into the arc lighted by the car's headlights. "I'm Doctor Duncan Cambell," one of them-a tall, elderly man with greying hair-said to Tom. "I was called out by Mr. Gaetz, here, who phoned in after the thing happened." He indicated a slight man with a little, mousey moustache and very well-kept hair for a farmer. "Parsons, poor chap, was dead long before I got here.

His heart must be penetrated in twenty or thirty places by that shot." "Killed by a shot gun at close range," Tom meditated.

He looked quizically at the man indicated as Mr. Gaetz. "I have the farm next to this one," Mr. Gaetz volunteered quickly, without being asked for the information. "That little house you see over there with the lights on in the windows is mine. This has been a very still night and sounds carry far in this neighborhood. At around half-past twelve or one o'clock I was disturbed by voices carrying on a loud conversation of some kind over here and, shortly afterwards, there was the loud report of a gun. Naturally, I suspected foul play and came right over to see what was up. I found George Parsons lying just as you see him now, with his terrier, Copper King, tugging at the collar of his shirt, trying to wake his dead master up." "I can see where the collar's been torn by the dog's teeth. 

That him over there?" Tom asked, indicating a brown terrier standing whimpering near the body. "Yes, that's him. After that, I noticed noises coming from Parsons' house and an automobile parked about fifty feet down the driveway with the lights out. It was an old Ford coupe. I was for going up into the house at first and investigating, but I decided it wasn't safe." "How do you know it was a Ford coupe you saw?" "If it wasn't an old Ford coupe, I don't know what other make of car it could have been. I stayed around a few minutes, listening to the noises coming from inside the house and trying to decide what I should do. Then I finally decided to beat it back to my own home and phone for the police and Doctor Cambell. I had just got out

of the clearing into the shadows of that clump of trees over there, when two men came out of the house. One of them, who might have been middle-aged or elderly, was carrying a shot gun, or rifle, and a thing which seemed like a bag. 

The other, who seemed like just a young fellow, wasn't carrying nothing and seemed to be remonstrating and bawling the older fellow out for what had just occurred." "Did you recognize either of them?" "No, and I don't know positively as I could recognize either of them, if I saw them again. I got an inkling of their outlines only. I might recognize them partly by their movements and gestures, if I saw them together again, acting as a pair like. The feller carrying the gun, I would say was the leader of the two, judging by the way he acted and seemed to be bossing the other, who was just a youngster, and telling him what to do. He seemed to be an oldish man, or man about my age; while the other was only about twenty or thereabouts, I would judge from his voice." "Did you catch any of their conversation, Mr. Gaetz?” "Nothing definite, no. They spoke a few words to one another that I couldn't make out; but when they got in the coupe, the young feller took the wheel and turned on the headlights of the car. The older man distinctly bawled him out for doing it-turning on the headlights, I mean -and the lights were turned out again. Although I couldn't catch what they actually said, I got the gist of it and the sound of their voices all right. They drove off without headlights on and then I went straight over to my house and phoned for you and Doctor Cambell." "You're quite sure that you can't recollect anything definite they said, Mr. Gaetz?” "Positive." "Will you show us where you saw the car parked."

The farmer led them to a point about fifty feet from where they were standing and turned a flashlight on the ground. "It was right here!" He pointed to a patch of mixed mud and thawing snow. "And here are the fresh tire tracks of the car."

HOMICIDE WITH MALICE 

115 Brooker turned his own flashlight on the spot indicated and squatted down close to the ground. He let out a soft whistle. "Here are some pretty clear footprints made from rubber-soled shoes," he let out. "You can just about make out a trade mark on one of them. We'll have to get moulages of them right away, Tom, if the wet snow will stand it. May I look at your boots a moment, Mr. 

Gaetz, please. . . . Leather-soled boots. Thanks.

How about yours, Doctor Cambell?... I'm not being suspicious of everyone, like a mystery book detective, but I don't want to put us to a lot of trouble if these footprints don't belong to the pair we're after, that's all. Now let's see the dead man's boots. . . . Hobnailed, eh? Did Parsons have anyone—any visitors-come up this way today, Mr. 

Gaetz ?" "No, he had none at all that I know of. It was very seldom that anyone came up here to see him. He was a lonely, queer sort of man, with all kinds of money, so they say. In all the twelve, come thirteen, years that George 

Parsons and I've been neighbors, I don't think, off hand, we've even spoken to one another on an average of more'n a dozen times a year, if that many. I'd be inclined to call him a miser myself, if t'weren't wrong to speak disrespectful of the dead. They say he kept most of his money hidden away in that house of his somewheres, instead of putting it in a bank. It was common gossip all around these parts and I can easily believe it. He came up from the States originally, so he told me, and didn't believe banks were safe or something. I guess those two who came here tonight got to hearing all about his reputation and murdered him for his money.' "We'll have a better idea of the motive behind it all after we've looked the house over," Paul told him. "Thanks for your suggestions, though, Mr. Gaetz. Look, Shields, how would it be if we split our duties to save time and cover the job better," he suggested, turning to Tom. "One of us can take the inside of the house, while the other stays out here to get those footprints and look things over." "Okay," Tom agreed, "you go along inside."

Tom looked over the footprints in the mud and slushy

snow. The mud and snow was too soft to use plaster of 

Paris on to get the usual moulages; he would have to resort to laborious measurements and sketches by flashlight. There were two sets of impressions; both made by rubber-soled shoes, the sizes of which differed by one or two standard shoe sizes. He made careful measurements and sketches of the sole patterns of each pair, by taking the most distinct prints of each and picking the clearest spots out of these prints to fit together in his sketches, as in a jig-saw puzzle. He also ascertained that the footprints led up to the house and back. This occupied considerable time and he became curious to know what was detaining the others so long in the house. 

He was about to go up there to find out, when he discovered something lying in one of the tire ruts, which he had previously overlooked. He picked it up. It was a twelve-gauge shot gun shell smelling strongly of freshly discharged powder. He went straight up to the house with his find.

When he got up to the summit of a slight rise in the ground and entered the house, he found the place in considerable disorder, with papers and various articles of clothing strewn all over the floors. A stream of light was issuing from one of the rooms-the living room, it turned out to be—and voices accompanied the source of light.

Tom found Paul examining a fireplace in the living room, while Gaetz and the local constable were each holding up an oil lamp to assist him. The back of the fireplace had been moved away to one side, exposing a rather frail species of strong box which had been smashed open, probably with an axe, which lay on the floor nearby. 

"Whoever did this job, knew something about this hiding place where Parsons kept his money and valuables,' Paul reflected. "It took us a little while to notice it, even with the back of the fireplace removed like this. It's a cinch they couldn't have hit upon it by a chance, random search, in the short time they had at their disposal. All those pulled-out drawers and overturned chairs are evidently a blind to make it appear they were casual thieves who had

HOMICIDE WITH MALICE 

117 no prior knowledge of where Parsons hid his money. I begin to get an inkling of this affair already. What have you found, Shields?" "Take a whiff of this," Tom said, handing Paul the shot-gun shell, wrapped in a handkerchief.

Grey streaks had begun to show in the sky by the time they were finished looking over the scene of the crime and had shipped the corpse away in a hearse. They accepted an invitation to have an early breakfast with Dr. Cambell and Tom availed himself of the opportunity to phone into Calgary from the doctor's house for further instructions. He was told to stay on the job and cruise around the countryside with Paul and the chauffeur in the car in following up any clues, however slight, that they might have obtained. These instructions were curt and final. He conveyed them to Paul. "The clues we've got are slight enough all right to start following up," Paul stated. "As far as I can see there's only two things to do: question old man Gaetz further, on the assumption he might know more than he has already told us; or look for sets of footprints around the countryside which correspond to those you've got the dope on. 

The one thing is just as absurd and as big a waste of time as the other. Anyone can see old man Gaetz is on the level: there's nothing subtle about that German type; they're as simple and open as a book." "Yes, I agree with you, Paul, but I've got an idea." "What is it?” "Crooks usually aren't athletes, are they?" "Well-sometimes, yes; sometimes, no. Quite often they're not because they're subnormal." "Then again, Paul, Gaetz said that one of the men he saw make the getaway seemed to be elderly.' "Yeah, but what're you driving at?" "Just this: what use would an elderly man have for a brand new pair of running shoes? Remember how clear and sharp those footprints were in the mud and snow? 

You could almost make out a trade-mark on one of them.

There's a good chance that pair bought a brand-new pair of running shoes each specially to do this job. Suppose

we spend our time looking around to see if we can't find out where they got the shoes?" "That's an awful long shot, Tom. They might have bought them anywhere from Vancouver to Winnipeg; and, if they're bums or transients or regular criminals, they probably stole them." "Yes, but they're not bums or transients or regular criminals just moving from place to place. They evidently live in this locality. What is more, they must have known 

Parsons at one time or another. How else could they have found that secret money and valuables cache of his?" "Sa-ay, I never thought of that. By heck, I believe you've got something, Tom. It's worth trying out anyway. When shall we start?" "As soon as it's early enough for any of the country stores to be open. We'll cruise around in a radius of twenty or thirty miles and stop at all the merchandise stores that look as though they might handle shoes. At the first one we stop at we'll find out what the sizes of them are to save time at the other ones. My sketches are exactly full-size. Whatever happens, we'll be able to claim we didn't absolutely waste our time when we get back to 

Calgary. How about money to pay for the gas?" "I've got about four bucks on me." "Okay, we're all set."

XV MURDER TRAIL 

They drove about the countryside all that morning, questioning the various store-keepers about the sale of running shoes in their establishments. The sizes of the shoes they were interested in turned out to be seven-anda-halves and nines, respectively. After having lunch at a small, country hotel, they set out once more and stopped in front of a country general store, about thirty miles from the scene of the murder. It was typical of the dozen or more they had called at during the morning. "Good afternoon," Tom greeted a wizened, bespectacled merchant who looked up at them from behind his spectacles and a long counter as they entered the store. "Looks as though spring is well on its way, doesn't it?" "Oh yes," the store-keeper drawled. "What can I do for you? You mounties are not stationed here locally are you? I thought Constable Wilson-" "No," Tom explained, "we're from the city. We're both stationed at Calgary at present. We're trying to obtain a little information right now and thought, perhaps, you might be able to assist us." "I'll be glad to," the merchant promised, "so long as it don't involve stool-pigeoning on any of my regular customers. My name's Jones-Mr. Wilfred Jones." "Glad to know you, Mr. Jones." "You know, I'm dead against stool-pigeoning, yes sir—" "So are we, Mr. Jones," Paul assured the merchant, winking at Tom. A hand-shaking followed to prove his point; then Paul asked the merchant if he kept running shoes in stock. "Why yes," Mr. Jones replied. "What kind do you have in mind? I have some miscellaneous stock on hand left over from last summer in that line. The tennis and 119

softball players didn't take as many pair as I had anticipated. The badminton club people are about the only ones who buy running shoes at this time of year as a rule, although I sold two pair only the day before yesterday to a man and his son."

Paul and Tom exchanged significant glances. "Let us see the kind you sold to those two, will you," 

Tom requested. "Why certainly."

The merchant ambled over to a shelf stacked with boot and shoe boxes and, after a few moments reflection, drew two boxes down from the shelf. "I sold old man Kenneth Macdonald a pair like this and his son, Cliff, one of these lighter kinds," he said, taking a shoe out of each box and handing them to Tom. 

Tom couldn't restrain his excitement. The tread patterns on the soles corresponded exactly with the sketches he had constructed from the footprints at the scene of the crime. "They're the ones all right, Paul," he almost shouted in his excitement. "This is a real mystery thriller come true. 

I don't even need to look at my sketches, because I know them by heart. What sizes were those shoes you sold to the Macdonalds, Mr. Jones?" "Just a minute and I'll find out," the storekeeper said stoically; and ambled off down his shelves again to take a look at the boxes. "Mister Macdonald's were size seven-and-a-half and the lad's nines," the merchant announced in a somewhat screechy voice from down the store aisle. "Thanks," Tom called back. "That settles it, Paul. 

What do you know about the Macdonalds, Mr. Jones?" he asked the store-keeper as he ambled back to them. "Not very much," the merchant answered diplomatically. "I just see them occasionally. They come into this store once in a while; just go and come and come and go, like most other folks. There's Mister Macdonald and his wife, Melia, and their only son, Cliff, who's around twenty-one. At least, I think Cliff Macdonald is about twenty-one, anyway. They hardly ever spend any money

MURDER TRAIL 

121 in this store these days, because they're on relief and haven't any to spend. They're as poor as church mice right now and away in arrears on their taxes, I believe. 

When wheat was up around a dollar an' a quarter a bushel, it was a different story with them. They were making real money in those days, like most everyone else, and were just about as reckless in spending it as anybody this side of Winnipeg. "When they come in here Tuesday, that's the day before yesterday, and bought them running shoes and ordered a dollar's worth of gas for their old crate, I thought the pair of them had gone bug house, because they lack some of the ordinary, bare necessities of life over on that farm of theirs, from what I hear. But, then, that's the way with people nowadays. I don't like the idea of people spending money on luxuries, like gas and running shoes, when they're charges of the Government, do you?" "No, it doesn't seem strictly right," Tom admitted, “although I guess everyone has the right to live the same as you or I. What make of car is theirs?" "An old Ford coupe. When I asked them what they were buying the shoes for they looked at one another and the old man answered me a trifle gruffly that they were going prospecting up North and that he preferred wearing rubber-soled running shoes in the bush to logging boots. 

I asked him out of curiosity what Missis Macdonald was going to do in their absence, since she couldn't be expected to run what is left of their farm alone. She's just a wee bit of a woman is Melia Macdonald. He told me to mind my own damned business and said he'd be coming into some easy money shortly and would, in future, take his business to some other store where they knew how to keep their mouths shut and their prying tongues out of a customer's private affairs. Cliff went away. "You know, I can't feel sorry for people like that somehow. It's about time they got into trouble over drawing relief. Only last summer and fall, the old man was work-

ing full time on the Government road construction gang and Cliff was harvesting full time for George Parsons, the fellow who was murdered last night. Parsons paid good wages, too, despite the fact that many people had him sized up as an old skinflint. They say both the Macdonalds drink like a pair of gold fish and they probably spent the money on liquor. S-say, you boys aren't after the Macdonalds for that murder are you? . . . By gum, I might have guessed it. It sure don't take the Mounties long to pick up the old scent." "Yes," Tom admitted, "it's in connection with the Parsons murder that we've been making these enquiries. We're much indebted to you for the information you've given us, Mister Jones. It should help considerably in clearing the case up. We must ask you, however, to keep anything that has passed between us just now a strict secret. May 

I use your telephone for a long-distance call into Calgary?" "Certainly, go ahead." 

A clerk at the Calgary C.I.B. answered Tom's phone call and, after two or three trips away from the instrument, requested him for a detailed account of what Paul and he had done. Tom complied with the request and sensed that someone else was at the other end of the line also, occasionally interrupting the clerk, but thought nothing of it at the time. Finally, after their conversation had been broken off by a long interruption from this third party, he gave Tom instructions to wait at the country store with Paul, until another police car arrived from Calgary to join them in proceeding to the Macdonald farm to make the arrests.

Tom and Paul waited at the store patiently for half an hour... three-quarters of an hour... a whole hour, without any car arriving to meet them. Then Tom phoned up the C.I.B. again. "What-no car arrived there yet?" the clerk asked with surprise. "Why Inspector Tweed left here with two men. and a chauffeur about an hour ago. Better proceed immediately to the Macdonald farm. You might be needed there." 

So that explained it!

123 "I guess your feud with Tweed was justified after all,"

Let's go, we might get into the tail end of it yet. 99 When they got to the Macdonald farm, they found Tweed and three other members of the Force sitting in the living room of the farm house at a long table with two other men handcuffed between them and a little, lithe woman sitting crying in a corner. "You two are too late to be of any use around here,"

Tweed informed them with just the trace of a scornful smile, but added condescendingly: "You can stick around. 

You might learn a thing or two about procedure." "I certainly learned plenty about procedure today already, thanks," Paul let out sarcastically and unwisely. Paul obeyed without a word. "Let's see, where were we before this unnecessary interruption?" Tweed asked one of the members of the Force, who had his hand on several sheets of stenographic notes. "Cliff Colin Macdonald, the son, sir," the man replied, "states that he had harvested several summers for George Parsons and, one time last summer, found out about Parsons' hidden strong box, when Parsons went there to get him some money to pay him his harvesting wages. He denies any prior thought of killing Parsons. States it was accidental. to make any correction to the statement?" Tweed asked the older of the two prisoners. The man shook his head grimly. "You got him drunk," Tweed continued, "so as to learn where Parsons' strong box was located. Right?"

The man nodded. "Then you argued him, in various ways, into driving you out to Parsons' farm last night and hid your shotgun in the rumble seat of that Ford coupe of yours, without his knowing it. Is that right?" Again the man nodded. "When you pulled the gun out of the car and moved towards Parsons, your son tried to stop you using it, but too late. Right?" Macdonald's eyes acquiesced and he barely nodded. "All I want to know now, Macdonald, is what you did with that shot gun. 

Where is it?" 

Macdonald remained grimly silent. "Surely you're not going to boggle down on us now, Macdonald! You've made a complete confession of everything. You might as well tell us where the gun is." "You surprised me an' jolted me when I was tired and had been doin' some drinking," Macdonald growled hoarsely. "I ain't a' goin' to place a noose around my neck for no one no how. My folks was fighters. They fought at the battle o' Shiloh, down south in the States for the Confederates in the big Civil War an' I'm a fighter, too. 

That confession don't mean a damned thing in law an' you know it. No, sir, I'm not tellin' you nothing more." "We don't need that gun," Tweed informed him. "We've got your Ford coupe and a record of its tire tracks. 

I've seen empty shot gun shells scattered all over this farm and if any of them check as being fired from the same gun as the one found on the Parsons place we don't need the gun. Then there's these pieces of stock certificate we raked out of the kitchen stove. They can be unscrambled by an expert and traced. I want you to save us further trouble, that's all." 

Macdonald remained silent. "How about it?" Tweed demanded. "How about it, Cliff Macdonald?" he repeated, suddenly ignoring the father and turning on the son.

MURDER TRAIL 

125 "I think dad threw the gun in a crik," Cliff Macdonald blurted. "Which creek?" "Gee, I'm so bloody bewildered I don't know what I'm saying. Better ask dad." "Human nature's certainly a queer, unpredictable thing," Tweed reflected, sighing. "Look here, Cliff Macdonald, you're in a spot. Your father's admitted that he killed George Parsons and that it wasn't your fault. You were with him at the time the crime took place, but didn't realize what he was up to. You even did your best to prevent his committing it at the last moment. But you were with him. You did drive him to the scene of the crime. And you did drive him away from it to safety.

That is liable to make you an accessory to the crime, both before and after the fact in a jury's eyes, unless you come absolutely clean. Where did your father dispose of that gun?" "I'm not going to squeal on my dad," young Macdonald answered. "All right."

In addition to a number of twelve-gauge shot gun shells members of the posse gathered from about the farm, Tom located two pairs of mud-caked running shoes behind a wood pile in a shed at the back of the house which corresponded exactly in sole patterns and sizes to those in his notebook. The find did not fill him with exultation. had seen the little woman cowering in the living room and had been a policeman long enough to discount the alleged glamour surrounding the life. He

It was getting dark by the time they were prepared to leave the farm, taking the two Macdonalds back to Calgary with them. As they were getting ready to leave, Mrs. 

Macdonald threw herself on her knees before Inspector 

Tweed and implored him not to take her son away with. them. "Take anything I've got, Mister Officer," she beseeched him, "take my poor, rotten old self, if it's any good to you-but spare my baby boy, spare him. He's my baby, I tell you, and he's all I've got left in the world."

Tweed ignored her with a complacent, half-contemptuous smile.

That evening Paul and Tom perused the late editions of the Calgary papers with a mixture of amazement and chagrin. What they read about the Parsons case both weakened their respect for the press and further angered them against Inspector Tweed. What they read in one paper was typical of the others. An inch-and-a-half headline announced:

R.C.M.P. CAPTURES MURDERERS 

Then a smaller, but still large, sub-heading stated:

Mountie Inspector Credited With Brilliant Work 

Then came:

Once more the thin, red line of the Mounties has triumphed in an exhibition of maintaining law and order that will ring from Coast to Coast and will further emblazon their motto of Maintiens Le Droit on the public consciousness.

Due to the brilliant work of Inspector Tweed and his subordinates on the Force, the two alleged murderers of 

George Parsons, wealthy Alberta farmer murdered last night, are already in custody. They are Kenneth Macdonald, 53, and his son, Cliff Macdonald, 21, both originally from Tennessee, U.S.A. They were arrested this afternoon at the Macdonald farm by the gallant Inspector and his men and are said to have made a full confession to the crime. The motive is stated by R.C.M.P. officers to have been robbery.

Dispensing with the technical thoroughness of a Sherlock Holmes, or the psychological meanderings of other fictional detectives, Inspector Tweed, brilliant and realistic officer of the Force, took the case over late this morning and then proceeded directly to its solution and the arrest of the culprits by late this afternoon. Inspector Tweed has had a distinguished and varied career on the Force. He was only recently transferred to Calgary from Vancouver,

MURDER TRAIL 

127 where he is regarded as an expert in narcotics and liquor cases. The transfer is considered fortunate and timely, in and out of R.C.M. P. circles. He was born in Hamilton, Ont., in 1893, is married and the father of two children.

He attended the University of Toronto for a time, but left there to take up his present career and become a member of the Force in 1914. 

The murdered man, George Parsons, was a widower, reputed to be wealthy and somewhat eccentric. He has lived on his farm alone since his wife died.

XVI MURDER TRIAL 

A preliminary hearing of the Parsons case resulted in the two Macdonalds being sent up for trial at the Spring Assizes for murder. An indirect result was the shifting of 

Inspector Tweed to a more suitable and responsible position for a man of his attainments than that of head of a riot squad. The grapevine rumours had it that his work on the case had won him back into the good graces of the powers-that-be at Ottawa and that he was slated for promotion from Inspector to Superintendent and transfer to

Headquarters of the Force at Ottawa. 

For Tom and Paul it simply meant being back again on the despised riot squad routine, which was practically the same as that which they had gone through at Vancouver under Smokey Harris, except that they were now under an easy-going, inefficient Northern Irishman who had a way of issuing wrong orders and getting himself and the squad in trouble as a result.

They both applied for a discharge from the Force on the grounds that they had understood they were joining a police force, rather than a cavalry regiment, at the time they had signed on. This availed nothing, however; the applications were turned down by the Commissioner of the Force at Ottawa, with a reprimand from him for insolence. They then decided to apply for Northern service. Providing their applications were accepted and they were selected, this meant being sent up to one of the lonely detachments of the Force in the Eastern or Western Arctic. 

where they'd be out of touch with civilization for at least two years, since that is the minimum time a member of the Force may apply for. It meant at least some adventure and experience or so they thought. 

As Paul put it: "It's a lot better playing around with 128

MURDER TRIAL 

129 Eskimos and seals than acting as nursemaid to horses and beating up a bunch of poor, half-baked reds. I've got so 

I can't stand the sight of a broom or shovel any longer and if I see a horse, excepting at the races, I want to run and hide. It's a choice of either 'go north, young man, go north' or else go do something, young man, to get thrown out of the Force." "Check," Tom agreed.

Actually, he had less to complain about than Paul, for he had Mary to spend his leisure moments with. They went out to shows and dances together and Tom dined at Mary's home on several occasions. Once he brought Paul along with him for an evening of bridge. Sometimes he helped Mary with her work by marking essays and quizzes with her and making suggestions about her lectures. These were on English literature and centered around the familiar Merchant of Venice, Julius Caesar and the romantic poets, Byron, Shelley, Keats and Coleridge. "I got the highest mark in my matric class for an essay on Shylock," he told Mary "I felt sorry for the old rascal; besides, I had a hunch our teacher was Jewish, or part Jewish."

He found Mary was strong for a woman and enjoyed long walks that would have tired the average man. They went on several such walks in the country on week-ends and usually spent some time sitting and chatting under a large, spreading maple tree. Tom confided many things to her on these occasions, including his clashes with Tweed and the Keufels. She listened thoughtfully. "I've heard of the Keufels, Tom," she told him. "They're certainly terrible people. But what can you do about it? We're both Canadians and yet-why not admit the truth, Tom?-Canada is snowed under with graft and corruption. Our own farmers are victims of an enormous, apparently respectable racket, when it really comes down to it. They work and slave and sell their wheat and produce on the world markets, without one iota of assistance from the government, and should be allowed to purchase their goods on the world market also. But are they? 

Can they buy a cheap Detroit auto or a good American

harvester, without paying an exhorbitant duty? They cannot. They are frisked of two or three hundred of their hard-earned dollars by having to buy a car made in Windsor or Oshawa and another two or three hundred by having to take an expensive, Canadian-made harvester. 

Graft, in one form or another, just scrapes the wealth off this country, like cream off the top of milk; and you know that. How else could a big country like this-bigger than the United States-be so far in the doldrums as to support fewer than a quarter of the population of little England or 

Japan? You could take either of those countries and drop them inside this one province alone and need a road map to locate them again. We have gold and copper; most of the world's nickel; the best wheat land in the world; oil; forests; water power-most everything, and yet.... Graft holds us down as a nation, like rickets will stop the proper growth of a youngster. You need to make up your mind,

Tom, which road you're going to travel. You're an idealist right now, and I hope you stay that way. I like you that way. But idealists don't get rich, Tom. People like the 

Keufels do get rich and they have their hands right on all the political wires and levers. It sometimes pays to avoid a clash with people like the Keufels." "It's not as bad as all that, Mary. The Keufels will come a cropper some day. So will Tweed and his like." "Sure that isn't just wishful thinking, Tom?" 

The day following this chat the Macdonalds were brought up for trial. Tweed, Tom and Paul, together with a host of other witnesses, were on hand to be called on by the Crown. Kenneth Macdonald, grasping at any slim chance to save himself, hired a clever, but disingenuous lawyer to defend himself and his son and accepted a bad piece of advice from him.

Macdonald had scraped together a few hundred dollars for his defense by the sale of his farm and the lawyer, fearful of not acquiring this small hoard, should Macdonald make a clean confession to the court to clear his son, had argued him into fighting the case out the hard way. He had also argued Macdonald into electing separate trials, rather than a joint one, so as to prolong the proceedings

MURDER TRIAL 

131 (and his fees), on the grounds that Cliff could be tried first and would make a better, impression on the court than his father, owing to his honest looks and youth. If Cliff got off, then Macdonald himself would likely get off also, so the argument ran.

However, the evidence that some member, or members, of the Macdonald household were involved in the murder was overwhelmingly strong. It came out at the son's trial that the Macdonalds had been hard hit by the depression and were drawing relief and in arrears in their taxes at the time of the crime. Cliff Macdonald had worked for 

George Parsons, the murdered man, and had lived on the premises in Parsons' house with him for a short time. 

The Macdonalds owned an old Ford coupe. On the night of the crime, an old Ford coupe was seen to drive out of their farm at around eleven o'clock by two separate witnesses. Half an hour later, an old Ford coupe with two men in it was seen by another witness parked near the Parsons farm. He did not recognize either of the men in it, however.

About an hour later, Gaetz, a neighbor of Parsons, was aroused by voices raised in some sort of altercation on the Parsons' property and, shortly afterwards heard the report of a gun, apparently a shot gun. On going over to Parsons' farm to investigate the noise, he found the dead body of George Parsons lying in a pool of blood, with a .30-.30 rifle lying alongside it.

There was a light coupe parked on the property, which attracted his attention, since Parsons did not own a car, and voices and noises came from inside the house. Later, two men came out of the house who, judging by their voices, were respectively middle-aged and youthful and tallied in height and profile very closely with Kenneth Macdonald and his son, Cliff. They got into the car and, after an altercation between the older and the younger man about the latter turning on the headlights of the car, drove off without the lights of the car burning. 

Evidence was introduced by the prosecution about fresh tire tracks having been found on the property the night of the crime, which substantiated Mr. Gaetz' story; of the

imprints of running shoes on the property, sketched by a member of the Force, corresponding in size and sole pattern to the two pairs found in a cupboard on the Macdonald farm; and of a freshly discharged shot gun shell found on the Parsons' property having apparently been fired from the same gun as discharged several shells found on the Macdonald property. The death gun itself had never been located, suggesting to the prosecution that it had been destroyed or effectively hidden by the accused. 

Mr. Grace, ballistics expert, with the jury clustered about him, pointed out the points upon which he based his conclusion that the shell found on the Parsons' farm was fired by the same gun as those found on the Kenneth Macdonald farm. He pointed out the identical expansion of the different shells when compared ; and a mark common to all on the rim of the soft metal of the primer, which indicated the existence of a small indentation in the firing pin of the gun which fired them. All members of the jury examined the shells under a hand lens. Enlarged photographs of the shells, showing the alignment of firing from marks on them, were also produced and explained to the jury by him, with special emphasis being placed on the crime shell itself.

There was some altercation between Grace and counsel for the defense over the fact that the cardboard portion of some of the shells was split, while that on the crime shell was not. Grace explained that the splitting of the cardboard portion of the shell was due to the individual shell itself and not the gun which fired it. He went on to assert that considerable research work done on firearms in laboratories had demonstrated, in the case of shot guns, that the only characteristics imparted to the shell by the gun were the distortions and scratches on the brass head of the shell and the centering and shape of the firing pin dent. The cardboard part itself was too perishable and the amount of splitting it underwent on discharge, too variable, to be given any serious consideration whatsoever. 

Counsel differed on this point and argued that it should be given serious consideration, in view of the fact that there was a man's life at stake.

MURDER TRIAL 

133 The argument lasted some time and probably weakened the case of the Defense in the eyes of the jury, since they had obviously been impressed by the logical outlook and competence of the ballistics expert. The orations of 

Defense Counsel around the point only seemed to suggest to them that his case was hopeless. "What's the sense of all this humbug?" Paul said to

Tom quietly, as they sat together near the front of the courtroom. "Why don't they just find old man Macdonald guilty and let the son off? There's something worse than barbaric about a murder trial in this country. It's like a cold, deliberate game of cat and mouse, played for the benefit of a bunch of soft, well-fed, big wigs and sadists." "What!" Tom let out ironically. "You wouldn't take away the bread and butter of some of our fine middle-class lawyers, judges and jurymen, would you? You're worse than a communist, Paul, in trying to change humanity that far. Why British Justice is like the Rock of Gibraltar." "Just as senseless as a rock, too. It's about forty per cent a parasite's game and it's dollars to doughnuts that this jury pulls a faux pas of some sort. I don't suppose they'll go so far as to hang Cliff Macdonald and acquit the old man; but it's possible. 

The trial of Cliff Macdonald lasted four days. For that length of time it supplied an overflow audience with a free drama-not as spectacular as a movie, perhaps, but every bit as tense. The bulk of the audience were there to see stern British Justice triumph over two ex-Americans from

Tennessee, who thought themselves too smart for its long, clammy reach. There was an audible hiss throughout the courtroom every time defense counsel arose to speak and, barring a false, handle-bar moustache, he acted up the part of a villain splendidly. After all, he already had the Macdonalds' few hundred dollars in his pocket-so why place himself out on a limb with the legal profession by defending them too zealously? Pretty smart of him to think of having them tried separately and thus squeeze all their savings out of them for legal expenses, instead of a mere half. He must appear sincere, however-so he questioned 

Tweed and Grace, the Crown's two toughest witnesses,

for hours on end, waving his arms and bombasting in a way that must have made the average juryman think: "He's a splendid orator, but we won't let that fool us.' 

He made his legal brethren merely smile. 99 The accused youth's mother-looking small and pathetic in her Sunday-best garments, consisting of a black dress, corsage of wild flowers and a black, "Queen Mary" bonnet -watched her son's every move with a desperate, wrapt attention. Her eyes were sore and bloodshot and she looked quite ill, but managed to essay a wan smile whenever she thought that the Defense had scored a point. 

Finally, the inevitable verdict came, after an hour's deliberation by the jury. "Guilty, your Honor!" "Cliff Macdonald," said the judge, slowly and dramatically placing the black hat of death on his head towards the accused, "stand up and hear your sentence. Before I pass it, is there anything, any statement you wish to make before this court?" "Yes, your Honor, I swear before God that I'm innocent." "Is that all you wish to say?" 

Cliff did not answer, but hung his head down despondently. "Cliff Colin Macdonald: you have received a fair trial in this court of the Province of Alberta, of the fair Dominion of Canada and have been found guilty of participation in a brutal murder by a sound and competent jury. 

The murder is of the first degree and the sentence of the Court upon you is, that you be taken from this place to a lawful prison and thence to a place of execution, and that you be there hanged by the neck until you be dead. And may the Lord have mercy on your soul."

There was a scream and a clatter from near the front of the courtroom. The little woman dressed in black, with the "Queen Mary" bonnet, had slumped over in her seat and was twitching all over convulsively. She was taken to a side room and given first-aid treatment. Coming to her senses, she wanted to know where they had taken her sonher baby boy, Cliff. The courtroom had been cleared by 

---

MURDER TRIAL 

135 that time and she was told that he was being detained in the basement of the court house under guard, until his friends, particularly she, had been able to say good-bye to him. He was to go from the courthouse direct to his death cell in the Penitentiary under armed escort (though no mention of death cell or armed escort was made to Mrs. Macdonald).

In the basement of the courthouse, Mrs. Macdonald's manner went through a marvellous transformation. All 

Cliff's friends and relatives were there-a strangely large number to be bidding farewell to a sentenced murdererand had broken down in one way or another, mostly by crying. It seemed that Cliff was a good athlete and huntsman and had made many friends. Mrs. Macdonald, however, kept her composure. Clenching her small fists and looking resolutely up into her big son's eyes, she said: "Damn you, Cliff, why did you have to bring this on yourself? Why couldn't you let your father take the sole blame for what he did, instead of trying to shoulder some of it yourself. My dear boy, you've been terribly foolish; but keep your chin up and remember, whatever happens, I'll always be fighting to get you off. Keep your chin up, son, damn you. If the worst comes to the worst, you won't be the first innocent man that's gone to the gallows.

I'm going to try to arrange for you to tell the truth at your father's trial; and you do it, too! None of this ‘share the blame' hero stuff any more. It may not be too late yet, if you get your wits about you. God bless you my boy, my" "There, there, Ma, better men than me have had to take it. It can't be so bad, or society wouldn't allow it." 

On the second day of the trial of Cliff's father, Kenneth

Macdonald, it became apparent that the accused had not the ghost of a chance of escaping the gallows. No money was bet on the eventual outcome of the trial, because there wasn't any doubt about it, but a few dollars changed hands on the time the jury might be in session arriving at its verdict.

Kenneth Macdonald's ruse in having his son tried first had not worked because the evidence against them was overwhelming collectively, rather than individually; something his mind had failed to grasp at the time. În effect, it had merely placed a noose around two necks instead of one.

Even Defense Counsel lost most of his assurance and his orations fell off to a monotone lacking in conviction, as the second day of the trial grew towards its close. Suddenly he arose from his desk and walked over to where Mrs. Macdonald was sitting, spoke a few words to her and led her over to his table.
