They became engrossed in subdued conversation for several minutes, seemingly oblivious to the evidence being presented by a Crown witness. Then 

Defense Counsel suddenly arose and asked the Court for permission to speak. On permission being granted, he said: "I have a great surprise in store for this Court, if it will adjourn now and permit me to produce another witness at this trial." "And who is this other witness, may I ask?" the judge questioned him. "Cliff Macdonald, your Honor."

There was scattered laughter in the courtroom. "He wishes to testify against his father at this trial," counsel explained, throwing a glance of irritation at the source of some of the laughter. "That can be arranged," the judge assured him. "It is quite constitutional for a man to be brought from the penitentiary to testify on a case of this nature; even though he, himself, be a condemned man."

XVII  

The trial of Kenneth Macdonald was resumed before a packed courtroom the day after the adjournment. Intense drama gripped the room as Cliff Macdonald, under

R.C.M.P. escort, was produced as a witness and began relating, in a calm, firm voice, events leading up, and subsequent to, the killing of Parsons; admitting unintentional participation in the crime, and placing the blame for it roundly on his father's shoulders.

When the young man first took the stand, his father stared at him with hard lines creasing his face; then, later, leaned back in his chair and listened to the testimony calmly and carefully. Examination-in-chief over, the 

Crown prosecutor took over the witness for crossexamination. "You're Cliff Colin Macdonald, son of Kenneth Macdonald, the man over there, who is jointly accused with you of having murdered George Parsons, are you not?" the Prosecutor opened casually. "Yes, only my father is willing to admit that he committed the murder himself now and that lets me out, I guess. I'm innocent." "Just answer the questions I put to you. You say that your father killed Parsons. Did you see him do it?" "Yes, I was within forty feet of him when he shot Parsons. I was standing by our car, a Ford coupe, which was parked inside Parsons' grounds, some distance in front of the house." "What did you do about it, just stand there and tell him to go ahead and shoot, or did you attempt to do otherwise?" "No, I did my level best to stop him. I am not guilty 137

of the offense held against me and if dad comes clean, he'll tell you I tried all I could to stop him. I argued with him and begged him to come away before anything happened.' "Why did you suddenly start doing that? Didn't you go with him intent on robbing Parsons and prepared to share in the risks you might run into doing it, in the first instance? What made you suddenly change your mind?" "I didn't suddenly change my mind: I simply had no idea what dad was up to. He told me he wanted to make a loan of about five hundred dollars off of Parsons to tide things over for us." "Five hundred dollars... hmm.... On what security?" "The equity still left to us in our farm. The banks wouldn't come through." "Couldn't your father think of a more suitable time than around midnight to ask Parsons for a five hundred dollar loan? Didn't that strike you as a bit queer?" "A bit, yes, but I trusted in dad." "I'd say that was very naive of you, putting it mildly.

If that was your intention, however—not that it makes any difference to the seriousness of the crime-why was the shot gun taken along?” "I don't know, it was dad's idea. I didn't know he had hidden it in the rumble seat before we left." "Everything connected with this affair seems to be your father's fault. What is the reason for giving all this damaging evidence against your father?" "Because I am not guilty." "Whose idea was this robbery originally-I mean, whose suggestion was it?" "Dad's." 99 "Where did he get the idea from?" "I don't know, but it was common gossip around the district that Parsons was a very rich man." "He may have been a very rich man, but how did your father know that he kept the bulk of his money and valuables around the house, instead of in a bank?" "That was also common gossip." "You don't mean to tell me your father was foolish

enough to plan a robbery of such magnitude and assume such risks, merely on the basis of common gossip do you?" "I don't know what else he based his plans on. 99 "Perhaps I can suggest that for you. It appears that one of you knew the exact place where Parsons kept his money and other valuables hidden away-hidden behind that false brick back of his fireplace. I think the Court is satisfied that such a cache could not have been hit upon merely by accident." 

Here Defense Counsel sprang to his feet. "Objection, your Honor! I maintain that it would have been quite possible for the accused, or anyone else, to have come upon that concealed strong box by chance." "Possible, yes," agreed his Honor, "but not very probable, in view of the short time they spent on the premises and the fact that it was night time when all this transpired. 

Logic forces me to incline towards the view held by the Crown Prosecutor, that the party, or parties, committing this crime had a prior knowledge as to where Parsons kept his money. The objection is, therefore, reluctantly overruled. The prosecution will please proceed with the crossexamination.' 99 "As I was saying," the Crown Prosecutor continued, "I think the Court is reasonably satisfied that such a hiding place could not have been hit upon merely by accident. 

Was its location also common gossip around the neighborhood, Macdonald?" "No," Macdonald faltered, "I wouldn't say as it was.' "Then how did you get to know about it?" 99 "I guess you mean, how did my father get to know about it. I don't quite know." "Isn't it true, Macdonald, that you harvested for Parsons at different times in the past and that this summer- I mean the summer of last year he gave you harvesting jobs and other jobs around his place, because he liked you and knew that your father and mother and yourself were hard up?" "Yes, I worked for George-I mean Mister Parsonsat odd times, on and off, since 1928. Last year he gave me all the work he could, I guess."

"It has been said that you lived with Parsons for a time, while you were working for him last summer. What have you to say to that?" "Yes, I guess I did." "You only guess you did; aren't you sure?" "Yes, I guess I'm sure about it." "While you were living with Parsons, don't you think there was a good chance that you might have discovered the hiding place where he kept his money-seen him going there, for instance?" "Not a chance; the old bird was too foxy." "I see he was too foxy."

A pin could have been heard to drop in the crowded court room as the intense drama of the battle of wits, with a life the stake, gripped the Court. Those slightly hard of hearing leaned well forward in their seats with a sadistic eagerness and curiosity. Cliff's mother was a deathly pale and sat wringing a handkerchief in her small, bony fingers, with an expression that suggested that her son might have been a defeated gladiator in an arena of Ancient Rome, with the crowd about her shouting for. his blood. "When you left your father's farm," the Crown Prosecutor continued, after a pause, "who drove the car, you or he?" "I did, all the way, both going and coming." "That's strange. You claim the crime was all your father's fault and yet you even drove him away from the scene of it, after knowing he had murdered a man. Wasn't that a little inconsistent of you?" "I don't know whether it was or not. I didn't know what dad was up to when I drove him out there. He got me to drive because he can't drive well at night on account of his eyes. 99 "His eyesight seemed good enough when he had the bead on Parsons." "He has always been a good shot. Besides, he was fairly close to Parsons when he let him have it." "It seems, though, that you not only drove your father

away from the scene of the crime, but were seen coming out of the house with him after he had plundered the strong box. How do you account for that?" "When I saw dad kill Parsons I drove away from the farm a short distance but, later, decided to return and take dad away before he was caught. I went up to the house then and found him cleaning out the strong box. I managed to drag him away a little later." "After he'd finished the job, eh? How many days before the murder did your father acquaint you with his intention of paying Parsons a visit?" "Let's see it was about five days. The thing happened on a Thursday and he first hinted at asking for the loan while I was greasing some harness on the previous Sunday afternoon." "Pious of him to pick on Sunday to break the news of this rather forceful loan to you. Since he did so, five days before the crime took place, you must have had some time to meditate and dwell on the incongruities of the situation." "The what...? I've already told you I just went prepared to drive dad out there to ask for a loan. That didn't require any special preparation." "You didn't use masks?" "Certainly not." "How about those new running shoes you wore?" "Both dad's and my boots were pretty badly worn. Dad told me he was going to get us each a pair of running shoes on that account. I objected that running shoes don't wear so good and are hard on the feet, but dad had his way." "You didn't get them to sneak quietly up on Parsons?" "I don't know about dad, but I know I didn't." "On the night of the murder, did you go direct to the scene of the crime?" "No, we stopped nearly a quarter of a mile from the farm and spent half an hour talking the thing over." "What was the nature of the conversation?" "I had a flash of suspicion that, perhaps, dad had in mind robbing Parsons. He was fidgety and had told me to be

quiet on entering Parsons' grounds, so's not to disturb the neighbors. That made me suspicious." "What happened when you got there?" "I drove into the grounds. A dog began to bark. It was Parsons' terrier, Copper King, and dad must have forgotten that he didn't know him. I made a noise which quieted him down, but he'd already woken Parsons up and, shortly afterwards, George came out of the house. 

He was on the south side of it. I knew, by then, that dad was up to some mischief." "Why didn't you warn Parsons and go for the police?" "I couldn't turn stool against my own father." "Oh yes, filial affection would come in there no doubt. 

Did Parsons have anything with him?" "Yes, a rifle." "You spoke to him?" "No." "What was said further between your father and yourself?" "I begged him to come away and made to start up the motor of our Ford. I told him I would drive away and leave him, if he took another step towards the farm. I was afraid if he went on up to the house he might get killed by Parsons. I whispered to him, loudly as I could without Parsons hearing, to think up some excuse to tell 

George. He seemed not to hear me. "Where was your own shot gun?" 99 "In a hock shop. I had pawned it some months back." "And your father's? Come on-out with it!" "It-it was in the back of the car. While I was starting up the engine to get us off the premises, dad slid around the back of the car and got his hands on the gun before I could do anything about it." "Was there any further conversation?" "Yes. I jumped out of the car, leaving the engine running, and told dad to put the gun back, as it might go off and kill Parsons." "What happened then?" "I made to follow dad to get the gun away from him. 

I was good and sorry, by that time, that I'd had anything

Parsons called out to know to do with the whole mess. what we were doing on his property at that time of night -I forget his exact words—and then dad's shot gun went off with a loud report." "Did it just go off of its own accord, or did your father deliberately take aim and pull the trigger?" "I guess he must have deliberately taken aim and pulled the trigger." "You only guess he did?" "No, I guess I'm sure he did. I was looking right at him when he did; it and was moving to take the gun away from him." "What then?" "Dad went up to the house and I followed him, trying to talk him into going away at once." "Did your father pay any heed to your advice?" "No, he just kept right on going. He was set on getting 

Parsons' money." "Did he know where it was kept then?" "Yes, I'd previously told him.' "You say you had previously told him. Haven't you denied all along that you knew where Parsons kept his money?" 

Young Macdonald's face turned a deep crimson and then reversed the process to a sickly pallor as he realized, respectively, that he had fallen into a trap and that the consequences were liable to be extremely costly to him. 

A farmer at the back of the room smilingly mimicked a man being throttled by a noose and winked knowingly at some acquaintances. "I-I don't know," Macdonald faltered after a considerable pause. "Yes, I guess I did say that. I was scared to tell the truth before because-because I thought you might take the wrong meaning out of it. Dad got me lit over a crock of gin one night, about two weeks or ten days before the killing and got me telling what I knew about where George kept his money. I'd seen Parsons go there one day during the summer, on a harvesting pay day, quite by accident." "Well, why didn't you tell us that story before, instead

of giving us all that stuff about your father planning the robbery on the basis of local gossip?" "It isn't a story, it's the truth and I was afraid to tell it because I didn't know if you would believe it."

The Crown Prosecutor turned to the Clerk of the Court and had him read over evidence given during the previous fifteen or twenty minutes, showing that Cliff Macdonald had denied possessing knowledge of where Parsons kept his money prior to the murder. He then produced the blood-stained shirt worn by the deceased on the night of the crime, pointing out to the jury a large hole and series of small tatters in the chest of it, encrusted with dried and blackened blood. The collar was torn and scraped by marks that appeared to have been made by teeth and which the prosecution alleged had been made by the dead man's dog trying to wake his master up. The court room hung on the Prosecutor's every word and followed his every move with a tense silence.

While this was going on, little Mrs. Macdonald kept a grim, pallid calm; while a girl of nineteen or twenty, in a seat near the back of the room, wept almost continuously. 

Cliff's attention seemed to be divided between her and his mother with a worried, fidgety alternation. The girl had not been among those who had said good-bye to Cliff in the court house basement, after he had been condemned. 

Perhaps her loyalty did not go as far as that, with a lot of people milling about and curious eyes prying into her young soul; perhaps, too, pressure had been brought to bear on her by her parents. Tom wondered how the women of criminals must feel and fare in such a crisis and the thought was not a pleasant one. He found his thoughts on the subject suddenly distracted by the recommencement of the cross-examination. "After you had located the secret hiding place and smashed open Parsons' strong box, what did you do next?" the Prosecutor asked. "I didn't break it open, dad did." "Well, after he'd broken into it then." "He came back down from the house. I had driven away, in the meantime, but had repented and returned

to pick him up. I realized that he was not only my father, but had probably done what he did as much for mother and me as for himself." "In other words, you thought he'd pulled the job safely and wanted to share in the booty?" "Before God, that isn't so. Hasn't a man, accused of murder, any rights left at all, in this country? I thought a man accused of murder had to be proven guilty before he was guilty in British justice." 

The judge pounded his gavel down angrily. "How dare you, a convicted murderer, criticize our British system of justice in that manner. Remember who you are, young man. The prosecution will proceed with the crossexamination." "I merely objected, your Honour," young Macdonald choked, "to having every little thing I did looked upon as being villainous acts. I didn't go back to pick up my dad, merely to share in the booty, like his honor, the Crown Prosecutor hinted. Nobody in this world is absolutely bad. It's an old trick to give a dog a bad name and then have the dogcatcher kill him. I thought with humans...' 

The pounding of the gavel drowned Cliff's words out, while the Prosecutor smiled caustically at his outburst. 

He shook his black robe with dignity, like a prize hen shaking its feathers, aware of his importance. "When you left the house, what happened?" he asked Cliff coldly. "On the way down to our car, I took another look at George Parsons to see if there was anything we could do for him, but he appeared to be stone dead. The shot had sprayed him over the heart. Then we got into the car, with me at the wheel. Dad said he thought he heard and saw somebody over in a clump of trees and bushes over at the north side of the house and had half a mind to investigate what it was, but I talked him out of it. I turned on the headlights of the car without thinking and dad said: 'What th' hell do you mean by turning on those damned lights for? Do you want to get us both caught?' I answered him, did he mean did I want to get him caught. 

I turned off the lights and then we started home."

"Did you go straight home, or did you stop on the way?" "We stopped once on the way. Dad wanted to ditch some stuff at the roadside and I pulled the car up about a minute or two, somewhere between our place and Parsons. 

"What did he throw away?" "I don't exactly know, but I think there was a purse and some other stuff. He then told me to remember, if anybody ever wanted to know, that we'd spent the evening in Calgary together."

After the Crown had finished its cross-examination, Defence Counsel did not keep Cliff Macdonald in the witness stand for a further examination-in-chief, but called upon Grace and Tweed to each face one brief and final barrage of questions. He then wound up the case for the defendant by the unprecedented procedure of ignoring his interests entirely and, instead, making a long and impassioned plea for mercy for his son-regardless of the fact that it was not strictly in order, the assembled jury having no jurisdiction over the case of Cliff Macdonald, who had been found guilty by another jury at another trial. The speech closed on a direct reference to Cliff's pathetic, little mother, sitting dejectedly near the front of the room.

When the jury filed out to make its deliberations, it was gone less than an hour. Asked by the judge if it had been able to agree on a verdict, its answer, delivered by the jury foreman, was in the affirmative. "And what is your verdict, gentlemen?" "Guilty, your Honor . . . However, your Honour, we feel strongly for going on record as recommending that some further consideration-some leniency-might be shown in the case of Cliff Colin Macdonald, son of the accused, under sentence of death from a previous trial for this same crime. There is the matter of the rather sensational evidence given by him against the accused, his father, today. Then there is the matter of his youth and the fact that he appears to have been led, or rather misled, into the affair by his father. We feel that our consciences

would not be entirely clear if we did not put in this word for Cliff Macdonald, although we fully realize that the matter is out of the immediate jurisdiction of this court." "Gentlemen of the jury," His Honour replied, slowly and deliberately, after considering their verdict for some little time in silence, "in the main, I absolutely agree with your verdict. I regard this as one of those cases in which there appears to be no reasonable doubt about the issue and 

I would like to congratulate you on the quick and efficient manner in which you arrived at your decision. "I have only one criticism to offer. A jury sitting on a case of this nature must disregard all sob stuff. Although, on the surface, it might appear as though Cliff Macdonald is entitled to some special consideration for the evidence he has rendered the court; legally-and we are dealing with the law, remember, gentlemen-he is not so entitled. 

He has been tried separately and found guilty by another court and his fate does not now rest in our hands. That is a peculiarity with separate trials for the same crime. 

Legally, the two trials must be regarded as entirely separate, as if segregated in two different air-tight compartments. I will see that your recommendation for leniency is brought to the attention of the Minister of Justice at Ottawa. That is as far as I can go in the matter; and any change in the course of the law must come from Ottawa. 

At any rate, the blood of Cliff Macdonald will not be on your heads. . . . Kenneth Macdonald, stand up to hear your sentence. If you wish, you may make a statement before the Court, before I pass sentence on you. Kenneth 

Macdonald, do you so wish?" "Judge, your Honour," Kenneth Macdonald said gruffly and loudly, so that everyone in the court room could hear him, "I'm too old to believe in Santa Claus and too experienced not to know what's coming to me. I've had to look death in the face before, so I'm not afraid to die. 

I can take it. Shoot the works!"

XVIII 

The Macdonald trial demonstrated a thing to Tom which he had always faintly suspected-the inflexibility and inertia of the course of modern law. Here was a youth, Cliff Macdonald, who had been sentenced to die for a murder he had not committed; yet, unless a scrap of paper came from a higher source, located at Ottawa, by a certain date, he would end his days on a scaffold, due to a twisted mass of legal red tape and a "boner" made by his father and their defense counsel in having him tried first. What if the correspondence on the case was misplaced or sidetracked by some careless clerk at Ottawa for just a day too long? The case seemed to mock, with a terrible leer, all the nerve-tingling movies and stories in which a miraculous race in an aeroplane or automobile to a death house, or sensational court scene, changes the course of the law in a split second. There would be several weeks yet, instead of split seconds, in which to save Cliff's life; but 

Tom had grave doubts that it would be saved. Canadian justice is too much like some big, modern, well-oiled machine-efficient; but hard on any innocent factory hand who, by mischance, gets himself caught up in its plunging flywheel and crushing gears. The thought reminded him of the unfortunate youth who was killed in the C.P.R. yards at Vancouver, the night that Paul and he had been on mail guard duty together. The grinding, crushing locomotive wheels symbolized the law-the status quo. 

However, a policeman's work is not always sordid; and there occasionally turns up some bright, amusing incident in his daily routine to satisfy his sense of humour. The day after Kenneth Macdonald was sentenced to be hanged,

Tom was engaged with an Irish corporal, named O'Keefe, on a duty, known as "hoboing," which consisted of arrest- 148

ing hoboes riding on incoming freight trains at the Calgary yards and bringing them before a judge. The sentences passed on the arrested parties were, ironically enough, in their order of popularity with the recipients: thirty days, fourteen days and a dismissal for jail has the same connotation as home to many vagrants who crave a roof over their head and regular meals. 

Most of the freight trains they had to watch came either from the East or West; for that is the way traffic and trade runs in the Dominion of Canada, due to tariff walls between it and the United States, which dictate that trade shall follow these lines, rather than the more logical ones running North and South, laid down by the physical conditions of Nature.

In the morning, they arrested five American boys, ranging in age from about fourteen to seventeen, who had answered the urge of spring by "hoboing" up to

Canada. They had some paraphernalia in their possession, including a mandolin and a guitar.

When arraigned before the judge, one of them unwittingly struck up a note on the guitar as he was giving them some words of fatherly advice. The judge was a cranky old fellow and immediately broke off in the middle of his address to pass sentence on each of them for the maximum of thirty days. Tom felt sorry for the lads.

It had not been so long since he, himself, had been of their age. He was detailed by the corporal to escort them in the police patrol car to the jail. "Say, Mister Officer, do they give us striped suits at the big house?" one of them asked him during the journey, after a somewhat solemn silence in the back of the patrol wagon, broken occasionally by brief whispers. "I presume you mean jail," Tom told him. "No. They'll most likely give you a khaki shirt and pair of pants without a belt, that you have to hold up all the time with one hand to keep from slipping down, a tooth brush and some clean underwear." "And no striped suits?" asked another of the youngsters in a tone of disappointment, almost amounting to alarm. "Gee, there's nothing to being in jail in this darned old

country. You're as far behind as England and the European countries. Down in Cook county, at home, they treat a fellow like a real tough hombre-beat him up and make him feel like 'Legs' Diamond or Johnnie Dillinger."

He was a hard-looking youngster with a pair of shrewd, materialistic eyes, ten or fifteen years older than the rest of him. Tom took him to be the leader of the quintette. "I guess we're behind the times up here all right," Tom explained sarcastically.

In the afternoon, the corporal and he arrested about a dozen hoboes off a freight train in from the West. One of them was unusually verbose and insolent. He referred to the Mounted Police as "cossacks of old R. B. Bennett" and "yellow-legged buzzards of bloody King George" to their faces and growled shop-worn phrases about the "rights and equality of Man and the down-trodden working classes." He made the corporal and Tom both angry with him.

His face was fringed with about a month's growth of beard and he kept his head bent down, but Tom felt certain he knew him. He suddenly chucked the man under the chin and raised his head to get a good look at his face.

When he did so, he let out an exclamation of surprise, for the man was none other than Felix Fonck, ex-special operative of the R.C.M.P. 

Obeying a sudden impulse, and without first consulting the corporal about it, Tom delivered a hard kick to the seat of the Teuton's pants. Fonck let out a loud whimper a split second after Tom's boot had made contact and immediately became the soft, wheedling sycophant whom Tom was familiar with. Gone was the word "cossack" from his vocabulary and the palaver about the "equality and rights of Man and the down-trodden working classes"; in their stead, big tears sprang to his eyes and he showered the corporal and Tom with profuse apologies that dripped respect and humility. "Guess that's the only way to treat a square-head, Shields," the corporal remarked, with a twinkle in his 

Irish eyes. "They don't savvy anything but force. Give

one a good, swift kick in the seat of the pants and he becomes human like the rest of us." "Yes, Corporal," Fonck whined, attempting a weak smile, "that's right." "It's a strange world, indaed, indaed, Mister O'Keefe," the corporal added, scratching his head as he eyed Fonck.

As the time drew near for the carrying out of the death sentences against the two Macdonalds and no reprieve or stay of execution for Cliff Macdonald came from Ottawa,

Paul and Tom both became uneasy and their conversations had a way of invariably drifting to the one topic: would 

Cliff Macdonald be reprieved? Tom even had a bad dream about the affair-dreaming that young Macdonald went to his doom cursing him and the other policemen connected with the affair for being too cowardly to come forward with the facts of the original confession he and his father had made on their farm.

The concensus of opinion around barracks swung to the belief that Cliff Macdonald would be pardoned at the last minute and that Tweed would play a leading role in obtaining the pardon as he had done in obtaining the indictments. Everyone had heard about the original confession of the senior Macdonald absolving his son of the blame, which had been suppressed at the trial; and all considered it the duty of Tweed to see that the Minister of Justice at Ottawa was informed of it so as to be in a position to see that a grave injustice was not done; nor did anyone doubt that he would do so. However, Paul and

Tom were not so sure on this point as some of the others: they had both had ample opportunity to detect the mountebank side of Tweed. "Either you or one of the other men present with Tweed when old Macdonald made that confession ought to get after Tweed to do something about it," Paul told Tom. "Time is getting short. Personally, I think Tweed is so hard-boiled, he doesn't care whether young Macdonald swings or not-probably regards it as just another score in his favor and unless pressure is brought to bear on him, Cliff Macdonald will hang. You're a university grad,

Tom, and higher education is supposed to carry an obligation along with it—" "That's all very well, Paul, but I wasn't really present when Macdonald made the confession. I only came in at the tail end when the stenographer read out what Macdonald had been saying before we got there." "It's the same thing isn't it?" "Well yes, I guess it is. The trouble is, I hate Tweed's very guts and to go and ask him what amounts to a favour-" "It's no favour. An innocent man's life is at stake. 

You'd be pretty nearly as bad as Tweed if you did nothing. 

How about it, Tom?" "Okay."

That evening, Tom paid a quiet visit to Tweed's quarters. He found Tweed alone with a bottle of Scotch.

Half lit. Despite his condition, he eyed Tom coldly and firmly and his gaunt, six-foot frame retained most of its usual stiffness. "Well, what's bothering your soul now, Hamlet?"

Tweed greeted him mockingly, probably detecting a worried expression on Tom's countenance. "It's about Cliff Macdonald I've come to see you, sir." "Well, what about him?" "He's innocent, sir, and the thought that he might possibly hang because of the way his lawyer bungled his case has been worrying me and some of the other boys." "What other boys?" "Those who know, or have heard, of that confession. 

Kenneth Macdonald made to you at his farm, sir." "If that's all that brought you here, Shields, you might as well forget about it." "Do you mean by that sir, that Cliff Macdonald will be pardoned?" "No, I mean nothing of the sort. That bum shyster lawyer of theirs just took an atrocious gamble and lost, that's all. Besides, Cliff Macdonald was foolish enough to tell an obvious lie in court and get caught red handed at it. It will be a lesson for that kind not to try and beat

British justice, like American shysters make monkeys out of their criminal law." "But Cliff Macdonald's innocent!" "When you've been at this game as long as I have, you'll have seen more than one innocent man punished for a crime he did not commit. I've seen innocent men plead guilty merely because they were afraid they couldn't clear themselves of an appearance of guilt and, by pleading guilty, they were sure of a quick trial and a light sentence, instead of standing pat and risking a stiff one. That's just their tough luck. Bad judgment. 

It was "I've seen one innocent man go to the gallows. in 1920, just after the war, at a small place near Winnipeg. 

A farmer was murdered-just like this Parsons case-and they picked up a travelling salesman as a suspect. You know how people are in small places in their attitude towards strangers-hostile, I mean. One man, probably drunk at the time, swore to having seen the salesman near the scene of the crime around the time it took place. Other witnesses against the man quickly appeared, although, on close examination, their evidence appeared pretty shaky. 

You know, people go clean bugs over a murder. They get excited like they do over religion. Some people will just read about a case in the newspapers and will then go to the police with all kinds of stories against the accused. 

Often, they'll actually believe they saw the accused commit the crime, though they don't really know him from Adam. 

If I was to accuse you of murdering your own mother or father tomorrow, Shields, and was to get it put on the front pages of the newspapers in big headlines, none of your friends would want to speak to you again and at least half a dozen cranks, who don't know you, your father or your mother, would come forward as witnesses against you. That's the way with the old world." "How did you come to know that the Winnipeg man who was hanged was innocent, sir? What happened in this case?" "Oh, they just hanged him. He was a married man, with a little daughter. A day or two before he was executed he saw his wife and requested her to leave Canada

and change her name so's the kid would never suffer from the stigma of his fate. She did as she was asked. The flu epidemic was bad at that time and a Polish immigrant farmer who was dying of it confessed to the murder, just to ease his dying conscience. His confession came a couple of weeks too late though. The Government tried to locate the wife and daughter without avail. Our Canadian government is like that. Maybe if they'd been collecting taxes, instead of paying out heavy compensation for the error, they'd have found them all right. The whole affair was hushed up as 'not in the public interest.' The woman is probably scrubbing floors now on her hands and knees, if she's still living, and, by the law of averages, the daughter's likely a prostitute. "That's certainly a swell commentary on our civilization," Tom let out with unrestrained disgust. "That's the sort of thing a damned 'red' would say, Shields. Hell, man-this world isn't perfect. You want to wake up to the fact that people who go about squawking and trying to improve it never get any place. If you don't believe me, just pay a visit some day to England, where your forefathers came from. They have the right system over there. They let people with radical ideas shoot their faces off as much as they please in a place called Hyde Park. It's a sort of twenty-ring circus in the open. People go there to be amused. Nobody ever believes anything they hear there, so it's all right. There you'll see all the reds you want-unshaven, dirty beggars, with stinking bodies and dissipated, blood-shot eyes, telling everyone else how to run this world. They sometimes chant a red song, which would be downright funny if it wasn't so pathetic. 

It goes: 'It's the rich wot gets the blooming pleasure. 

It's the poor wot gets the blinking blime. 

They 'ave to sell virtue's precious treasure. 

Ain't it a blinking, blooming shime?' "Then you go into some of those English upper-class homes over there and, boy, you won't find a more upright

and respectable society anywhere. Unless you're crazy, you can't help but see the contrast right away.' 99 "I have no doubt about the material disparity over there, sir, but I can't see what that's got to do with the Macdonald case-why a man, innocent of a murder, should hang for it." "In the public mind, Shields, Cliff Macdonald is as guilty as his father. The newspapers and, therefore, the public see it that way: and they do so because he and his father failed to make a clean, truthful statement of the facts at Cliff's trial, in an attempt to save the old man's life as well as the son's. If I got him off now by calling Ottawa's attention to the original confession, it would be regarded by everyone as a softening up on the part of the R.C.M.P. 

British justice would suffer, by losing some of its sternness and sting in the public mind, as a result; and that must never happen!" "That sounds very fine, sir, but if, suppose, the Keufels or Hore-Sparlings were on trial, instead of the Macdonalds, wouldn't British justice lose plenty of its sternness and sting then?" 

Tweed jumped to his feet, scowling at Tom. "That's enough, Shields," he glowered. "You've said enoughtoo much, for an ordinary buck constable. Do you understand?" He poured himself out a half tumbler of Scotch and drank it neat. "Jush remember thish, Shields," he admonished.""It's th' rish wot gets th' pleasure. It's th' poor wot gets th' blame.' If you make that your theme song, you can't go mush wrong in thish country. Itsh th' theme song of th' British peoples. Now get th' hell outa here!"

Tom returned to the men's quarters, and told Paul about the interview. Paul listened in silence until Tom had finished, then observed: "It's men like Tweed that make a fellow lose his faith in human nature. I still think there's a good chance that a pardon might come through from Ottawa for young Macdonald. These things have a way of getting around somehow. I'm applying for a pass to the hangings to see what does happen. I want to see them whisk young Macdonald off the scaffold with a last minute

stay of execution. I also want to see Tweed there, too, regardless of what happens and watch how he behaves. 

He'll have plenty on his conscience if young Macdonald does swing. He's sure to be there-they say he's a great man for attending hangings. I hear our old friend Sergeant

Schlitz, is coming up from Lethbridge to see the show, too. 

How about coming along, too, Tom, to witness the fireworks? They'll give you a pass, if you ask for it." 

Tom hesitated. "Oh, all right," he finally said with a shrug.

On the evening before the hangings-which were scheduled to take place about dawn-it seemed to Tom that he had barely fallen asleep when he was awakened by 

Paul shaking him. "Time already?" he murmured drowsily. "Sure, it's a quarter to three and the hangings are scheduled for four-thirty."

Tom got out of bed and commenced to dress. He felt cold and his skin became goose-fleshed. He decided that attending an early morning hanging was not as conducive to a healthy flow of blood as going on an early morning hike or duck shooting trip.

They drove out to the jail in a private coupe, belonging to a comrade. When they arrived at the jail, they were admitted to the grounds by a night watchman. They entered the building itself by a side door, over which hung a single, high-powered electric light bulb, burning brightly beneath a dusty reflector and somehow conveying a cold. and dismal note. From within the building a bell began to ring loudly. "The death watch," Paul murmured. "The what?" Tom questioned. “On the night of an execution," Paul explained, “the bells all over the prison ring regularly at half-hour intervals. Don't know why they do it; since they say it drives the convicts just about bug house. That's four o'clock."

Upstairs, they were met by two turnkeys, one of whom examined their passes and conducted them to a room filled

with tobacco smoke, the odor of alcohol, portable typewriters, cigarette butts and about a dozen men. The men were the representatives of the press, whose reports on how the Macdonalds passed their last moments and went to their deaths would make a half column or so of news in newspapers throughout the Dominion the following morning.

Tom and Paul both felt some uneasiness as they were met by the glances of the group. Despite the fact that they were policemen and supposed to be hardened in such matters, they were the only ones in the group that appeared in the slightest degree ill at ease. One or two of the newspapermen looked completely bored; another, as if he resented the Macdonalds being hanged at such an early hour on account of its spoiling his night's sleep. Probably it was the way he actually did feel about it, too. Some of the faces were familiar to them as those of representatives of the press who had covered the Macdonald trial for their various newspaper connections. "Well, it looks as if the boy will swing, too, after all," one of the newspapermen reflected. "I hear no word has come through from Ottawa yet about a reprieve for him and it's not likely that one of our big bugs at Ottawa will get out of bed in his silk nightie to sign one at this time of the night and have the wires made hot between here and Ottawa. I just can't somehow picture old 'H. G.' doing a thing like that, can you?" "Nope, I can't either," agreed a lean reporter, who was sitting reading over a sheet of copy on the drum of his typewriter. "I wonder if either of them gets his head jerked off when they take the drop. Remember that

Italian bootlegger, Pecadillo, who murdered the policeman, coming down in two parts? Wasn't that a dowser? I never laughed so much in my life, before or since, as at the embarrassed look on the hangman's face. Claimed he'd miscalculated the drop by about six inches; although I suspect the beggar was so drunk at the time, he couldn't have calculated his own height." "Yep, but Pecadillo weighed around two hundred and

forty pounds and his neck was only about the same size as yours or mine.”

The warden entered the room just then. "Gentlemen of the press and Constables er-Brooker and Shields," he said in a quiet, but very distinct, voice, "I'd like to say just a word or two before conducting you over to the other wing of the prison, where the death cells are located. I trust you will treat this matter with discretion and a certain amount of reverence. An occasion of this sort demands it. You will be expected to reduce any conversation in the vicinity of the execution chamber to a minimum and, while an execution is in progress, I must demand absolute and complete silence. The executions are scheduled to take place in a few minutes now. 

If you'll come with me, I'll show you where you may watch the proceedings from."

They followed the warden down a long corridor to another wing of the building. Everything in this wing was painted a prosaic, battleship grey, even to the smooth, concrete floors. Two of a group of several cells in this wing had canvas sheets and blankets thrown over them; the others were empty. Four or five turnkeys and the hangman were in the vicinity, in addition to a doctor, Inspector 

Tweed, Sergeant Schlitz and members of a jury assembled for the occasion. 

Kenneth Macdonald was led out of his cell a minute or two before half-past-four o'clock was rung off by the prison electric bells. He was very stiff and pale and looked straight ahead of him. As he came opposite the group of newspapermen, however, his manner changed slightly and he looked about him. "This is one hell of an end for a man who has gone straight most of his life," he remarked, with the grim trace of a smile on his lips.

Tom's knees rocked together as they led Macdonald to an opening in a wall, about the size of a clothes' closet and located about thirty feet away from the death cells. 

He was conscious that the group of onlookers, as a whole, looked more nauseated than the condemned man himself. 

One of them-the lean news reporter who had been

discussing hangings so flippantly in the waiting roomtook a long, fumbling swig from a pocket flask, poorly concealed behind a handkerchief in a pretense of blowing his nose.

A noose was placed about MacDonald's neck and the hangman seemed to take an interminably long time adjusting it. Then a hood was placed over his head. A matter of four or five long seconds more and the trap was sprung beneath his feet. About a minute after it was all over, a horrible groaning suddenly came up from below the trap door. "Don't be alarmed," the warden reassured those present. "They're merely taking the noose off the corpse's neck and that lets the air out of its lungs and sometimes causes that weird groaning with the vocal chords. Strange isn't it? He'e quite dead." "Let's hope so, anyway," Paul remarked uneasily.

Hearing the man who, a minute or so before, had walked past them a living human being referred to impersonally as "the corpse" had a disquieting effect on Tom. He wanted to leave then and there, but decided he would have to face the remainder of the act to escape ridicule. 

The son's turn came next. Cliff Macdonald walked out of his cell steadily and with chin up, at first; the only immediate sign of fear about him being a decided paleness. He seemed to be inwardly tottering, however. He stopped before Tom. "Tell my mother that I'm sorry I turned out as I did," he said mechanically, with a bewildered and far-away look in his eyes. He spoke to the group as a whole but to Tom, in particular; possibly because Tom had a more sympathetic, and somewhat less sinister, look on his face than most of the others, under the dull glare of the prison lights. "Tell her tell her," he faltered, seemingly tottering on the brink of a nervous disintegration, "that I might have repaid some of the love and care and preachin' she threw away on me by making good, if I had had some better breaks in life. Will you do that?" "Sure we will, Macdonald," Tom assured him, something catching in his throat as he spoke. "We'll tell her

you took your medicine like a man and would have done all right if you'd only got the breaks." "Thanks a lot," Cliff said hoarsely, while the group as a whole glanced at Tom with a frigid, nervous surprise and the lean reporter took an extra long swig from his flask.

As he turned away to take his last few steps as a living being, Cliff's composure left him and a helpless, terrified look came into his eyes. It reminded Paul of a look that comes into a steer's eyes, when, on passing through a slaughter house, it catches its first scent of the blood of its slaughtered predecessors. The group shifted slightly and looked at one another uneasily, sensing a mental and nervous disintegration going on before its eyes, before the hangman's noose had had time to get its work in. A few seconds later, Cliff had followed his father through the trap door.

When a group including the warden, hangman, doctor, Inspector Tweed, Sergeant Schlitz and Tom and Paul went down a narrow flight of stairs to the bottom of the scaffold, a terrible sight met their eyes. A pair of assistant turnkeys had stretched Kenneth Macdonald's corpse out on a sheet of canvas, where it lay, stiff and cold and grey; and they were now engaged in a scuffle with the kicking, distorted form of Cliff Macdonald. His neck had become stretched to twice its normal length and showed a hollow gap in it two or three inches wide, where his vertebrae had become clearly parted by the fall.

Dr. Boving, the doctor, rushed up to the form excitedly, as the men stretched it on another sheet of canvas, still kicking. With chattering teeth and terror in his bleary, protruding eyes, he placed a stethoscope over Macdonald's heart and his blotchy features turned pale as he listened to the boy's heart pounding. "His heart's still beating," he informed the warden and 

Tweed falteringly. "That can't be, Doctor Boving," Tweed said emphatically. "I can see where his damned neck's broken quite plainly. Why there's a gap three inches wide there." "Yes, I know," Dr. Boving chattered, "but his heart's

still alive just the same. The fall has apparently separated the vertebrae without severing the spinal nerve chord completely. This boy's body's still alive, while his brain's apparently dead. He doesn't know anything of what's going on." "How long is he likely to remain in that state, Doctor 

Boving?" the warden asked. "I don't know. Perhaps three or four hours." "Three or four hours!" Tweed let out. "We're not staying here that long. That's out of the question."

The body of Cliff Macdonald continued to kick and flop about helplessly on the canvas, like a fish out of water. "I'll try an adjustment in the position of his neck," Dr. 

Boving volunteered. "It might help him out."

Dr. Boving's "adjustment" consisted of twisting Macdonald's neck from side to side violently in an effort to tear the remaining shreds of the spinal nerve column apart.

Big beads of nervous perspiration sprang to his fat forehead, as he unsuccessfully performed this grotesque operation and the body adhered even more violently to its kicking antics. "Here, for God's sake get out of the way Doctor and let me put a bullet through the poor wretch's brain," Paul volunteered, stepping forward and removing his Colt .455 from its holster. 

Sergeant Schlitz gripped Paul's arm from behind and stopped him, however. There was a look of outraged amazement on the N.C.O.'s features. "Constable Brooker, are you out of your mind?" he spluttered vehemently. "Do you realize what you're proposing doing?" "Yes," Paul answered, "taking a poor devil out of his misery." "You'd be breaking Rules and Regulations of the R.C. 

M.P. under section-" "You'd be laying yourself open to a charge of manslaughter besides," Tweed cut in coldly. "With your permission, Doctor Boving, I'm going to order the corpse placed in its coffin. As long as the brain's dead, the rest of it doesn't matter.”

"As long as the brain's dead-why, yes, of course."

Dr. Boving hesitated a long time while he cleared his throat; then spluttered a wavering consent to Tweed's wish. The body was picked up by the two turnkeys and placed in a coffin of rough lumber. The sound of its stockinged feet and elbows knocking against the coffin's sides came in muffled beats to the ears of the group, as they stood in dead silence, after the lid was closed. 

The hangman, a short, effeminate individual, looked at the warden and doctor with a worried frown. "I hope this won't get me into any trouble," he volunteered uneasily. "I adjusted the noose right. It must have slipped." "There's nothing to worry about," Tweed assured him. "It's just one of those things. Death was instantaneous to all intents, wasn't it, Doctor Boving?" "Why-er-yes. Death was er-instantaneous," Dr.

Boving stated none too convincingly. "Atta boy, Doc. No use getting anyone in trouble unnecessarily," Tweed opined. "We're all in the same boat, more or less." "This is certainly a lovely example of fine, stern British justice," Paul let out morosely, "—regular love feast." "Constable Brooker, I don't want to hear another word out of you!" Tweed commanded. "Just remember who you are. Do you understand?"

Paul remained stoney silent while everyone in the group glanced at him uneasily. The body continued to knock against the sides of the coffin like a fish out of water. "You know," the hangman confided uneasily, to divert the group's attention, "I'm opposed to capital punishment in any form; but I think the Frenchman's way of chopping a person's head off with the guillotine the best of any. It leaves no doubt about the issue and gives the spectators a real run for their money." "How do you like the job of hanging a woman?" Sergeant Schlitz let out, nudging the hangman with a forced good nature. "I don't. The only time I ever hanged one, I allowed a police matron to truss her belly up for me. She didn't do a good job of it and, when I sprang the trap, the

woman's insides fell out all over the place. I needed a shovel to put her all in the coffin." 

Sergeant Schlitz leaned back with his hands on his hips and roared with loud, bass laughter at this anecdote. "Nice people," Paul said quietly and sarcastically to Tom. "If you say another word, Brooker, I'll place you under arrest," Tweed, who apparently had heard his remark, thundered in a tone of angry finality. "Yes, sir."

Muffled sounds continued to come from inside the coffin. "And remember," Tweed warned, "no word about what you've just seen. If the outside public gets to know the details of some of these executions when things just don't go right a lot of silly sentimentalists amongst them might make trouble. We don't want to abolish capital punishment; nor do we want to copy the Yankees with their beastly electric chair."

In the evening, when his duties were finished for the day and he had changed into his civilian clothes, Tom visited Cliff Macdonald's mother at the little housekeeping room she had taken in Calgary since her husband and son had been arrested. He had a strong feeling of remorse, which he couldn't well hide, as he looked at her. For hadn't he aided and abetted, though only slightly, a vindictive society in murdering her son?

Her eyes were swollen and blood-shot, her hair, which was an even mixture of white and grey, dishevelled, and she regarded him, at first, with an open mouth and bewildered stare. He realized that he was looking at the real victim of the murder for which the two Macdonalds had paid with their lives an innocent victim, left with the status in society of wife of a murderer, mother of his accomplice.

Tom controlled himself, wishing that a deep, vague sense of guilt had not drawn him into her presence like a magnet. 

Yet the sense was there and was little weaker because society pointed a finger in the opposite direction. He told

her that her son had met his end bravely, while thinking of her. "And how about God? Was he saying his prayers at the end?" she questioned.

Tom hesitated a moment, since, for the last minute or thirty seconds of his life, Cliff's features had been obscured in the hangman's hood. "Yes," he finally brought himself to say, "his eyes were turned towards Heaven and his lips mumbled a prayer shortly before it was all over."

He was glad almost immediately of the lie, for Mrs. 

MacDonald's bleary eyes lighted up and he could see that she gained considerable solace from the news. She asked him if he believed in Spiritualism and the ideas of Sir 

Oliver Lodge and Sir Arthur Conan Doyle on the subject.

He didn't believe in them, but, as he guessed that she might be finding some sort of mental and spiritual relief in things of that nature and was hanging on his answer, he told her that he thought "there was a whole lot in them." 

Finally the conversation veered around to her husband and Cliff. "You know," she whispered tragically, "if Kenneth had only come forward and testified in Cliff's behalf, as he should have done, it would have saved the poor, innocent boy's life. He was as innocent of that murder as you or I; I can feel it in my very bones. Getting up there and having to give evidence against his own father got him all befuddled and conveyed a poor impression on the court. 

That Crown Prosecutor was too clever for Cliff, too. I could tell that his sarcasm was riling Cliff all the time, knowing the poor, dear boy as I do did, I mean—and placing a noose around his neck which didn't belong there at all."

She read Tom some extracts from a book of prophecies for that year, 1933. It had cost her a dollar and prophecied everything from a stock-market boom to the end of the world in the brief span of twelve months and distorted the meanings of numerous Biblical quotations in support of its various forecasts. She got him to promise he would come back and see her again.

After the interview, he went straight to Mary's to seek some solace himself. Mary was in, waiting for him, and 

Tom lay on the couch and placed his head against her lap. "What's the matter, Tom?" "I'm disgusted with everything, Mary. There's not an ounce of real decency in the whole world. I realize it more and more every day; especially after seeing the two 

Macdonalds hanged this morning and visiting Cliff Macdonald's mother just before coming up here. I shouldn't have gone to that hanging at all. Seeing that boy hanged and visiting his mother afterwards have given me a bad spell of the blues and a headache. Relaxing like this with you though, with your hand on my forehead, is making the headache better."

The spring of 1933 had come to Calgary and gone, merging into an early summer. The time had been drawing near when the personnel of the Force detailed for 

Northern detachment service would be leaving up the tributaries of the great MacKenzie River by rail, boat and plane to relieve those members of the Force who had spent two or more years of their lives on lonely Arctic and sub-Arctic detachments. The names of this personnel had not yet been posted. Tom and Paul had both waited impatiently for the list to appear, confident, but not quite certain, that their names would be on it. Both had expressed a desire to be placed on the same detachment as the other. When the list did finally appear-which happened to be during the morning after the Macdonalds had breathed their last-Tom's name was on the list, but Paul's was missing. He was detailed for the Great Bear Lakes region. "Congratulations, Tom," Paul said, concealing his personal chagrin well. "Looks as though I'll have to look around for a new buddy now. He'll have to go some to come up to you though, you old buzzard." "I don't altogether like the looks of it, Paul," Tom confided. "I don't mind stating frankly that you're a better man than I for that part of the country. I've wanted to go up there all right and now I've been chosen, I'm glad of

it. But there are better men for the job than I, including yourself, who've been turned down. Why? It almost looks, in a way, as though someone wanted me put out of circulation for a time." "Maybe Tweed had something to do with it," Paul suggested. "I wouldn't be surprised. However, let's forget about it. There's the other possibility, too, that they're giving me a real break. Guess I'll be going up to Edmonton in the next few days; it's the jumping off place for all detachments in the Western Arctic, excepting the Liard district of British Columbia." "Yeah."

XIX "GO NORTH, YOUNG MAN, GO NORTH" 

The following day, during luncheon in the mess, the divisional sergeant-major stalked in to the mess room and called out Tom's name in a crisp, clear voice. On getting up from his place at the table and marching up to the senior N.C.O., he was told to get his things packed, his affairs in order and be in readiness by the following morning to take the early train to Edmonton. He was excused from all duties for the rest of the day.

Tom had already packed his effects in anticipation of such short notice, since it is the rule rather than the exception in the Mounted Police, and had only to make arrangements to have his box trunk and equipment moved down to the railway station. He left the barracks earlier than usual that afternoon and picked Mary up at her high school.

They dined together downtown and Tom suggested afterwards they go to a movie. Mary, however, prevailed on him to come home with her. Both were outwardly gay and nonchalant, as if bidding a cheerful farewell to an affair that had been good fun while it lasted. 

Mary mixed three or four drinks and they sat before a crackling fire of boxwood in a small fireplace and laughed and joked. There was a hollowness about the jokes, however, and an insincerity about the laughter.

Finally, it got on Tom's nerves and he broke off in the middle of telling an amusing anecdote about a rookie in Vancouver, who was sent out with a bucket of whitewash by his comrades to paint the last post, to suddenly clasp his hands in front of his knees and look down gloomily at the floor. "Let's not fool one another, Mary," he said suddenly, "this will be our last meeting for a long time to come; per- 167

haps forever. I feel the same way about you as you do about me.

Call it what you like. People have called it love and all that sort of hooey and I guess that's as good a name as any for it. "You know, Mary, I used to think that there were two kinds of love: one, that kind of sentimental, rather substantial, variety that we feel for our parents and sisters and brothers, if we have any, which is deep-rooted and solid, but somewhat unattractive, like an oak tree; the other, a sort of pure physical attraction, or straight lust, which is the outcome of the er-sexual side of us. Until 

I met you, I never realized that there could be a combination of the two which beats either of them. I now begin to see that the whole secret of not only life, but happiness, too, revolves around a man and a woman, just as we are now. A man by himself, or a woman by herself, are just isolated, meaningless units of a beautiful pattern, which needs to be fitted together to make sense, like a jig-saw puzzle. "It's a funny world, indeed, Mary. I don't suppose I'll ever stop kicking myself because I let you slip out of my life, even if it is only temporary; yet, right now, there is nothing I can do about it. I'm like a leaf blown about in the wind. I think one of your classical poets once made that analogy. Byron was it? Anyway, the analogy goes for me, too.' 99 "You're positively 

Mary laughed good naturedly. dripping with sentimentality tonight, for a man," she told him. "I'm glad, too, Tom, in a way. I feel just the same. 

It's going to seem a terribly long time while you're up 

North. You must take good care of yourself and write to me often.” "No fear of my not writing. You'll likely get a couple of letters from me by every plane down." He bade her a farewell and made her promise not to go to the trouble of coming down to the station to see him off the following morning. He disliked public farewells as being unreal and ostentatious. 

The following morning, Paul managed to get off duty for half an hour to accompany him down to the railway

"GO NORTH, YOUNG MAN, GO NORTH" 169 

station and see him off. Tom realized that he was saying farewell to another person who had come to mean a great deal to him in a short space of time. 

Paul had never suffered the inhibitions of an upper class upbringing and education like Tom. He came of that class which probably possesses the highest quota of common sense and rugged mental and physical health on the North American Continent-the English-speaking, Canadian middle class of the Western and Middle Western provinces. 

He had something more than mere book learning-he had a first-hand knowledge of life and the necessary analytical powers to make head and tail of it.

The weather was very hot and close for a place as far north as Edmonton when Tom arrived there and the mosquitoes were bad. Edmonton with its population of around 80,000 is North America's northernmost metropolis. It is the gateway to the great MacKenzie River basin and the Peace River wheat block.

Tom was introduced to the other members slated for northern service by the divisional sergeant-major soon after his arrival. They were all strangers to him with the exception of one-the detested Corporal Symes from Vancouver. Why they had picked Symes, undersized and unfamiliar with the country, ahead of a born and bred Canadian he-man like Paul Brooker, Tom couldn't understand. The thing angered him.

Another member of the group who interested Tom was a swarthy young French-Canadian, named Maurice Patenaude. Tom had kept in mind what Paul had told him at Calgary about the rumored narcotic smuggling activities of the Keufels. This Patenaude turned out to be the same person who had been working on the secret files in the office at Calgary and had mentioned a connection between Inspector Tweed's transfer from Vancouver and these activities.

Tom took Patenaude to one side the day after his arrival in Edmonton and questioned him on the subject. 

Patenaude looked Tom squarely in the eyes with a pair of honest, brown ones of his own and told him frankly: "I don't know you at all, Shields; but you seem a real

nice fellow. Things I've let drop to some of the other boys about what I saw on the secret files at Calgary have gotten me into plenty of trouble, though, and I don't care to say any more about those things. I've already been transferred out of a swell job in the C.I.B. on account of not keeping a frozen mouth. The trouble is, I drink too much beer. Beer lubricates a fellow's tongue, as well as his bowels, you know. Now I'm being sent where there isn't any beer at all for two years. 

If you take my advice, you'll practice eye and mouth closing exercises in this Force as long as the Tweed-LaGauze clique keeps the wool pulled down over the eyes of the Commissioner and the Justice Department at Ottawa. They have the greatest racket in the world working in this Force; they're criminals within a police force. Boy oh boy, are they a smooth bunch! The day Tweed arrived in Calgary, all sorts of stuff began vanishing out of the files in the Calgary office. Important stuff. I didn't keep my eyes closed though and that's why I'm no longer in Calgary." "The plot deepens," Tom reflected. "Who is LaGauze, Maurice?" "Do you mean to say you've never heard of Inspector LaGauze?” "Why yes-come to think of it I've read his name once or twice on General Orders; but I never thought for a minute you meant him. He's supposed to be a pretty smart policeman." He's "So he is. He's just like Tweed in that way. cleaned up some of the biggest cases in Eastern Canada when he's been working on the level; but he's only on the level about half the time. He's an Ottawa Catholic, like me; but I'm not boasting about it any." "Thanks, Maurice." 

The following day Tom received three surprises. In the morning the Divisional Sergeant-Major called him into his office for a brief interview. "Shields," he informed Tom, "we're sending you on ahead of the other two members of your detachmentprobably tomorrow-into the Peace River district for a week or so. The personnel of our detachments up there

"GO NORTH, YOUNG MAN, GO NORTH" 171 

are snowed under with work in connection with government relief and they have wired down an urgent request for an extra man or two to relieve the pressure. You're well educated and a sort of handy man, from what I hear, so I'm sending you. You'll go up there by train and make connections with the balance of the northern personnel by meeting their boat on its way up the Peace river." "Very good, sir."

On leaving the office, Tom received the second surprise. 

He found a group of about a dozen men clustered about the barrack notice board, reading a mimeographed announcement and, peering over their shoulders, he was able to read his own name on the sheet. He was listed as one of a group of three slated for a Great Bear Lakes detachment. By some quirk of fate, the other two were 

Corporal Symes and Maurice Patenaude. 

Tom's heart sank as he scanned the name of Symes and he read it a second and third time. It had been one of the most disheartening days of his life when they had promoted the Englishman, instead of him, at Vancouver. 

Tom had been well out in front of the recruits' class; 

Symes, never better than fifth or sixth; and the corporal's chevrons had been clearly promised to the man making the best showing in these classes. There had been an element of mystery about the whole thing. 

To do Symes justice, Tom knew that he possessed a fund of worldly experience and cunning, which are decided assets in a policeman. That might have tipped the scales in his favor; but it did not make it any pleasanter being placed under him on a lonely northern detachment for two years. Symes was preponderantly of that undersized, cockney English class of career men that have a wonderful capacity for intrigue and ability to rise in the world by taking all the credit for the successes of subordinates and sucking their brains like a vampire. Tom would have rather been under Sergeant Schlitz or Smokey

Harris, or even Tweed, under the same circumstances; at least they were he-men, despite their various faults, and could command a certain respect as such. To have to put

up with the overbearing antics of a physical and social inferior for two years, on account of five cents worth of gold ribbon, however, was a real trial for an educated man. 

Tom kept these misgivings to himself and when Symes swaggered up to the notice board on his bandy legs and eyed him with a sly mixture of insolence and triumph, Tom managed to conceal his chagrin sufficiently to congratulate him. He quickly learned, however, that it doesn't pay to offer the olive branch to one of Symes' temperament. "That's nufin' to congwatulate me on," Symes spluttered with a forced indignation, "sending a man h'up to a wild countwy like that with a couple of young gween 'orns." "Green horns be damned," Tom let out. "Patenaude's people were settling this country and fighting for it, when yours were bloody well losing themselves in a fog over in London, England. As for me, I've worked in mining camps and in the bush from Vancouver to the Alaska Panhandle" "Just wemember 'oo yer speakin' to, Shields.” "I can't forget it-unfortunately." "Yer think yer clever, don't yer, Shields?" "No, not half clever enough, or I'd be wearing those stripes instead of you." "Such h'impudence!" Symes shrugged.

The third surprise came late in the afternoon when a new Ford coupe drove into the barrack grounds and Mary Andreachuk got out just as he was leaving barracks for the day. "Tom!" "Mary!" "I've driven all the way up from Calgary, Tom."

He got into the car and took over the wheel from her and drove about half a mile away without speaking a word. 

There was no need to say anything; he could sense what she was thinking. "Well?" he questioned finally, as he brought the car to a halt on a lonely gravel road.

"GO NORTH, YOUNG MAN, GO NORTH" 

173 "You remember how you spoke to me, Tom, when we first met?" "Yes-and I'm still ashamed of it, Mary." "There's no need to feel that way about it, Tom. I've been thinking about you, oh, ever so much, since we said good-bye at Calgary. It'll be two years until we meet again, Tom, and I love you and if it will make you happy-" 

Tom smiled and kissed her gently as he would a child. "Some day, Mary, but not now. I love you too, but there's lots of time. When I come back from the North we'll get married."

Tom felt good and his conscience was at ease as the locomotive of the Edmonton, Dunvegan and British Columbia Railway pulled his train slowly over the meandering, shallow road-bed which leads up to the Peace River block. The line is a relic of a former Western boom era in one respect; in another, it still serves the vast and slowly growing area above Edmonton. He had turned down a pearl of great price when it had been offered to him for nothing; no mean feat in a man of his virile disposition; for virtue comes hardest to those who can gain the most pleasure by trampling on it; easiest to the weak and unventuresome. True, one side of him did cry, "Fool! 

Fool! Fool!" but it was in the minority. He found conditions in the Peace River area somewhat appalling and quite unbelievable to the ordinary individual living in a Canadian or American town or city. While many people living in cities like Vancouver and Calgary were undoubtedly destitute and going, at times, hungry, due to the depression, and while the numbers of such persons ran into the thousands; they nevertheless constituted only about ten percent of the population of these larger places. In the Peace River area, however, a half to three-quarters of the population over wide areas had reached this condition. This was so at such well-known centres as Grande Prairie, Peace River, Fairview and 

Spirit River. At others, conditions were even worse. At 

Sunset Prairie, for instance, eighty percent of the popula-

tion were forced onto the Government relief rolls at the same time; while at Hythe, the figure hovered around the ninety percent mark.

A spirit of hopelessness held sway throughout the country, quite the opposite to that of rural robustness and independence pictured for agricultural communities by the fiction in certain Canadian and American periodicals which were vigorously advocating a "back to the land movement" at the time.

Some years previously, the Peace River Block had undergone a great boom. Hailed (by transportation companies, armies of real estate dealers and speculators and allied politicians) as the last great frontier on the North

American Continent to conquer, farmers and their families had flocked into the area by the hundreds from all over Canada, the United States, the British Isles and Western and Central Europe. 

Trouble was that many of those who boosted the Peace River country in its halcyon days were of the theoreticallyminded, university professor-economist variety, or else had an axe to grind in the way of real estate. These people forgot to ascertain, in their enthusiasm, such vital factors as the depth and fertility of soil in the area. 

It just so happens that the soil in the "Peace" is very shallow over wide areas, not being more than four or five inches deep over representative tracts. 

There are exceptions; some notable ones which include some of the finest farming land in the Dominion, but they are only scattered ones.

Now four or five inches of soil are sufficient for cultivation, providing a wind storm does not come along and blow them away. The original farmers in the belt fared moderately well, because their ground under cultivation was protected from wind storms, in most cases, on all four sides by standing, virgin timber. As new farmers migrated into the area and cleared the ground for cultivation, however, the virgin timber became less and less and the wide, open tracts of farm land lying openly exposed to the whims of wind storms, more and more.

Finally, large areas of farm land in the "Peace" reached

"GO NORTH, YOUNG MAN, GO NORTH" 

175 the stage where a farmer was liable to go to bed one night with a certain area of arable farm land under cultivation on his property and wake up the next morning to find himself endowed with a few acres of large rocks and pebbles and his good soil deposited over on his neighbor's plot, or vice versa.

Fences were built to stop this migration of the precious films of mother earth, which meant so much to the hands that toiled on them; but the effort required to drag a bank of earth, four or five feet high, heaped against a fence, over fifty or sixty acres of rock and clay with a scraper, after a wind storm has taken its toll, is something more than heartbreaking hard work, it is uneconomic. 

Hundreds of families drawing relief from the Alberta government, ranging from eight dollars a month and up was Nature's answer to the gorgeous, optimistic picture painted by the "back to the land movement" cranks of the United States and Canada and another way of her demonstrating the universal law that something cannot be obtained for nothing.

In thinking of the Peace River area Tom was invariably put in mind afterwards of a certain Czecho-Slovakian farmer, who came rushing out of his farm house one day as Tom was passing by it over a bumpy stretch of road in a light sedan, belonging to the Force. The strain of events had become too much for the man and he whined to Tom like a child. "Oh meester policeman-meester policeman," he choked, arriving at the car as Tom stopped it, “you mus' do somet'ings for us. We have no bread, no meat, not'ings in the house. We jus' starve out here; me, my wife and our poor lil' Jan.' 

"I'll do what I can about getting you emergency relief right away," Tom told him. "Watch out for this car when I come back this way and tell me all about your case then." He reached down in the pocket of his tunic, as he had done at other times since coming into the district. "Here's two dollars to help things along for a little while. 

Sorry I can't let you have more-it's all I've got."

Tom left the "Peace" with the plaintive cry, "Oh meester policeman-meester policeman," ringing in his

It seemed to be the unofficial cry of hunger and destitution of the large foreign-born section of those on the relief rolls of the district. ears.

He would have liked to have been present over again at a speech he had once heard, in Vancouver, by a wellknown politician, lauding the Peace River country to a youthful audience; merely to be able to get up before the audience and confront the speaker with the truth. The orator had reeled off the phrase, rather obviously inspired by Horace Greely: "Go North, young man, go North to the Peace."

Tom had to wait around for a day doing nothing when his duty in the Peace River district was finished, as the boat carrying the other members of the Force up North was eighteen hours behind schedule. It was a paddlewheeler with a high, old-fashioned smoke stack that vomited clouds of light grey smoke from the wood burning in its fire-boxes as it ploughed languidly up the shallow waters of the Peace.

The scenery, for the greater part of the journey was the most unattractive he had ever seen. The country was very flat and regular, with low, forested areas and bleak, almost barren, stretches of uninhabited land, occasionally broken by a small white settlement or Indian village, which came down to the water's edge and stretched a little pier out from the shore with a red or white freight shed on it with a monotonous regularity. The boat stopped at most of these places for a short time to unload supplies and mail.

The day after they had changed to another river steamer, at a place called Vermillion Falls, Corporal Symes walked up to Tom on deck and asked him how he was enjoying the journey. It was a stupid question, as, on top of the depressing quality of the landscape, the black flies and mosquitoes were beginning to get bad. They had been avoiding one another rather obviously during the journey and this had caused a certain amount of gossip

"GO NORTH, YOUNG MAN, GO NORTH" 

177 amongst the balance of the R.C.M.P. personnel and other passengers. "All right," Tom answered non-committally. "Yer know, Shields," Symes speculated airily, "my pwomotion to corpowal 'as set me 'a thinkin' a good deal about yer." "Oh?" Tom shrugged. "In spite of the fact that yer made such 'igh marks in yer wecwuits' exams, they pwomoted me to the wank of corpowal a'ead of yer. Wonder why they did that? 

Yer've got lots o' pull, 'aven't yer, Tommy? Yer old man's a bloomin' major, ain't e'? They must figure yer'we one of those well-h'educated, but impwactical, beggars.' "9 "Shut up, you damned lime juicer," Tom let out angrily. "And listen-if you want to run our detachment, without having a one-man mutiny on your hands, you'd better lay off rubbing it in like that. I know why I didn't get those stripes better than you do. It isn't my fault police forces are meddled in by a bunch of grafters and politicians all the time. And I can tell you why you got those stripes, too, ahead of half a dozen men who should be wearing them. You crawled to the O.C. and Sergeant-major in

Vancouver and went to them with long stories about some of the men in training there being radicals, just because they weren't Imperialistic tories, like yourself, but ordinary 

Canadians. That eliminated them from the promotion list and you picked up a pair of stripes that were going begging.' 

Symes blushed and blustered something angrily about 

Tom putting himself "outside the pale" by such remarks; then turned on his heel and walked away. Later, Patenaude came up to him and asked him how he liked the surrounding country in his slightly broken English. "Not so well, Pat," Tom answered. "I begin to wonder why I was ever fool enough to ask to be sent up to a country like this. It's one hell of a place. I've squished more blood out of dead mosquitoes today than I knew I had in me." "You'll like it as you go along, Shields," the French Canadian youth told him. "I've been up in this country

before and the life up here sort of gets a man after a while. You never want to go back to civilization again.
