It's a great country when you get to know it. You trek and you shoot and you eat and sleep well and everyone takes you at your true value after a time. "You wait till you meet a few of the people; especially the Eskimos. Boy, they're the sweetest people in this whole world. You know, if you go and visit an Eskimo, especially if you have to put up at his place for the night, he'll offer you his wife every time; and it's the biggest insult you can give one of them to refuse her. It's like going to a friend's house down in Calgary or Edmonton, say, and turning down his best wine and food and other ways he may have of showing you hospitality; only it's ten times worse than that, from the Eskimo's point of view, to turn down his wife. That's Eskimo hospitality for you. Boy, they're the most hospitable people in the world!"

XX 

From the Peace river their boat branched up the navigable waters of the Slave river. The last of the civilian white passengers, a trader named McPherson, got off at 

Forth Smith, a settlement about 250 miles below Great

Slave lake. After an overnight stop at Fort Resolution the craft traversed a distance of ninety or a hundred miles across Great Slave lake the following day to the head of the great MacKenzie river, at the southwest end of the lake.

The MacKenzie is the Mississippi river of the North. 

Probably the least known and least sung of the world's great rivers, it nevertheless ranks second in length and area drained to the Mississippi on the North American continent and drains an area approximately two-and-ahalf times as great as either the Colorado or Columbia river; 682,000 square miles. Its basin means as much to the North as the Mississippi basin means to the Southern United States. An area, two thousand five hundred miles long by hundreds of miles wide, extending from below Lake Athabaska to the Arctic ocean, is fundamentally influenced by the presence of this great, shallow, sluggish river and its tributaries.

It is a slow-moving, sluggish band of water because the elevation of the lowland at the west end of Athabaska lake is only about 700 feet above sea level and the slope from that point to the Arctic ocean, a distance of over 1800 miles by the river, averages around six inches or less to the mile. The whole distance from Forth Smith, Slave river, to the Arctic ocean, 1300 miles higher up the river, can be navigated by steamers with a draft of five feet, without encountering any obstruction during the "summer" months. 179

They branched off the main body of the MacKenzie within a hundred miles of Great Bear lake and branched up Great Bear river to the lake. On the boat traversing the lake and dropping them off at their destination they found themselves situated 900 miles above Edmonton as the crow flies, providing one could fly that far. The trip, from the Peace river area, had taken twelve days.

The country in the vicinity of Great Bear lake is a bleak, flat region, consisting of countless small, shallow lakes and semi-forested, rocky lowlands. The surface of the land, as a rule, does not rise more than a hundred or hundred and fifty feet above the level of the bodies of shallow water; rarely two hundred feet. There are two mountainous features only on the shores of the main lake itself which achieve heights of over a thousand feet. One of these breaks in the monotonous regularity of the lowland is Grizzly Bear mountain which occupies the peninsula between Keith and McVicar bays. It is a massive, roundtopped ridge, quite devoid of trees for several hundred feet of its upper portion and attaining a maximum height of around fifteen hundred feet.

The other high feature is the Scented Grass hills, a ridge which attains a maximum height of twelve hundred feet on the north shore of the lake. This is a broken, round-topped ridge, similar to Grizzly Bear mountain in general character, particularly bleakness, and terminates to the East in a prominent point known as Gros Cap. 

All such sparsely forested hills and mountains have a way of forming prominent peninsulas or "points" with the spurs at their feet as they sweep down into the waters of the shallow lakes. Several of these points have achieved historical importance, the most famous being Labine point. 

It was here that Gilbert Labine and his partner, Charles St. Paul, made their first discovery of pitchblende ore, running rich in radium, thereby breaking, overnight, the world monoply on the precious substance held by the Belgian Government and startling the civilized world into a realization that this vast, little-explored territory had great potentialities. In fact it would appear as if the wealth spent in spectacular, news-making expeditions to the Polar

regions could have been better employed in ascertaining more data about the whole, vast MacKenzie region extending from below Athabaska lake up to the Arctic ocean. 

It is already known that the lake Athabaska area contains one of the largest low-grade coal deposits in the world, that will, at some future time, cause a major shift of population into the area.

The regions of Great Bear lake are rich in numbers and variety of game and fur-bearing animals, birds and food fishes. Caribou are abundant at different seasons of the year, both in the barren and wooded stretches of country. 

They are migratory in their habits, however, and travel southward to the shelter of the woods and a warmer climate in the autumn and north again in the early spring. 

Moose, grizzly bear and ordinary brown bear frequent the area but are not numerous. For other fur-bearing animals the area ranks, along with the rest of the Mac- Kenzie river basin, as the best on the North American continent and probably the world. Many of the highgrade furs, including the best silver fox, come largely from this region. The most important fur-bearing animal to the natives, however, is the humble rabbit, on which they depend largely for food, clothing and sleeping robes for winter use.

Great Bear lake itself is the fifth largest of the big lakes of the North American continent, having a superficial area of about 11,400 square miles and being exceeded in this respect only by lakes Superior, Huron, Michigan and Great Slave, in that order. Unlike the other lakes of its region, it is not shallow, but attains a considerable depth in places. Its greatest depth has never been ascertained, but in Smith and McTavish bays soundings of around forty-five and fifty fathoms have failed to find bottom. 

The shores of the lake are in general low, excepting in the region of Grizzly Bear mountain and the Scented Grass hills. The former mountain is responsible for a bold, rocky shore rising for hundreds of feet at McTavish bay, but this is the only striking feature of its kind on the whole lake. The southern and western shores are well wooded, while the northern and eastern ones are only

sparsely so. The immediate shores are mainly sand and gravel and are usually devoid of trees, but are covered with willows and various ericaceous shrubs and herbaceous plants. In most places along the south shore this treeless stretch is only a few hundred yards in width, while in most of the bays the forest extends almost to the water's edge.

Great Bear lake, though containing the finest quality of fish of any of the northern lakes, has always been relatively unfished. Besides containing millions of white fish, it is well stocked with lake trout and herring. The water of the lake is a light bluish color and so clear and transparent that a white object dropped into it can be followed by the naked eye until it reaches a depth of almost a hundred feet. 

This fifth largest of North America's lakes has never been completely or accurately surveyed, despite important mineral discoveries having been made on its shores and the delineations of it appearing in all atlases ultimately go back to the approximate surveys and sketches made by 

Sir John Richardson in 1825 and 1826 or to J. M. Bell's work of 1900.

Ice begins to form on the shores of the lake and in the bays early in October, but the whole lake does not usually freeze over until the beginning of December. The innumerable small, shallow lakes of the region, however, freeze over completely much earlier, forming an ideal surface to travel over with a sled drawn by dogs, or landing surface for an aeroplane equipped with skis. 

For ordinary purposes of individual travel, the dog team is still the automobile of the North during the winter months and the canine is not being displaced up there, like the horse farther south, by the march of mechanization. 

A woolly, barrel-chested Alsatian has no lubricant to freeze or piston rod to snap when bucking a blizzard at fifty below. Evidence of this lies in the fact that the R.C.M.P., once one of the most famous mounted units in the British Empire, now owns more dogs than horses on account of its Northern detachments. Men have been known to cover over seventy-five miles in a day by this method when

pressed. On the average, a man conversant with the country and having a good team of dogs can do forty-five or fifty miles in a day's hard travel, under favorable conditions.

For bulk transportation, or where speed is essential, the aeroplane is the most important means of transport in the North. The enormous numbers of shallow lakes, with no high obstructions on their shores to hinder a plane in either landing or taking off, introduces a range of natural landing facilities unequalled anywhere else in the world; either for a seaplane during the summer months, or plane on skis in the winter. The Eldorado mine at 

Great Bear lake was developed by aeroplane, including the flying in of heavy equipment for a mill and modern mining machinery; as has also been the case with many of the gold mines of Northern Ontario.

Paradoxically enough, transportation is slower and more difficult over wide areas in these regions in the brief, black fly and mosquito-ridden summer than during the winter months. This is because transportation is carried out by canoe and the numerous short portages between lakes, in which canoe and supplies must be carried overland, sometimes in relays, cuts down the speed of travel considerably.

During the summer, for about two months in the year, July and August, the area is subjected to hot weather and, because of the large surface of water present, becomes a good breeding ground for insects, particularly black flies and mosquitoes. These insects become so numerous as to present not only a menace to a person's health and comfort, but to his life itself. They will settle on a man in a thick cloud, sometimes so dense that it is impossible to distinguish the color of his shirt or other covering the pestered individual is wearing. 

It was in July that Symes, Tom and Patenaude arrived to relieve three other members of the Force who had been policing the country for the past two years. Two of these men were in splendid physical condition and were anticipating an early return to civilization with an eager, youthful zest. Their names were Mel O'Brien and Jack

Shannon. The third was dull and morose and had been "touched" by his two years spent on the lonely detachment. 

He was an Englishman, a graduate of Oxford or Cambridge, and of the sensitive, intellectual type that the lonesome life on a Northern outpost is most likely to affect mentally. Shannon confided to Tom that they had stopped him trying to commit suicide on two occasions and wouldn't allow him to go farther than a mile away from the detachment alone. They kept his gun loaded with cartridges from which they had extracted the lead bullets with pliers and made him shave with a safety razor, Shannon further confided. He had been that way for weeks and they had contemplated sending him down to Edmonton by plane before the boat arrived. 

It was a sad case and by no means the first of its kind in the annals of the R.C.M.P. While Shannon was discussing the subject with Tom, the unfortunate man jumped up on a chair, his eyes flashing crazily, and let out a crowing noise like a rooster. Directly afterwards, he became silent and morose again and refused to speak to anyone. 

O'Brien was the detachment corporal and, while he and Symes went over the inventory of the detachment together, Patenaude, Tom and Shannon busied themselves carrying goods up from a little pier onto which they were being unloaded from the steamer. They carried the goods up to the detachment building, which was a plain, frame structure painted a prosaic red and built of finished lumber which had been transported hundreds of miles up the MacKenzie for the purpose. They carried them up a ladder to a good-sized attic, where Patenaude finally remained aloft, arranging the different crates and boxes, which contained their year's supply of food, fuel oil, ammunition, matches and tobacco, in such a way that the goods they contained would be available in their desired sequence. 

Late the following day, the steamer, having unloaded its cargo and taken on a supply of water and newly-cut fuel wood, churned slowly away from their small wharf with O'Brien, Shannon and the other member of the

Force aboard. Her soiled, white hull and high smoke stack put Tom in mind of one of the dishevelled, dusty swans at Stanley Park in Vancouver.

The sight of her drawing slowly away in the dull haze of early evening made him feel very sick at heart. She seemed to symbolize the last connecting link for him with civilization and the life he loved. It meant two years isolated in an arctic wilderness: living in alternate periods of long days and long nights; away from Mary, his parents and friends and the sports and other activities he cherished. 

She, or another boat like her, would be back again about July of the following year, 1934, with another year's supply of rations and ammunition for the detachment; then again in the summer of 1935 to take them out-back to civilization.

They would not be cut off completely from civilization, however, as the mining activity of the district brought aeroplanes to the lake frequently all through the winter. 

About once a month they would be able to make a trip to a plane landing base to pick up any mail for the detachment.

Exactly a week after their arrival at Great Bear lake, a large white seaplane with the markings of the Force on it appeared out of the distant horizon and landed opposite the detachment building on the lake. Patenaude went out to meet the plane in a canoe and came back with an R.C. 

M.P. Inspector and the plane's pilot, an N.C.O. The Inspector was an active, thick-set man with pudgy features and dark skin. He proceeded at once to make a thorough inspection of the whole detachment, saying little to Tom and Patenaude, but spending a whole hour closeted with Corporal Symes after he was through. "Do you know who that Inspector is?" Patenaude asked

Tom significantly, as the man remained closeted with Symes. "I didn't quite get his name," Tom admitted. "You know how Symes speaks as if he had a hot potato in his mouth. Who is he?" "LaGauze.' 29

"So that's the other half of the clique you were talking about, Maurice." "Ah oui." 

Inspector LaGauze decided not to leave the detachment that day, but to depart for another inspection farther north the first thing the following morning. It was a hot night and almost as light as day. About midnight, Tom decided to take a stroll down to the lake and have a swim to cool himself off. When he got down to the water's edge, he stripped and went in quietly alongside the small pier. 

He swam and floated about aimlessly for a few minutes; then, as the water cooled him off and aroused his ambition, he decided to swim out to the seaplane and back.

When he came within about seventy-five feet of it, he suddenly realized there was a large Indian war canoe resting alongside the plane with four men in it. Three of the men were plainly Indians, by their dress and profiles; the fourth, a white man. The whole group were acting with an extraordinary, almost eerie, quietness and Tom felt tempted to make a noise to attract their attention, but checked himself. Instead, he continued to float about noiselessly in the water watching what the group were doing.

The white man suddenly arose to his full height in the canoe and unlocked the door at the side of the plane. As he slipped inside the plane's cabin, Tom was startled to recognize his silhouette as that of Inspector LaGauze. 

After a short wait, the Indians commenced to hand armfulls of obviously light burdens to LaGauze inside the cabin. After watching for a short time, Tom decided that the objects were furs seemingly about the size and shape of silver fox pelts. 

Mystified, he swam quietly ashore again, dressed under the pier and ran quietly back to the building, shaking off the mosquitoes as he ran. The following morning, after the plane had droned off over the horizon, Tom took Patenaude to one side and told him of what he had seen. 

Maurice listened calmly. "Smuggling out silver fox furs," he decided quietly.

"That's about the most harmless act that crowd have ever done." "But it's plainly against Rules and Regulations of the Force for members to trade in furs," Tom insisted, "besides being illegal. If it were known, LaGauze would be dismissed." "If it were known-yes," Patenaude agreed philosophically, "but who's going to tell? You might have a tough time proving it on what you saw last night. What actual evidence have you, Tom?" "None." "That's the way Tweed and LaGauze always act." "I hate to see a splendid police force prostituted by a bunch of crooks like Tweed and LaGauze. 99 "I don't blame you, Tom; but just remember what I said about taking eye and mouth closing exercises. Better make it ear muffs as well. Sometimes it pays to be dumb; 'specially on police forces. Look at Corporal Symes. 

You can't tell me he got where he is because he's brainy.

Someone besides himself must have wanted him to be promoted pretty badly, or he wouldn't have got there. I want to have a corporal's stripes myself, but before I get them, one of the higher-ups on the Force must want me to have them, too." "Yes," Tom reflected, "someone besides Symes, himself, must have wanted him to be promoted pretty badly. 

Who would want a man promoted to corporal, who was so dumb he thought Canada still a British Crown colony and that every man who disagreed with him was a communist ?" "Inspector Tweed, maybe." "I think you're getting warm, Patenaude."

Tom's first police duty up North came after they had been settled in the detachment about three weeks, amid sweltering heat, black flies and mosquitoes during the day and encased in mosquito netting over their beds at night. 

During this time they were kept too busy straightening things out around the detachment for Symes and him to indulge in any personal squabbles. Their chief chore consisted of cutting a winter's supply of fuel wood. They

worked with axe and buck saw from early morning till late at night, until their backs and arms ached and their bodies were covered with sweat and bumps and sores raised by the bites of black flies, mosquitoes and no-see-'ems. 

One day an official of the Hudson's Bay Company arrived at the detachment in a gas speed boat to lodge a complaint with them about some trouble brewing between Indians and Eskimos on the Coppermine river. His concern was more with a possible loss of business to the company, resulting from such trouble, rather than any peace-loving scruples and he made no bones about it. He undertook to conduct one of them to the mouth of the Dease river, one of the few important tributaries draining into Great Bear lake, and introduce him to a reliable Indian guide who knew the way up the Dease over to the Coppermine river, via some lakes, known as the Dismal lakes.

Symes would have liked to have gone himself, but he was anxious to get the detachment records and office returns arranged in some sort of order, so that he understood them; in addition to learning something about running a detachment from Patenaude, who had served on a Northern detachment before. He could do this best by pumping 

Patenaude for the desired information while Tom was not around to ridicule him. So Tom left with the Hudson's 

Bay factor early the following morning.

The company official took him to the site of old Fort 

Confidence at the mouth of the Dease in his speed boat, where they arrived at dusk and put up in the open for the night. Early the following morning he started up a trail along the Dease with an Indian guide. 

In addition to other supplies for the journey, Tom had taken with him some crisp, new, one-dollar Dominion of

Canada bills that would be charged to his expense account. It had lately become a custom in the country to pay Indian guides in bills of this denomination; always in new, pristine condition. Just as formerly they had insisted on being paid their fees in newly-minted twentyfive and fifty-cent Canadian pieces; so now, these guides

favored the one-dollar bills with King George's kindly countenance on them.

They went part way up the river by foot and part way by following a network of small lakes by Indian dugout. 

The Indian had several dugouts concealed at strategic points along the route, where there were stretches of land of any considerable length between bodies of water, which was a decided improvement to having a single canoe to be carried in tedious portages overland. 

They were three days in travelling a distance of only about twenty-five miles to the headwaters of the Dease in this manner. From then on they spent two days passing through a stretch of swampy, little known territory, appropriately called the Dismal lakes area, which consisted of a maze of small, insect-infested lakes and swamps; then spent another two days following an unnamed creek, by canoe and portaging, down to the Coppermine river.

XXI 

The Coppermine river is another great, unsung feature of the Northwestern Canada that, for actual length and area drained, ranks among the large rivers of North America. Tom found the people living in this region the most interesting study in humanity he had ever come in contact with. Little of an authentic nature has ever been written about the Coppermine Eskimo. To the average man, Eskimos in general are a squat, pug-nosed people whose whole life consists of trapping, fishing, paddling a queer-looking boat made of hides and living in an ice igloo. The squat, pug-nosed part of the picture is the only part that fits fact in the case of the Coppermine river 

Eskimo, however.

Tom found these people navigating their river in splendid, forty-foot gas boats equipped with twin Diesel engines and living in frame houses, having electric lighting, radios and other modern conveniences ahead of the ordinary Prairie farm house. He came upon one point on the river where sixty or seventy of these large gas boats were gathered at an Eskimo settlement, representing a very large investment of capital. He could not have been much more surprised by finding a tennis court at the North Pole than this display of Western civilization so far North in the middle of what appeared like an inaccessible wilderness. He decided to take time out from his mission to discover what lay behind it all.

He found out that the mysterious fleet of gas boats originally came into the country from the North instead of South and had been brought around the Alaska peninsula by the Hudson's Bay Company and traded to the Eskimos for furs. Each boat had originally had a price tag on it for $23,000. Not that the $23,000 meant anything, for 190

cash does not actually enter into transactions between the "Company" and the Eskimos. The Hudson's Bay Company gives credit to the Eskimo for the furs he turns in and with such credit as he builds up he can purchase what he likes from the company stock. 

Before the depression, the Eskimo of the Coppermine river had built up a large fur credit with the company; hence the number of $23,000 gas boats on the Coppermine river and the settlements of good frame houses equipped with electric light. It is an interesting fact that the average Eskimo of the Coppermine, instead of being a rather pathetic, dispossessed barbarian, is several times wealthier in the goods he possesses than the average citizen of a Canadian or American city.

The crude oil for their fleet of large gas boats is brought around the Alaska peninsula once a year in a tank steamer and delivered at a storage plant on the arctic coastal shores. 

The electric light in their frame bungalows is the outcome of Eskimo initiative coupled with the march of civilization. The Eskimo comes of similar racial stock to the 

Nipponese, originally, and possesses the typical Nippon faculty of being able to absorb anything new in the twinkling of an eye and improve upon it and adapt it to his own use. It is believed he migrated to the North American continent centuries before the white man set foot on it, across the straits between Siberia and Alaska.

It is said that in recent years an Eskimo, on taking a trip down as far South as the City of Edmonton (which is the northernmost metropolis of the continent) from the Coppermine district was greatly impressed with the marvels of electric lighting. He immediately went about ascertaining what lay behind the scientific miracle and returned North with a supply of electrical fixtures and a demand on the Hudson's Bay Company to keep such things in stock from then on. Now, for three months in the year the Coppermine Eskimo goes without electric light, then, when the winter months set in, his boat is hauled out of the river high and dry before the freeze-over and its Diesel engines taken out and installed in a back shed to generate electric power for his bungalow.

The Eskimo is one of the best natural mechanics and man-around-machines in the world. He has an uncanny sense and "feel" of a motor that a white man, with ears and senses not sharpened by a life in the wilderness, can only surpass with a far superior theoretical training. Their large gas boats are always in perfect running order and one rarely, if ever, hears of an Eskimo being caught with engine trouble of any kind.

Tom found that the Eskimo had a great respect for the red coat of the Royal Canadian Mounted Policeman. On visiting each settlement he was besieged with outbursts of the Eskimo tongue, interspersed with English and chinook from all sides. At one such settlement he was conducted to a leading Eskimo of the community who spoke good English. He was a man under thirty, not much older than himself, in fact, and held a high place in his village largely on the strength of his ability to speak the English language well. He spoke in a well-modulated, unaffected manner and enunciated most of his words nicely. Tom asked him how he came to speak English so well. "I worked roun' the Hu'son's Bay Company trading pos' for some time," he answered, "an' picked up a lil' English from the white boss an' his family. I learn to speak English first when I use' to play with the chil'ren of the ol' boss who was here some years ago. He great man-go back down Sou"." "Did he go back to one of the big cities?" "Yes, I tank it be Toron'o." "Did you say Toronto?" "Yes, Toron'o."

Tom ascertained the Eskimo side of their dispute with the Indians from the young Eskimo. It seemed that the 

Indians had ventured too high up the river, in their opinion, and were fishing in waters that had traditionally been regarded by the Coppermine Eskimo as part of his heritage. Tom could sense an attitude of silent hostility and contempt on the part of the Eskimos towards his Indian guide.

The Eskimos and Indians of the North are like oil and water, or Frenchmen and Germans, as far as mixing with

each other amicably is concerned. The Eskimo regards the Indian as an inferior; possibly not without some justification, since over ninety percent of the Northern Indians of the MacKenzie basin are either syphilitic or tubercular. 

Both of these diseases were originally introduced to the Eskimo and Indian of these regions by the white missionary and trader, along with the Bible and firewater, but for some reasons have ravished the latter more than the former. Biologically, it would seem that the Indian's robust body had never built up an immunity to these diseases, since they had been unknown to his ancestors, unlike the white man or Eskimo who came from other continents originally, and, when they visited him, they ravished his race like a forest fire sweeping through dry, virgin, standing timber.

Tom went down the river some distance in one of the big Eskimo gas boats, accompanied by his Indian guide and four Eskimos, until they reached a settlement where Indians were fishing the waters in their dug-outs and smaller gas boats alongside Eskimos in the bigger craft, while at intervals along the shore small groups of either stood in sullen conclave.

He discussed the situation with a group of Indian and Eskimo chiefs, about equal in numbers and, while he was talking, those of them who understood English acted as interpreters for the remainder in undertones. He was successful in obtaining a compromise agreement from both sides, in which the Indians undertook to go back down the river a certain distance, while the Eskimos, in their turn, agreed not to fish below a certain point or otherwise encroach upon the Indians.

It was an informal, verbal agreement, but as such was as binding in the sub-arctic as one defaced with signatures and involved phraseology and loaded down with seals in so-called, higher civilization; more so, in fact, if one considers such noble documents as the Briand-Kellogg and Locarno peace pacts.

Tom was proud of the achievement, if it could be considered as such and had no hesitation in so regarding it. 

It put him in mind of a story he had had read to him by

his mother, as a small boy, about a mountie who rode into a camp of red-skins alone and placed the tribal chief under arrest before the eyes of his own followers; and the case of Cecil Rhodes when he performed a similar feat with a handful of companions on a South African tribe of natives, with the notable addition that he executed the malefactors on the spot in full view of their relatives and friends.

He found the Eskimo code of morality as regards their wives very much as Patenaude had represented it, with the exception that an Eskimo wasn't necessarily insulted, as the French-Canadian had claimed, if the offer were turned down. On the contrary, he suspected that he might have lost the Force considerable prestige in their eyes had he accepted any of the several offers he received after returning to the main Eskimo settlement of the many boats and electric lights; a conquering hero and wise arbitrator in their eyes.

Like Great Bear lake, the Coppermine river country has never been accurately surveyed. It was originally mapped in an informal, approximate manner over a century ago by explorers and the position of the river and its tributaries as shown in maps and atlases today is indirectly derived from this ancient data obtained under conditions of indescribable handicaps, hardship and suffering by these pioneers.

Among the early expeditions that proved most fruitful in this respect was that conducted by Sir John Franklin, accompanied by Sir John Richardson, George Back and Robert Hood in 1820-1821. This small party traversed and mapped a huge area from Great Slave lake up to the Arctic ocean in the brief space of two years, travelling by dog team and living, of necessity, entirely off the country. The same work as they did would rank as a great achievement today, even when carried out with the added use of the aeroplane and improved instruments; but with the facilities they had at their command it was prodigious. Their amazing sub-arctic treks have only been approached in recent decades by those of Hornby, eastward from the Coppermine river over the wastes of the Lauren-

tian plateau to Hudson's Bay. More recent geodetic surveys of portions covered by these early explorers have as yet revealed few gross errors in their work. 

Tom's return trip to the R.C.M.P. detachment was uneventful, except for a week spent in torment from black flies and mosquitoes. These insects teem in millions about the shallow lakes and swamp areas of the Great Bear district, making mosquito netting for sleeping under at night almost as much of a necessity as food in the country, during the brief summer. 

Shortly after his return to the detachment he and Patenaude took out the police runabout, which was equipped with an outboard motor, and travelled to a settlement on the lake where planes frequently arrived from Edmonton and points south with supplies and mail for the mine prospects of the district. A plane had arrived that day and had brought some mail for the Mounted Police detachment. Among the letters was one for Tom from 

Mary. He tore it open and read it slowly, like a man on short rations chewing his food, with a yearning in his heart akin to sickness. 

Dearest Tom: Calgary, August, 1933. 

I am forced to use pencil for this letter. Nicki has escaped with the only pen I own in one of his absent-minded moods. I believe he has a delicate nerve operation to perform in a day or so, which may mean a lot to his reputation, and just anticipating it seems to exhaust all his reserve nervous and mental energy. 

Yesterday, Millie Basil, a fellow high school teacher, and I went for a long, long walk together. It was a baking hot day and we lay down on our backs for a while in the shade of a certain big tree and imagined things. Millie imagined that she was on a ship and the noisy black birds were gulls. 

Do I have to tell you where we went? Try and guess.

We went down to an old bridge and later North along a certain river bank. I deliberately sought out a certain

tree away from the path-a big, tall, spreading maple tree, whose leaves rustled high up aloft and formed a nice, cool, shady spot at its foot, for the weather is really hot now. I made the heat an excuse to stop for a while under the tree and it was there that Millie imagined about her ship and gulls.

But I had thoughts of something far more important. 

While I lay there it didn't seem as if you were nearly two thousand miles away from me, but ever so near at handonly a few steps away, in fact. It was a bitter sweet moment. I may never go back there again.

In the evening, Millie, Miss Johnson and Margaret Lindsay (two other school teachers whom I forget if you ever met or not) and I went to see William Powell in a mystery movie which we enjoyed immensely. Tuesday, Millie and I are going to a meeting of the Twentieth Century club.

Don't become too morbid or sentimental, Tom. You have a slight tendency towards both these ends and I don't think the North is really the best place for you, unless you watch yourself, on that account. It's wonderful to know you are (sentimental I mean), but it has its pitfalls and it would worry me a lot if you neglected your complete well-being. 

I will never learn to love you more than I have. You may be sure I think of you many and many times a day. 

You seem very near to me always. At moments when I'm not busy teaching, my mind has a way of almost immediately wandering back to you. This has a way of happening even in the middle of a hand of bridge and similar moments. The other night I bid a little slam and went down four, doubled and vulnerable, when I should have made it. My prestige as a bridge player has slipped considerably lately, I'm afraid. But what is bridge or dancing or anything, for that matter, compared to us two. 

I know that is the way you feel, too, and the thought makes me happy. 

With much love, Mary.

XXII 

'IF WINTER COMES... 

Patenaude and Tom did not attempt to return to the detachment that same day as it was getting late. They put up at the plane landing base for the night, instead, and started back the following morning. Tom seized the opportunity to pen a reply to Mary's letter to catch the next plane out.

He did so by the light from an oil lamp which seemed to attract all the insects in the neighborhood in droves. He was sufficiently preoccupied with his task, however, to partially ignore them-in fact, to the extent of fourteen pages of ordinary writing paper. He also penned a twopage note to his mother; then checked up on the fresh bumps on his face, hands and ankles and decided to let it go at that.

His long letter to Mary painted Great Bear lake, with its bleak landscape and few, low mountains devoid of trees, as a land of beauty and interest, rivalling the coast of British Columbia or the approach to the Rockies on the Alberta side for scenic grandeur. He made considerable mention of the marvellous clearness and loveliness of the deep, light blue water of Great Bear lake, which actually required no exaggeration on his part, and the surprising (to him) intelligence of the Coppermine Eskimos and their great show of personal wealth acquired in trading with the Hudson's Bay Company.

About a month later, when the weather began to get cooler and they passed into a cycle of long nights and short days, with but little sun, he made a similar trip with Symes for the same purpose.

Symes seemed to be in a congenial mood during the trip and Tom concluded he must have arrived at the rather obvious conclusion that two years isolated on a northern 197

R.C.M.P. detachment, with only two other men to talk to and work with most of the time, is a big enough trial, without making it worse by holding a standing grudge with one of them. Symes' idea of friendship, however, was a sort of forced, synthetic good-fellowship and familiarity, which consisted largely of telling stale and smutty stories and jokes and reminiscing on uninteresting events in England and out in India. However, on this occasion he seemed in a reflective mood and began to really unburden himself. "Yer know, Tommy," Symes reflected, above the purr of their outboard motor, "I likes yer in some ways. Yer're clean-cut and 'andy in a lot of things. But Lord, man, yer've never been h'up against it like I 'ave. We don't always speak the same language, we don't. I'm a Londoner. Born in th' bloomin' London slums; weaned in 'em; dwagged up in 'em. God, man, you should see the ghastly h'ugliness of th' place. This countwy is 'eaven compared to over there for th' likes o' me. The next time 

I talks like h'England was th' only bloomin' place to live, yer can just check me h'up, Tommy. "Over there, I've h'eaten left over food out'a ash cans; slept in a box, th' size of a coffin, at tuppence or a pennya'penny a cwack 'an been glad of it. Yes, sir, the likes o' me over there 'aven't sometimes even th' cost of a bed, so we goes down in 'em places where they 'as boxes th' size of coffins, laid out on floors and on shelves. An', Tommy, I've slept next a Chinaman with syphillis an' 'ad an 'Indu with some kinda lepwosy step wight over my box one night, down Lime'ouse way. "They likes to boast about men like me, over there, oo've come up fwom the slums an' got a title an' become 'is Lordship, or 'is h'Excellency, or Sir Somebody-th'-other. 

Makes 'em feel democwatic like-but we ain't nufin' to boast about an' they knows it. They just likes to pwetend they're democwatic over there, like the United States. But they ain't. There ain't no democwacy over there—just bloomin' class distinctions. Don't I know it."

Symes became suddenly silent again and glanced down meditatively at the clear, clean water swishing by their

"IF WINTER COMES..." 

199 boat. Tom's eyes flashed a pitying glance at him: for that one moment, he understood the man, by getting a glimpse of the terrible handicaps he must have surmounted and why he was intriguing and mean of spirit—and almost respected him. It was the nearest he ever came to liking Symes.

When they arrived at the seaplane landing base, the plane had just landed and was still taxiing up to its moorings. The roar of its engines suddenly ceased and it was quickly secured by ropes to a buoy and a small pontoon pier, used for landing its cargo and passengers. Before the mail and freight were unloaded two passengers, a man and woman, alighted on the pontoon structure and proceeded up to dry land. The woman, a tall blonde with bobbed, shimmering golden hair, suddenly caught sight of Symes and Tom in their scarlet tunics. She nudged her companion and they both smiled and strolled up to them. "We're newcomers up here," the woman said boldly, tossing her hair in a shimmering, golden mass. "I wonder if it's all right to trust ourselves to the care of two members of the gallant Royal Mounted temporarily?" "Quite all right," Tom said, embarrassed as much at the woman's looks as with her apparent stupidity-for she was undoubtedly beautiful in a standardized, Hollywood way. "You're Americans, are you not?" "Yes," the man put in, "we come from New Haven, Connecticut. This is my wife, Mrs. Franklin Elliott." "How do you do, M'am." 

The man stroked a light blond moustache and pointed chin and regarded them with a pair of friendly blue eyes. "We're going to start a small general store and restaurant up here," he informed them. "Just as an adventure, of course," the woman explained. "We've only been married a short time and this is going to be a sort of honeymoon. We're not business people." "I see," Tom nodded. He felt like adding, "It's obvious you're not," but said instead, "This is Corporal Symes of the Royal Mounted; and my name's Shields-Constable Shields."

"Glad to know you, gentlemen." "Welcome to the gweat land of womance and mosquitoes," Symes said gallantly, shaking both Mr. and Mrs. 

Elliott's hands. He held Mrs. Elliott's longer than her husband's and looked at her in a way which made Tom angry.

After they had given the Elliotts some directions and advice and collected their mail, Tom said to his superior: "Look here, Phil, you oughtn't to look at a woman the way you looked at Mrs. Elliott. You're a policeman; and she's a respectable married woman. "Wespectable or not, 'ere's 'oping she sticks awound for a while." 

Tom reddened. "You'd better change your mind, Phil," he advised. "That type of American girl likes to be as full of allure and seduction as a Hollywood screen star, without ever coming across. It's just a game with them seeing how close they can come to the old flame without getting singed by it. They're like a bull fighter with the bull." "This bull ain't bein' thwown," Symes said defiantly. "You never can tell," Tom opined. "Better stick to 

Eskimo women-they're a sure bet." "No woman can wesist the scarlet and gowld of 'is 

Majesty's uniform," Symes insisted patriotically. "Amewican women ain't no diffewent. Their 'usbands gives 'em no womance, because they're too busy chasing the bloomin' Almighty dollar." "And getting it," Tom pondered.

The Elliotts camped in a tent on their arrival at Great Bear lake, but they had more ambitious plans about domiciling in the area. Mrs. Elliott had designed a log bungalow on the lines of some small mountain resort she had stayed at once, as a co-ed, in the Adirondack mountains back in New York State. They hired a gang of 

Indians at five dollars a day each, with a half-breed trapper as overseer, and started erecting a cabin based on the plans a few days after their arrival.

Mrs. Elliott had been an art student for a time at a leading New York school of art and had a fine sense of

"IF WINTER COMES . . .' 

201 99 what constituted beauty and cosiness in a log bungalow. 

Her plans were lacking a trifle in practicality, however, and had to be modified somewhat by the builders to meet the demands of a severe northern winter. Walls which are substantial enough in resisting the cold of a New England winter are as effective as a single layer of shiplap covering in sub-Arctica.

It was a high-speed piece of work to undertake in the region, where building facilities were still not much different from those of the early white pioneers of the country, but the cabin was completed shortly after the southward migration of the caribou in droves-a sight surpassing a huge cattle stampede and, once seen, never to be forgotten -which was by mid-October; just as the winter of 1933- '34 set in in earnest and the lake began to freeze over at the edges and in the smaller bays. Four sets of supplies and provisions, window and door sashing and furniture arrived for the Elliotts by plane and assured them of spending a reasonably comfortable winter in the district. 

The last of the four planes landed on the lake on skis. By then, the land had gone Argentine with the winter snows. 

Tom and Frank Elliott became fast friends. Learning makes the greatest of all fraternities, regardless of language or race the fraternity of sympathy towards man and interchange of ideas. Like Tom, Elliott was a university man. He and Symes went to play bridge with the couple at their bungalow on several occasions, when things were slack around the detachment and it was Patenaude's turn at inlying picket. One aspect of this friendship made Tom uneasy from the start. Mrs. Elliott was terribly attractive to him-and to Symes also. This may have been partly due to the abnormal surroundings they were in. A white man misses contacts with civilized white women in an isolated wilderness, far more than he, himself, often realizes; especially in a frigid climate where his physique is tuned to concert pitch and all his impulses stimulated. An average white woman becomes a pearl of great price in such surroundings; while one possessing the beauty of a Mrs. Elliott assumes the status of a goddess or Helen of Troy.

XXIII 

MAD TRAPPER 

One day a lone figure trudged quietly across frozen expanses down upon the detachment building. They watched him coming for nearly two miles and it was only when he got quite close that they realized he had his arm in an improvised sling and was stumbling on his snowshoes. Tom and Patenaude went out and assisted him into the detachment office for the balance of his journey. 

He turned out to be an Indian trapper whose facial nerves had received such punishment in the cold that he had lost the intricate power (to him) of speaking English. He could only babble in his tribal dialect which only 

Patenaude understood. "He says," Patenaude explained, "that a white trapper, named Hendrickson, has run amok and shot down two other Indians and himself. He says both the others are wounded seriously and that Hendrickson has sworn to kill every Indian and Eskimo in the MacKenzie River country he meets." "As bad as Adolph Hitler and the Jews," Tom reflected. "You and Patenaude dwaw cards to see which one goes out and bwings 'Endwickson in," Symes ordered nervously. "How about you taking part in the draw, too," Tom suggested. "Is your life too valuable to risk since you've been given those stripes?" "These two stwipes of 'is Majesty gives me the pwivelege and authowity to decide such matters," Symes shot back, his little, crafty eyes flashing with indignation. "Since yer don't seem to appweciate my suggestion to dwaw cards for it, yer can bwloody well go an' let Patenaude stay." 99 "Is that a command?" Tom asked. 202

MAD TRAPPER 

203 "Natuwally it's a command," Symes stated. "Trust a cockney Englishman to abuse any authority that's placed in him," Tom let out.

Symes' ears reddened with indignation and the Indian looked at them with amazement, despite his semi-frozen condition. Tom harnessed up the detachment dog team and set out over hard snow for the trap lines and cabin of Hendrickson. It meant spending two nights, or rather eight-hour periods, buried beneath the snow in a sleeping bag.

He set out with his sled well loaded with white fish for the dogs. White fish are the gasoline of the North. 

They usually weigh about a pound each and are kept exposed on the sled and frozen until the dogs' feeding time comes round. This occurs three times a day and synchronizes with the traveller's own feeding time. The fish are thawed out on a fire and thrown to the dogs at the standard rate of two to each dog, while the traveller is cooking his own meal. Tom usually threw in a fish bonus for hard work or good behavior. Barring such bonuses and extra dividends, it was simple arithmetic to calculate that each dog required six pounds of white fish a day; the whole team of five dogs, thirty pounds. In a good day the team might make sixty or seventy miles. A car goes just about that far on thirty pounds of gasoline, Tom reflected, allowing eight-pounds-and-a-half per gallon. 

Funny how Nature seems to set economic limits on everything and how they tend to be all equal in the end. 

Tom found that the dogs get to know when they are going to be fed by a series of reflexes. First, they are brought to a halt and their boss builds a fire. They get to know that the fire means the thawing out of white fish for them and saliva can be detected running freely from their mouths as the flames of the fire leap up. Then when the white fish are produced they yelp and sniff and shudder in ecstasy at the sight and occasionally appear a trifle wistful for fear that some sort of partiality be shown. When the fish have finally thawed the smell of them turns the hungry dogs' excitement into a rhapsody of wagging tails, bobbing, shaking, woolly Alsatian chests and eager,

plaintive snorts. In spite of their hunger, however, a well-trained team will stand around the fire and wait for their master to throw each one his rations, instead of fighting among themselves over individual fish. 

To maintain an efficient team an intelligent lead dog is obsolutely essential. Contrary to popular fiction on the subject, he does not have to be a great, snarling brute who can lick any other dog in the pack. Some of the greatest lead dogs of the North have been little thoroughbred terriers, because the leader's function is to pick the way and set the pace for the other dogs, like a boat's coxswain, and the traces between him and the next dog should be normally loose and pulling little or no weight. 

Occasionally, however, brains and brawn go together in dogs as in men and when a lead dog is like that and can pull as well as lead, so much the better. The R.C.M.P. leader, a big Alsatian called Danny Boy, fell into this category and was one of the most valuable leaders in the district as a result. His cleverest trick was in sensing when one of the other dogs was in an excremental frame and swinging the team out on a crescent so that the sled would not scratch its bottom by passing over the frozen, rock-hard dung. The second dog, Minnie the Bitch, was also a good animal and endowed with sufficient canine intelligence to make a potential leader if anything happened to Danny. She was Danny Boy's mate.

When Tom finally came upon some blazings on trees which defined the Hendrickson trap lines he followed them up cautiously, keeping his eyes open for a possible ambush. 

Mounted policemen had been the victims of men driven mad by the lonely life in the North before his time and he had no ambition to join their immortal numbers. It seems that one feature of an extrovert type gone mad is a hallucination against constituted authority. 

Finally a cabin loomed up ahead of him in the middle of a small clearing. Smoke coiled up from its chimney lazily. Tom's heavy, buffalo-skin coat covered his uniform and he stopped to remove the brass badges from its shoulders and the one from his fur cap. After he had accomplished this he still hesitated to push forward to the

MAD TRAPPER 

205 cabin until he began getting cold from waiting around. 

Finally, a sense of physical misery from standing in the cold, coupled with a certain amount of courage, drove him into the clearing.

He crossed the clearing with his heart in his mouth and, after a moment's hesitation, knocked on the cabin door. 

There was no response from within, so he began to open the door slowly. It creaked badly.

Suddenly, from behind him, there sounded a loud peal of laughter and, jerking round, he found himself facing across the clearing a tall, ruddy-complexioned man-almost a giant-aiming a shotgun at him and standing in the exact spot from whence he had himself entered the clearing with his team but a minute before. "What do you mean by trespassing on my property like this?" the giant boomed in a voice like a fog horn. "Didn't you know I won't stand for any claim jumpers around here ?" "I'm not a claim jumper," Tom protested. "Well stick your hands up anyway, if you value your life."

Tom did value his life and didn't see any sense in not doing as he was told, since he was faced with a shotgun and, even if his revolver had not been well submerged in its leather holster beneath his buffalo-skin overcoat, his right hand was encased in a pig-skin mitten which temporarily placed his trigger finger out of commission. The giant walked across the clearing with slow, dramatic strides, keeping his gun pointed at Tom the whole way; while Tom regarded the two barrels of the gun uneasily lest the man trip and discharge one or both of them accidentally. When he arrived alongside the R.C.M.P. sled he dropped one eye on it quizzically. "R.C.M.P.," he read aloud off the varnished wood of the sled, where it was stenciled in black lettering about an inch high, "well, well, well. So the law has sided with my persecutors, too, has it?" "Is your name Hendrickson?" Tom asked. "Never mind what it is. Just get inside that cabin ahead of me and don't make any false moves, because this

gun's loaded-both barrels. Want me to prove it to you?" "No certainly not; I believe you.'

Tom found himself inside a large, comfortable cabin with one of the finest rough fireplaces at its far end he had ever seen. A large map, about fifteen feet long and a yard high, of the Great Bear lake region adorned one of the walls. It was done on birch bark sewn together in large sections and exhibited a crude, unschooled ingenuity. "That's a nice map," Tom remarked congenially. "Who did it, you?" "Yes I did," the man growled, "but the order about keeping those hands up still goes.' "But for how long? A man can't keep his hands held up indefinitely, because all the blood runs out of them. 

Mine are about half frozen to start with." "All right then-take that forty-five out of its holster, with your fingers on the tip of the butt only and lay it down on this table. Then you can sit down in that chair if you like." "Thanks," Tom said, complying with instructions to the letter, "but must you keep that gun pointing at me all the time?" "Yes," the man replied, "because I know, if I don't, that you'll try to put something over on me. Even the law has joined hands with the persecuting forces against me now and seems determined to keep me as busy as Jesus Christ at the Resurrection. As if I haven't been persecuted enough already, what with Indians and scalliwags and all sorts of things in hiding trying to rob and persecute me. 

I tell you those two Indians had it coming to them; they tried to steal my mine."

He rested the shotgun partly on his hip and partly on the table, pointing straight at Tom and, with one finger constantly on the tripper, proceeded to light an oil lamp with his free hand. He accomplished the difficult feat with a sort of mad, ambidextrous ingenuity that won 

Tom's qualified admiration. "What two Indians are you talking about?" Tom asked.

MAD TRAPPER 

207 "What two Indians, indeed! You know very damned well I mean the two I winged. The law wouldn't give me any protection against them, so I had to protect myself. That is the way in these parts; every man for himself and to hell with the weakling, the Mounted Police notwithstanding. Now they send you along to persecute 

Those damned Indians have more pull with the Government and the Mounted Police than a white man has these days. It means I'll have to kill you, I guess, to protect myself. Take that hand back down to your side or 

I'll give you the works now." me. "There's no sense talking that way," Tom let out with considerable alarm, aware that beads of perspiration collecting on his forehead were not all due to the heat of the cabin and his fur coat. "Look at that, will you," the trapper ejaculated, ignoring his remark and picking up a piece of rock suddenly and tossing it at him, "-free gold!"

The piece of rock hit Tom in the chest as he was too nervous to make an attempt to catch it and rolled to the floor. He reached down and picked it up and made a pretense at examining it carefully, not daring to look at his captor. It was a piece of typical country rock containing small particles of pyrite, popularly known as fool's gold. "Pretty good looking stuff," he lied. "Where did you get it ?" "Wouldn't you like to know. That's what every man, woman and child in this country would like to know, but 

I'm seeing to it that they don't, get me?"

Tom wished that he'd kept his mouth shut and looked about the cabin uneasily. The oil lamp threw out a smokey, yellow light that made queer, grotesque shadows with some traps and other objects hanging from the rafters of the cabin. The trapper suddenly threw off his fur cap and revealed a head of long, wild blond hair. "I gather that you're Carl Hendrickson," Tom ventured uneasily, remembering that he'd put the question before without receiving either an affirmation or denial. "Yes, the one and only Carl Hendrickson," the trapper

responded this time, "who all the North country; including man, bird, beast and plant would like to persecute, but without success. I have risen supreme over them all and the lakes and rocks besides. Yes, I have beaten even 

Nature at its own game and spotted it a handicap besides. 

I am master of myself and the whole MacKenzie river country, even as Mussolini is in Italy. Of the two men, I am the greater, because what has Italy got compared to the furs and minerals of this country and what would 

Mussolini be without his troops? He'd have to go back into the macaroni works. I fight my battles alone and my brain takes the place of whole armies. It is all just a matter of self-control." "Don't you think you could get along even better than 

Napoleon by not shooting at people when you get mad?" 

Tom asked nervously. "No," Hendrickson answered emphatically. "Besides," he added with a vindictive glint in his eye, "who says I get mad at people?—I'm not mad."

His finger tightened perceptibly on the trigger of the gun.

Suddenly a violent snarling and yapping of dogs coming from outside the cabin announced that the R.C.M.P. huskies and Hendrickson's canines had met and were sniffing at the possibilities of a good, honest fight, something always more than a remote possibility where two strange dog teams are concerned. The noise reached a snarling, barking crescendo as they began to mix it in earnest. 

Hendrickson, with the instincts of the North deeply instilled in him, leaped to his feet and made for the door of the cabin, almost as a reflex action. Not until his hand lay on a horizontal, wooden bar bolting it, did he realize his mistake and, turning, found Tom covering him with his rescued Colt .455. "Stop where you are, Hendrickson!" "What about the dogs," the trapper let out in a tone of mingled anger and contempt, "are you going to leave them to kill one another?" "No," Tom answered, "you go ahead and separate

MAD TRAPPER 

209 them. Don't try to escape though, or I'll ram a half-inch slug of lead through you."

Hendrickson took the small beam of wood barring the door out of its position and went out after the dogs. 

Tom watched him as he rushed about in the snow; a blond giant, dealing terrific smacks at the fighting dogs' hides with the piece of wood and converting belligerent, snarling huskies into whimpering curs that straggled off with heads down and tails between their legs into the tall timbers. 

Two of the police dogs were slightly hurt and one of Hendrickson's more seriously so as a result of the fray. 

Tom felt a strange mixture of pity and respect for Hendrickson. Like most big men whom Tom knewand Hendrickson was at least six-foot-four in his stockinged feet-there was something more open and naturally likeable about him than the average man. As he came back towards the cabin door after completing his task as armed peace-maker among the dogs, Tom tossed him a pair of light, steel handcuffs nonchalantly. The giant caught them with a look of surprise on his face. "Put them on, Hendrickson."

There was something flattering to Hendrickson's vanity in being asked to handcuff himself: it was a confession of his physical superiority at close range over a mountie; and an admission that he was capable of recognizing the inevitable like a true man. He hesitated a moment; then snapped the steel bracelets on his wrists with a shrug of bravado. 

Tom's trip back to the R.C.M.P. detachment with

Hendrickson was uneventful. The giant did not only make no attempt to escape, but assisted him materially in picking good camp sites and setting up at them on the way back, despite his handcuffed condition.

They had many tense and interesting conversations before camp fires, which developed ultimately into a mental struggle between Tom, on the one hand, trying to argue the trapper into a more sane and logical outlook on life; 

Hendrickson, on the other, adopting the attitude that there were many different ways of regarding life and that

society had no business domineering over an individual and branding him a madman, merely because his ways of thinking differed from those of the majority. Their acquaintance was too short-lived for either side to drive home its case conclusively.

Tom's heart had reason to lighten as they neared the 

R.C.M.P. detachment. His trek to Hendrickson's cabin had been overshadowed by misgivings about the danger he was running. Now that the danger was past and the patrol almost completed something loomed up as a probability which had been only a remote possibility beforea trip down to Edmonton by plane as Hendrickson's escort.

Of all journeys he had ever wished for, this one ranked first; for Edmonton and Calgary are only about two hundred miles apart. It wouldn't matter whether Mary came up to Edmonton, or he went down to Calgary. He could easily arrange for a few days leave once down there. Any nostalgia he had ever experienced before was as nothing to a yearning he felt at heart to see her again, since reading her last letter. A premonition that he might not make the journey, or only a part of it, caused him to frown darkly and snap his dog whip too often, much to Hendrickson's dismay.

SNOW BATH 

"Good work, Shields," Symes congratulated Tom, after Hendrickson had been safely confined to the detachment cell. "Yer've earned a 'oliday on the stwength of this twip; and yer're goin' to get it, too. The day after Phil left with Hendrickson by aeroplane for Edmonton, Tom paid the Elliotts a visit. He found them both in and their cabin very warm and cosy. Elliott offered him a drink of brandy which he promptly accepted. "You'll have to come and spend Christmas dinner with us," Elliott told him as they sipped the brandy. "We are optimistic enough to have sent down to Edmonton for a couple of big, fat fifteen-pound turkeys, or thereabouts, if we can get 'em. Think they'll make it in the freight compartment of the plane, without smelling too much of gasoline or castor oil?" "Why sure; aeroplane is the cleanest way in the world of shipping food, Frank, because the higher up you go, the less oxygen there is to decompose it." "Let's avoid scientific arguments, Tom, because you always seem to have the edge. Your Canadian schools and universities sure seem to give a fellow a sound grasp of simple fundamentals somehow." "Well then, even avoiding simple stuff like Newton's

He made the statement more as a camouflage of his own subordination and vulnerability to Symes than any heroic defence of his superior. "Just after you left on this last trip to get Hendrickson, Symes was in here to get a bite to eat just in passing through. Fortunately, Mrs. Elliott had ours put away somewhere at the bottom of a trunk and managed to locate it after a little trouble. "When I handed it to him, he had the darned impudence to hint that it might be a fake and that we were an unmarried couple up here on a spree for the advantages of local color and getting away from gossip and scandal mongers at home. I told him straight that he was the most stupid and ignorant policeman I had ever come in contact with, if he didn't know a genuine marriage license when he saw one. He even implied that he was legally entitled to my wife's affections as much as I was. "I marvel at the self-control I showed in not knocking him clean out of that door. I think he wanted me to start something like that. You see, Shields, the trouble is we're Americans and are classed, legally, as aliens in this country of yours. I didn't like to run foul of the law in any way, since, being an alien, I'm not just sure where I stand in a case like that; besides, I understand that the law's pretty darned strict in this country. What was perhaps more important, he returned with a suitcase well-stock with Johnny Walker and Hennessey's. 

The same suitcase also contained some women's garters and lingerie and an envelope full of newspaper clippings proclaiming him the "flying mountie." His photograph had been reproduced in several newspapers, which carried along with it a column or so of comment and surmise on how the Mounties now arrested their man by aeroplane, instead of covering the terrain by the ancient and honorable dog team; for it seemed that Phil had kept some discreet silences as to whom actually did the arresting of Hendrickson during his stay in the Northern metropolis and the press reporters had assumed that the small, silent corporal was being away too modest about it all. One newspaper columnist even went so far as to mention two other little corporals, Napoleon and Hitler, in connection with Symes, inferring, perhaps, that a man need only be a runt in stature and a corporal to possess the makings of a man of destiny.

Tom was torn between extreme mirth and anger at the write-ups. He was disappointed and disillusioned with the newspapers, as he'd always maintained a high opinion of the Canadian press and had been led to believe that that type of journalism was common only in the United States. 

About a week after Phil's return from Edmonton, Tom was going through some routine office correspondence looking for a report on a skull that he and Patenaude had found on a lonely hillock, when he came across the typewritten carbon copy of his own report on the Hendrickson affair. At the bottom of the report Symes, as was customary for the N.C.O. in charge of the detachment, had inserted a paragraph of remarks of his own. 

Tom knew that these remarks were confidential between the N.C.O. in charge of the detachment and the Superintendent in charge of "G," the northern detachment division, at Edmonton. The copy must have got mixed up with some other correspondence by mistake, he reflected, and been left out of the drawer containing confidential matter which Symes kept under lock and key. Obeying a sudden impulse, Tom picked the sheet up and read Phil's remarks: 

"It would appear to me that Constable Shields has, perhaps, drawn on his imagination a trifle when he claims there was some danger attached to the arrest of Carl Hendrickson. Hendrickson is definitely of a docile nature, which is prone to undergo brief emotional eruptions occasionally. These can be checked and kept under control, if watched for carefully. The only part of this case involving any real danger was the long transport of the culprit from this district to Edmonton by aeroplane and 

I felt compelled to assume that risk myself, rather than pass it on to either of my subordinates. "I have the honour to be, Sir, Your obedient servant, (Sgd.) Philip Cliveden Symes."

Tom had barely finished reading the paragraph when Phil entered the office from outdoors. He immediately

217 noticed what Tom was reading and turned rather red, particularly around the ears. "What is that you're weading, Tom?" he asked with a lump in his throat. "I'm reading this swell report of yours. Lovely grammar and all that for a man reared in the slums of London. 

Symes, you've gone far, considering your start in life; but one thing you can't gloss over: you're still a bounder and a guttersnipe and run true to form under your veneer."

Phil's color deepened. "I-er-What d'yer mean by pewusing confidential detachment files, anyow'. Yer knows vewy well that stuff is for yer supewiors only to wead.
