Phil's eyes flashed and, for a moment, he appeared overcome with rage at Tom's bantering and on the verge of striking at him. That would have given Tom the right

to strike back, however, which meant a fight and a showdown between them. Phil wavered a moment as his eyes took in Tom's lithe, athletic build and relaxed, confident smile; then he snatched the sheet from Tom's. fingers and rushed headlong out of the building, tearing it to shreds in his anger. 

The following morning at breakfast neither one spoke to the other; both spoke freely to Patenaude, however. 

Suddenly Tom remarked, without first considering the effect of his words with Patenaude present: "By the way, Corporal Symes, I want to warn you to lay off Frank Elliott's wife in future. Do you get me?"

Phil was taken by surprise and almost dropped a cup of coffee he was lifting to his mouth; but quickly regained his composure. "What the 'ell business is it of yours, meddlin' in my pwivate affairs, Shields," he stormed. "You tell Elliott to mind his own damned business, too, the next time you see 'im; and yer might add, if you think of it, that I wouldn't touch that blonde 'ussy of a wife of 'is with the end of a gweased, forty-foot pole. I can see yer're just imagining dirty things because yer're stuck on 'er yourself, you wat!"

Tom reddened up to the ears and ground his teeth, but remained unhealthily silent. 

After breakfast, they both went out of the front door of the building naked and dove quickly into opposite snow banks to take a morning snow bath. It was thirty below.

This mode of cleansing one's person is one of the healthiest known, but seems quite unbelievable to a person not versed in the ways of the North. The principle is roughly the same as that utilized in the sleeping bag buried beneath snow. The snow, relative to the cold atmosphere, is very warm and acts like a hot tub bath on the naked person immersed under it. The trick lies in choosing a near-by snow bank first; then undressing and dashing into it at top speed from inside the cabin and staying under the snow until one's ablutions are over. The return into the cabin must be as quick as the exit. Even when done prop-

SNOW BATH 219 erly, the recipient of the bath often finishes up in the cabin with one or two major muscles convulsing violently from the cold, while his teeth will invariably be chattering. 

Suddenly, for no clear reason that either of them could recall afterwards, other than their skirmish at the breakfast table and antipathy for one another, they tangled, naked, like two wild animals, in the snow. They fought like a pair of madmen-punching, wrestling, clawing, even biting at one another's flesh, while Danny Boy and the other huskies commenced yapping and barking encouragement at the perplexing but delightful (to them) spectacle. 

The dog's barking brought Patenaude to a window and thence out of the door as he saw what was happening. He plunged through snow up to his waist and pulled, first Symes, then Tom, back into the warmth of the detachment building.

Their flesh was a mixture of blue and bright pink all over and whole muscles in their limbs and bodies convulsed violently. In addition, two fingers on Tom's right hand were frozen down to the second joints and Symes' whole nose had fared likewise. The idea of a snow bath is to keep under the relatively warm snow as much as possible and, in their mutual antipathy, they had both neglected to do this.

Regarded at a sane and impartial distance, the grudge between Tom and Corporal Symes was educational in one respect it exploded the slushy, sentimental "hands across the sea" myth; it demonstrated the fallacy of the assumption that men who speak the same language and come of a common stock (in their case, Anglo-Saxon) should get along together better than with men of different mores and background. As a matter of fact, a common language and mores is more apt to magnify differences, rather than common interests, most of the time. Thus, they both got along far better with Patenaude than with one another: yet Patenaude, born amid the tall timbers of northern Quebec Province, spoke French better than he spoke their language, English; and his ancestors had been the conquered people on the Plains of Abraham, Quebec.

XXV 

CHRISTMAS CHEER 

Tom did not share Christmas turkey and other cheer with the Elliotts, although the birds arrived in good condition by plane and did not smell of either gasoline or castor oil from their marche funebre in its freight compartment. He had to stay in on Christmas day as inlying picket to guard the detachment and be on duty in case a sudden emergency arose.

Actually, it should not have been his turn, according to statistics which both he and Patenaude had kept, and the duty should have fallen to Symes. Patenaude, however, changed his story after a long chat with Phil alone in the office and it evolved that he had to stay in on Christmas eve and Tom on Christmas day. Not that Christmas held much significance from any other day where they were living, excepting that Tom had the invitation to spend 

Christmas with the Elliotts. He sensed that Phil had acted deliberately to prevent his doing this out of spite and jealousy, but kept these feelings to himself so as not to give Phil the satisfaction of knowing he was upset.

He spent most of Christmas day writing letters. These included ones to his mother, father and two brothers and numerous friends on the Coast who had remembered him at Christmas. (He had received more letters and 

Christmas cards than Phil and Maurice put together). He penned a long letter to Paul Brooker thanking him for a present of some current books of fiction and autobiography. Last, but not least, there was one to Mary, in reply to a letter from her thanking him for some Indian moccasin slippers and silver fox furs he had sent her for Christmas.

Phil returned to the detachment early in the evening, slightly drunk and stamping too much snow off his moc- 220

CHRISTMAS CHEER 

221 casined feet onto the office floor. Tom realized he had returned too early to have spent Christmas with anyone in the district and nodded a greeting in response to a hiccupy one from Phil with a feeling of mingled pity and contempt for the man who had gone out alone in cold, blizzardy weather merely to make it impossible for him to be relieved of his inlying picket. 

The first ten weeks of the New Year, 1934, turned out to be bad ones for some scattered groups of Indians living in isolated localities in the Great Bear lakes region. Tom and Patenaude were each called upon to rush supplies to such groups to save them from starving or facing extreme hardships.

Patenaude performed one such mission just after Christmas with a super-human effort that got him to a group of Indians just in the nick of time to save them from starving. The amazing young French-Canadian made a 150-mile trek over an uncharted stretch of wilderness in time that compared well with that made in dog derbies over carefully-selected, measured courses in performing the task. 

Tom also performed two such services in slower time and under less spectacular circumstances than Patenaude. 

On returning from the second of these, he was suffering from hunger, which bordered on the acute himself, having run short of provisions on the return run and encroached, reluctantly, on the dogs' stock of white fish. 

After attending to the dogs' needs he entered the detachment kitchen. Patenaude was out in the woodshed cutting some wood, while Phil was in the detachment office alone. 

In spite of the fact that Phil must have seen him coming in on the trail there was no bacon frying and no coffee pot on the stove. Tom cursed him out loud and strode into the office. Symes was sitting in the comfortable office chair reading a magazine. "Why 'ello, Tom, me boy," he greeted with a transparent joviality, looking up from the magazine. "Your boy, hell," Tom retorted. "Why doesn't it ever strike you to warm up something to eat on the stove when

you see a man coming down the trail, as you damned easily can for a couple of miles?" "I'm too h'occupied with other things on me mind to bother cooking me men's meals for 'em." "Is that so? Well it's time you realized, if that's the way you feel about it, that a hungry man's needs are more important than reading a three-months-old copy of 

MacLean's magazine.”

Phil ignored the remark with a yawn, then questioned good-naturedly: "Guess 'oo I 'eard fwom by the last mail, while yer were away, Tom?"

Tom couldn't guess nor did he care whom Phil had heard from by the last mail and he knew Phil knew he didn't care; but Phil's sudden congeniality and refusal to get ruffled aroused his apprehension and curiosity. "Who was it?" he demanded. "My dear old fwiend and fellow N.C.O., Smokey 'Arris," Phil announced pedantically, "none other than 'e. Just fancy. I'm goin' to wite a letter to 'im by weturn mail and I'm seeing to it that it gets on the next plane out, if one of us 'as to trek thwough a blizzard to make it. 'E never seemed to take a gweat fancy to you or Bwooker or Johnson for some weason. I wonder why it was. 

Want me to send 'im your wegards, Tom?" "Sure, you can tell the old buzzard I hope he goes straight to hell!” "Tom, Tom, tut, tut, tut . . . Such language to use on one's supewiors! If it was anyone else but just you I'd be quite shocked. By the way, there's a letter that came in on the same batch of mail from the bohunk 'ussy of yours down at Calgawy-Mawy Skookumchuk, or whatever 'er name is." "Shut your foul trap, Symes," Tom stormed, snatching a letter, addressed in a familiar hand, from off the top of the office desk. “Another crack like that out of you and 

I'll show you what it's like when a skunk gets kicked around for calling a lady names." "Inferwing that I'm the skunk, of course." "Of course."

CHRISTMAS CHEER 

223 "Wait a minute," Phil warned. "Don't get the h'idea that because I condescended to play with you out in the snow once, that yer can get away with that sort o' thing a second time. Don't forget that I'm the man in charge of this 'ere detachment an' that I cawwy a Colt forty-five awound in me 'olster. If it ever comes to a show-down of me 'avin' to pwotect the pwestige an' honor of the Force by shooting down a dirty mutineer, I 'ave considewable faith in me fwiend, h'Inspector Tweed's influence at Ottawa gettin' me exonerwated, or even gettin' me a pwomotion out of it." "In other words, Corporal Symes, you'd murder a man, if you thought you could get away with it, just as complacently as your friend, Inspector Tweed, will commit a judicial murder to bolster his career and the fictitious prestige of this Force."

Tom tore open the envelope and read Mary's news eagerly. The innocence and feminine touch in her letter were like a soothing, cleansing bath, compared to the life he was leading, and he became momentarily oblivious to his hunger and fatigue and the presence of Symes. 

After feeding and washing, he rolled up in a blanket and started dreaming uneasily. He slept for eleven hours. 

Despite this long sleep, the following morning found him still tired. When one leads a hard, outdoor life approaching that of the wild animals, one must sleep like the animals also that is, long and thoroughly. Tom was sitting in the office the following morning, nursing numerous aches and pains in his whole muscular system-he felt almost like a man drugged, he was so tired-when Phil burst into the office, his eyes wide with excitement. “Tom-I means Shields-get the dog team weady and get pwoperly dwessed wight away. A twapper 'as committed suicide or been murdered up the line. Snap out of it, d' yer 'ear me?" "Yes, I heard you all right," Tom growled. "Giving me the dirty end of the stick again, eh? How lousy will the report be this time that you turn in on my investigation? Why don't you get out on a tough case yourself once in a while, instead of slinking away from the detach-

ment only when there's a soft touch involving an Eskimo, so's you can spend the night with his wife?" "Another wemark like that out of you, Shields," Symes spluttered indignantly, "and I'll lay a charge of h'insolence and h'insubordination against you. Patenaude, you get the dogs weady while Shields gets dwessed. Don't stand there gaping at me like Shields. Yer ain't no bloomin' imbecile. Get busy, d' yer 'er me? Gad," he added as

Patenaude disappeared, "I've never seen nor 'eard such laxity and h'insolence in the Mounted Police before." 

Tom went into the sleeping quarters and kicked a pair of moccasins out from under his bunk. The building resounded with epithets he hurled at Phil during the next few minutes; while his superior sat very stiff and quiet in a chair out in the office, eyeing a photograph of a bad man on the wall coldly and absently, while his ears reddened. 

Tom had anticipated at least two or three days in the detachment to settle his stomach with a few filling meals, catch up in his sleep and rest out some of the stiffness and aches in his muscles. Now, after a night's sleep and three meals in the detachment, he was due to hit the trail again. He lighted a cigarette and began lacing on a moccasin. "Well, Danny Boy, guess we're in for it again," he said to the lead dog as he took the team over from Patenaude. "Sure all the traces are tight, Maurice? Okay. Here's a letter for my girl, Maurice. I want it to go out by the next plane. Look after mailing it for me and see that 

Phil doesn't get it, will you?" "Yes, I will, Tom," Patenaude assured him, “but I think you're unfair toward the corporal. Phil isn't as bad as all that, Tom. The big trouble with him is that he's never had to work for a living like either of us has and thinks he's entitled to sit on his tail all day long and let other people do the work. If he'd only get that idea out of his head he'd be all right I think." "Well anyway, where is this suicide or murder?" Tom asked. "I didn't ask Phil as I want to ignore him as much as I can."

225 "It's one of the Iverson and Johnson pair, Tom. I'm not sure which one. Do you know where their place is?" "I'll say I do. It's a mere hundred-and-fifty miles from here. They all look okay but you'd better check them over at feeding times, because a fox has been in there lately. XXVI 

SUICIDE 

Tom treked all that day over snow-covered, sparse woodlands, muskeg and frozen, shallow lakes. He knew the locality of the Iverson and Johnson trap lines and, once there, knew he would not have to waste too much time finding their cabin. The blazings on trees and, even possibly, tracks in the snow would lead him to it. Near third feeding time for himself and the dogs-which correspond to nightfall—he found himself near a good clump of Arctic spruce projecting prominently above the flat, snow-covered landscape and decided to camp there for the night. 

Accordingly, he built a fire, laid in a supply of fuel from the spruce trees, thawed some white fish for the dogs and cooked himself a caribou steak. With his sleeping bag buried in the snow, he crawled into it to the distant accompaniment of wolf howls. He lay awake for a time listening attentively to make sure that the howls were spaced well apart, for lone wolves are the most cowardly of all northern animals and will not attack a man, but in a pack, a species of mob psychology grips them and they become mad, snarling fiends of the animal kingdom that only a rifle bullet or a strange terror of fire they possess, common to most wild animals, will stop. 

On the morrow he was up and away again early and had made another forty miles by nightfall. The third day out the sky began to cloud over heavily and he realized he was in for a race with a storm to the trappers' cabin. 

Early on the fourth day he came upon some blazed trees. 

Some of the blazings were fairly recent and he surmised he must be in the vicinity of the Iverson and Johnson trap lines. He followed the blazings, while the storm, which had been threatening ominously overhead, suddenly broke. Snow began swirling up off the ground in little 226

SUICIDE 

227 eddies, while the trees swayed slowly and heavily at first; then near and distant landscapes alike became obliterated in an atmosphere of flying snow particles, while gusts of wind swirled and hissed dismally.

Tom kept plodding on uneasily, his eyes peering out from under snow-encrusted eyebrows for further blazings on the sparse Arctic spruce trees, but the way ahead of him had become a white wall of flying snow. Suddenly, one of the dogs let out a yelp of pain, followed by a continuous, plaintive moaning and the team came to an abrupt halt.

He floundered up to the dogs and found 

Minnie lying down sideways in the snow, with Danny

Boy standing over her and licking her nose comfortingly. 

Tom's mittened hands ploughed in the snow for a few seconds and he blasphemed bitterly as they uncovered the metal bit of a bear trap. Both of Minnie's front legs were caught in the trap and badly crushed and broken. "Well you plucky little bitch," he said, stroking Minnie's nose, while only the wind and cold prevented tears from springing to his eyes, "I hate to have to put a gallant little lady out of her misery, but it's sure got to be done." 

His hand fumbled under his fur coat for his revolver holster. On locating it, he drew forth his .45, cocked it and placed its steel muzzle over one of the dog's ears. "Could have been worse," he reflected, as he pulled the trigger, "might have been Danny Boy." 

Minnie stretched out very stiffly in the snow and lay still and quiet, while Danny Boy and another dog howled dismally. Tom readjusted the traces and proceeded on his way more cautiously than before. 

He came upon a row of vertical logs amid the blanket of snow quite unexpectedly and could almost have touched them by stretching out his arm before he was sure they were the upper half of the wall of a cabin half submerged in snow. 

He groped his way around the cabin to a door, which he found tightly fastened, and commenced to pound on it with his fists. To his relief, he could hear something moving inside the place above the noise of the wind and shortly afterwards the door was opened by a tall, scraggy man with stark, staring eyes whom Tom had seen once

paddling a canoe down McTavish bay of Great Bear lake in the summer.

Tom moved his sled so that, with the cabin wall, it afforded a temporary protection for the dogs to huddle between and stepped quickly inside the cabin. The door was quickly closed and bolted behind him by the man and Tom was at once aware of a sickening odor pervading the place. He realized its origin when his eyes rested upon a discolored human body, lying partly uncovered on a bunk. "Looks as though he's been dead quite a while," he commented. "Yes and the heat in this cabin don't improve matters any either," the trapper replied sullenly. "I was going to keep him outside in cold storage, but I understand as how you have to leave a body alone until the Mounted Police have had a look at it." "Yes, that's right," Tom agreed, "but under the circumstances it would have been all right to keep him outside. Is his name Johnson, by the way?" "No, Iverson, officer. My name's Johnson." "I'm sorry," Tom said.

Johnson regarded him with a stark, blank expression as he walked over to the body and went through the motions of a rapid post mortem with a feeling of complete revulsion. 

There was a bullet hole in the dead man's right temple, fringed with black powder stain, suggesting that the wound had been self-inflicted by holding a gun directly against his head and pulling the trigger. Tom had Johnson help him undress the corpse from a fur sleeping robe to examine it for signs of violence, but found none. Johnson pointed out an automatic pistol to Tom, lying on the floor near the bunk. Tom picked it up in a handkerchief and examined it carefully for about a minute; then wrapped it up in the handkerchief and slipped it into the pocket of his fur coat. It was a .32 calibre weapon, containing a discharged shell in its firing chamber. He made the mental observation that the hole in the man's forehead ap-

SUICIDE 

229 proximated closely the diameter of a .32 shell and then turned on Johnson. "What made him do this?" he asked the trapper. "He went nuts," Johnson answered shortly. "How?" "He spent too much time in his own company." "I get it. . . .' "He began getting very queer in the things he said all the time. One time he tells me that he intended following the caribou down South in an airship he was going to make out of old, discarded newspapers and toy balloons inflated with helium gas. He had heard something about them filling a big airship with helium gas down in the States. "Then one day, about six weeks ago, he asked me to shoot him dead while he turned his back towards me. I had half a mind then to trek into the Mounted Police post, but he wouldn't come with me and I was afraid to leave him alone by himself. 'Hard Rock' Sam Grenig was in here several times and knows all about him. Then, a week ago the day before yesterday-that was a Thursday -I come back from a trip round our lines to find him just like he is now, only he was a greyish white color then, instead of black, and didn't stink at all. I knew old 'Hard Rock' was going down into the vicinity of your post, as he often does, so I went over to his layout and told him what had happened and he said he would drop you the news."

Johnson's face had hardly changed its expression all during this strange and perverted narrative. It was far from a tangible, everyday explanation of a homicide, by ordinary, civilized standards, but Tom had become sufficiently inured to the North with its womanless problem and killing loneliness, leading always eventually to madness, to sense that it rang all too coldly and brutally true. "Well I guess it's all right to take him outside and bury him in the snow, Johnson," he told the trapper. "I'll leave it to you to give him a proper burial in the spring." "Should I spend much time making a crucifix for the grave in the spring?" Johnson asked.

The question impressed Tom with its sad, pathetic side, rather than the ludicrous, and he answered with a certain degree of reverence in his voice: "No, you don't need to spend a lot of time making a cross. I'd put up a small one that will keep standing even in a blizzard and soak a little pine tar into it to preserve the wood, like you do with a ski. Suit yourself." 

They carried the body outside and buried it in a snow bank about a hundred feet from the cabin. Tom uttered a brief prayer for the soul of a "womanless man" which he composed out of his own thoughts in the midst of the swirling blizzard, while Johnson put a mark over the spot.

After Tom had attended to his dogs and seen that they were comfortably settled for the night, he turned in with Johnson. The trapper offered to sleep in the dead man's bunk and give Tom his, but Tom refused the offer and slept on the floor instead. He was over tired and fatigued, or he could never have gone to sleep amid the foul, sickening odors of the cabin, and slept soundly. 

He awoke when Johnson shook him the following "morning"-after eight hours sleep-with a disagreeable taste in his mouth and a bad headache. They cooked a meal together, which Tom had little appetite for, ate it in silence and shook hands before parting. 

The blizzard had completely spent itself during the night and the morning was one of the most beautiful Tom had ever seen, despite the bleak landscape. They were in the midst of a cycle in which the sun put in an appearance for a few hours each day at a low angle and there it was glowing over its frozen domains and making them crisp, clear and bright, with the snow attaining a magnificent sparkle that would have exhausted the vocabulary of a poet. For the first time since being up North Tom understood the partial truth in Patenaude's words on the river steamer the previous summer about the North eventually "getting a man." He didn't want it to get him, though, which perhaps made his having Phil to contend with a good thing, after all.

SUICIDE 

231 Tom gauged his approximate course home by compass and then set his abbreviated dog team in motion. A little more than half way through the day, his system purged of the foulness of the trapper's cabin and functioning normally again, he developed a fierce hunger. He had arrived at a bleak point above one of the small, frozen lakes of the area, from which most of the snow had been blown to lower levels by the blizzard. There was an inviting clump of Arctic spruce nearby that offered fuel and a potential shelter in the event of another storm. He decided to stop there and build a fire and cook himself and the dogs a meal.

Accordingly, he located a small ridge of boulders and glacial drift near the spruce trees, from which the snow had been driven in the storm, and built a stone fireplace above the harder snow. He then built a good fire in it and laid in a supply of fuel, grilled himself a carbou steak in its flames and thawed out the usual quota of white fish for the dogs.

The atmosphere was crisp and invigorating and the temperature not much colder than a bare ten or fifteen degrees below zero. He felt slightly languid and pensive as he lay comfortably on the sled, his feet turned towards the fire, which registered the passage of time by gently and slowly sinking in the hard snow. He threw white fish to the dogs as he ate himself and gazed thoughtfully into the shooting flames.

The charred stones of his fireplace suddenly became symbolic and took on a meaning as he gazed. Two of them suggested Mary and him. They were separated by a flat slab of granite that had once drifted on a glacier, even as he had drifted through life. Perhaps it represented the northern existence which had just begun to "get" him, as it had "got" other men; perhaps merely life and the force of circumstances in general. 

Suddenly he sprang to his feet, his day-dream shattered. 

There were grey and white streaks appearing before his eyes, running down and across the stones of his fireplace. 

Breathlessly, he disengaged one stone at a time from the fireplace and let them cool; then examined them care-

fully. They contained a soft, black, metallic substance that showed white and silvery in streaks, where the flames of the fire had licked out against it. He tested the metal with his pocket knife and found it to be soft and ductile. 

His university training in minerals left no doubt in his mind as to what the substance was. It must be native, wire silver. He took a deep, healthy breath of exultation. 

He spent the remainder of the day prospecting around the neighborhood and uncovering small outcroppings of the rock which contained the native silver. He also uncovered considerable outcroppings of a similar rock containing a bluish-grey, metallic substance which he took for either molybdenite or grey copper. He camped by his fireplace for the night.

On the morrow he arose early and put up the requisite "legal posts" of a mineral claim by cutting down small Arctic spruces about four feet above the level of the snow at approximately the correct locations for the claim and squaring their stumps to "legal post" dimensions with his axe. He decided, when he got back to Great Bear lake, to obtain several proxies from acquaintances to enable him to stake further claims in the vicinity that would give him a property approaching in size the Eldorado. 

His claim staked, he left the locality at around noon, after putting several pieces of selected rock in his sleeping bag roll and making a number of cross-blazings on trees that would facilitate finding his way back to the spot. 

The weather was fine and clear for the remainder of the journey and he arrived back at the R.C.M.P. Post two days later from his 300-mile trek.

XXVII 

LAKE TROUT 

For five weeks following the Iverson suicide patrol things hung fire in Tom Shields' career and he ate and slept well and put on nearly ten pounds in weight. His mineral claim gave him a new interest in life that pushed his feud with Symes well into the background for the time being. They spoke to one another quite freelyeven congenially-on topics not related to themselves; and listened in, along with Patenaude, to the broadcasts of the Stanley Cup hockey play-offs coming in over the radio from "down East." These, Symes could not help but compare unfavorably to the Soccer play-offs over in England, both as to the game and deportment of the crowds attending them.

Tom had his mineral claim recorded, shipped his rock samples by plane to an assayer he knew in Vancouver, and made two uneventful patrols out of the detachment on duty. During these patrols, he made the acquaintance of several old time prospectors of the North country and obtained permission from them to stake claims for himself on their free miner's licenses in return for granting them the same favour. This practise is known as exchanging proxies, or, simply, "staking by proxy." Such transactions were invariably pledged to secrecy over a hot rum toddy or brandy.

One of the men Tom made such an agreement with was known as Hard Rock Sam Grenig-the same man who had brought the news of Iverson's suicide into the detachment. Sam was a seventy-year old, or maybe older, trapper and prospector, who still showed as much signs of life and activity as most men a third his age. He had personally known most of the leading explorers, geologists and adventurers who had visited the region over four 233

or five decades, including the great, but eccentric, Hornby. 

He called Hornby the "trekking and exploring fool" and styled him "the Jack Dempsey and John L. Sullivan combined of all explorers." He regarded Hornby's trek by dog team from Great Bear lakes to Hudson's Bay and back, in which he mapped and explored a vaster area of Northern Canada single-handed than had been covered at the Poles by all the well-financed and publicized polar expeditions over several decades, as the greatest feat ever accomplished in the North. Nevertheless, he looked on 

Hornby as being “a stark, staring madman and fiend” on the debit side of the ledger and labelled the last stand of the Englishman, in which he took two inexperienced, English nephews of his on a dangerous, badly-provisioned expedition that invited disaster from the start, as “a crime against two fine, enthusiastic, but inexperienced, lads." 

Their three bodies had been found months too late by a Mounted Police rescue patrol on account of "Hornby being too damned conceited and pig-headed to keep the Mounties properly informed of his intended movements," according to Sam.

The men whom Tom exchanged proxies with kept their words with him about keeping their agreements a secret, but Tom could tell that his unaccounted-for visits to certain of their cabins had aroused Phil's curiosity and suspicion. One afternoon when he returned to the detachment from a short ski patrol, he found Grenig having a talk with Phil in the office. It seems that old "Hard 

Rock" had dropped in to have a chat with Maurice about trapping and trading permits and one thing and another and, finding Patenaude out, had tried to elicit the same information from Symes without success. "I tell you, Corporal Symes," he was telling Phil, as Tom entered the office, "lake trout have teeth, only they shed 'em at some favored spot downstream, so's you never catch 'em with 'em in; but they have 'em just th' same. 

I've found nests of their teeth, whole nests of 'em, time and time again-thousands of 'em in a pile, like old, discarded false teeth. Them's the junk piles of the animal kingdom, I tell you."

LAKE TROUT 

235 "Don't waste my time with bloody cwap like that, Gwenig," Phil told him. "You make me tired and set me 'a thinking you're going out of yer mind, like Hendwickson or Iverson. I'll find out what you want to know fwom our 'G' Division at Edmonton by air mail. Call in again in about a month or six weeks' time and I'll be able to let you know all about it then. "All right, thanks, Corporal."

Tom went outside the building, ostensibly to take a look at the thermometer, as an excuse to follow Sam as he went away. "You know, Constable Shields," Grenig confided, "I don't know what's been coming over the Mounted Police these last few years. The mounties I used to know in the old days had a sense of humor and could appreciate a joke when it was told 'em and go a man one better every time, no matter how tall a story he told 'em. Corporal Mel 

O'Brien was a trifle pig-headed an' stuck up an' fussy, but he was human once he got a good slug of rye or scotch in him. Gin didn't go so well in his case. 

This man,

Symes, though; well he ain't just human or something in my estimation, that's all. He can't see the funny side to anything. I told him a perfectly good one about lake trout being born with teeth and shedding 'em in the spring and he ups an' tells me I'm crazy. Why, hell, I know damned well that lake trout never have teeth that they shed anywhere, but I was jus' trying to make conversation, that was all. "The only idea you young bloods have of conversation these days is about what's on the radio an' what people are doing down South, damn it all. It's enough to make a man weep, it is. Why don't you try to be like us real 

Northerners? You know," he added with a wink, "Symes wanted to know what you were talking to me about down at the aeroplane base last week when we exchanged proxies and I told him we were discussing my theory about lake trout and their teeth. That's what led up to the argument."

One day when Tom was busy cutting firewood behind their woodshed, Patenaude, who had been down to the aeroplane base, brought him a registered letter. It contained the assays on the samples of rock he had sent down to Vancouver. The sample containing the wire silver ran 

2060 ounces to the ton; worth $618 to the ton, with silver at 30 cents per ounce. The other samples showed small values in silver, uranium oxide and molybdenum which may, or may not, have been of commercial value. Tom realized that the latter were the important results as there probably wasn't much of the wire silver around. He wasn't disappointed, however. Even an average mineral claim in that country brought $500 to $1000 cash at that time and that was sufficient, added to what he had already saved, to enable him to finish his engineering course at university and get married. He was content to let other matters wait.

He experienced an urge to play on Symes' avarice by sauntering into the office and waving the assay sheet triumphantly and tantalizingly under Phil's nose, but he overcame the urge. He would stake other claims in the locality as he originally intended before letting anyone know about it. The opportunity for doing so would be sure to present itself before the heavy thaws set in.

That night he sewed the sheet of paper into the pillow on his bunk while Phil and Maurice were engaged in a game of cribbage out in the kitchen, alongside the stove. 

The following afternoon he was left alone in the office for two or three hours and seized the opportunity to type out two business letters to the managing directors of two leading Canadian mining concerns; one located in Eastern 

Canada, the other in Britsh Columbia, giving the assays and approximate location of his mineral discovery and enquiring if their firms would be interested in it financially. 

He got the letters off two days later on making a ski patrol to the aeroplane base.

On returning to the Post, he threw himself down on his bunk wearily to rest up for a short time and immediately noticed that some feathers came floating loose from his

LAKE TROUT 

237 pillow. He glanced at the slit he had opened up when he hid the assay sheet and immediately detected fresh stitching, which was not his own, along the seam. His fingers ran over the pillow and located a sheet of paper of about the same size and crispness as the assay sheet. It was in a different spot to where he had placed it originally, however.

Puzzled, he took out his pocket knife intent on slitting open the pillow again, when he instinctively glanced round him at the doorway leading from the sleeping quarters into the office. Standing there, framed in the doorway and regarding him with a smirk across his features, was 

Corporal Symes. 

"Can I 'elp yer, Tommy?" he questioned. "Can you hell..." Tom growled. "What're you flitting around for, like a drunken angel lost in one of your London fogs?" "Just cuwious, Tommy, just cuwious." I discovered the property when I was returning from the patrol on the Iverson suicide case, which you hoisted on me because you wanted to dodge out of it yourself.”

As far as your brains and important connections back East go, I don't rate them as highly as you do-in fact, on a four-star basis, I don't rate them half a star; and, as far as Patenaude goes, I'd a damned sight sooner give him an interest in my mineral claim than you, any day."

239 "Well, if that's the way yer feel about it, yer'd better watch yer step, Tommy, me boy," Symes threatened. We all makes mistakes in the 'eat of the moment an' sometimes lets the personal side of us wide wough-shod over our better judgment." "Personal side be damned!" Tom stormed. "That's just 'ow I puts it, Tom."

For two or three days after their argument, Tom and Phil refused to speak to one another, although both spoke a good deal to Patenaude in one another's presence. Perhaps, too much, for Patenaude was getting tired of their squabbles and constantly acting as a sort of go-between

or shock absorber between them. He began getting peevish and his nerves began showing signs of wear, too. 

Then a little incident occurred which put them all back in a good humor again for a time. They had been plagued with rats up in the storage attic getting into their food supplies, particularly the flour and sugar, and fouling them. Rat traps hadn't proven entirely satisfactory in curbing the plague, so Phil had written a letter down to 

Edmonton requesting that Headquarters dispatch them a pair of good, strong tomcats to take care of the situation. 

One day one of the "Sigs" arrived at the Post on skis with an R.C.C.S. radiogram that he had trekked with all the way from the militia wireless station, the text of which ran: 

Cpl. P. C. Symes R.C.M.P.

Am sending the two special rat traps requested in your letter of 6th inst. by plane aaa 

Please have someone meet same.

The tomcats arrived in good condition, little the worse for their 1000-mile plane ride and at once set about demonstrating that the rats in lonely, northern outposts are no tougher than those found elsewhere. 

All three of them became congenial once more, particularly Phil, who behaved as if he'd completely forgotten about Tom's mineral claim and assay sheet. He was freer and more talkative with Tom than at any time since they had known one another. Tom knew that Phil hadn't forgotten and sensed some reason underlying his changed behavior, but could not fathom it.

One evening the three of them were lying on their bunks exchanging amusing anecdotes and smutty stories concerning women, when Phil abruptly asked Tom: "How would you like to go on another patrol up to the 

Coppermine, Tom? There may be need for one shortly." "I'm not fussy about it," Tom replied unenthusiastically. "If I had my way, that's one part of this country

LAKE TROUT 

241 we'd give clean back to the Indians and Eskimos without any strings attached." "You must have a grudge against the Indians and Eskimos, Tom," Patenaude remarked. "I don't know, Maurice, they seem happy enough up there. I guess it's about the same up there right now as here."

Nothing further was said on this topic after that and nothing came up to suggest that such a patrol would be necessary during the next few days. Tom never thought anything more about it. Less than a week later, however, Phil asked him how he would like to make two other patrols, in different directions, that didn't materialize either and it suddenly dawned on him that Phil was testing out his reactions to various suggested patrols, as an electrician might test out telephone lines for a short circuit, in order to get some inkling of the location of his mineral claim. Apparently Phil had not believed him when he told him the truth about the claim having been located during his patrol on the Iverson case. 

Phil started making all patrols to the aeroplane landing base himself, where before they had been about equally divided between the three of them. Coincidentally, Tom stopped receiving mail from then on. At first he hesitated to consider that the responsibility for this rested on Phil's shoulders, despite his grudge against Phil; later, however, he clung to the belief that it did; while ultimately the idea became an obsession with him. Symes made to the landing base. Phil arrived back at the detachment eight or nine hours later than they had expected him on this occasion and explained his tardiness with the statement that he had 'spent a few pleasant, fleeting moments with his old friends the Elliotts en route.'

XXVIII TREK Patenaude had probably been right about Phil, after all, when he expressed the opinion that he wasn't such a bad lot, Tom reflected as he hitched up the traces of the dog team, prior to starting out on his claim-staking patrol the following morning. Maurice had shown himself to be a better natural psychologist than himself it seemed. 

Probably a humble, rural background, such as Patenaude had had, was superior to the more cultural and intellectual one he had been reared in, in making a person conscious of human values.

If Phil had something up his sleeve and had tricked him, then the man was far too deep for him and deserved to get away with whatever he was trying to do, in his estimation. He used a special, short pair of lines to the new dog they were breaking in in Minnie's place, to keep him in alignment with the rest of the team until such time as he acquired the ability of following Danny Boy's lead. 

The Elliott's bungalow was nearly half a day's travel off the best route for him to follow, but he decided to pay them a visit enroute nevertheless, as he considered a whole week was a good two days more than he required to stake the nine mineral claims his proxies entitled him to locate and make the round trip there and back. When he arrived at their place he found Frank Elliott adjusting the traces of a dog team out in front of the bungalow.

The team was a magnificently matched one, all the dogs being of the same breed, size and color and the harness and the sled brand new. "Hello Tom," Elliott greeted him as he came within speaking distance of him. "Hello yourself," Tom answered. "That's the prettiest 245

dog team I've ever seen, Frank," he added admiringly as his team closed the remaining yardage between him and Elliott. "You don't want to let an Indian or Eskimo look too long on it though, or he's liable to go the limit to get it off you. A fine, matched team like that is as big a distinction with them as a Rolls Royce down in civilization. Where did you manage to pick it up?" "I got three of the dogs from the Hudson's Bay Company," Elliott answered, "as well as the sled and harness.

I got the other two from an Indian for a hundred smackers apiece. The whole outfit cost me around seven hundred dollars. Think I was gipped?" "Seven hundred is a little high for an ordinary team,' Tom opined, "but not for an outfit like that. The sky's the limit there. That's the finest-looking team I've seen since I've been up North. You can't get a good dog, even in these times, for much under fifty to seventy-five bucks and you couldn't be much in the hole by averaging around a hundred apiece for those beauties. They certainly show up our team. If it wasn't for Danny Boy, the Mounted 

Police team would be one of the world's worst right now. 

He's about the best lead dog in the North, though, and would make a good team out of any set of growling, yipping mongrels. Isn't that so, Danny Boy? You betcha

He hasn't been quite so good lately since he lost his mate. 

She got smashed up in a bear trap, poor little bitch." "Who was that, Minnie?" "Yes." "She seemed to be a pretty good dog; I remember you telling me about her getting crippled and you having to shoot her. That's one task I don't relish-having to shoot a dog or a horse, especially one I know well." "She was the only other really top-notch dog in our team, worst luck. That new number two dog we got to take her place isn't worth a damn yet; doesn't seem to know what it's all about. By the way, have you taken that team out at all yet?" "Yes, but not very far, Tom. Only a short spin or two over the lake up till now to get the dogs limbered

TREK up and myself accustomed to driving a team. It's a great way of travelling, isn't it?” "I'll say it is.' 

"I'm going out some time this afternoon on a trip that will last over a week, though. I wish you were free to come with me and show me the country, although I guess that wouldn't be so good for me. The main thing I need to do is build up confidence in myself in this country and get used to being out by myself, with only myself to look after my own and my dogs' wants. I need a change from hanging around our bungalow all day long, too, because

I'm away too soft and frankly admit it. I figure on staking a couple of mineral claims on the trip-one for myself and one for Dorothy-if I see a likely looking spot anywhere that isn't already staked. There's only one thing that's worrying me." "What's that?" "Dorothy. I don't know if it'll be safe to leave her alone in the bungalow in this darned country of sex-starved men." "Oh nonsense, Frank, a woman like Missis Elliott can look after herself. Just leave her your thirty-eight revolver; it's no damned use on the trail. These Indian and half-breed trappers that assault white women up here won't try anything on a woman they know is going to put up a fight, especially one with a gun." "I'm not so sure about that, Tom," Frank said a trifle peevishly, "besides, it isn't Indians or half-breeds I'm worrying about, but someone on whom Dorothy wouldn't be at full liberty to use a gun." "Who do you mean?" "I don't know if I should tell you, Tom." "Well why not for goodness sakes?—I'm a policeman and supposed to look after the public's interests in such matters. That's my job.” "Well then, it's Corporal Symes. He was in to see us yesterday-honored us with another of his visits." "Was he er-disagreeable at all?" "No, quite the opposite."

"Well you can forget about it then," Tom almost commanded Frank, with a trace of anger in his voice. "Phil would never do a thing like that. You just leave your thirty-eight with Missis Elliott and set out with a clear conscience and head. The clear head is the more important thing of the two in this country; just as dry powder in your famous American's saying, "Trust in God, but keep your powder dry.' Take it from me: Phil's a real white man in spite of the fact that he was obviously dragged up in the limehouse district of London." "Here's hoping you're right, Tom, for everyone's sake. 

You've about half put my mind at rest at any rate." "Oh baloney, Frank, forget about it. You know, I've always mistrusted Phil, too; in fact, up until only yesterday; but now I know better. Phil is all right once you get to know him. He has an unfortunate, cocky manner about him which gives everyone he comes into contact with a wrong impression of him. I've personally found out that the idea of a first impression of a person being a sound basis to go on is the bunk, just from him alone. 

The real Phil Symes is deep and hard to figure out, that's all." "I hope you're right about that, just for his sake alone,

Tom. How about having a bite to eat and a spot of rum toddy in the shanty, before you go on your way? Any objections? If so please raise the right hand and let out a neigh like a horse." "Absolutely none."

After completing a hearty, mid-day meal and feeding their dogs, Tom and Frank both left the snow-covered Elliott domains at the same time, but travelling in almost opposite directions, while Mrs. Elliott stood out in front of their cabin, in fur coat and cap, waving a farewell to them.

Four or five hours later Tom found himself travelling over the surface of a series of small, frozen lakes and ponds, with narrow necks of land rising from ten to fifteen feet above the level of the flat surfaces in between. 

The going was smooth and fast, since the more recent

TREK snows had been fine and packed well and the sled glided along easily. It was a dismal, monotonous tract of country and set his thoughts moving in a gloomy vein. 

When he started brooding he had a way of prying behind people's actions for a motive and thus placing a purely materialistic interpretation on them. This is something not uncommon in high-strung, mentally-alert persons.

When such temperaments find life pleasant and endurable they tend to rise to exaggerated heights of exultation; but when the shoe is on the other foot, they go to the other extreme. The net result, in the latter instance, is often a species of psychological nightmare in which they interpret everything from a crude, materialistic angle, along the line of Karl Marx or Engels. Sometimes this approach, in which life is stripped down to skeleton dimensions, without possessing either soul or flesh, in an attempt to interpret it, leads ultimately to the truth; but more often than not down a mental blind alley.

Tom began to wonder what, after all, had really motivated Phil in allowing him to go off for a whole week with the detachment dog team to stake mineral claims on his own account. Was it possible that some important officer of the Force was about to pay a flying visit to the Post by plane, which Symes had kept a secret from Patenaude and him, and Phil wanted him out of the way during the visit to make an unfavorable report on him behind his back? Such short visits, which amount to informal inspections, are a regular routine on R.C.M.P. northern detachments. Tom remembered the one made by LaGauze in the summer.

Then something flashed through his mind which ignited his misgivings in another direction. . . . Dorothy Elliott.

Phil's visit to the Elliott bungalow the previous day. . . . Frank's trip with his new dog team that would take him away from his wife for about a week. Had he made himself an unintentional accessory before the fact to a criminal assault on a virtuous woman, by talking Frank out of his suspicions of Phil? Moreover, why had he been so unnecessarily emphatic in defending Phil? He had known Phil too long not to realize he was very cun-

ning and a thorough roué. Why had he been so childish as to allow a temporary relaxation in Phil's oppression of him to completely change a permanent impression, gained over long months of close, personal contacts with him?

Hadn't he acted too impulsively and optimistically, under the stress of a sudden lifting of a psychological burden?

He pulled his team up to a halt. It was almost five o'clock in the afternoon by his watch; not that five o'clock on Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday or the rest of the week meant anything in a northern wilderness, excepting to gauge his probable mileage from the Elliotts' place by.

He commenced to puzzle over the situation in earnest, but found himself in a greater quandary than before; his thoughts about Phil swinging from one extreme to the other and back again to where they had started. One moment, he thought of Phil as a splendid fellow whom all the world, including himself, had underrated, wronged and maligned; the next, as a very low, debauched intriguer possessing an unusual amount of cunning. Neither conception seemed to properly fill the bill somehow. Never had anyone, excepting Lucienne, the little French prostitute at Lethbridge, so thrown his mind into such mental quicksands of uncertainty as Phil. 

He had momentarily overlooked one important point, however: Phil might not, probably did not, know that 

Frank was going away from the bungalow; for the very good reason that Frank, who did not trust him where his wife was concerned, would not have likely told him. 

What, though, if Mrs. Elliott had mentioned it to her husband in Phil's presence? What if she had mentioned it to Phil not in her husband's presence?

Tom dismissed the last thought as quickly as it had come to him but it served to momentarily shatter the complicated picture he was trying to construct out of events that refused to dove-tail together and switched his thoughts abruptly and surprisingly to Mary. His jaw firmed and he snapped his whip over the dogs' heads, bringing them to their feet and starting off once more. 

He must think of Mary and himself first; they were more important to him than Mr. and Mrs. Elliott. If he were

TREK to turn back now merely on the chance that the so-called honor of another man's wife was at stake, he would be risking their own chance of happiness.

Besides, what was the true value of this thing honor which he'd had drilled into him as a boy? His two uncles had been blasted into eternity during the Great 

War, defending what they considered the honor of the

British Empire, which was pledged to a scrap of paper. 

His father, too, had been partially ruined in a business way performing a like service. What had it done for them? 

Where had it got them? Got him?

He was going to do the sensible thing and not let hereditary instincts and prejudices get the better of him. 

He would take a leaf out of Phil's book. Phil was the type of man who invariably gets ahead in the world—the type who looks after his own interests first and crushes and feeds off the smaller, subordinate man, like a superior, predatory beast of the jungle. What was the Anglo-Saxon ideal about the honor and chastity of woman anyway, but a mere myth built up by romantic poets and idealists out of touch with reality? Men and women had never been built for it; there was nothing real about it all. 

He drove his team on and on and on at a terrific clip. 

He lashed his dogs fiercely as thoughts ran through his mind in a mad, kaleidoscopic procession. He may have been imagining bad things about Phil ever since he had been up North, through resentment and envy at Phil being promoted over him. Then, too, there was the possibility that the country had affected him to a small degree mentally, without his realizing it. First thing he knew, he might find himself down to the mental level of 

Hendrickson or even Iverson, the deceased. The last thought brought tears of emotion to his eyes and he whipped his dogs blindly and furiously.

He was suddenly aware that the dogs were yelping plaintively and stumbling in the snow from an unnecessarily severe whipping he was administering them. He was shocked at his own cruelty, because he naturally loved animals, and immediately ceased using the whip. A feeling of remorse towards the dogs put a subconscious check on

his malevolent brooding. Perhaps, he reflected, all brutal men who were cruel to animals were merely men who had reasons to be permanently distracted in the same way as he was at that moment. "Blood is thicker than water." He began repeating this favorite expression of his father's over and over to himself. What an utterly inane saying it was, and yet how potent if one really chose to stop and give it a meaning. 

Perhaps his father, too, had been divided in his mind as to what he should do when he went overseas early in 1915 in answer to his ideals. Despite the sacrifices he had made in going over there for the duration of the Great War, there was a placidness about him in his declining years that bespoke the man at peace with his own conscience. Placidness was a negative quality in youth, but one does not always stay young and there would come a day when he no longer had the inner fires burning to maintain the furious pace of youth and would have to settle down and wrestle with his conscience, too.

He brought his team to a sudden halt again and stood like a statue for several minutes with one leg pressed against the sled supporting his weight. When he finally moved, it was to swing the team around in a wide crescent and commence retracing the trail he had been making since mid-day from the Elliott bungalow. About the same time as Tom turned his dog team back over the fresh tracks made by the R.C.M.P. sled, Maurice

Patenaude returned to the detachment from a short hike on skis, made with the object of shooting a few rabbits for food. He had his usual successful bag of five or six slung over his shoulder as he pushed in the back door of the building and hung them up in the kitchen. He found 

Phil lounging at the office desk with a quart bottle of 

Scotch whiskey lying alongside the typewriter and came upon him somewhat unawares. Phil was talking and laughing to himself, something not uncommon in the North. “Well, Phil, ol' boy," he was saying, lidy a taste of the bold, bad ol' North. “you'll show the 

It'll be a 'ole lot

Good thing I was able to get Tom out of the way. 'E fell for it like a twue sucker-'ook, line an' sinker. I'll put the squeeze on 'im for a fifty-fifty split now after 'e's staked a 'ole lot more claims-the sucker! "Sa-ay, 'oo's that? . . . Oh, it's you, Patenaude. XXIX 

HOSPITAL COT 

Tom's right wrist began aching intolerably as the specially-chartered "Airways" plane droned along, dipping in short, quick drops as it hit air pockets and immediately straightened out again in its run down to Fort McMurray, midway refuelling station on the air route down to Edmonton. A .45 bullet makes a nasty gash in human flesh. 

Luckily, he had knocked Phil's revolver down as low as he could and the bullet had only torn through the superficial tendons and arteries of his right wrist.

It had been a terrific brawl, more intense, primitive and brutal than any that Tom could have imagined himself taking part in. The strange part about it all was that it had been over a woman who did not belong to him, whom he was not even in love with and was protecting for another man; half out of his friendship for that other man, half out of spite for his rival, Phil Symes. His reward for the brutal courage he had shown in eventually overcoming Symes, despite his injured condition, was not the hand of the fair lady, but, instead, a seriously injured arm that throbbed ominously as the plane droned along and a chance thrown away to stake nine valuable mineral claims that might have made him wealthy overnight.

A sergeant of the Force had been rushed up on the plane from Fort McMurray and was detailed to escort 

Phil down to Fort Reliance by dog team, under arrest, from whence he would be taken to Edmonton, perhaps for a court martial when the summer break-up came, charged with attempted rape and administering grievous bodily harm to a fellow member of the Force-unless he could muster sufficient political pressure, or pressure within the Force, on his behalf to have the charge reduced by that 254

HOSPITAL COT 

255 time. Patenaude had been left in charge of the detachment.

Finally, Edmonton and the hospital, where he passed into temporary oblivion as a result of prolonged pain and sudden relief at the strain of the journey being lifted and finding himself in surroundings where he would receive proper medical attention. 

The next day he was wheeled out of a two-bed hospital room, down a long, high-ceilinged corridor, into an elevator and upstairs to the operating room. He was dully, frigidly aware of nurses and attendants wearing masks, flitting quietly about. Then the operating table. . 

Beads of perspiration sprang to his forehead, as he looked up from the operating table into the faces of Inspector LaGauze and Doctor Boving. Despite his nerves having been thoroughly fatigued from the tension they had been subjected to since his fight with Symes, the tension returned as he looked up into the two faces and he sat bolt upright on the operating table. The memory of the hanging of young Macdonald came back to him very vividly. "What's the matter?" Dr. Boving asked him nasally. "I haven't much confidence in you, Doctor Boving,'

Tom frankly admitted. "Would you mind calling in another doctor, please. It would put me a lot more at ease." "Relax, Shields," LaGauze commanded. "Doctor Boving is going to fix that wrist of yours up."

Tom hesitated. The wound was not very serious. Perhaps, even a "horse doctor" like Boving could attend to it. Besides, Rules and Regulations of the Force clearly specified that a member must accept the medical treatment of doctors prescribed by the Force. On the other hand, he'd seen Boving at that hanging— "Are you quite sure you can perform this operation properly?" he asked Dr. Boving.

Boving only spluttered with indignation and evaded the question by demanding that Tom lay his arm out alongside the table. He then opened a bag of surgical instruments and laid them out on a cloth without bothering to sterilize them.

"Aren't you going to sterilize those instruments?" Tom asked him. "No, they're already clean." Boving's eyes had the same dilated, hysterical look about them that Tom had witnessed at the Macdonald hanging; and the look failed to reassure him. "Are you quite sure you know what you're doing, Doctor Boving?" he insisted.

As an answer, Dr. Boving took out a hypodermic instrument and filled it with solution. "Relax, Shields," LaGauze commanded vehemently a second time.

Tom relaxed and Boving jabbed the hypodermic into the wound on his wrist. He then took a pair of scissors from the bag and commenced to cut up Tom's wrist. Suddenly, Tom jerked upright on the table as if a terrific electric shock had passed through one side of him. Blood was spurting from his wrist and Dr. Boving's blotchy features had turned an unhealthy white. "Look the other way, Shields," he ordered; then, turning to LaGauze, he spluttered: "I'm afraid I can't do this operation, after all. Will you go and get Doctor MacKay or Doctor Gay right away, please. They're in the hospital somewhere."

LaGauze complied with the request reluctantly. Presently, he returned with another doctor. "Tut, tut, tut, tut," this other doctor decided, as he looked at Tom's wrist. "Don't tell me you tried this operation with a local, Doctor Boving? Good God, man-is that your incision there?" "Why er-yes. What's wrong?" "Nothing, excepting that you've cut the median nerve and probably crippled this patient for life. That's all."

The next thing Tom knew, Boving had snatched up a gauze cone, clapped it over his face and begun pouring ether on it. Tom sensed he was doing it to stop his hearing any more of the current conversation. "Have a heart, Boving," he heard the newcomer tell 

Dr. Boving, as if through a fog, "you don't want to suffocate the patient, do you?"

HOSPITAL COT 

257 "He can stand it," Boving's voice babbled. "He has a very strong heart. All these mounties are as strong as horses." "I haven't a very strong heart, I-" Tom protested, moving his head sideways. Then oblivion. . . . Belching into a porcelain or enamel thing at his bedside and dizziness. . . Sympathetic, and often pretty, nurses tip-toeing almost noiselessly in and out of his room to see how he was faring and to ask him if he wanted anything. Injections, up to the limit of a quarter of a grain of morphine, to kill most of the burning pain in his arm and enable him to rest. Then a visit from LaGauze. "How are you now, Shields?" LaGauze asked him. "Not so good, sir," Tom groaned. "I've been bringing up brown stuff all the time.' "That's too bad. I hope we don't have to invalid you out of the Force on account of that wrist of yours." "If you do, it's yours and Doctor Boving's fault," Tom told him frankly. "That old horse doctor shouldn't be allowed to touch a sick dog." "It's not as bad as all that," LaGauze said quickly and nervously. "Don't take it so hard." Then he became complacent as Tom remained silent. "Any man who's worth his salt," he sermonized, "doesn't need to let a disability spoil his career. Look at President Roosevelt."

Then his tone changed to one of gravity. "I have a statement here I want you to sign, Shields," he said. "That wound in your wrist wasn't very serious originally, as you've just said. Symes didn't know what he was doing. 

He was drunk. The whole thing must be regarded as purely accidental. If it is ever made public, then it's curtains to the fine reputation of the Force. Shields, I love the old scarlet and gold. It's been my life work and every patriotic Canadian should hold its interests sacred and dear in his heart. Just imagine a member of it being publicly tried and court-martialed for attempted rape and shooting a fellow member of the Force. Think what all those damned reds and C.C.F.'s and Social Creditors would say. Why it would be the end of everything." "What does the statement say?" Tom breathed feebly.

Actually, he didn't care much what it said. His head was terribly dizzy and all he wished for was to be left alone. 

LaGauze put him in mind of a great, predatory bird of prey hovering over a dying man-a swarthy, pudgy-faced vampire. Anything to get rid of him. The memory that LaGauze was connected with the Tweed crowd in some way and of his visit to Great Bear lake kept coming to him vaguely; but thoughts were all too painful to him at that moment. "It says Corporal Symes shooting of you was entirely accidental and had no connection with Missis Elliott," he heard LaGauze say, as if repeating it a second or third time. "All right," Tom groaned. "Give me a pen and show me where to sign. Then do get out of here, because I'm a pretty sick man." "That was a stupid thing of you to do, LaGauze,"

Superintendent Tweed told Inspector LaGauze, as they sat closeted together in the latter's office at Edmonton. 

Tweed was now a newly-promoted superintendent and on his way out from Ottawa to Vancouver-his old stamping grounds-to make an inspection of the Opium and 

Narcotics and Customs and Excise branches of the Force out there. His return to the Coast was, therefore, in the nature of a triumphal home-coming. "There was no need to cripple the man by having that old 'horse doctor' Boving slash him up. Why that old bird couldn't take out a pair of tonsils now without slitting his patient's throat. I've actually seen him put a live man in a coffin because he was told to pronounce him dead." "You've done what?" LaGauze snapped. "It was only a hanging case," Tweed shrugged. "I don't mind doing a thing like that if there's any necessity for it, but there wasn't in this case. It was small and petty of you to do a thing like that. Now he'll be invalided out of the Force and there might be hell to pay. You know how stingy the Force is about paying out any pensions or compensation. Shields will have to put up a fight

HOSPITAL COT 

259 for his compensation and that will drag this whole mess, involving Symes, out into the open. "But what about the statement I got him to sign in the hospital ?" "No good legally. Obtained under duress. It's only use will be to clear Symes providing there isn't a thorough investigation. If there is one, Symes is sunk and you'd better tear the paper up. There's those witnesses from up 

North and then anyone can see that cockney's a bit screwey and not on the level. If he ever has to face a grilling, they'll become curious to know how he ever came to be a corporal on the Force. That'll make them jump on me, since I recommended him for promotion in Vancouver, over the heads of several university men, including Shields. 

On top of the jam I got into at Vancouver about a year ago, that's liable to make them guess why I recommended him. It might easily blow the lid off the whole works." "There might be a way out for us if the thing is handled in the right way," LaGauze put in, pouting his plump cheeks. "How?" "I understand Shields has been to university and can get a degree in engineering if he goes back there for one more year." "Yes, I think that's correct." "Maybe we could make him want to leave the Force and go back and finish that year; so much so, that he'd even be willing to sign a waiver to all compensation from the Force. That would avoid an investigation altogether." "I doubt if he'd be fool enough for that. I know him fairly well. He knows, or will know pretty soon, that he's crippled." "Supposing he doesn't know until he's out of the Force, though? He'll only be entitled to about twenty dollars a month, anyway, and what's twenty dollars a month to a three-hundred-a-month engineer?" "A whole lot, if he's too badly disabled to hold down the three-hundred-a-month job." 

"Well what of it? He needn't know that." "How do you propose going about it?”

"By putting the screws on Doctor Boving for one.

I happen to know it was his fault that Shields is crippled. 

I was in the operating room when it happened, as I've told you. I'll tell him he's got to make Shields believe his hand is going to get all right again, or else—" "I can also throw a mental monkey wrench into the good doctor's thoughts over a certain matter," Tweed reflected. "But supposing Shields won't take Boving's word for it? I know if I were in his shoes I wouldn't. 

"Neither would I, but we can get some other 'horse doctor" to back him up. As a matter of fact, we don't need to get a horse doctor-I know of a first rate man we can get Boving to send him to. He got mixed up in an abortion case about three years ago and I did him a favor and got him off.” "Splendid. Then what?" "I'll have Shields given the dirtiest jobs around the

Division. You know-the sort we usually don't get members of the Force to do and give to wops and bums instead. 

Garbage. Stables. Latrines. Night watchman. By George, that's it-I'll give him the night watchman's job around barracks that we always keep for men convicted of minor offenses, defaulters. It's a mean job for two men. We'll cut it down to a one-man job as a labor-saving device and slip him into it-permanently." "In his injured condition?" "Why sure. He won't be in it long. He'll be hollering for a free discharge from the Force, without compensation, inside of a month. Who wouldn't?"

Dr. Boving kept away from seeing Tom for a week after the operation on his wrist, then stalked into his room, looking very surly, one day and informed him that he was having him moved to barracks in a day or so. "What can I do around barracks with my hand in a cast?" Tom asked him. "Nothing," Boving admitted shortly, "but the government of this country isn't keeping men in hospital for rest cures. Too many mounted policemen swing the lead and the Commissioner at Ottawa knows about it."

HOSPITAL COT 

261 "You aren't suggesting I'm swinging the lead are you?" "No, I didn't say that." "I'm applying for sick leave, so I can convalesce at home and get some proper medical attention," Tom told 

Dr. Boving frankly.

Dr. Boving remained silent and proceeded to remove the stitches from the wound and incision-about a week too soon, Tom learned afterwards-and the entire mass opened up ominously like a sack of wheat being unstitched. "I want to get him out of here," Boving explained to the attending nurse, who looked considerably shocked at his incompetent work. "back to barracks. Here, get me some adhesive tape. I'm going to pull it together with an adhesive bandage. These mounted policemen are as strong as horses, anyway. They can stand things that would finish off an ordinary man. Can't they, Shields?" "There's a limit to what any man can stand," Tom told him, without a trace of humour. "Even a horse couldn't stand butchery like that!"

Boving coughed and spluttered and became silent. His eyes evaded Tom's guiltily. Later in the day, Mary arrived at the hospital unexpectedly. Her summer holidays had commenced and she had driven up from Calgary in her car to see him. She took a flat in Edmonton and stayed in the city while he was convalescing. She spent the remainder of his afternoons with him in hospital, reading and chatting to him.

As soon as he was out of hospital, but with his hand. still in a plaster-of-paris cast, he found himself placed on permanent night guard duty at the Edmonton barracks. 

The duty had always been reserved for defaulters and had been done by two men before. Tom made a protest to the O.C., but it was referred to Inspector LaGauze and availed nothing. He then applied for sick leave, something which is invariably granted in such cases in the Force, unless very special reasons exist for turning it down. His application was referred by the O. C. this time to Dr. 

Boving who refused to recommend it on the grounds that he wished to keep Tom under his care until his hand was out of its cast.

He was told by both Doctor Boving and another doctor, whom he was sent to by the Force, that his injury would not prove to be permanent. It was late in the summer by the time he had done several weeks of the night guard duty and his weight dropped fifteen pounds. 

Then, largely through Mary's efforts, he found a buyer for his lone mineral claim up at Great Bear lake. The buyer was a Calgary man and the price was only $600, but it meant he would be able to return to university and get his engineering degree.

After a long consultation with Mary on the subject, the same afternoon as the deal went through, he decided to apply for a free discharge from the Force, in return for waiving any claim to compensation from them; and to return to university and finish his course before they got married. The latter was Mary's suggestion. 

An answer to his request-which had been strongly recommended by Inspector LaGauze-came back from Ottawa in about two weeks, granting it. Thus, in September, 1934, he found himself a free agent again—invalided from the R.C.M.P. at his own request. Once again, he and Mary parted company; this time with her wishes for his success at university giving him a send-off as he boarded a train home to Vancouver.

XXX 

Tom's contacts at university were refreshingly fine and clean after his life in the R.C.M.P. He felt like a man. bathed in clean, fresh water and sunlight, after spending months in a foul dungeon. Memories of Tweed, the perverted, intriguing Symes, LaGauze, Dr. Boving, Sergeant 

Schlitz and Smokey Harris now seemed grotesque nightmares; for, however much a great police force might be romanticized and however imposing its record, the fact remains that the old adage, "set a thief to catch a thief,” holds true in actual practise and the personal contacts in police work are often nothing to write home about. 

It was, psychologically, like passing from one planet to another and he decided one must base his beliefs in one sort of life or the other; never both together. He wanted to get back into his old ways of living; making friends who were bright and gay and honest, not always engaged in snaring a fellow man or intriguing to gain a petty promotion ahead of a comrade; people who were worthwhile and didn't gauge everything on a basis of dollars and cents only. Above all, he wanted to forget that such people as crooked politicians, intriguing policemen, stool-pigeons, agents provocateur and reactionary martinets existed; and that people like Adolph Keufel were great powers behind the scenes in the community where he lived.

He did surprisingly well in his university work; in fact, his record was unique in applied science for a man who had been out of school for as many years as he had been. 

In addition to his three years spent in the R.C.M.P. he had also been out of school three years between high school and his first year college. Nine out of ten men who try 263

taking university engineering under similar handicaps fail; that is, men without the serious disability he had. But Tom had a great reserve of energy, health and experience, which he was now able to draw upon, while the youths he was competing with, while alert and brilliant, were mostly soft and inexperienced. He had worked during his summer holidays at university previously to put himself through and had gained a wide range of engineering experience: one summer prospecting for minerals for a leading Canadian mining concern; another, working in a mine, mucking and drilling; a third, stripped to the waist working before a copper blast furnace and doing some assay work. He was tall and lithe, keen-eyed and muscular and could "take it." He not only did not fail in his final year, but won a scholarship to help him through.

When his hand and wrist failed to recover properly, he decided that, perhaps, the doctors at Edmonton had been a trifle optimistic in their forecasts. He still expected it to show an eventual recovery, for a major nerve injury must be given a total of eighteen months for its maximum recovery. He never ascribed any base motives to the medical opinions expressed at Edmonton, however. 

During this time, Mary and he regularly found sufficient time to write one another long letters. One day, shortly before his final examinations in the spring, he ran across Paul Brooker in downtown Vancouver. Paul was in civilian clothes and seemed to have lost some of his Mounted Police stiffness and smartness. After a hearty handshake, Tom asked him if he were doing plainclothes work at the C.I.B. or on a holiday. "Neither," Paul answered. "I'm out of the Force for keeps. I'm now purchasing agent for a gold mining syndicate up at Bridge River. 99 Tom congratulated him and they talked about the new boom in gold mines and about old times for some minutes. 

Then Tom asked Paul when he had left the Force. "Just this winter, Tom, and I was thrown out." "How on earth did that happen?"

"To cut a long story short, I had a row with Sergeant 

Schlitz, at Calgary, and struck him." "That sounds serious. How did it happen?” "Well, last winter things got bad again among the

Calgary single unemployed, as they always do in the winter. You know-frozen stiffs being pulled out of empty box cars and barns when its twenty or thirty below zero. 

A group of several hundred of the poor devils asked for some kind of public work to keep them from starving and tide them over the winter. You know, like that WPA or

CCC stuff down in the States. As usual, it was intimated to them they might try eating icicles. They had enough guts to occupy a government building in protest and a riot squad of us, under Sergeant Schlitz, were sent along to clean them out. "Well it took us damned near two hours to do it. Twenty or thirty of the poor beggars were badly hurt and Johnson and Callahan, from down in Lethbridge, landed in the hospital. At that, we needed plenty of tear gas to do the job. We were throwing in the bombs like old tomatoes for half an hour, trying to get them out; and those tear gas bombs cost thirty dollars apiece. I ask you, what's the sense of a government refusing decent aid to poor devils like that in the form of useful, or partly useful, work and then throwing money away on them in thirtydollar tear gas bombs! It makes even less sense than the money blown on the Great War or in all those useless grain elevators, railways and big hotels that the government has built all over the country for the benefit of big, political party contractors and then allowed to fall apart because they never should have been built in the first place.
