I tell you, Tom, one has to either be a moron or an extremely clever cynic, like Superintendent Tweed, to pretend to have any faith in things as they now stand. We're neither an honest democracy, nor an efficient dictatorship.

Our only salvation, so far as I can see, is for the decent people of this country to get together and work for annexation with the good old U. S." "Where's your national spirit, Paul?"

"I haven't any, thank goodness. 'O Canada' fails to get any bigger rise out of me than 'God Save The King.' "How about your scrap with Schlitz?" "Well, I was coming to that. During the riot, Schlitz was rushing about like a big, delighted bull, as if he thought himself Napoleon crossing the Alps. Finally, he settles on a little, shaky, crippled fellow, wearing a returned soldier's badge, and just about beats his head to a pulp with his riot stick. I stepped in to stop his going any further and he turned and took a swipe at me. Cut my cheek wide open. See, you can still see the scar. That was enough, I just stepped in and knocked the big tramp cold. I got thirty days in the jug and my discharge from the Force over it."

Tom took down Paul's address and they parted with another hearty handshake. Despite his not seeing eye to eye with Paul in many of his political and sociological views, he had a natural liking for his vital, breezy personality and character. 

On graduating from university with honors, Tom obtained a position with an American mining company operating a gold mine, known as the Prospector, in northern 

British Columbia, not far from the Alaska Panhandle.

Mary and he agreed that it would be better to wait to get married until he became properly settled. The position called for a junior engineer to do assay work, mill tests and the usual analyses in connection with a mine which concentrated its ore and shipped the concentrate to a customs smelter, in this case an American one, located at Tacoma, Washington.

Tom liked his new work and did well at it from the start. He applied for married quarters at the mining camp as soon as a vacancy should occur and his name was duly accepted and placed well up the list by the mine superintendent. He became very popular in the mining community, representing the mine in track sports with some other ex-college men on the staff in the summer and playing a little basketball, despite his injured arm, in the fall.

The country was cold and damp, being only some fifteen

miles from tidewater, and had the unusually heavy annual snowfall of about seventy feet. As a result, Tom's hand commenced to bother him as soon as winter set in. He realized for the first time with alarm that the doctors at Edmonton had deceived him about its eventual recovery. 

Accordingly, he wrote a long letter to the Commissioner of the Force at Ottawa, setting forth the facts of the deception, without going into any of the scandalous details as to how the original "accident" occurred, asking for compensation from the Force in view of these facts, despite the waiver he had signed at Edmonton. About a month later, he received a short reply from the Adjutant of the Force, stating that the Commissioner had referred the matter to him and he was instructed to inform him the matter was being looked into.

During these months at the Prospector mine, Tom often read extracts in the newspapers about the Keufels, Hore- Sparlings, Tweed and other persons his police work had brought him in contact with. The Keufels and Hore- 

Sparlings names usually appeared in the society columns of the Vancouver newspapers about once a week; while 

Tweed was mentioned in connection with one or two routine inspections he made by aeroplane of northern detachments and the arrest of a minor Chinese opium peddler in Vancouver. Then one day when a batch of newspapers arrived with the Alaska boat, Tom read the sad news of the death of Sergeant Greenleaf and Constable Johnson in a gun battle with a group of bank bandits, near the Alberta-British Columbia border. Tom had liked Greenleaf the best of any N.C.O. on the Force. He had borne a sharp contrast to men like Harris and Schlitz. Poor 

Johnson! Tom well remembered his good nature, brilliant stick-handling at hockey and the introduction he had given him to Mary as he left Lethbridge. He sat down and wrote a letter of condolence to Johnson's mother in 

Calgary and Greenleaf's widow "care of the R.C.M.P." to get out on the same boat as brought the news. 

Without Tom knowing it until the following day, the same boat had brought a sly, disparaging letter from In-

spector Tweed to the General Superintendent of the Prospector mine, asking for information about him. The letter was written on the Force stationary and in the vein usually used in making enquiries about paroled, or suspected, criminals. Tom was surprised, on being called into the Manager's office, to find him not in his usual cheerful spirits. 

He looked quite stern and closed the door of the office. "Shields, I want to speak to you about a purely private matter-alone," he stated slowly. "Your work here has been absolutely satisfactory in every way, as far as I'm concerned. You're probably the best man we've ever had in the lab here and you've done some analyses for us that I, myself, didn't believe possibly could be done with the equipment we've got here. We've always sent them down to Idaho before and have had to wait a long time for the results to come back here. However, we must have men in the lab we can absolutely trust-" "Of course, sir." "We have to handle so much high-grade ore through the lab." "You're not suggesting, are you- 99 "I have a letter here, Shields, which I don't quite understand. Take a look at it and please explain clearly what it's all about. Since I've been up here, I've received only five letters like it from the R.C.M.P. and they've meant only one thing—'

Tom took Superintendent Tweed's letter from the Manager's fingers and read it slowly. It was a long, meandering letter and, tucked away in the middle of a long, closelytyped paragraph, was the brief statement that the enquiry was being made "because Shields is an ex-member of the Force and it took an interest in him as such." Tom pointed the line out to his employer and the latter breathed an audible sigh of relief. "Why, how stupid of me not to have noticed it, Shields!

I'm glad I disregarded what they said here about the matter being strictly confidential and called you in, otherwise it might have escaped my notice. You know, I'm a very busy man and I just glanced over the letter quickly.

It's all Greek to me why the R.C.M.P. want to pester employers with long rigmaroles like this. It looks to me like a waste of government stamps and stationery." "It certainly looks like it, sir." "I'm going to tell them, Shields, that your work is not only satisfactory around here, but that I wish all the engineers on our staff were as good in their lines as you are. 

Will that be okay with you?" "Certainly, sir, and thanks a lot."

Tom left the Manager's office without telling him what was really on his mind: that the letter was an attempt on 

Tweed's part to discredit him with his employers and possibly lose him his position. The one, short extract 

Tweed had inserted alone saved the missive from being libelous. Probably, that is why he had inserted it; although 

Tom couldn't ignore the possibility that the Force did retain some sort of interest in its ex-members and that Tweed might have acted in good faith. The fact remained, however, that had he been an ordinary miner or laborer, the letter would likely have meant his dismissal without any explanations being given or asked for. 

As the winter progressed and weeks went by without the Force taking any action on his request for compensation, Tom became bitter and impatient. On top of the injury and the untoward circumstances surrounding it, the underhand letter from Tweed added fuel to his bitterness. One evening, he went over to the Company's office and worked into the small hours of the morning typing out a long letter to the Minister of Justice at Ottawa, laying his case before him. (The Minister of 

Justice of Canada is the ultimate head of the R.C.M.P.; the Commissioner being its highest-ranking officer actually in the Force itself). 

Although he again avoided bringing Symes' name into the case, the letter was a bitter, open denunciation of the treatment he had received while a member of the Force and went at some length into the liquor and other activities of the Keufels, estimating that they had smuggled at least $40,000,000 worth of liquor into the United

States during prohibition, with the full knowledge of the Canadian authorities, and boldly speculating that his transfer out of the Vancouver C. I. B. and away to 

Alberta had been on account of his investigations in that direction. It was an indiscreet, as well as bold, letter to write, since he was fully aware of the power the Keufels exerted in Canadian politics, due to their large subscriptions to the campaign funds of both major political parties.

He received a polite and attentive reply from the office of the Minister of Justice which listed some of the major points of his complaint numerically and promised his case would be looked into and any injustice done, rectified. To his comments on the Keufels, however, the letter stated evasively that "proof is very difficult, if not impossible, to obtain in cases of this nature and many of your charges cannot be supported by facts." "Which is as good as saying that they're either not interested or that I'm a liar," Tom reflected.

XXXI 

A LITTLE CORPORAL COMES BACK 

Two important world events had had a direct and almost simultaneous influence on the career of Gottfried 

Von Altenburg, last of the proud Prussian junker Altenburgs, in 1933. First, the United States quietly slid off the leaky water wagon of Prohibition; while, secondly, a scraggy-necked ex-corporal of the great German war machine of 1914-18 came out of a cheap boarding house and poverty to take over the Germanic ship of state and steer it away from its docile acceptance of Versailleism into the more ambitious, but dangerous, waters of Nazism and reassertion of the Germanic spirit. 

The first of these events meant that ex-U-Boat Captain Gottfried Von Altenburg was now out of a lucrative job of rum-running for the Keufels; the second, that he received a clear, cryptic letter from the German consul at

New York reminding him of his war services to the Vaterland and instructing him to be ready to report at New York for passage on the liner Bremen at an early date to act as a "technical adviser" at the new German Admiralty.

As in the case of thousands of other young Germans, scattered all over the United States, Canada and South 

America, the letter made Altenburg's blood tingle; for, however much they might have abandoned the Germanic ideal, despaired and indulged in too heavy drinking since those black and stunning days in the fall of 1918, when even the great Ludendorff lost his nerve, young Germans of Altenburg's class had always relished the conception of a new "der tag" in the offing at some future date. Most of them had not taken Hitler seriously at first and had even asked one another with despairing shrugs what the Vaterland was coming to when a bunch of school boys and yokels was allowed to parade about the streets shouting and 271

waving their arms and defying authority. They despised, at first, the ostentatious Nazi salute, preferring the more modest one that had been used in the greatest war machine in the World's history. However, all the world loves a winner and Germans are no different to any other human species in this respect. Thus, Germans, for widely varying reasons, eventually decided to bet their chips on the 

Little Corporal. 

When Altenburg reported to the German Consulate in New York, the staff there was greatly impressed with his fluent English. A series of cables was exchanged with Berlin as a result of this discovery and his passage on the Bremen cancelled. He was instructed, instead, to go to Chicago and attend the University of Chicago for a special post-graduate course for one year. 

In a non-German, all this regulation of one's destiny, especially on such short notice, might have proved bewildering and irritating; to Altenburg, it was exciting and inspiring. He was taking orders from the Vaterland once more and joyously clicking his heels; even though the orders did not quite have the direct, obvious assurance they would have had when coming from a von Tirpitz or 

Von Hipper. 

They informed him at the Consulate that all his expenses at the University of Chicago would be paid for him; whereupon Altenburg, with tears of indignation springing to his eyes, scornfully threw down a wallet well filled with

U. S. currency and rejected the offered financial assistance. 

The Consulate staff clicked their heels in respect at this exhibition and warmly shook his hand one after the other. 

Gottfried Von Altenburg found himself far from being a lonely German ex-naval officer and ex-rum-runner in Chicago. There was a sizeable German colony attending the University, including a large contingent from Nuremburg, and they treated him with respect and warmth and introduced him to many likeable and influential American friends of theirs.

He came to like Americans exceedingly well-second only to his own people, in fact—especially the fairer sex, whom he found delightful and fascinating. He almost,

A LITTLE CORPORAL COMES BACK 273 

but not quite, forgave America for entering the War against the Vaterland on account of these friendly contacts. Like many Germans, he had never seriously considered that American entry into the Great War might have been due largely to outrageous and stupid acts of violence by Germany, such as the sinking of the Lusitania by a brother U-Boat captain; preferring, instead, to blame it solidly on Franco-British propaganda, combined with American credulity and avarice. Now, he blamed it more on the fact that Americans appeared to him like a nation of delightful novices, full of the zest of life, but possessing the analytical powers of children and, therefore, easily taken in by the wicked Franco-British propaganda machine.

As with many Germans, Altenburg's secret pet hate was England, though he kept the secret well. He kept it locked up in the inner recesses of his mind, because he had a mystical fear of the widespread influence of British power. It was his only fear. He regarded Britain as the 

Machiavelli amongst nations that must eventually be destroyed if virtue, as exemplified by Germany, was to survive. Britain and the Jews!

Fate can sometimes be poetic, even though roundabout, in its justice. During his work for the Keufels, Gottfried had collected a bundle of documents, signed by one or other of the members of the Keufel household, or by a Mr. Gregory Hore-Sparling. These documents gave the exact quantities and values of shipments of liquor on the 

Pacific Coast Transit Company's fleet of speed boats operating out of Vancouver to points along the Washington-Oregon-California coastline. The data had come into his possession on account of a regular bonus the "Company" had paid its three leading skippers at the end of each month. These three persons had each been rendered a signed statement by their employers at such periods, setting forth the value of their own shipments alongside that of their two leading competitors. There may be no honor among crooks, but there must be exact accounting.

Since Altenburg had been the Pacific Coast Transit Company's star skipper, and had never been worse than third in these monthly compilations, he had a set of data that showed a goodly fraction of the total liquor smuggling activities of the Keufels during the Prohibition era in the U. S. A. This mere fraction totaled $21,000,000. 

Thus, for divers motives, which ranged from a reluctant liking for Americans to contempt and hatred for Britishers and his ex-employers, Gottfried appeared at the offices of the Federal Bureau of Investigation at Washington one day. It was the day prior to his scheduled departure for Germany and he brought with him his complete set of data on the illegal activities of the Keufels. 

The proteges of Uncle Sam were very much interested and permitted Gottfried to leave the country unmolested for turning State's evidence.

In one respect, Altenburg's action was the very antithesis of Brutus' share in the murder of Caesar: for, whereas Brutus' motives were noble, his action warped; 

Gottfried's action in betraying his former employers was worthy, but his motives were entirely vindictive and savored strongly of ingratitude.

The important thing as always, however, was the end result, rather than the motivation; for it is actions which rule the world.

The F.B.I. at Washington acted fast on the Von Altenburg data and immediately set about investigating the activities of Adolph Keufel and his associates. They discovered that, since Repeal, they had been rapidly building breweries and distilleries in the United States and transferring a large part of their liquor business across the border overnight in answer to the threat of American competition. In the vernacular of the times, they were "going legitimate.'

The G-Men kept a close watch on Adolph Keufel and Gregory Hore-Sparling at the border and, one fine day when they crossed it in a big Cadillac belonging to the former, to supervise the construction of a new distillery at Portland, Oregon, they found themselves placed under arrest and held on $75,000 bail each. The bail was quickly

A LITTLE CORPORAL COMES BACK 275 

put up by the Keufel family at Vancouver and, despite its size and the large value of the newly-acquired Keufel American interests that might be prejudiced by their action, both Mr. Keufel and Mr. Sparling jumped the bail as if it had been $75. The U. S. Government had but recently completed a mass of mortar and stone in San Francisco bay they named Alcatraz Penitentiary and both gentlemen had heard about it.

Later, the Keufel interests found themselves forced to pay another million-odd dollars into the U. S. Treasury for unpaid customs revenues on the smuggled liquor shown in the Altenburg data, on threat of confiscation of their newly-acquired U. S. properties if the claims were not met. Even at that, the settlement was an act of generosity by the U. S. Government, since it only represented about five per cent of the known value of the "imports" and this known value was only a fraction of the grand total.

The Canadian press handled the Keufel and Hore- Sparling arrests with the same amazing "discretion" as it later handled the Edward VIII-Mrs. Simpson affair; that is, it suppressed the news altogether, in spite of the fact that any Canadian could pick up a current issue of an American newspaper and read anywhere up to half the front page devoted to the case. It is the invariable attitude of the British and Canadian press, however, that unfavorable news concerning prominent persons must be suppressed where possible. This means that wealthy prodigals have a life of ease further eased by not having to worry, if they are ever caught in a gambling den or house of ill-fame, about the adverse publicity; but it also means that the public is kept in a fog and that the possession of wealth brings ex-officio respectability along with it. The term used in Canadian journalism for such hidden censorship is "news not in the public interest." Since the Keufels did a large amount of advertising in Canadian news organs and held large blocks of stock in many respectable enterprises, the publicising of them as large-scale smugglers and racketeers was "not in the public interest."

Apropos of this incident, a visiting American gentleman once told a sophisticated Canadian: "I admire your

Canadian newspapers a lot. They make most of our newsprint rags look like low scandal sheets in comparison. 

One has only to glance over them to get a feeling that journalism is on a higher plane up here than down South. 

You don't find the front pages cluttered up with news of racketeers and scandals in your papers the same as ours. 

Just between you and me, the opinions of half the newspapermen in the United States can be bought, too. It's a public scandal the way they'll accept bribes to print deliberate lies in their papers. I'll just bet it isn't the same up here."

This was a little too much for the patience of the sophisticated Canadian, even though he was of Scotch descent and a loyal tory. "M'on," he ejaculated, "you're right. Our Canadian newspapermen are such damned scoundrels and liars that they lie out of force of habit without being bribed. You have to bribe them to tell the truth up here. How else d'you suppose that such things as thousands of our best young men rotting in unemployment camps at twenty cents a day are hushed up and kept from being a public scandal? 

As for our racketeers: it's true you won't read much about them on the front pages; but just turn over to the society news and you'll see plenty of their pictures." 

Since the Prospector mine was American-controlled and there were a number of Americans on the staff, the spacious reading room in its recreation hall contained many American newspapers and magazines. These included the Saturday Evening Post, Time, the New York

Times and the California and Seattle newspapers. Tom read about the arrest of Adolph Keufel and his large settlement with the U. S. Government in the latter. 

The news caused him to furrow his brow deeply in a mixture of contemplation and indignation. Among other things, he immediately sensed that the million-dollar settlement was, by inference, an admission by the Keufels that they had smuggled about $21,000,000 worth of liquor into the United States. To be sure, this was only

A LITTLE CORPORAL COMES BACK 277 

about half his estimate of $40,000,000, but he reflected that the $21,000,000 might only have been the fraction of the total that the U. S. Federal agents had been able to obtain exact data on. Actually, it is an unusually honest and efficient law enforcement machine that can nibble that close to the affairs of powerful criminals. 

When he returned to his room in the staff quarters, he read over the letter from the Minister of Justice carefully and the furrows in his brow deepened as he scanned the passage: "... proof is very difficult, if not impossible, to obtain in cases of this nature and many of your charges cannot be supported by facts."

In a sudden moment of dejection it seemed to Tom that the Canadian Department of Justice was in collusion with the Keufels. He asked himself why, if the United States 

Federal agents, working from outside Canada, could gather the necessary data to bring the Keufels to book, his own much romanticized R.C.M.P. and Canadian Justice Department could not do likewise in their own back yard. It was a disheartening and demoralizing line of thought. (Here Tom was obviously doing his own people and country an injustice, not through any inherent bad judgment or ill-will, so much as a lack of knowledge of the peculiar circumstances under which the G-Men had obtained their data against the Keufels. Had Altenburg gone, say, to the Minister of Justice at Ottawa, instead of the Department of Justice at Washington, the result would likely have been the same).

Of one thing Tom now made up his mind: he would go out to collect damages from the R.C.M.P. for his injury, by making a full statement of the circumstances of his case to the Prime Minister and leader of the opposition in the House of Commons. Accordingly, he wrote two almost identical letters to these two personages setting forth his claim. However, his feelings were in a white hot state when he wrote the letters and some of his statements went far beyond the bounds of moderation and discretion. He not only stated that he was coerced by 

LaGauze and Dr. Boving into leaving the Force without compensation and that his injury was due to the bungling

of the latter; but also claimed that he was transferred out of "E" Division, Vancouver, on account of the Keufels' influence and that they had definitely had a hand in the murder of James Small, late in the fall of 1932. The latter charge led also to a reflection on the coroner's jury which had pronounced a death by "suicide" in the Small case. Admitting that the Small case was a genuine mystery and had not been handled satisfactorily by the authorities, it was still a far call from the necessary proof which would have stood up in court and convicted the Keufels of having a hand in it.

Tom further claimed that the Keufels had attempted to have him murdered about the same time as Small met his doom and that he had been "taken for a ride in a car by three men, allegedly private detectives, in Stanley Park, Vancouver, and shot at and wounded by them." This, while perfectly true, did not identify the men, nor did it prove that they were connected in any way with the Keufels.

Lastly, instead of laying a few well-chosen facts con cerning Superintendent Tweed and Inspector LaGauze before these personages, so that they would be in a position to draw their own conclusions, he made a wild accusation that the whole R.C.M.P. was fraught with corruption from top to bottom and in league with the underworld; and made an ill-judged reference to the comments of the Minister of Justice's office in reply to his earlier complaint, implying that that Governmental Department did not want the Keufels' activities investigated too thoroughly.

Naturally, this line of attack did not invite the wholehearted co-operation of the Canadian Government, since the Prime Minister depended on the Minister of Justice, who in turn, depended on the Commissioner of the R.C. 

M.P., who in turn, depended on several subordinates to carry out any investigation of his various charges and allegations. Since these charges and allegations had been twisted by him to throw an unfavorable light on these departments, it placed them on the defensive and in an unfavorable position to cooperate with him.

A LITTLE CORPORAL COMES BACK 279 

It must be remembered in justice to Tom that he had been done a great injustice in his treatment by the Force and that his actions were not alone due to hot headedness. 

Besides, he still had little conception of the maddening evasions and inefficiencies that go with any "red tape" government, like that at Ottawa. He had youth's tendency to blame such "red tape" evasions and inefficiencies on graft and corruption rather than, at least partially, on honest, but bewildered, bureaucracy. 

As the winter of 1935-36 progressed at the Prospector mine, Tom suffered more and more from his injured wrist. The damp, cold Alaskan-B.C. climate penetrated to the injured nerve, through its covering of scar tissue, as if he had had an axe blade wedged in his wrist. He tried to relieve the pain by massage, warm coverings and hot bathings, but to no avail. Finally, he received a letter from the Commissioner of the Force stating that his letters to the Prime Minister and leader of the opposition had been referred to him, through the Minister of Justice and that, while he disagreed with the charges and allegations contained in them, agreed to his claim for compensation for the injury. The letter further stated, that he would have to appear before a medical board in Vancouver before the percentage of disability could be determined.

That decided Tom. He gave the company a month's notice with an explanation about the injury troubling him in the cold, raw climate at the Prospector mine and left for Vancouver in the spring of 1936. It was a disheartening change to have to make; it was also a disheartening fact to have to face that he was now a cripple, something he had put off thinking about as long as possible; but the sea air on the trip down braced him up and revived his flagging courage and ambition. He still had Mary. That was also what troubled him most about giving up his position on the mine staff, however: it meant another long delay now before they would be able to get married and settled.

XXXII CANADIAN FRUGALITY 

If Tom's trip down on the boat from Alaska had been bracing, a week spent waiting, sitting on a wooden bench, in the hall of a military hospital for his medical board dissipated the good done and more than offset it in gloom. 

If one has a morbid curiosity for sadness and horrors, he need go no farther afield than the inside of many soldiers' hospitals. The armless, legless, sightless and other broken figures vie with an atmosphere of despair and helplessness to pall on one's nerves. That is where one should look if he would appreciate General Sherman's famous saying, "War is Hell," rather than on the battlefield itself.

These were not the only things which griped Tom during that week of constant waiting. He found some of the staff at the Hospital as bullying and callous a group of individuals as he ever wished to meet. Apart from the doctors and nurses, they consisted largely of Englishmen and Scotchmen of Symes' class, who were timid and cringing to their superiors, but tyrannical and overbearing to any of the patients or "out" cases, like himself, who came into the place for a medical board. 

In one instance, the wife of a returned soldier came into the office where he was waiting, to collect her husband's pension cheque. She seemed to think there was some error in the amount of the cheque and stated as much to a surly British clerk who handed it to her. "Eere, yer don't need to give us that stuff, lidy," the clerk informed her. "Yer 'usband's in the bug 'ouse with that 'ead wound of 'is and yer damned well knows it. We tike orff fifteen a month for 'is h'upkeep. Now get goin', will yer. Next case." 

So, a boy who had gone "over there" somewhere in 1914-18 from Canada, ostensibly to save democracy, had 280

CANADIAN FRUGALITY 

281 gained a head wound that placed him in an insane asylum and the right to have his wife addressed like a common street walker by a surly office clerk. Democracy must indeed be worth saving, or else Civilization had been badly taken in! Tom decided.

He saw another case of a man so badly injured and shell-shocked in the war that his legs and arms were atrophied like those of a drug addict and his hands shook continually. He had been getting a pension of $20-a-month for his disabilities and the doctors cut it down to $12, because they decided "he hadn't been putting forward the maximum effort towards recovery." Just what such maximum effort could be, or how they decided he hadn't been putting it forward, was a mystery.

When Tom's turn came, he got an insight into medical boards which was indeed an unpleasant revelation. First of all, the board, consisting of four doctors, told him there was nothing the matter with his arm and that they would not recommend him for any pension. On his demanding that they give the arm a thorough examination, however, they found disabilities which ranged from ten to twenty percent of total disability. This test included scalding two of his fingers in boiling water with him blindfolded, to test whether the sensation had departed from them, due to the nerve injury, as he claimed it had. The fingers became infected as a result and he had to wear dressings on them for two weeks. When he finally lost his temper and told them that he would go down to San Francisco, if necessary, to get a proper estimate of the disability made by a first-class neurologist, they looked at one another and raised their estimate of his disability to forty percent, which was probably a shade too high.

Even at that they weren't squandering public funds. 

The compensation for a total disability in the R.C.M.P. is only forty dollars a month! Translated into familiar terms, this means that a member of the Mounted Police losing his right arm on duty (a sixty percent disability) receives only twenty-four dollars a month to "live" on.

No self-respecting nation should allow itself to pay such an absurdly low level of compensation to an injured public

servant. It is equivalent to a sentence of death by starvation or a life of crime when analyzed intelligently. But then Canada is not yet a nation, merely a "dominion" and, perhaps, can dispense with such abstract things as self respect. 

Tom visited an Irish-Canadian lawyer, one Bob Mac- Gusty, whose son he had known at University, and told him of the situation. "What can you expect," MacGusty shrugged, "when the pensions board is so loaded down with high-salaried doctors and political office holders? There's too much patronage in this country. Too much bureaucracy. It eats everything up. In the department you're interested in right now, there's not enough left over for the recipients of pensions and compensation, after the doctors and office clerks have gotten their cut. The big kick down in the 

States is that the Government is too generous in such cases, particularly with war vets. There's always a beef, one way or t'other. "I'll tell you of a case I've been handling for over eighteen months as an illustration. This client of mine's a logger. He got a small chip of steel in one of his eyes off'n axe blade. The camp doctor tried to remove the piece of metal with a small, toy magnet, and failing to get results, sent him down here to Vancouver for treatment. 

They had to remove the injured eye here on account of infection having set in and for fear it might spread to t'other eye. 

"The man came under the Workmen's Compensation 

Act all right and was entitled to a permanent pension of twenty-five dollars a month. But do you think he got it? 

No sir. Not yet, anyway, and I doubt if he ever will. It was his left eye which was injured and had to be removed and the camp doctor, who is quite a booze artist of sorts, had put down the right one on his report by mistake. That compensation board refused to grant the man a nickel and have so far beaten us in court, too. That's Canadian justice for you, with all its fine trappings and what all!"

Tom spent much time in Vancouver looking around for a new position in a less severe climate than the Alaska Pan-

CANADIAN FRUGALITY 

283 handle. Besides applying to local engineering firms, he sent in an application to a leading South American copper producing company with its head office in New York. This enterprise accepted him immediately for a position as a junior metallurgist at one of its properties in Peru, out of a group of nearly 300 applicants, on account of his scholastic showing. They instructed him by wire to have an immediate physical examination and mail the result into their head office. The salary offered was $200 a month to start with. Tom accepted the offer and had the examination, only to be turned down by the company's head medical officer in New York on account of his arm. 

Finally, he was notified from Ottawa that he had been awarded a pension of sixteen dollars a month for his injury. Since $16 a month is not a negligible item when paid over a life time, but is of little practical use when paid in such small installments, Tom applied to have the matter settled in a lump sum. This they refused to do on the grounds that it was not their usual policy and because the injury might yet not prove permanent. He had begun taking electrical and massage treatments for the arm, which were costing him about $60 a month and rapidly exhausting his savings, so he applied to be given these treatments free at the military hospital, on the grounds that the Government would save the cost of his pension if his arm got well. The Force refused this request on the grounds that the injury was permanent and, therefore, the treatments would be a waste of money. They thus contradicted themselves. Tom paid his lawyer, Bob Mac- 

Gusty, another visit on receipt of the second letter and laid the correspondence before him. "This is downright queer, Tom," MacGusty told him, stroking a round, soft chin that formed the base of his handsome Irish countenance. "They clearly contradict themselves here. They refuse to settle your pension in a lump sum because they claim the injury may not prove permanent; then they refuse you treatments because they say it is permanent and the treatments can do you no good. 

I've never seen a case like this before. Do you have any

enemies on the Force, by any chance, Tom? Did you leave them with a clean record?" "Absolutely. Here's my discharge." "Hmm. . . . I can't understand it." "As regards your first question, Mister MacGusty” and Tom hesitated a moment . . . "I did have some enemies on the Force"... and Tom went ahead and told MacGusty in some detail about his three years with the R.C.M.P., omitting some features which threw unnecessary discredit on the Force in his estimation. MacGusty listened intently and, when Tom had finished, asked to see copies of the letters he had written to the Prime Minister and leader of the opposition. Tom produced carbon-copies of the two letters from an envelope in his pocket and MacGusty proceeded to read them with a frown, frequently shaking his head disapprovingly. "These letters are bad," he told Tom. "They were better never written. In fact, you must have been in quite a hot temper when you knocked them off. You already had the Keufels down on you and, believe me, those people will stop at nothing. If that gang ever gets a down on a man, he's sunk around this neck of the woods. On top of that, you go and antagonize your own people, the R.C.M.P., by writing these two letters. You should have known their contents would be referred back to the Commissioner to act upon. I know the Commissioner, Sir 

James MacBrien, fairly well. He was our Chief of Staff overseas in the war. If there's one thing he hates, it is political interference in matters dealing with the Force. 

You've probably turned his back up against you over that alone, even neglecting your unwarranted criticisms of the 

Force. You shouldn't have cast accusations against the whole Force, merely because there happen to be one or two crooked officers on it. There're blacksheep in every family." "Yes, I realize that," Tom admitted. "I happen to know some of the top men in the R.C.M.P. pretty well," MacGusty continued, "-the bunch who were overseas in the war, I mean-and, believe me, there's not a squarer bunch anywhere. You happened to have a tough.

CANADIAN FRUGALITY 

285 experience in running up against this man Tweed and his French-Canadian side-kick, LaGauze, and it's warped your whole outlook. Can't you see, that all you've got on

Tweed is what has passed between the two of you behind closed doors, or where there has not been a third witness? 

It would be merely your word against his. How can you expect the Commissioner to know about those things? 

Tweed wouldn't talk that way to him. All the Commissioner probably knows about Tweed is such things as his part in clearing up the Macdonald case in Calgary and such like." "Yes, I see that now, Mister MacGusty, but there's such a thing as justice, despite feelings and prejudice. I admit 

I acted hot-headedly, but that doesn't alter the plain facts of the case." 99 "There's where you make a big mistake, Tom, it does.

Two wrongs don't make a right, they just pile up the misery; besides, in this country, red tape and correctitude run neck-and-neck for first place and justice usually isn't even in the money-unless a matter is handled discreetly. 

This isn't England or the United States, you know. "Frankly, Tom, you're up against it. Just between you and me, the big liquor, vice and narcotics ring is a powerful influence in the politics of this city. Vancouver is the opium and narcotics capital of North America. You should know that from being on the Force. Even Scotland Yard couldn't outsmart them; so why get mad at your own people, the R.C.M.P.? Fifty years ago, son, there was only a logging camp here, where there is now a city of over three hundred thousand people; and that new city has proudly taken up part of the crown of vice and corruption that San Francisco abdicated at the turn of the century. Yes, sir. Vice. Corruption. Murder,

Everything. On the surface it's a swell place, but underneath the surface. At least San Francisco had its Barbary 

Coast, where the underworld was more or less segregated. 

We have 'em scattered all over town: Shaughnessy Heights and the other classy parts of town hold our Barbary 

Coast, worst luck. "Some of the scum's risen to the top around here,

Shields, and is sitting plumb in the driver's seat. We've turned out a couple of Chiefs of Police, tried other things, but what's the use? You've been fighting and spilling your blood for a cause that don't even want to be saved. 

Don't I know it? You're a Don Quixote: only that old

Don Quixote guy had enough sense to take his wrath out on windmills; whereas you've picked on the toughest human hornets' nest in existence. You're up against a stone wall and there's no use bashing your brains out against it. "Don't get me wrong. I admire your courage and perseverance all right; but what's the use? There's no use in fighting when you know you're licked before you start.

Besides, courage and perseverance are virtues; and all virtues have passed their dividends since 1929." "Is there no legal action I can take?" Tom asked. "None that I know of, Tom, and I'm a lawyer. Your case is entirely up to the Mounted Police. If I were you, I'd try to forget about it." "That would be a lot easier if I wasn't crippled." "Yes, I can see that all right. Still-" 

Tom had no intention of "forgetting about" his case; nor of accepting defeat complacently without a struggle. 

As soon as he left MacGusty, he set to thinking of possible ways and means of fighting the impasse he was in. Mac- 

Gusty's analogy to a stone wall was not far out, he decided, after due reflection. . . . The ordinary legal channels seemed closed to him. . . . His only chance would be to arouse public opinion in his behalf and precipitate a government enquiry into the case. That would be difficult. 

In the first place, rabble-rousing methods did not appeal to him; in the second, such things were usually accomplished with the aid of a press hostile to an administration in power, such as Clemenceau's paper when it broke the famous Dreyfus case in France. Tom knew that the important newspapers and magazines of Canada were controlled by a few wealthy families and groups of business men who were, naturally, strongly conservative in outlook and regarded the correct function of the press as one of sustaining the status quo and suppressing the news, rather than digging it up, of cases that reflected any discredit

CANADIAN FRUGALITY 

287 on existing Canadian institutions. Such a case was his. 

Nor did Tom, himself, wish to see the record of the Force unjustly tarnished.

Suddenly it occurred to him that, since the Keufels had been prosecuted successfully from the United States, his case could also be publicised from there as a starting point for a probe. American magazines and periodicals have a greater circulation in Canada than Canadian publications themselves, anyway. Accordingly, he spent several days writing a scathing article on his case for magazine publication and mailed it to a leading American periodical.

Now it so happens that editors of important magazines are exceedingly busy persons, even when they have a staff of understudies to open their mail for them and look over all matter therein, before submitting any of it for pontifical consideration. Consequently, if one of these understudies pilfers an idea or story from this routine stream of correspondence, sends a rejection slip to the real author and says nothing more about it, the chances are roughly nine out of ten that he, or she, will get away with it. Not that this is a frequent occurrence at all—perhaps one in a million.

A bespectacled young chap who perused Tom's missive under these circumstances was considerably impressed with it, both as to its value as an article on underworld corruption of politics and police work in the great northern neighbor of the United States and as a possible means of furthering his own interests by blackmail. Accordingly, that evening when he left the editorial rooms of his magazine, Tom's article went along with him in the breast pocket of his overcoat. At home, he read it over carefully, again and again, underlining many passages in colored pencil. A few days later, he was able to obtain the use of a Vancouver city directory and look up the name A. Keufel in it. He then mailed off a letter to the bearer of this name at Canada's Western metropolis.

XXXIII SUPERINTENDENT  

Early in the summer of 1936 Superintendent Tweed was detailed to make a lightning inspection of the opium and narcotics situation out in Vancouver. He was busy at the time on an important counterfeiting case in the East and had localized the headquarters of the gang down as far as a suburb of Montreal, but the narcotics situation was more urgent and called for nothing short of a topnotch investigator to take it in hand. Accordingly, he had been detailed to handle the job by the Commissioner personally.

Tweed was in a holiday mood as he arrived at Vancouver and settled down comfortably in a suite at the Hotel Georgia and unpacked a tuxedo from a light wardrobe trunk. He had married a woman of some wealth and social position, who was seven years his senior, to help further his career. The venture hadn't turned out badly either, considering that it had been a marriage of convenience so far as he was concerned. She was fond of him; had borne him two fine children-a boy and a girland made a good home for him, whenever he happened to be around to enjoy it. But love seldom comes to a man, even of Tweed's broad views, more than once; and it had come to Tweed from the lips and body of one, Lucille Danielle, a Frenchwoman and former mistress of 

Mr. Gregory Hore-Sparling. 

Tweed had really first noticed her at a cocktail party given by Adolph Keufel in 1927, about the same time that Mr. Hore-Sparling was either tiring of her or losing her interest and wished to shake her off as cheaply as possible. Accordingly, a deal was reached between them. Nor had the thing been too difficult to carry out: since Hore-Sparling was pot-bellied and blotchy faced 288

and chirped his speech with an irritating, synthetic Oxford accent; whereas Tweed was a straight-backed, striking figure, really handsome but for a certain sinister quality about his cadaverous features, and all man. He had always looked forward to trips out to the Coast on account of Lucille; while once she had indiscreetly followed him 

East to Ottawa and had taken up residence there for a time.

Consequently, when the phone in his suite suddenly rang, he quickly gave up an attempt at tying a bow tie and eagerly grasped for the instrument with his long, alert fingers. He was disappointed when a male voice, he at once recognized as that of Adolph Keufel, asked for him. 

Tweed didn't like the quiet, guarded manner in which the tycoon spoke. It presaged trouble. "You must come up here to my residence right away, Tweed," Keufel told him. "But I've got an engagement, Mr. Keufel, that's important to me." "I said must," Keufel reminded him. "I don't care to say more over the telephone. "Oh-all right." 

Tweed took a taxi out to the large Keufel estate, his thoughts gyrating nervously on what the "trouble" was. 

His guess was that it had something to do with the very narcotics mission he was presently in Vancouver on. Perhaps, old man Keufel wished to tell him to "go easy." It took a lot to upset Adolph Keufel. In his anxiety, Lucille dropped completely out of his thoughts. "Have a drink, Tweed?" Mr. Keufel invited him, as they stood before a fireplace in the vice baron's private den. 

He was a thick-set man with a vigorous and commanding personality, despite a double chin and soft, unhealthy folds beneath the eyes. "Yes. Make it a good stiff whiskey and soda for me." "Here you are Inspector-I mean Superintendent Tweed. Let's drink to the return of prosperity and the downfall of that rabble-rousing Roosevelt and his crowd

down in the States. They're going to ruin big business all the world over, unless they're kicked out." "Even your kind of business, Mr. Keufel?" "Why yes-didn't you know? I'm going completely legitimate. I've washed my hands of the red light business already. From now on, the only red lights I'll have any dealings with will be red stop signs on streets. I've been putting everything into good New York industrials, bonds and some of our own dividend-paying gold mines. 

I'm practically out of narcotics, too. I'm even selling out my liquor interests. They're a dirty business." "Especially for a gentleman like you, Mr. Keufel." "Thanks. Besides, you know there ain't the money in that stuff there used to be. What with the U. S. off Prohibition and those new G-men watching every nook and corner along the International boundary for men slipping across with suitcases of the powders. Guess Vancouver's due to lose a lot of business. I'm going to buy me some race horses and run 'em dead straight so's the sporting public will get to like me. I'll lose money on the horses, of course, but it's the surest way of becoming popular and being a gentleman, my wife claims-just owning race horses and losing money on them. I don't know how much money yet." 

"Then what was it you wanted to see me about which was so important, Mr. Keufel?" Tweed asked with a note of irritation, putting down his half empty glass on the mantelpiece. 

"About a magazine article I've been blackmailed over. 

S'cost me plenty. The man putting the screws on me is an employee of an American magazine. I've got the whole thing tied up legally now so he can't come down on me for a cent more and can't have it published.' "Good. Then what's worrying you?" "He wasn't the author of the article. The author is an ex-member of the R.C.M.P., who lives right here in 

Vancouver. Name's T. I. Shields. I haven't been able to get a great deal of dope on him yet. Know him at all?" "Do I know him! I'll say I do! He's the fellow who snooped around Coal Harbour and got taken for a ride

by Spike and his crowd, back in '32. If that gang hadn't bungled things, he'd be where Small is now.' "Oh, so that's the man. I remember now." "Yes." Tweed picked his glass up from the mantelpiece and drank the remaining half of the whiskey and soda. "What troubles me mostly, Tweed, is that he knows all about Small-or seems to, at any rate. How did he find out? Only five persons know the truth of what really happened to him. Nobody pays any attention to that affidavit Small swore to a week or so before he met with his 'accident.' He was a discredited man. I had had him put in a nut house by a couple of psychiatrists. Cost me plenty, too. The editor of that dirty little radical paper, the Common Good, has the affidavit now, but he daren't do anything with it for fear of a libel action. I've been told that Small knew that I wanted him out of the way and says as much in the affidavit." 66 "He was merely a good guesser," Tweed opined grimly, '—just the same as Shields is." "If I thought that way about it, Tweed, my mind would be a whole lot more at ease.' 

"Well, put it at ease right now. Who would have wanted to tell Shields, in the first place? Everyone who knows about it, excepting me, is liable to a murder indictment; and I certainly wouldn't escape with a clean suit of underwear, either. Have you ever met a man who would put a noose around his own neck just for the sake of shooting his face off ?" "No-and yet-” "Shields had a talk with me back in 1932, down at our

C.I.B., before Small was rubbed out and I learned all he knew about things at that time. He knew quite a bit, at that, but it was all about what was happening at Coal Harbour-nothing about Small. I mentioned Small in our interview, because I didn't know then that his number was up. Shields probably got talking with one or two of your men down at the docks, after Small was out of the way, and picked up some gossip. You know, yourself, a lot of people knew you wouldn't have minded Small being

out of the way and there were plenty of rash guesses that your organization had had a hand in it.” "Who might have given Shields the idea in the first place?" "My guess is Von Altenburg." "Why, of course, the dirty ingrate!" "I hope he falls out with Hitler and gets beheaded." "He's too smart for that. You know, I admire that man Hitler-even though I'm a loyal Britisher now myself. We need somebody like him or Mussolini over here to keep the communistic rabble and the Jews in their proper places." "Yes, a good sprinkling of bolshevik and radical blood and sawdust in our prison yards wouldn't do anybody any harm. But what can one expect with that sentimental old fellow, MacKenzie King, in power over on this side?" "We could use a man like you, Tweed. You're military in your outlook, you've got class and don't give a damn for the mob." Tweed maintained a modest, respectful silence at this covert overture. "I do my bit in a small way," Mr. Keufel continued. "I contribute to our Citizens' League, which has broken its share of strikes and run a good number of red agitators out of town. Still, that's neither here nor there. Do you think Shields is in league with this American who's blackmailed me?" "I don't know, but doubt it. He's not the kind. He's one of those bally, high-principled codgers. You know what I mean. He likely sent that article into a magazine for publication all right and this other bird got hold of it and acted on his own. Is it well, or badly, written?" "Well written and it's entitled, Canada's Al Capone." "That sounds bad. It also suggests that Shields meant it for publication, rather than blackmail." "If this man, Shields, is really high-principled," Mr.

Keufel said with alarm, "it means he won't talk reason with us." "I'm afraid that's right." "Suppose this article was published and it caused enough stir around here to have an autopsy made on Small,

Tweed? I understand he was beaten unconscious before that poison could be poured down his throat and that means his skull was probably fractured." "There's no need to get alarmed there," Tweed reassured. "Pour me out another whiskey and soda, will you please. Less Scotch this time. It's nearly four years now since the thing happened. A wooden coffin always caves in and settles after about eighteen months and that is liable to mess anything up. Any small skull fracture could be accounted for by the cave-in." "I see. But this isn't a question of merely escaping the gallows, Tweed. That's ridiculous, anyway, for a man of my wealth and power. It's the scandal and loss of face 

I'm scared of. That article can ruin us both, even if we can beat every charge in it in court. I have a beautiful wife; three lovely children; a fine home; everything that money can buy, in fact, to lose if things go wrong. You -you have your career, too." "Wait, I think I've got something, Mr. Keufel.” "What is it?" "Pour me out another drink and let me think a while." "With pleasure. That's what I brought you here for.

There's no use in having money, Tweed, if one can't hire first-class brains, like yours, occasionally." "I've got it figured out already, Mr. Keufel. We could land Shields in the pen for blackmail. Once in there, I could see to it that he got treated the right way-you know, plenty rough, I mean. He'll come out the pen squawking, broken in mind and body, with an idea the world's crooked-which he already suspects it is—and a discredited man. Nobody will ever pay any attention to what he says or does after that. That's the way they break dangerous radicals and busybodies over in the Old 

Country and Europe.' 

"That's all very fine, Tweed, but how're you going to work it? He's got to be tried in court and we don't want this stuff to be made public in a court of law. Besides, you say yourself the man's innocent and only sent his article into the magazine to get it published. He'll naturally stand up and swear to it. Then where are we?

I'll likely have to get into the witness box and it will be discovered that I paid that American hush money. Then everyone will believe every word in the article." "We could have the case tried in camera, Mr. Keufel, so's the public don't know a word about it. That's your privilege as the alleged victim in a blackmail case. Get it? With the right lawyers and people working on the job, who's going to believe his story? It's either that, or a case of having to rub Shields out." "All right, Tweed, but I don't want any bungling. I'm not out to gamble on things like I used to. I'm getting old for this sort of life and I want a certain amount of peace and quiet from now on. I took my last big chance on Small." "I can fix it so there won't be any element of chance, Mr. Keufel." "How?" "By framing a set-up locally and springing a police trap on Shields. It's a cinch." "I certainly wish I had your brain, Tweed. I'd pay a cool million for it. How're we going to go about it?" “Well, first off, you'll have to get in touch with the local police authorities—not the R.C.M.P., because that would bring me into it and it would look fishy-and complain that a man, named T. I. Shields, has been writing you threatening letters and attempting to extort money from you." "What if the police want to see the letters?" "Tell 'em you threw them away threw them in the fire because they made you mad.' "Will they believe that?" 99 "Sure, I'll get someone from our C.I.B. to drop them a word." "Then what?" "Get a private detective you can trust. And what I mean is, can trust. Have him get in touch with Shields and tell him he is the editor, or one of the owners, of that magazine Shields sent his article to." "I get it. Up here on his summer vacation, like." "Right. He can make Shields an offer for the article,

which I imagine he'll accept. Of course, to make it plausible, the detective must have some brains and he'll have to explain why he has waited to come up here before deciding to accept the story." "He could say that he took the article along with him to conduct a personal investigation of it, before deciding to run it.' "Yes, that's what I had in mind. That's perfectly plausible. Have you a man for the job, Mr. Keufel?" "Yes, just the right guy. How will the trap finally work?" "Well, providing Shields accepts the offer, which will be a good one, we'll get your man to make a rendezvous with Shields for payment. Then we'll send our man to the local police to tell them that he has introduced himself to Shields as your go-between and has, under threat of publication of a libellous article against you, offered him a large sum of money on your behalf as hush money. Blackmail. He'll collect a sum of marked money from the police to pay Shields for the article. The police will arrest 

Shields at the rendezvous with the marked bills on him and the article in the possession of our man. In court, our private detective will deny the story about the magazine deal and will simply tell one of an ordinary extortion plot. 

What more evidence does a court need ?" "Splendid, Tweed. I didn't think anything of that sort could be so easy or so much fun. You certainly know your way about. The whole thing's like taking candy from a kid-if it works; and I think it will." Adolph Keufel rubbed his pudgy hands together exuberantly. "Here, Tweed, have another one on the house."

XXXIV MAN TRAP 

Tom sat writing a letter to Mary in the room he occupied in a small West End hotel in Vancouver, when the buzzer in his room sounded, indicating that someone was on the hall telephone line wishing to speak to him. He got up and went out into the hall telephone booth, hoping it was an offer of a job. When a strange, nasal voice asked, "Is this Mister Thomas Ingram Shields?" his hopes mounted. His friends merely addressed him as Tom, or 

Shields. He answered promptly in the affirmative. The voice then took him by surprise by asking: "Are you the same man who wrote a magazine article exposing the Vancouver underworld recently?" "Well-yes and no," Tom responded. "I did write some sort of article along that line a short time ago, but as far as I know, it hasn't been published. I haven't received any word back from the publishers or seen it in print yet, at any rate. Why, what about it?" "Just this, Mr. Shields: I'm Mr. Jack Gordon, one of the associate editors and part owner of that chain of magazines you sent the article to. We were all very much impressed with it; so much so, that I took the trouble to bring it along with me on my holidays up here in the great Pacific Northwest, among these magnificent mountains and trees, to check over some features of it right here on the spot myself. I was so much impressed with those beautiful big trees of yours out in Stanley Park and those lovely mountains, all your magnificent Canadian bays and beaches and wonderful lakes and trout streams-" Tom was wishing impatiently that the man would stop eulogizing on the beauties of nature in his very unlovely, nasal voice and get down to the business he had phoned him up about . . ."that I clean forgot about the article. 296

MAN TRAP 

297 Since getting around to it, though, I've decided we'd like to clinch a deal with you for its sole publication rights. 

The article's a wow in my opinion and I think that every free-born Canadian and American should have the privilege of reading it exclusively in one or other of our magazines and drawing his own conclusions." "That's fine," Tom said. "Where can I meet you?" "I'll drive around to your hotel and pick you up in my car in about half an hour, if you like, Mrs. Shields." "Oh, all right. Thanks very much."

Tom hung up the receiver with a thoughtful expression. 

The news was exceedingly good, but something about the nasal commercialism of the voice and a synthetic enthusiasm accompanying it annoyed him. When he got back to his room, he re-read a passage in Mary's letter he was answering, two or three times over. 

It ran: ". . . I wish you would forgive and forget your unfortunate experiences in the R.C.M.P., Tom. Nothing in this world is perfect. If you let the vindictive side of you get the better of you, you're licked. Hatred and illwill just burn a person up-and out; and the people you're up against are too powerful to be ever touched by the law in this country. They make the law work for them. If you go in for extra legal methods against them, then you're not only taking terrible chances, but playing right into their hands as well. Please abandon the idea of revenge. It never pays. Look what happened to Hamlet. 

Tom, and take the advice of your Ophelia . . .' 99 

Funny Mary thinking about Hamlet and Ophelia. The reference threw Tom's thoughts right back over four years to what Tweed had said to him that day down at the

C.I.B. . . . “Ever read Shakespeare's Hamlet? There was a young fellow who figured things out, too, and worried a whole lot about what makes the wheels go 'round in this old Universe. If I remember rightly, he went bugs -clean bugs—and ended up on a slab. . . . It didn't get him any place. . . . Shakespeare was a mighty wise old bird... 

Tom finished his letter and waited about ten minutes before Mr. "Jack Gordon" arrived to pick him up. Gordon

drove out along Georgia Street and slowed down in the vicinity of Stanley Park. 

"It's nice and cool out here, Shields," he decided. “Mind if we talk this matter over here, instead of going up to my stuffy hotel room downtown? I'd like to keep this thing as quiet as possible anyway. You can never tell who's snooping around. When this thing appears in print, we want it to strike like lightning-sort of take the public's breath away." "All right," Tom agreed. "Gordon's" argument struck him as stupid, but he didn't say so. He then remembered that Gordon came from a large American city where thugs had been known to commit political murders in a very nonchalent manner in broad daylight. "We always do business in a 'take it, or leave it' basis," he told Tom. "Before leaving New York, I talked the matter over with my partners and we agreed on a price of twelve hundred dollars for the article. Okay with you?"

Tom hesitated a moment so as not to appear over eager. "Yes, all right," he agreed. "That's a fair enough price for an article by an unknown author." "It is. As a matter of fact, it's the tops with us for that type of article." "Fine." "I'll have to wire my connections in New York this afternoon, Shields, to let them know the price has been agreed to and that I've investigated the article and found it okay. It's just a formality with us, but we always adhere to it. One of us never takes a step without consulting the other partners in our business. I'll likely hear back from them tomorrow morning or afternoon. How would it be if I call for you at seven o'clock tomorrow evening to make payment and have you sign the agreement. 

I'll draw up the forms at my hotel when I get back. I'm playing golf tomorrow afternoon, or I'd get the matter cleared up then." "Yes, that'll be fine."

When Tom got back to his hotel, he tore open his letter to Mary and eagerly inserted a long postscript, explaining about the twelve hundred dollars and telling her to start

MAN TRAP 

299 getting her trousseau in order. His feelings were not those of unqualified elation, however. There were angles to the situation which gnawed at his conscience. In the first place, he didn't like the man, Gordon. There was a faint something of the professional Judas Iscariot about him which Tom had noted in dozens of police stool-pigeons, including Felix Fonck, but couldn't accurately define. 

Then, too, although the article was nominally an attack on Adolph Keufel and the small host of crooked lawyers, policemen, government officials and politicians that were an essential part of his vast and powerful organization, it was also, inferentially, an attack on law enforcement in the 

Dominion of Canada. It would more than likely be read by the public, which seldom views things in moderation, partly in that light-even as a violent indictment of the 

Capitalist system by persons of leftist leanings-rather than a simple and direct exposé of corruption. 

That was the trouble. Tom had been on the Force long enough to know that the large majority of its members were faithful, honest and industrious individuals whose hearts and souls were in the organization; who were putting the best years of their lives into it, only to receive a small government pension when they were no longer of service to it, or a government-financed funeral, as in the case of Sergeant Greenleaf and Constable Johnson, if they met with a fatal "accident." These hundreds of members were going to be hurt and humiliated by the article, along with the handful of Tweeds, LaGauzes and Symes on the 

Force. And who would be the gainers?—a group of commercial, dollar-loving magazine publishers, who didn't really care whether Canada was in North America or

Asia, so long as they could make a profit by causing sensations in their columns once or twice a month. 

At seven o'clock sharp the following evening, "Jack 

Gordon" called around at his hotel for him. The man impressed Tom as being somewhat excited and had obviously had a drink or two. Tom got in the front seat of the car with him and Gordon drove around to a quiet, secluded part of the West End and pulled up at a curb. "Will it be all right if we settle our business here, Mr.

Shields?" he asked. "I've got another appointment in about half an hour, or I'd ask you up to my suite to have a few drinks and meet some girls."

Tom did not object in the least, in fact he felt somewhat relieved, for he was getting to instinctively dislike Mr. 

Gordon the more he knew him. "That's perfectly all right," Tom told him. "Here's the contract I've drawn up. If you'll sign the three copies down here on this line, we'll call it a deal and I'll pay you the twelve hundred dollars."

Tom looked over a typewritten document which was headed: "An Agreement Re Publication of the Article, entitled, CANADA'S AL CAPONE." He read the first two or three paragraphs carefully; then commenced some judicious skipping, as he noted the document consisted of several pages of closely typewritten material. Mr. Gordon suddenly became fidgety and began glancing at his wrist watch, so Tom merely threw a glance over the last two pages without actually reading them. They looked all right. He took out his pen and signed the three sheets with a mixture of relief and apprehension. "Here's the money," said Gordon, taking a roll of bills from his pocket. "Go ahead and count it to see if it's okay." "There was no need to bring along all this money, Mr. 

Gordon," Tom told him, "a cheque would have been all right."

Tom began counting out the money, which was made up of bills of twenty and fifty-dollar denominations, rapidly. 

Suddenly a car drove up alongside theirs and screeched to a halt with its brakes on. Two men in civilian clothes jumped out of it quickly and came up on each side of the one "Gordon" and Tom were sitting in. "What are you two men doing here?" one of the men demanded curtly. Tom recognized him as a police inspector on the Vancouver Force. "Talking over a business matter between ourselves,' 

Tom said. "There's no hydrant or anything along here." "I know there's no hydrant, but what're you doing with all that money?" the Inspector asked sternly, reaching out

MAN TRAP 

301 and laying one hand on the bills Tom was counting. Tom caught a glimpse of a revolver in his other hand. "What is this?" Tom demanded, puzzled. "It ain't a stick-up, anyway, Shields. You're just coming with us to Police headquarters. Got the article there?" he asked "Gordon." "Yes and a signed statement as well," Gordon boasted excitedly.

Tom handed the money over to the Inspector with a shrug and stepped out of the car; puzzled, but not unduly excited. His impression at the moment was that the 

Keufels had used their political power to exert pressure on the Vancouver police to confiscate his article and arrest him, in a desperate, Fascist-like move to prevent its publication. It might be their downfall at last, he reflected. 

Al Capone and other underworld leaders had brought down their shaky temples of corruption by showing their hands in just such high-handed ways. The thought caused him to smile grimly.

His smile seemed to puzzle the two policemen. No doubt, if he'd known all the shifts behind the scenes he wouldn't have smiled. The Keufels were no clumsy Capones from the Italian ghetto of Chicago, obsessed with delusions of grandeur and knowing of no other means of striking down an adversary excepting by murder, but people of superior guile and cunning, with the blood of feudal Polish barons surging in their veins, blood which had successfully defied all authority, other than their own, since the fall of the Roman Empire.

He got into the car with the two policemen and was driven down to police headquarters. There, still puzzled, he was asked by the Inspector if he had any statement to make, with the warning that it might be used against him at a later date. "Why certainly I have a statement to make," Tom said. "In the first place, I'd like to know what I'm charged with; in the second place, I'd like to get out of here." "So far, there are only two charges against you," the 

Inspector said grimly. "First, writing threatening letters to a prominent citizen. Second, attempting to extort

money from him. You're an ex-policeman, I believe, so 

I needn't show you the exact sections in the criminal code. 

Under the latter section, you can get up to fourteen years. 

Have a cigarette?" "Cigarette be damned! Who is this prominent citizen

I've been writing threatening letters to and trying to extort money from?" "Adolph Keufel, the liquor magnate. "Vice baron, you mean. That explains everything. This whole thing's a frame-up, eh?” "I don't know what you mean, Shields. Not as far as we're concerned it isn't. It's a serious business to us."

XXXV 

Tom was led to a row of cells in the police station and locked up there with three or four other prisoners. He hardly noticed them. Before leaving him, the Inspector promised he would be allowed to use the telephone in fifteen or twenty minutes to phone a lawyer or relative. It was still fairly light outside and, because of this, the lights hadn't been turned on in the cells. The light about him was dull and hazy and made the whole affair seem momentarily unreal and dream-like to him. He had to pinch himself to make quite sure it was all real. 

Slowly, with his head in his hands, he began to piece the probabilities of what had happened together; and realized that "Jack Gordon" was an impostor and part of a skillfully planned human trap laid against him by the Keufels. Just how they had come to learn about his article in the first place was beyond his comprehension. Nor could he imagine what part the police had played in the affair: whether one of guileless cupidity in springing a police trap for persons with the criminal record of the Keufels, without first looking into the bona fides of the case; or of deliberate cooperation with the underworld. Both could make sense. 

Fifteen or twenty minutes passed. Then an hour. Still no sign of a policeman to take him to a telephone. Tom started making a noise by banging a wooden bench against the bars of the cells and quickly brought a young, blond policeman to the door. "How about allowing me to go to the telephone?" he asked the constable. "Nix," was the curt reply. "I have orders you're to be kept away from the phone-incommunicado for tonight. 

303

"But the Inspector promised me I could use the phone." "He must have changed his mind, then. An' listen!- 

Quit banging that bench around or we'll have to put you in the cell below where there ain't no light and damned little air."

Tom spent a sleepless night lying on the steel bunk in his cell, turning the case over in his mind. Ignorance, in such a case, is far from bliss. There was no point from which to start reasoning the situation out and preparing to battle it on the morrow. In the first place, besides the 

Keufels, he didn't know who his antagonists were, or what evidence there was against him. Unfortunately, he had read the long contract "Gordon" handed him carelessly, skipping whole paragraphs on the last two pages and signing it partly on trust. Had there been a joker inserted somewhere in it that would make the whole thing look like a blackmail deal? Then, the police inspector had mentioned threatening letters he had allegedly written Adolph Keufel. Had someone forged some such letters for the vice baron? If so, he had little to worry about: his handwriting was unusually bold and sure and, therefore, difficult to forge.

In the morning, Tom was permitted to use the telephone. 

He immediately phoned his father and told him what had happened, with a police stenographer taking down their conversation. He asked him not to inform his mother, who was in poor health and living down in the United States, and his father agreed not to. He seemed peculiarly unperturbed for a high-strung man of his age as he listened to Tom; and Tom admired him for it. When asked if any news of the case had appeared in the morning press, his father replied: "Yes, I'm afraid so, son. There's a big headline on the front page and a very ugly write-up about the whole thing. 

They seem to be out to sink you as much as they can. I was terribly shocked. I was just reading about it this very minute and was going to try to get in touch with you. 

I'll do everything I possibly can. I'm going to get in touch with the best lawyer I can get for you right after breakfast."

There was a hollowness in his voice and Tom's heart went out to him. For a man just in his seventies, who had been through two wars in his lifetime and had a good name to his credit, it was a terrific shock to have a son thrown in prison charged with blackmail. Tom momentarily felt more sorry for him than himself. He was getting a bitter taste of the power and temper of the underworld of a big city, when it dictates to police and press alike. 

Tom was informed that he would be arraigned in court at about ten-thirty that morning. At about eight-thirty, he was mysteriously led, without any explanation, out of the cells into a small room, about the size of a cupboard, with a wire screen across the middle of one wall. There he stood facing the wire screen, looking into the sharp, rat-like features of one Jimmy Johnson, a shyster lawyer whose record was all too well known to him from his police days. Johnson had the reputation of doing the dirtier jobs for the Keufel crowd, which their better class lawyers wouldn't handle. (Tom found out afterwards that, while Johnson was thus speaking to him, his own father was being refused the same privilege.) "Well, you're in a tough spot it seems, Shields," Johnson gloated with a nasal, theatrical sympathy. He didn't like 

Tom, because he had rejected him as a fitting person to recommend an Italian haunt of vice operator, Celona, for Canadian citizenship papers when Tom had been working in the C.I.B. and Tom knew it. "Too bad a nice feller like you getting in such a jam. Guess we all make mistakes sometimes, though, when we want to pick up a little spare cash. You need help pretty badly, eh?" "Right," said Tom, "got any suggestions?" "Look here now, Shields, ain't you had enough of bucking the right kind of people in this town? You know what 

I mean you could do a lot better around here if you'd only get wise to yourself. Learn to play ball with the boys. Old man Keufel's a swell guy if you play ball with him and, what I mean is, Shields, play ball. You know what I mean. He throws thousand dollar bills around to the churches and other organizations in this town like they were old newspapers and everyone's for the guy. You

know, if it weren't for him, this town would be dead and buried in a lot of things, including sport. He's a swell guy." Johnson suddenly dropped his voice to a whisper. "Look here, Shields: if you'll plead guilty when you go up in court this morning, old man Keufel will guarantee to fix it with the judge so's you get off on a technicality and he'll pay you a coupla thousand besides, just to show there's no hard feelings. Let me know who your lawyer is and I'll arrange the whole thing with him." "Go to hell, you damned shyster!" Tom stormed, tears of fury springing to his eyes, "and go back and tell the people who sent you I said they could go to the same place, too." "Think it over, lad," Johnson advised suavely, "and don't do anything rash against real nice people who are only trying to help you. The Keufels are lovely people, really-oh, okay, suit yourself." 

That morning, when Tom appeared in the prisoner's dock in court, he looked out into a packed courtroom and beheld many friendly faces, despite the unfavorable publicity given his case. His father was standing at the long counsels' table, beside Mr. DuBussey, an outstanding legal figure of Western Canada. The sight eased Tom's mind considerably. DuBussey asked for an adjournment of the case, which was immediately granted and Tom's initial appearance as an accused ended in less than a minute.
