It was one of those quirks of fate with no logical explanation to it that had sent Major Shields to DuBussey, rather than to some other lawyer; for DuBussey had legal connections with the Keufels. Not that this was anything against him, for the huge wealth of the Keufel family meant that their interests branched out far and wide beyond their smuggling and vice activities, into legitimate financial, real estate, mining and business deals. It was this branch of the Keufel interests that DuBussey handled and he had no part whatever in any of their shadier dealings.

He informed Major Shields of these things quite candidly and told him that he couldn't, therefore, defend Tom

against his own clients' interests, as an ordinary matter of ethics; but would go to court and ask for an adjournment, so that Major Shields and his son would, in the meantime, have time to find another counsel. In this connection, he recommended George Manning, a young and brilliant criminal lawyer, who had made quite a name for himself in both law and politics. 

Meanwhile, there was the matter of bail, which was set at $5000. Major Shields and Tom could not raise the amount between them; but Major Shields was confident he could find one of his friends to put up the necessary bond. While he was out for the afternoon attending to this matter, a Reverend Doctor and Mrs. MacCormickwho had come to know Tom during his university career -called at the station and put up the bail in the form of a city lot registered in Mrs. MacCormick's name.

Dr. MacCormick was a United Church of Canada minister and one of the finest speakers in the Vancouver pulpit. He was a big, hearty man of strong, North of Ireland stock, whose people had emigrated up to Canada from the Dakotas a generation before. To Tom, he was one of the rare species who could approximate Christianity in practice as well as theory and still keep from starving to death. As a result, he was one of the few ministers throughout Canada who was waging a gaining fight against the cynical recoil of the public away from the Christian Church since the Great War. He and Mrs. 

MacCormick had done this act of simple and practical Christianity without being asked to do so and in spite of the fact that Tom was nominally an Anglican. 

Tom had not suggested the MacCormicks to his father as possible bondsmen, because he considered them too nice to be mixed up in the filthy mess he was in and because he feared Dr. MacCormick might take up the cudgel against the Keufels too vigorously and ruin himself on his account. 

Churchmen were the easiest people for crooked private detectives to frame in the world.

Dr. MacCormick had read the news of the case in the papers, including an extract which stated that no one could be found who would put up bail for Tom. This both

amazed and interested him. The statement, of course, was false and obviously designed to reflect on Tom's character, since he had not been allowed to get in touch with anyone until the morning after his arrest. Dr. Mac- 

Cormick had at once talked the situation over with his wife and they had come down to the police station together and put up the bail.

They invited Tom to come and stay at their place, about eight miles out of the city, while his case was on, so as to get away from news reporters and possible further complications with the Keufels. Tom accepted the invitation gladly and gratefully. That evening, however, when he read the newspapers at the MacCormicks' he was far from being cheered up at the turn of events. One of the newspapers-and its treatment was typical-dealt with the case by announcing in its main, front-page headline: 

CITY MAN CHARGED BLACKMAIL 

Then a sub-heading ran:

Tom Shields Accused of Taking 'Hush Money' 

from Prominent Citizen. 

A lesser headline ran: 

Police Say Suspect Wrote Victim Threatening

Letters. Trap Set. Officers Waiting When Marked 

Money Paid Over.

Arrested at about 7 P.M. Wednesday on a West End 

Street after he had allegedly accepted 'hush money' from a prominent Vancouver citizen, Tom Shields, 28, is being held in the city jail on a blackmail charge. A short time ago the victim notified police that he had received a threatening letter. Two letters followed the first one, the last of which carried instructions for the payment of $1200. 

Members of the C.I.D. at once laid plans to trap the 'blackmailer.' Accordingly, as the suspected extortionist left the scene he was arrested by the officers who were waiting

in a parked car nearby. At police headquarters, Shields named his occupation as an engineer. 

After the arrest, the officers raided Shields' room in a West End hotel and seized some notepaper and a typewriter. The paper will be compared to that on which the blackmail letters were written and tests will be made of the typewriter in an effort to establish whether it was used in turning out the letters. These examinations will be made by secret, scientific tests by the police science bureau. 

Bail has been set at $5000, but no one could be found to act as a bondsman on Shield's behalf. 

The following morning, Tom and his father called on

George Manning, K.C. Manning was a tall, bespectacled man, with silken, sandy hair. Since first seeing the light of day, two years prior to the turn of the century, he had become one of the foremost criminal lawyers of Vancouver and had found time to dabble a bit in politics as well. 

Mingled with an air of deep astuteness and duplicity about him, there was the innocent suggestion of a lanky college boy who had failed to age and wear appreciably with the vears. After a brief introduction, he listened to Tom's detailed version of the whole affair intently. "How about that document you signed?-Was there any clause in it that might suggest blackmail?" he questioned. "None that I read. I read the first part of it thoroughly and it was simply an ordinarily-worded legal contract for the sale of my magazine article. I didn't read over the last part of it properly, though, because that man Gordon had said he was in a hurry and kept looking at his watch and fidgeting." "That was part of his act. However, unless the police and those other people both chose to doctor up that contract, you have nothing to fear from it. They were just a little too wise. By their juggling a proper, legal contract in with their cockeyed clauses, they've enabled you to say you only read the proper part of the contract and misunderstood the rest." "I didn't misunderstand it, I just didn't read it. "9

"It amounts to the same thing. Didn't you smell a rat when this Gordon asked you to meet him in his car like that? I mean, didn't it strike you as a bit fishy his not meeting you at a regular lawyer's office, or some place downtown, to settle your transaction?" "Certainly not. Why should it? His explanations all seemed quite logical." "I see. Then there's that marked money. How did you come to have it on you?" "I suppose he simply paid me in marked bills he got from the police." "I see after he'd gone to the police with his blackmail story. It makes sense all right. How about the threatening letters Mr. Keufel claims he got from you?" "There just weren't any. They must be forgeries." "Well, if there were no threatening letters, Shields, there's no case against you. Funny the police acting in that way. It begins to look to me that the police have gone haywire, if what you say is true-especially in view of the extremely biased statements they've apparently issued, or allowed to get into, the press. 99 "I'm telling the truth, all right," Tom said, reddening with irritation that his word was being even partially doubted. "Do you think it would be any good my going down to the police station to see just what they have got down there as evidence ?" "It wouldn't do any harm. I'd go down there myself for you, but I'm too busy these days. I'm defending those three men who are held for that bank robbery and the murder of that teller. On that account, I won't be able to see you for a couple of days. There's lots of time, though; we have a week's adjournment on the case. If you go down there, of course, don't lose your temper or get hostile with them. That might do a lot of harm." "All right, we won't."

When Tom and his father got down to the police station, they were at once greeted in a hostile atmosphere. 

The Chief of Police refused to see them and a deputy chief they saw in his stead was evasive about the 'blackmailing' letters Tom was supposed to have written. When Tom

told him plainly there were no such letters in existence and demanded to see what they were holding as evidence against him, the man's eyes evaded his and he spluttered something about their having to amend the charge sheet. 

Until then, it seemed, they were not supposed to show anyone the evidence. "If that's the case," Tom said quietly, keeping his temper with difficulty, "then why did you give all that false information to the press? It was nothing but a pack of lies, and you know it! Do you want to make me out a villain to justify your crooked frame-up?" "No use becoming violent," the deputy chief warned him uneasily, reddening and avoiding his glance, "that won't get you no place.' 99 As they left the office, they ran into Superintendent Tweed in the hall, who must have been waiting there on purpose to meet them. Tweed ignored Tom completely with an aggrieved expression which was a masterpiece at deception, but stopped and introduced himself to his father and engaged him in conversation to one side. "We're terribly sorry. . . We'll do all we can. 

Ex-member of the Force. Disgracing the good name of the R.C.M.P.," Tom heard Tweed saying in snatches in a tone of injured nobility. His father listened to him with a pathetically bewildered and inane expression, with a slight crimson smarting his cheeks. The brazen effrontery of the man, on top of his father's timidity in allowing himself to be buttonholed by him, stung Tom deeply; but he kept his temper. Losing it at that moment; starting a row with Tweed; might have cost him dearly, for there was no denying he had been maneuvred into a bad enough position as it was. Probably that is just what Tweed wished him to do. "What did you let Tweed talk to you like that for, Dad?" Tom asked his father, after Tweed finally let him go. "You know very well what I've told you about the man. I first told you he was a crook as far back as that evening in the fall of 1932 when I was shot by those men in Stanley Park. Surely your memory isn't so short you don't remember that."

"I remember it quite well, old boy," Tom's father said in an aggrieved tone of voice, "but beggars can't be choosers and Tweed seemed to be quite nice and anxious to help you, judging by the way he spoke just now." "Judging by the way he spoke!" Tom let out with an exhausted sigh. "How thoroughly English! Dad, when will you ever learn not to judge people by the way they speak? Judas betrayed with a kiss and I don't doubt but 

Tweed has one of the most convincing personalities you'll ever run across; but I know him well enough to know he's not on the level." "All right, old son, but let's not argue over it. You wish to see the Crown prosecutor also, don't you?" "Yes."

They went up to the office of the Crown prosecutor on the floor above, Tom still smoldering from the memory of the smirk on Tweed's face as they left him. The prosecutor acting for the Crown on the case was Mr. John 

Willis, K.C. 

John Willis, K.C., came of proud, United Empire

Loyalist stock and inherited all the old U.E.L. traditions. 

This meant that his ancestors had backed the losing horse in the American race of Independence; had scoffed at the idea of one, George Washington, and his undisciplined band of followers nosing out the Imperial might of Great Britain at the wire; and had lived to regret it. They had been known variously as "tories," "loyalists" and "traitors" by their opponents, preferring the second of the three designations. They had looked forward to the day when Washington's ragged urchins would finally be soundly thrashed, their leader hanging from a gallows tree and the property of all talkative, impractical whigs, like the Virginian, Jefferson, and Philadelphian, Franklin, ready to be divided amongst themselves for their loyal, even though pacific, adherence to His Majesty's cause. 

Great had been their chagrin, therefore, when events took the opposite turn and they found themselves in a similar plight to that of the defeated secessionists of the South, a generation and a half later-faced with a "reconstruction" period.

They had emigrated to Canada by the tens of thousands in covered wagons to escape such an era and had retained, as a cherished possession in their hearts, an everlasting hatred of the United States, which they had partially succeeded in passing on to their children, and even children's children, like a poisonous virus in the blood. Nor did time heal the wound as it should have done for the descendants of many of these emigrés. For, as the United States forged ahead to a leading position among nations, due to its pioneering spirit and thirst for new ideas, Canada, with its greater area and considerable resources, lagged behind, a mere appendage in the Constellation of Nations. 

Part, at least, of this failure to progress must be blamed on the reactionary, restraining influence of this large U.E.L. influx; and the slow realization and secret admission of this fact by its membership has rather served to increase, rather than decrease, its bitterness.

As an escape from the cold, factual realization, Canadian tories proudly embroider upon any slight points of comparison between Canada and the United States which seem to favor the former. Foremost amongst these comes the widely accepted myth that law enforcement in Canada is the best in the Western Hemisphere and that Canadian courts are almost incorruptible. The myth that “the

Mounties always get their man" has come to be so widely accepted as to almost rank as an axiom in some people's minds. It 

Consequently, to Canadians of John Willis' class, the reputation of the R.C.M.P. is something to be guarded at all hazards regardless of the actual facts of the case. is a religion; a mania; it is something that can be thrown in the faces of those vile liberals who talk of lower tariffs with the United States and even mention that most dreadful and spine-chilling of all words, "annexation."

After a long chat with Superintendent Tweed of the Royal Mounted, the previous evening, over the case, he became convinced that it would be in the interests of all loyal Canadians if Shields were offered up as a human, social sacrifice and sent to the penitentiary, where he could

not say or do anything more to besmirch the good name of the Force, whether he was guilty of the blackmail charge or not. Had he been a less prejudiced individual, he might have questioned the motives behind Tweed's obvious vital interest in the case as evidenced by his paying him a visit about it in the evening, just after Shields' arrest and before a single word about it had appeared in the press. 

However, no one can be prejudiced and honestly observant about anything at the same time. 

On checking over the meagre evidence against Tom, however, Mr. Willis experienced some of the uneasy qualms of a Pontius Pilate, for his alert, legal mind detected immediate flaws in the case. It had all the ear marks of a deliberate frame-up on an innocent man; especially the long and amazing contract Shields had signed. 

Moreover, he knew the reputation and methods of the Keufels all too well; knew of the peculiar conduct of witnesses the Crown had called against them on various occasions and of the suspicious 'suicide' of James Small. 

Hence, when his office clerk announced that Shields and his father wished to see him, he was not over anxious for the interview. He was afraid that it might cause his intelligence to override his pet tory prejudices and accept the view that Shields was innocent and the police, or Keufels, or both, guilty of a monstrous frame-up. 

After keeping them waiting outside his office for over an hour, however, sheer force of habit caused him to order the clerk to show them in. He braced himself for a short, crisp meeting and glanced up vindictively at them out of a pair of little, pig-like eyes that were too small for his large head. Nor was he otherwise a prepossessing person, for some pieces of shrapnel had scarred his face badly in the World War and a thick-set frame, topped by a slightly hunched pair of shoulders, cast a suggestion of a morose brown bear about him. He spoke not a word and merely regarded them with his peculiar, unsocial pair of eyes, lurched back in his chair. "We came here to ask about the evidence in connection with that blackmail charge against my son," Major Shields

explained politely. "The papers mentioned some threatening letters he's supposed to have written. I presume their information came from the police. He never wrote any such letters, and, if there are any in your possession, I'd like to see them, sir, because they're forgeries and I don't imagine the Crown wants to go into court with a case based on forged documents." "We ain't got none of those letters, Shields," Willis stated flatly. "Do you mean the whole thing's a fake?" Tom's father questioned. "Naw, I didn't say that. Mr. Keufel just boined 'em up. They were doity, threatening letters and they got his goat. He just threw 'em in the fire." "Listen, Mr. Willis," Tom let out, "is it like a man of

Adolph Keufel's brains and leadership to destroy proof like that, after he has notified the police he is being blackmailed and obviously wants a man convicted?" "I tell you, he boined 'em up." "What proof have you got that he did? What proof have you got that he received any such letters at all?" "I don't need none. I've got his woid for it." "Do you mean to say the police of this town act just on a person's word in a case like this?"

Willis looked a bit ruffled. "Mr. Keufel is a prominent citizen," he stated, as if that clinched the argument. "He may be a prominent citizen," Tom agreed, “but other people besides prominent citizens have some rights around here, don't they? Or do they? Besides, he's been convicted of big crimes, including smuggling and he's our number one crime and political boss—' "You're talking like a damned 'red', Shields," Willis growled angrily. "Superintendent Tweed's already warned me about your radical leanings and other heresies." "Oh, I see." "And you don't need to get smart about it, neither, Mister Shields. You're very much in the hands of the police right now, my smart young man; even certain of your possessions, like your typewriter."

"You're not suggesting that my typewriter will be used for making any letters as evidence against me, are you?"

Tom questioned boldly. He didn't really mean the ugly accusation which was inferred in the question, but the overbearing, hostile attitude of Mr. Willis had worn his temper thin and aroused his desire to retaliate. "Now I've already told you the letters were boined. 

Why did you ask that ugly question? Get out of this office, the two of you." "You shouldn't have said that about the typewriter,

Tom," Major Shields told him nervously, as they left the 

Crown prosecutor's office. "That sort of thing leads to a lot of bad feeling sometimes." "Good feeling and well wishes won't clear up this case,"

Tom parried, "it's already in the knock-'em-down and drag-'em-out stage.' on my nerves. "It's all very well for you to talk like that, my boy, but 

I'm just past seventy don't forget, and this thing is getting

I'm not as strong as I used to be and it's making me a little sick. God only knows if I'll be able to see it through. I got nary a wink of sleep last night, just worrying over the whole beastly mess." "I'm sorry, dad." Tom's tone contained more than an ordinary note of remorse, as he realized his clash with the Keufels was harming more than just himself. "Old son," Major Shields rambled, "you've placed yourself in the power of the devil himself. These Keufelsthe Polish swine-hate all returned soldiers, like myself, because they know we realize what they are. You being my son, they are doubly out to get their pound of flesh Their treachery to this country and to all real Christian ideals—although they make their damned, gluttonous contributions to Church funds as a smoke screen-won't let their consciences rest until they've covered every returned soldier and decent Anglo-Saxon with their Eurasian filth. 

They hate us, because they know they're under obligation to our kind for ever being foolish enough to let them into this country; and people like that have to overcome their sense of obligation and inferiority by knifing their bet-

ters in the back. It never fails. I thought you knew better than to get in their power." "I'm not the only one in their power in this neck of the woods," Tom retorted. "Take our friend Willis, dad . . .”

XXXVI 

During the ensuing week, Tom was to learn the truth of what Tweed had told him in Calgary about the time of the Macdonald executions: that when a man is accused of a crime in the newspapers, unless he can promptly refute the accusation, his lukewarm friends start falling away rapidly and there immediately springs to life a group of "I told you sos," who had decided from the start that he was a bad egg. People usually believe what they read in print, regardless of the source of its inspiration. In that lies the power and, therefore, potential menace of the press.

Tom went to the editors of the Vancouver newspapers and asked them to print a refutation of their articles about him (he knew they wouldn't publish the complete truth, on account of the tremendous political and financial power of the Keufels), but they tacitly maintained that they would accept only the police version of the affair and would require a signed document from the police department retracting the information given out, before they would take any action. When Tom, therefore, called around at the police station to obtain such a document, he was only allowed to see a police inspector "because the Chief and deputy chiefs were too busy," and the inspector, after frankly admitting the justice in Tom's demands, evaded giving him such an affidavit, on the grounds that "it was up to the Chief alone to do so."

Such is British red tape: which many maintain is the polite term covering up British craftiness, evasiveness and intellectual dishonesty. It has lain in the background of 

British triumphs; but also its notable failures, like the loss of the American Colonies and its diplomatic duplicity just prior to the Great War. 318

Mr. Manning was too busy, so he said, to carry the case into court after one week's adjournment and asked for a further week. Tom gave his consent to the further adjournment reluctantly, as he was getting impatient at the turn of events and thought he detected a sliding off and trace of insincerity about his counsel. 

Old friends began to pass him on the street without recognition and this hurt him deeply. He began to fully appreciate the power and Machiavellian cunning of the Keufels. They had morally tortured and put him to death with the same easy assurance with which a Medici might have acted against an opponent, physically, during the Italian Renaissance. He made a further attempt to obtain a retraction from the police on the statements they had issued to the press, but without result. He merely met with further evasions, dodgings and blasé passings of the buck.

Finally, after Manning had asked for, and obtained; a third adjournment, Tom lost his patience. He knew of a little, radical paper with a fair circulation amongst Vancouver's parlor pinks and reliefers, called the Common Good, which had given some of British Columbia's less scrupulous politicians and vice barons an occasional touch of the jitters, due to a gossipy comment on some of their shadier doings. Thither he went to its editor, an 

Englishman with a fair education, some radical ideas and a war wound, and told him his story. 

This man, whose name, George Smith, was as prosaic as his appearance, listened to him with considerable interest, especially about his transfer out of the Vancouver C.I.B. while still a member of the Force. "I suppose you know why you were transferred, Shields?" he suddenly asked him. "I'd sooner not discuss that," Tom said. "I want to avoid casting any reflection on the R.C.M.P. where it isn't absolutely necessary." "I see. Just the same, Shields, the date of your transfer in the Fall of 1932 came just after another date I'm very familiar with that of the mysterious death of James Small. Since you got mixed up with the Keufels enough

for them to want you out of the way, it's certain they would have wanted you out of the Province at a time when they came near to being indicted for that murder. 

James Small, let me tell you, was murdered." "Yes, he probably was," Tom admitted. "It is a very strange thing, indeed, your coming to me like this, Shields," Smith told him. "Goes to show what a small world it is, after all. I happen to be one of the witnesses, still living, to a sworn affidavit Small had taken out less than a week before he came to that mysterious end in Stanley Park. They called it suicide. In the affidavit, Small swore that the Keufels were out to get him and that he had been tipped off about it by one of their men, Spike Macdonald-a thick-set fellow with a bashed up nose. 

Ever meet him or hear tell of him?" Tom remained silent. "Anyway, Shields, Small was the chief witness in a government enquiry into the Keufel organization several years ago. They also had other reasons not to want him around. 

He was, at one time, associated in various rackets with 

Adolph Keufel, you know." "Yes, I know." "However, as against that, Keufel managed to get Small certified insane and placed in a mental institution for several months at one time. Any friends and relatives who were permitted to visit Small during those months state that he seemed absolutely sane. As against their word, though, there is that of the two psychiatrists who certified him insane. That means that the affidavit he signed can be questioned and probably discredited in court. 

That is why nothing ever came of it. You see, with people with the money and power of the Keufels, even murder can be made legal." "So it seems. 

"However, if I can possibly break this case and publish the truth, I'm going to do so, Shields." "All right, thanks."

Several days elapsed without a word about his case appearing in the Common Good, so Tom went to see 

George Smith a second time. Smith looked ill at east as soon as he caught sight of Tom.

"I see you haven't got around to saying anything about my case yet," Tom observed. "No-o," Smith admitted slowly. "One of the people I got in touch with happened to be your lawyer, George 

Manning. Mighty clever fellow that. One of the cleverest criminal lawyers in the country." "Yes, and what did he say?" "He told me he'd see I got landed in the pen for libel if I printed anything about the case." "That's funny. I don't understand-he's my lawyer." "Yeah, he's a mighty tricky one, too. Seems to be working with the Crown prosecutor, John Willis, for some kind of a compromise with the Keufel crowd to get you off." "A compromise to get me off?" "Yeah, so he says. The Keufels are out to get their pound of flesh where you're concerned, from what I hear. 

You'd better talk it over with him yourself."

Tom went straight to the law offices of Manning, Rimstad and Manning, his temper rising at every step. He found Manning in. "Hello there, Shields," Manning greeted him affably, "what's on your mind?" "I've just been to see George Smith, the publisher of that paper, the Common Good," Tom informed him. "He says you're working for a compromise on this case of mine. Is that right?"

Manning dropped his eyes uneasily and fingered a pencil. "Since last seeing you, Shields, I've done a lot of thinking on your case. I find I was a little over-optimistic at our first few meetings. I'm still convinced myself that there isn't a case of blackmail against you, but I've talked things over with John Willis and he's convinced there is one. 

Now the Keufels are a mighty dogged and determined bunch of people and have all the power and money in the world at their disposal. I've found out they very definitely have it in for you and would do almost anything to get you in the pen." "Buy me in, you mean." "Put it that way if you like, Shields. You see, if you

elect to be tried by a judge and the judge happens to be a Keufel man or the Keufels-let us say-convince him you are guilty; then you're going to be sunk like the

Titanic. Then again, supposing you elect to be tried by a jury: the police select the jury and, right now, they all seem a little panicked down there at the station over this case, because they're in the wrong and know it. They are. therefore, prejudiced against you on that account. Then, too, you've got to consider that there are a lot of good men who've been up against things since the depression and who might do a whole lot for a couple of hundred dollars these days: even to rendering a decision against their better judgment on a jury. An item like a few hundred dollars is mere chicken feed to the Keufels. I know for a fact that they've paid a hostile witness at a government enquiry against them several thousand dollars to change his mind—and evidence. I know about it, because the man had the gall to come to me and want to sue the Keufels in court for not paying the last installment of his bribe. They finally straightened the matter up out of court. So you see what you're up against." "Yes I do. And," Tom added bitterly, "I also see that this country is becoming no longer the place for white men, but the happy hunting ground of racketeers and their 'yes men' politicians. All our high falutin' justice is just a myth. The press of this country can tell any lies and boost any bum into Parliament its bosses tell it to; but if any man dares to get up on his hind legs and tell the truth, even for an instant, he's a criminal and liable to find himself behind bars. To think that real men, like 

Wolfe and Montcalm, threw away their lives fighting for it!" "You forget that the truth is sometimes dangerous,

Shields, and that, when it is, it ought to be suppressed." "How do you mean?" "A lot of important people-good, honest people, I mean-have taken an interest in your case and they'd like to see you get off, but not at any cost-not at the cost of undermining Canadian prestige, for example." "I don't get you."

"You may not realize it, but this case of yours has more possible percussions and repercussions to it than a case of T.N.T. It doesn't stop at men like Tweed or the Keufels, but goes right on up through politics to Ottawa. If gone into the way you want it to be, it would probably end up by involving a cabinet minister or two.' "Well, if it does, it serves them right. The grafters deserve to be thrown out on their ears or sent to the pen!" "That's what you say, Shields, but I'm afraid you miss the point. Ever hear of the Dreyfus case in France? "Yes." "Well, you might remember it pretty nearly precipitated a civil war in France: a nation of one race, one language and only two Christian denominations of any importance. 

Ask yourself what a similar scandal would do in this country: where we have gathered just about every race under the sun; where in Alberta, Saskatchewan and Manitoba there is a growing secessionist and annexationist sentiment; and where there is no real cohesive, nationalist sentiment as yet. Do you see what I mean? "I've looked into this case of yours a lot since last seeing you and I sympathize with you and all that sort of thing; but I've also become convinced that if the case is publicly decided in your favor, it might have serious results politically. I'm, first and foremost, a loyal Canadian.

There are a lot of other people who think, or would think, along exactly the same line, too—jurymen and judges, amongst others. While their consciences might not allow them to find you guilty, they might arrive at some sort of compromise verdict as to your sanity, for instance, to clear the atmosphere of the ugly things this case shows up. In that case, you might be worse off than ever in a practical way. "So you see, the Keufels aren't the only people you're bucking. As a matter of fact, among other things, a stranger called at Mr. Willis' office the day you were arrested and told him you were a bad lot and said the police had done well to catch up with you at last." "Who was the stranger?"

"Willis never found out his name. Have you ever done anything to justify anyone saying that sort of thing about you?" "Certainly not. 99 

The man must be a crank. "That's what I told John Willis, as soon as he said the man hadn't left his name; but the tough part about it is, he apparently made an impression on Willis." "It's a fine sort of thing when strangers, perhaps private detectives hired by the Keufels, can run in and out of the 

Crown prosecutor's office and upset his judgment like that, without even leaving their names," Tom protested. 

Manning reddened slightly as he recognized the truth in what Tom said and also its obvious reflection on an important member of his own profession. "I wouldn't talk that way, Shields," he reproved. "Let's look at the realities and disregard all sentiments. If you make a martyr out of yourself and go to the penitentiary, you'll likely come out of there a broken man in mind and spirit-if you come out at all. Then, there's a good chance you may never come out; and you want to keep that in mind. Just take the case of Tim Buck, the communist. He's a good speaker and many extreme reactionaries consider him a dangerous man on that account and because he doesn't quite see eye to eye with them. He was sent down to a 

Canadian penitentiary for three years. One night, the guards fired a volley of bullets into his cell, but he lived to tell the tale and the pen authorities had to account for their actions. They made the feeble excuse that Buck was noisy and they fired the volley into his cell to quiet him down; and that was accepted as a bona fide excuse by a commission which investigated the incident. yourself what chance you'd have under conditions like that-with the Keufels down on you. With Keufel money back of them, the guards wouldn't miss in your case, it's a cinch. Get me?

Ask "Another thing you want to get out of your head is that there's absolutely no case against you. As a matter of fact, legally, there is, because the Keufels have maneuvered you into a bad spot with that set up. Ask yourself what witnesses you can produce in your behalf to counteract the

sworn testimony of Mr. Keufel, that private detective who called himself Gordon, and the evidence of the police that you accepted marked money. On top of that, just remember that any jury sitting on the case will have been prejudiced against you by that adverse newspaper publicity you have been unable to erase. "There's no use being an idealist. The laws of this land were made by and for the rich; not the common people. If an ordinary person has the finger of accusation pointed at them by a rich person in this country, they've practically got to prove their innocence, rather than the other party prove their guilt; all the theory of British justice notwithstanding. "I know of a case where a leading citizen of this town seduced a decent, virtuous girl of ordinary, middle-class family on the promise of marriage and then ditched her when she was pregnant by him. A brother of hers, who was a young mechanic, spent all his savings sending her down to a first-class California clinic to have the child and evade local gossip. When the girl returned from the 

States, she and her brother went to her seducer and demanded that the wretch make good her brother's financial sacrifice and provide a proper fund for supporting her child. He ran them both in for blackmail and they got seven years each. They're still in there now and the child's in an orphan asylum. The rich man, whose crime of seduction under promise of marriage was a serious one, couldn't be prosecuted under the protection afforded under the extortion act. Some big time offenders, when they think the law is about to catch up with them, will deliberately frame these extortion things to obtain the protection of the act. The law protects the rich only; not the poor, in this country. "Now if you're not satisfied with the way I'm handling this case, you can drop me and go to someone else for legal advice. Suit yourself." "I'll think it over," Tom said.

He went directly to see Bob MacGusty, who he was wishing by now that he had told his father to go to in the first place, even though the Irishman did not measure

up as a top-flight, or even brilliant, lawyer. Bob's welcome to him was warm; his advice, grave and sober. "You can't throw over Manning now," he told Tom. "If you did, it would look as if no lawyer wants to handle your case for you, since Manning isn't chump enough to let people think you took the case out of his hands because he double crossed you. He'd simply convey the impression he'd thrown the case up because he thinks it no good. 

On top of DuBussey declining to handle it-for other reasons, of course-that would look bad. It would be a bad legal move and it is legal foils you're fighting with. "If you decide to take it out of George's hands, I'd be only too glad to take it on for you for nothing, but I'm warning you not to do it. Besides, I'm a pretty slow thinker compared to Manning. You know, I might as well admit-professional ethics nothwithstanding-that a topnotch lawyer must have just a wee bit of a crook about him. Maybe that's why I've not done so well myself."

There was a twinkle in MacGusty's Irish eyes. "Sure that isn't just self-justification?" Tom smiled. "Well, whether it is or isn't, Manning's got a really brilliant legal mind and his personality sways a jury every time. If he plays even half square with you, you're better off with him than with me. He knows all the ropes and then some. "What is more, a surprising number of people in this town I've spoken to are getting wise to the truth of this affair, in spite of the newspapers. It's funny how such things get around. On that account, George Manning won't sell you down the river completely, Keufels or no Keufels, because he's an aspiring politician and quite a bit of a demagogue; and a demagogue, in this country, must retain public favor. If I know anything about George at all, he'd rather be Premier of this Province some day, than pick up a loose million bucks. Mind you, I'm not saying he's any angel; and if he can get away with anything behind one's back, he'll do it; but he knows he can't get away with it in this case.” "Thanks, Mr. MacGusty, I think I'll stay with him."

XXXVII 

Several more adjournments on Tom's case advanced it through the summer into the early fall of 1936. It was a period of nerve-strain and frustration for him, as everywhere he turned, he found new evidences of public indifference on his behalf. Apart from Dr. MacCormick, churchmen who had been clamouring for reform and a war on crime from the city pulpits suddenly became mysteriously quiet in their demands and criticisms. One of these men, whom Tom knew quite well, literally ran away from him as fast as his chubby legs would carry him when he met him on the street. Tom learned afterwards, that 

Mrs. Adolph Keufel had subscribed heavily towards buying a new organ for his church. Another, a quiet little 

Anglican minister who had known him for fifteen years or more, told him frankly: "You can't apply the Holy

Gospel today, at all, Shields; all one can do is preach it and hope for the best. It's no use. The only thing to do in a case like yours is to do like Saint Paul and climb a mountain and sing and pray." "I don't need to climb a mountain," Tom replied. "I'm out far enough on a limb as it is." The minister of the 

Gospel looked puzzled. 

People's memories are short. Perhaps, that is one of 

Nature's guards against too many persons perishing through the ill effects of a bad conscience. While Tom's case was hanging in the balance, Mr. Keufel made a generous bid to obliterate some of the more unpleasant memories of himself and associates by making one of his periodic, large contributions to a charitable fund and by paying the expenses of a group of needy local athletes to a big athletic meet in the East. For which generous acts he received due credit in the press. Tom was learning, if 327

he had doubted it before, that money is the great salve that will soften most human hearts and cause almost any conscience to lapse into drowsiness.

Also militating against him as an intangible obstacle, was the American gangster movie. Through its influence, since Prohibition in the States, the public in both the United States and Canada had become softened in its attitude towards the successful vice baron and racketeer. 

Such persons had invariably been sympathetically and idolatrously portrayed on the silver screen by such movie idols as John Gilbert, Edward G. Robinson, George Raft and Clark Gable. On the same screen, policemen were usually mere drab shadows in uniforms and brass buttons, or block-headed vaudeville stooges of detectives. 

It was simply the 2000-year-old case of the mob crying, "We want Barabbas! Give us Barabbas!" repeated over and over again throughout history-like a distant, feeble harmonic of an originally loud, pure musical note. In 

Tom's day, the high priests had given way to corrupt politicians and racketeers, with the press, instead of paid agent provocateurs, their mouthpiece and power over the mob.

Strangely enough, the situation made him less bitter against the Keufels than the allegedly upright and moral section of the Vancouver public, which literally scattered for cover and lent him no aid, with the notable exception of Dr. and Mrs. MacCormick. He despised them as poltroons and cowards for thus leaving him to fight alone.

Their cowardly desertion of him was, in a sense, a surrender of the honor and self-respect of the whole community to an overbearing, truculent Polish familyexactly similar to the English surrender to bullying Germanism on a much larger scale at Munich at a later date. 

He understood why some corrupt eras last so long. Ninetenths of the opposition to them turns out to be mere windy verbiage when it comes to a real show-down.

During this time, civil war had broken out over in Spain and Tom took an interest in events over there partly to keep his mind off his own troubles. He met Paul Brooker frequently on his visiting the city from the Bridge River mining area to purchase various supplies for his company.

On these occasions, their main topics of conversation were his case, first; then the Spanish Civil War. Paul blamed both on what he termed "toryism": the one, domestic and political; the other, international and imperialistic. "There's a big show-down coming between Liberalism and Toryism right in our time," Paul stated. "Either the world is going to find a new way of getting along, or we're going to slip right back to all the old tory imperialism, wars of conquest, ignorance and a low standard of living amongst the masses. You see it in the news every day. 

Fascism and Nazism are simply decadent feudalism and toryism dressed up in cellophane."

One day George Manning got in touch with Tom on the telephone and reported to him that he'd come to an understanding with John Willis about his case and that, if Tom would meet them half way, the case against him would be dismissed. Accordingly Tom proceeded down to Manning's office, accompanied by his father. "The Keufels want you to sign a retraction of your charges against them," Manning explained. "If you'll do that, Willis has consented to drop the charge against you and admit there never was a case against you." "That's absurd!" Tom protested. "What does Mr. Willis mean by making a proposition like that? If he knows I'm innocent, then he should come forward and say so and drop the case without any further argument or asking me to sign such a paper; if he thinks I'm guilty, then he should prosecute the case to the limit. What is he, a Crown prosecutor, or an agent of Adolph Keufel?" "I wouldn't adopt that attitude, if I were you," Manning warned. "I've spent considerable time on this case of yours and have got just about the best bargain I can. You can't push people like the Keufels around in this town, you know." "For God's sake, old boy, listen to reason and sign that statement," Major Shields pleaded with Tom. "If this thing goes any farther, your mother's bound to hear of it. 

I don't know what will happen to her if she does. Sign!

Sign! Sign and stop arguing! This whole beastly mess is

wearing me down. It's ruining my nerves." Tom's father blurted out the last few words with his hands over his eyes with a nervous whine that jarred Tom badly. In all due fairness to Major Shields, it must be remembered he was a fairly old man and had been through plenty of trials in his day. "I'll think it over," Tom promised. "Call around here tomorrow morning and I'll have the paper that Mr. Willis wants signed typewritten out for your signature," Manning told him. 

The following morning Tom scratched his signature to the document George Manning held out in front of him, while his father watched with a pathetic anxiety, as if fearful Tom might change his mind even in the middle of signing. Tom barely read the document, beyond noting its general text. He felt as if he were signing away his self-respect and right to further consider himself a man and gentleman. The pitiable, eager way in which his father congratulated him over the transaction did not lighten his heart any. He would rather he had scowled on the whole dishonorable transaction. 

Two days later, the case came up in court for the last time. Tom was amazed at the statement John Willis made to the court. He knew the charge was to be withdrawn, but the Crown prosecutor, despite the previously biased view he had held, stated flatly that, in his opinion, no case had ever existed against him; that the whole thing had been a misunderstanding, due to certain prejudices held by the accused; and that he wished to drop the case. Manning immediately moved for its dismissal and the case was dismissed.

This machine-like, rehearsed court drama, along with Willis' about face, struck Tom with its total lack of sincerity and morality, rather than with a feeling of relief. 

He smiled grimly for such a young man—a smile without humour and containing only smoldering contempt—as he left the courtroom. Outside in the hall, he was met by 

Paul Brooker, who asked him to come up to his hotel with him. He had made a special trip down from the Bridge 

River area to be present at the final hearing.

"WE WANT BARABBAS” 

331 Instead of the newspapers printing any sort of retraction, or attempting a lucid explanation of the whole case that afternoon, they went after Tom with hammer and tongs a second time: this time, giving the impression that he was some sort of vindictive, crazy crank who had maneuvered himself into a false position with certain prominent citizens, presumably by writing them some meddlesome, threatening letters; had repented of his action and made a retraction of the statements. No mention was made as to who the "prominent citizens" were, but it was inferred they were amongst the city's most benign and benevolent and, therefore, his attacks on them had been particularly scurrilous and uncalled for. "You were crucified," Paul told Tom on the evening of the dismissal, referring to the newspaper write-ups. "The feudal big shots use the newspapers these days, instead of the thumb-screw and the wrack." "They're certainly pretty lousy write-ups," Tom conceded. "It's a cinch they won't go in my scrap book." "The mobs today holler 'We want Barabbas!' just like they did two thousand years ago," Paul continued, “and no wonder, with the newspapers under the control of people like the Keufels, to form their ideas for them. Today, it's 'We want Keufel! Give us good old Adolph Keufel!' "Quit comparing my case to that in the New Testament," Tom let out irritably, "it doesn't make me feel any better, it just gets on my nerves. Hell, you know I'm no saint; just a fool Don Quixote, at best! Besides, I'm a lot better off than I might have been." "What do you intend doing, now the case is over, Tom?" "I don't know." "All this publicity won't do you any good in the engineering profession, will it?" "It's just ruined me, that's all." "That's what I thought. Listen, Tom, I've got a proposition to put up to you. No one can fight corruption and feudalism in this country, because they're like a hidden cancer in its early stages. That's why you failed: you were battling a cancer; and there's no cure for it as yet. Over in Spain, it's been forced into the open in their civil war,

like a burning fever. Over there it's an honest-to-goodness struggle between the forces of democracy and the common people, versus those of feudalism and fascism. 

There, if a man's packing a gun, there's no doubt and double crossing about it: if a man's on your side, he's your friend; if he's on the other side, he's out to get you. All the big international Adolph Keufels have their chips down on that guy 

Franco. What do you say we go over there and fight for the Spanish Republic?" "Good Lord, Paul, you're crazy. Why you've got a good paying job-something that's mighty scarce these days. War's a tramp's and fool's game, unless your own country is involved in it." "I've got more than half a mind to go anyway," Paul insisted. "I'm really sorry for those poor beggars over there. If Europe goes all Fascist, it'll eventually spread over here for sure and our own people will have what little progress and high standard of living they've already achieved taken away from them, anyway." "It would be a bad proposition, even for me," Tom insisted. "It would just be jumping from the frying pan into the fire."

The same evening as Tom's case was dismissed, Superintendent Tweed paid Mr. Adolph Keufel a visit at his large estate. Tweed had been away in the East again, but had returned to Vancouver on leave for his summer holidays to see Lucille. "And how is the gallant Superintendent Tweed?" Mr. 

Keufel greeted him jovially. "Quite hotsy totsy, thanks," Tweed replied with a synthetic heartiness. "How is Mrs. Keufel and everybody?" "Just lovely-beautiful.” "I hear our friend, Shields, got off." "Yeah, but did you read the write-ups we got in the papers about him? Sa-ay, that guy'd better go away and shoot himself or hide some place. His name's mud from now on, around this neck of the woods. He'd have been better off if he'd gone to the pen. 99 "Wouldn't it have been a good idea to have had a couple

"WE WANT BARABBAS" 333 of psychiatrists examine him? You know what I mean.' "Nope, I don't think, Tweed. Those damned psychiatrists are the biggest mental prostitutes and blackmailers in the world. You know what they did to me with Small, don't you? The beggars certified him insane for me all right and then, about every two or three months, they'd ring me up on a private wire on the telephone and say: 'It might interest you, Mr. Keufel, to know that Mr. 

Small is getting much better. He's made marvellous progress and we can release him any time now.' I'd say: "The hell you can,' and would send 'em each over five hundred dollars cash in an envelope by special messenger. When they got the money, they'd phone back and say that Small was much worse and was saying bad things about me and, therefore, showing distinct symptoms of his dementia praecox, or something or other, all over again. 

That went on for almost a year, until they both raised the ante on me to an even thousand each time for Small to have one of his relapses. That was too much. It was cheaper for the chappie to commit suicide instead." "Heh, heh, heh. . . . You've got a wonderful sense of humour, Mr. Keufel. Pour me out a good stiff one, will you. Sa-ay, how would it be if I suddenly turned out to be on the up and up and working against you, Mr. 

Keufel, like one of those movie dicks? Wouldn't I have some great dope on you?" "Ha! Ha! Ha!... talk about having a sense of humour, Tweed. That's the funniest thing I've ever heard, but don't joke that way, please, because too much laughter's bad for my heart. What do I owe you, anyway, over this 

Shield's business?"

Major Shields, at long last comprehending the situation despite his rectitude, fell into the typically English habit of letting off his excess steam by writing letters. 

Instead of being so completely English as to address them to the London Times, however, he sent them to leading members of the Canadian Government, including the Governor-General. Not that it made any difference, excepting. perhaps, that the letters probably weren't read at all, as they

might have been in the Times, but were merely pigeonholed by the secretaries of the representative of His Britannic Majesty in Canada. Nor can one blame these secretaries a great deal, for the lengthy epistles the old man wrote were as vague and incoherent as they were ineffective. They were also as short on facts as they were long in protestations of fealty to the British Commonwealth of nations, for Major Shields was getting old and his mind, strained with indignation and a feeling of impotence, got the facts of his son's involved case easily muddled. Thus his letters probably did more harm than good-providing they had any effect. 

Nor is it likely that they would have done much good had they been clearly and coherently written, for Eastern Canada is not much interested in the affairs of the West, beyond regarding the West as a field for exploitation. 

Toronto and Montreal are great Imperialistic vampires which suck the lifeblood out of Western Canada while giving it nothing in return. Their protective tariffs and high-priced manufactured goods bleed the West white. 

They are modern Babylons, retarding the growth of an otherwise flourishing nation; they keep the West in economic serfdom through their ownership of everything of value, save the actual land, which they keep mortgaged to the hilt, and including the "Western" newspapers, with which they attempt to mould Western thought into their own channels and build up a synthetic loyalty to the status quo to abnormal levels.

Toronto makes an exaggerated show of loyalty to British ideals and pretends to copy London; but this is merely a smoke screen. Actually, there is nothing save an external, supercilious resemblance. London, even at its worst, has always given something back in return to the peoples it has exploited. It has pumped the blood back into the trade arteries of the far flung lands from which it has drawn it and helped in a general stimulation of business, in the manner of a heart.

Thus Major Shields' protests to Ottawa, which is essentially the child of Toronto and Montreal, fell on ears well muffled against any scandal 'not in the public interest.'

"WE WANT BARABBAS" 335 Such hidden censorship is as old in Canada as Confederation and probably more insidious than the open censorship adopted by the newer Fascist states. 

The case of Constable Shields just couldn't make the slightest capital for any of the myriad political and lobbyist cliques at Ottawa, intent on furthering their own ends. 

No one from the office of His Britannic Majesty's representative in Canada, the Governor-General, down could be found who would take it up. There were just no possibilities of political gain or aggrandizement in the thing. It was merely a case of a grave injustice done some sucker, in the political "parliamentary" view; and this view, despite its open verbiage about the rights of Man, doesn't really care whether Man has any rights or not, excepting in the most abstract, political sense. 

Thus, Major Shields' protesting letters were pigeonholed into oblivion, just as Tom's had previously been, by smirking, tight-lipped clerks working on the Government files in the office of the Governor-General, Prime Minister, Minister of Justice and the Commissioner of the R.C.M.P. at Ottawa. They were put away and allotted a number like life-term convicts: to make up a few more in the hundreds of thousands of dusty, "red-tape" items kept on file at Ottawa and credited as work to each department. As if by irony to crown the whole humbug, the correspondence was allotted the lettering S. F. No. ..., which stands for Secret File No. ....

Every form of government carries with it a seed of decay in its organism. History has demonstrated this a thousand times. The seed of decay in the case of democracy—and this is meant analytically, rather than prophetically—is the comparative ease with which graft and corruption can flourish under it; and the hidden injustices which ensue therefrom. That democracy may persist, there must he human watchdogs ever ready to check these tendencies. The real defenders of democracy are not its louder mouthed protagonists, ever quick on the trigger in condemning any and all other forms of government, but the understanding Thomas E. Deweys and

Fiorello LaGuardias, who realistically recognize its weaknesses as well as its strength and try to combat them. 

The tragedy of British democracy and its decay (and it is decaying despite the gaudy front it still maintains) has been its consistent failure, or complacent refusal, to grasp its shortcomings. It has always made it an unofficial crime to criticize inefficiency or corruption in the various branches of the civil services throughout the Empire, or to question the wisdom and honesty of certain imperialist policies. 

The punishment for non-adherence to this unwritten law is to feel the full force of the underhand spite and vengeance of an aroused officialdom. The so-called government commissions which are ostensibly formed to investigate charges of graft and corruption in British countries are, in reality, usually hand-picked bodies of men, whose sole purpose it is to whitewash those parties charged. And so the various government services continue to impress outsiders with their smartness, uprightness and loyalty, while the growth of internal decay flourishes like a stomach cancer.

This is especially true of Canada. Certain sections of the Canadian official class will stop at practically nothing to retaliate against anyone who breathes of their shortcomings. Nor is it in the interest of British imperialist policy to have anyone look behind the gaudy window dressing of imperialist items, like the scarlet and gold of the R. C. M. P. Show is the very essence and life blood of imperialism and the imperialist, be he of the British, Fascist or any other variety, believes it his bounden duty to strike down the hand that lifts the veil of tinsel aside. To the thinking person, this might account for the many strange stories of injustice, like that of Constable Shields, that are clandestinely bruited about despite suppression in the "free" press of British countries. Perhaps, too, it might account for the fact that the heads of the British state have to be more closely guarded than most dictators.

A Dewey or LaGuardia would be an impossibility in Canada. He would emigrate to the United States after being deliberately framed or otherwise discredited by a

combination of political racketeers and British imperialists. It has long been a part of British imperialist policy to build up mediocre human material for high political posts in "empire" countries, through fear of the bogeyman of certain of these countries seeking more beneficial and obvious political ties elsewhere, if their ablest native sons held the reins of government in their hands. This policy is largely carried through by the ownership of the Canadian press, almost in toto, by the tory element (whether the newspapers are nominally “liberal" or not). Mere mention of the fact by a newspaper columnist that Sir John A. Macdonald, Canada's first prime minister and foremost imperialist, had been proven guilty of accepting a "political contribution," or bribe, of $300,000 in connection with the building of a Canadian railroad, would be cause for his dismissal from almost any leading Canadian newspaper. Yet such is one of the sad facts in a long history of malfeasance of office-which is analogous to the history of Canada. 

That is why the cream of Canada's youth is not to be found serving and building up its own country; but working for American corporations; sweating under the Kleig lights of Hollywood; singing in the Metropolitan opera; or doing a thousand and one odd jobs from Singapore to Rio de Janeiro. That is the set-up back stage that made it impossible for Tom Shields to receive justice. 

In effect, his treatment was an act of official vengeance coming uncomfortably close to an attempted murder. For what faces a young man these days if he be deliberately cut down in the full strength of manhood to the status of a cripple and be provided a mock pension of sixteen dollars a month, but starvation or a life of crime? If he is too proud to become a criminal, what then? And what is the precise definition of murder, but the causing of death with malice aforethought? It is cases like this that ail the British Commonwealth of Nations and occasionally show a startled outside world unsuspected fissures of weakness at dramatic moments like that at Munich when real moral strength cannot be substituted for by a synthetic prestige.

A short time after his case had been dismissed by the court, Tom received a phone call from a Mr. James 

Reynolds, inviting him to come to see him about a job. 

Tom knew that Mr. Reynolds was in the wholesale business and had supplied the Keufel fleet of rum boats with provisions out of his warehouses during the Prohibition era. Just what other connections Reynolds had had with the Keufels, Tom didn't know; but he had heard that he was generally regarded as a good citizen. Tom needed a job, so he dropped in to see this unexpected benefactor willingly. He was greeted with a cold, clammy handshake and sly smile by a small, rotund man with an air of importance about him. "You know, Shields," Mr. Reyonlds expounded, his eyes fluttering like a butterfly's wings, "I'm a real Christian and believer in applying the Holy Sacrament to my own life. One of my friends once said that I was the truest Christian he'd ever met. Of course, that was probably a slight exaggeration, but I do have a hobby that enables me to apply the Sermon on the Mount. I employ men in my business who have slipped up along the thorny road of life." "Do you mean criminals?" Tom asked bluntly, eyeing 

Mr. Reynolds uneasily. "Er-well I wouldn't put it quite as bluntly as that, Mr. Shields. You know, we all fall into sin at one time or another. The word criminal is a nasty brand to place on a man.' 99 "It fits all right in some cases," Tom stated. "May I ask what you had in mind for me, Mr. Reynolds?" 

Reynolds smiled slyly and Tom had a feeling that he was placing himself within reach of the coils of a serpent. "I think I just explained that I am a practical Christian, Mr. Shields," he reminded Tom. "That doesn't mean I sell myself out of house and home for my employees. I am a practical benefactor of humanity. The wages I pav are not quite as high as my competitors', on account of the records of my employees." "I see."

"WE WANT BARABBAS" 

339 "I am interested in a lead-zinc mine prospect near Rossland at the moment. I am a director of the company and supply all the provisions for the camp. How would you like to manage the prospect at seventy-five a month salary?" "That isn't enough to offer a qualified engineer," Tom protested. "Take it or leave it." "I'll leave it." "This is the first time I've ever had such an offer turned down, Shields. Looks bad, my boy. It would appear to some people as though you favored a life of crime." 

Tom ignored the insult and implied threat. "I'm merely a free man at heart," he stated, "and object to being exploited like the men you usually employ. 

Mr. Reynolds' eyes darted an anything but Christian look at Tom. "You think you're too good for the people around here, don't you?" he almost spat in fury. "Well, we in this community have shown you what we can do to anyone who goes around with that attitude."

There is usually an added shock when one confirms an unpleasant fact, even though there may have been some anticipation present. Tom got such a shock when he slowly realized the publicity the Keufels had had planted in the press against him had ruined his engineering career. All avenues seemed at once closed to him in Canada: for 

Canadian universities graduate a slight excess of men in applied science each year, over and above the positions available. This means that the ablest graduates only can be sure of securing jobs. The balance diffuse out into every sort of calling from sales work to playing professional hockey. The frame-up had definitely offset Tom's ability by spoiling his reputation. Some of those persons who eulogize so-called "freedom of the press" might do well to consider one or two such examples as this. Quite often a small section of the so-called "free press" in both Canada and the United States and elsewhere is free just so long as no one offers to buy its soul. Then it often trips over itself seeing if it can't raise a bid on its body

as well. So much for one of the most famous fallacies 

Man has ever died for a free press. 

Early in the Spring of 1937, Paul Brooker looked Tom up again and told him he had definitely decided to offer his services to the Spanish Republican Government in its "fight against the forces of darkness and reaction!" "Darkness and reaction, my eye!" Tom corrected. “You talk as if we were bloody race of angels over here. 

Leave Europe to settle its own squabbles. They're no worse than we are—just a little more cynical perhaps." "No worse than we are, indeed! Why, the Germans are a race of criminals and nothing else. This world is going to have no peace until the whole damned lot of them are wiped out like rats. Look at Dutch Schultz. Look at Hauptmann, who kidnapped the Lindbergh baby. Look at-" "No use my looking, Paul, I know better. I heartily disagree with you and my own dad on that line. Germans, as a whole, are amongst the cleanest, most decent and industrious people in the world. I know they are from too many personal contacts with them-university professors, workmen, people of all kinds. I was even nursed by an old German woman one time and came to love and respect her. Every race produces an occasional monstrositylike Adolph Keufel, who is a Pole. "No, Paul, there is no use blaming the troubles of this world on the Germans, or Jews, or any one race. It isn't as simple as all that. Our troubles here in Canada are much more likely due to a tired and decaying Britain which has let the reins of Empire slip out of her hands irretrievably, leaving Canada neither a nation, nor yet a colony, but a soft, bloodless conglomerate called a Dominion. 

Such a setup is bound to benefit grafters and racketeers, because it lacks the moral and spiritual cohesion a nation needs to combat them. "That's likely one reason why the majority of our best young people eventually go down to the United States to build their careers. You can't build a house on a foundation of quicksand.

"No, Paul, there's plenty for Canadians like you to do here at home to reform your own country, without indulging in racial hatreds, or going over to Spain or elsewhere in Europe to rot on a foreign battlefield for dear old democracy. I hardly think democracy is an apt term for our 

Canadian form of government anyway. What with our crooked politics and weird courts and tory press, which spouts hatred against Communism, Fascism and Nazism to distract public attention from home, there isn't much democracy for anyone, excepting in the abstract sense. 

Maybe I'm soured against it all. I don't know. Anyway, I'm not going to let my animosity for one Polish family make me into a cat's paw for stirring up racial hatred against any one section of the population of this country. 

I'd sooner accept defeat gracefully than lend myself to anything like that.” "No one will ever thank you for taking that magnanimous attitude, Tom. They'll merely look on you as a sucker.' 

"I don't agree with you, Paul. One has to lose his faith in human nature to take that view!"

THE END
